Introduction
Latvia’s accession to the European Union occurred at a time when most Member
States felt that European integration had achieved its highest possible level and that
only a bit of effort was still needed to turn dreams about a merged and unified Europe
into reality. Ratification of the European Constitutional Treaty (ECT) was to be a
symbolic conclusion to this historical process, marking the beginning of a new period
in time.
When referenda on the ECT led to its rejection in France and the Netherlands,
however, there was much bemusement among politicians and European citizens about
the further development of the EU. Many European research institutes and centres
began to debate the future of the EU. That is why the International Environment
Research Group of Latvia’s Strategic Analysis Commission decided to focus its attention
on the development of the EU, as well. One of the most important aspects of the
research was the need to define a precise conceptual framework for analysis so as not to
sink into a swamp of policies, facts, events, statements, laws and documents. A review
of achievements and approaches taken by scholars in other countries allowed us to
conclude that there has not been sufficient evaluation of the interaction of values and
interests in terms of how this affects the further development of EU institutions and
policies. While the ECT was being drafted and signed, politicians constantly made
reference to the values which Europeans supposedly all have in common. If one looks
at the positions which Member States take in response to various policies, however,
one clearly sees that interests are on the front burner, while common values are forgotten
altogether. For this reason, the authors of this book have evaluated European values as
such and applied these to manifold EU policies, pointing also to difficulties which
Latvia shall have to take into account in the future.
This is both a timely and a very diverse issue, and that is what encouraged us to
invite different kinds of scholars to take part in our work – economists, philosophers,

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 8

8

ÞANETA OZOLIÒA

sociologists, political scientists and jurists. Several discussions among experts were
organised during the development of the project. These were attended by representatives
of the Latvian Foreign Ministry, the Bank of Latvia, the European Commission’s office in
Latvia, the Cabinet of Ministers, various political parties, and the Latvian Confederation
of Employers. Also participating in the analysis of Europe’s future were the former
French ambassador to Latvia, Michael Foucher, as well as Willem Noe from the European
Commission. During the concluding phase of the research, Latvian representatives at
various EU institutions offered their contribution to the discussion. These included
Ingrîda Labucka, Egils Levits, Igors Ludborþs and Inna Ðteinbuka.
2007 will mark the 50th anniversary of the Treaty of Rome. In reviewing the historical
importance of this treaty, Latvian President Vaira Vîíe-Freiberga, who established the
Strategic Analysis Commission, has offered her views about the most important issues
of the EU. She calls on us to be courageous in naming problems and in offering equally
brave solutions, as opposed to dealing only with that which has been achieved before.
Professor ÞANETA OZOLIÒA
Chairwoman, Strategic Analysis Commission

DR VAIRA VÎÍE-FREIBERGA,
President of the Republic of Latvia (1999–2007)

GOAL Europe – on the 50th Anniversary
of the Treaty of Rome
Historical anniversaries provide us with the opportunity to evaluate the
influence of past events on our contemporary lives. The fiftieth anniversary of
the signing of the Treaty of Rome should inspire us to contemplate the vision
of those who sought to forge a united Europe in 1957. As the inheritors of the
Europe that these illustrious men and women envisioned, we must consider
how we can further our predecessors’ ideals and generate a fresh vision for
those who will look back upon our efforts fifty years from now.
The drafting of the Treaty of Rome occurred with the devastation of the Second
World War still fresh in the minds of the Treaty’s authors, who formulated the
major principles that form the foundation of the European Union today. Four
basic freedoms – namely the free movement of goods, capital, services and
persons – remain the basis of continued European integration.
No treaty has so effectively served to ensure peace and stability on this
continent for such a lengthy period. The Treaty of Rome helped to promote the
economic growth of the European Community for a full three decades before its
first major revision, the Single European Act, took effect in 1987. Other treaties
have followed since then, at times obscuring our capacity to study the effects
of one before another has superseded it. Now our ability to offer Europeans a
viable vision for the future will require the same type of boldness that was
displayed by the authors of the Treaty of Rome.
To my mind, four fundamental matters must be at the centre of public
debate if we are to clarify our vision of the future and continue to construct a
European Union that is as sound as the edifice bequeathed to us by those who
drafted the Treaty of Rome. We could christen these concepts all together as
the GOAL Programme – governance, openness, accountability and leadership.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 10

10

Dr VAIRA VÎÍE-FREIBERGA

Governance
European integration has been facilitated by a firmly rooted civil society
among the EU Member States and effective institutions of governance. However, the challenges posed by the ongoing expansion of the EU, the continuing
need to improve the quality of life of our inhabitants, and the necessity of
enhancing Europe’s presence on the global stage require a thorough renovation
of the EU mechanisms of governance.
Although the EU has distinctly defined policies on various issues and
considerable financial resources, many people are dissatisfied with what they
perceive as irrational expenditures and with the slow pace of economic growth.
They are concerned about the possible consequences of planned reforms.
Politically, we have agreed that multi-level governance should be implemented,
but we have not yet agreed on how to do so. This is hindering the ability of the
EU Member States to adapt to multi-level governance at the national level.
Nevertheless, it is already possible to identify some common aspects of
multi-level governance that will be critical to our success. Firstly, economic,
social, environmental, regional and other policies that are introduced at different
levels should be implemented with unified goals in mind, taking care not to
constrict one’s view to any one level alone. Secondly, it is vital to maintain
effective co-operation among the institutions of different Member States at each
of these levels.
Thirdly, if multi-level governance is to succeed, then the public, private and
non-governmental sectors must all work in concert. Fourthly, while each
Member State must show initiative and defend its national priorities, we must
always keep the common goals of the Union in mind. Above all, the citizens of
the EU must be involved in policymaking at every step of the process.

Openness
The EU decades-long success has been partially due to its openness. Europeans
need to be open to new approaches to the changing global challenges, as well
as to new potential members of the organisation. Expansion has strengthened
the EU as a whole and has motivated positive transformation not only within
the new Member States, but also among the EU remaining prospective

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 11

GOAL Europe – on the 50th Anniversary of the Treaty of Rome

11

members. Latvia is an excellent example of how setting clear goals and taking
advantage of the opportunities offered by the EU can lead to rapid growth, for
Latvia now has the fastest growing economy in Europe.
Freezing or delaying the EU further expansion is not an acceptable option
for Latvia. I believe that such a loss of openness would result in lost time and
inflict irreparable damage on the democratic reforms that a number of aspirant
countries have undertaken. The Member States that joined the EU in 2004 implemented sweeping and painful reforms with the knowledge that these
would pave the way to full membership in a community of modern, liberal
and democratic states. Knowing that our efforts would be rewarded gave us
the strength to accomplish an exceedingly difficult transition. As one of the most
recent beneficiaries of the EU openness, we are convinced that maintaining
this historically important value is vital to the future of the Union.

Accountability
The rapidity of the European integration process and the need to ensure
that our structures adapt to it has created an unusual situation for Europe’s
democracies. In recent years, the European project seems to have trapped itself
in bureaucratic corridors, its theses and themes created by an elite whose voice
has become increasingly distant from the ears of the public. The two negative
results in referenda on the European Constitution should be read as clear
warning signals.
The reaction to these warnings was no less worrisome. Rather than analysing
what went wrong and subjecting the ‘Eurocracy’ to deserved self-criticism, the
electorate was made a scapegoat and condemned as ill-informed. The communication gap between the EU bureaucracy and the general public is one of
our most serious stumbling blocks. The surge of Euroscepticism in some of the
EU Member States is a clear danger sign. If we are to take these warnings seriously,
then we must convince all Europeans that the European project is truly their
project and not only that of a Brussels elite. But in order for this to happen,
politicians and the bureaucracy must be held accountable for their work. The
inhabitants of the EU need to see a clear and understandable picture of how and
where common resources are being allocated, and what benefits they derive
from common policies and projects.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 12

12

Dr VAIRA VÎÍE-FREIBERGA

Leadership
The European project is not a mere abstraction. It is an actual edifice that
was planned by politicians with high ideals, but with their feet firmly on the
ground. If we are to emerge from our current crisis of self-confidence, we need
leadership of the sort that does not stoop to populism. Unfortunately, it does
happen for some of the Member States’ political leaders to lose their way at
times, focusing on the ephemeral moods of voters rather than inviting their
citizens to a serious debate about a vision for our future. Instead of leading,
there are politicians who pander to shifting political winds by setting up
perceived national interests as incompatible with pan-European interests, thus
imperilling a project that will be our legacy to future generations.
Jean Monet and Robert Schumann, the principal architects of today’s Europe,
dreamt of a new form of international partnership that would prevent war and
bring prosperity to a continent devastated by ever recurring conflicts. Their dream
came true – Europe has maintained a course of peace, stability and prosperity
for over half a century. It is our duty and responsibility to future Europeans to
ensure that the stability, well-being and security which our predecessors
bequeathed to us is maintained and strengthened, as well as being allowed to
extend beyond the borders of the present-day European Union.

IVARS IJABS

The Dimension of Values in European Integration
Each era in time has its own recognised problems. The European Union
was established in a post-war world and at a time when major totalitarian
‘value projects’ had discredited themselves. It is not all that long ago that
masses of individuals were destroyed in the name of ‘preserving European
culture’ or ‘liberating the working classes.’ European civilisation was brought
to the brink of destruction in the name of strictly defined values, along with
attempts to support them in a consistent way. Therefore any mighty political
vision today is, with good reason, suspected of involving too much ideology.
The idea of the European Union, too, was not originally based on any
universal concept of a completely just society or the emancipation of society.
The initial goals appear to have been quite specific from today’s perspective –
peace, security and wealth in Europe. These are very practical ideals, and they
were quite sufficient back then to launch an expansive political project. As Kaube
has pointed out, the Union was established as an ‘entity which is liberated of
ideas or ‘visions’.’1
The post-war world dictated the activities of this unique organisation to a
considerable degree at first. Common values and identities are still not the
main driving force behind the EU – administrative, economic and technological
mechanisms perform that role instead. The average citizen is familiar with the
Union mostly on the basis of taxes, subsidies and directives, not political
manifestations or major ideas which everyone holds in common. It cannot be
denied, however, that over the course of time, the issue of common values has
become more and more important in the EU’s development. Initially, it was
quite an elite project, but today the Union has changed significantly toward
the direction of democratisation, and these processes of democratisation have

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

14

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 14

IVARS IJABS

expanded the dimension of values in EU politics to a very considerable degree. We are persistently asked today to define the foundations for a common
European sense of ‘us’, for a new and common identity.
The subject of values has been topical in recent EU documents. The ‘suspended’
draft of the European Constitutional Treaty, as well as a document from the
European Commission which is called ‘The Future of Europe in a Globalised
World’ are just a few examples. The values which are declared in official documents,
however, are seldom exhaustive. The problem with politically declared values
is that the authors of such documents are politicians who do not want to paint
themselves into any colour. For that reason, they define values in a very broad
and abstract way. This allows for various interpretations in the future, but it
does not make it possible to evaluate policies on the basis of their correspondence
to the declared values. A good example is the concept of ‘respect for human
dignity’, as defined in the draft Constitutional Treaty.2 It is clear that Auschwitz
and the Gulag held no respect for human dignity, but were anyone to ask
whether human dignity is best served by agricultural subsidies or investments
in science, that person would hear many different answers. Clearly, a more
specific explanation of the value orientations that are typical of Europe must
therefore be sought outside of the EU’s official documents.
The European Union has undergone rapid and dynamic development over
the last 15 years. In the late 1990s, Latvia had little hope of joining the EU in
2004. Similarly, the pace of EU integration has sometimes moved ahead of
even the most courageous predictions. This rapid development has often created
the impression that European integration processes are best understood in the
context of future projects and developments. The name of the EU ‘constitutional
assembly’ – the ‘Convention on the Future of Europe’ – makes this clear. The
fact is that the future of Europe has become both less clear and less tempting.
Recent failures such as disagreements among Member States on the issue of the
war in Iraq, the failure to ratify the Constitutional Treaty, and the fundamental
helplessness of the ambitious Lisbon Strategy – all of this means a pause in the
rapid development that has been experienced. There is a new level of confusion
about the identity, desires and capabilities of a unified Europe. Increasingly,
Europe as a community of values is questioned, and this indicates that there is
a need to balance projects of the future with an understanding of the experiences of the past, with the value orientations that have been inherited from the
past and that still have very much to do with European life.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 15

The Dimension of Values in European Integration

15

Any attempt to evaluate Europe’s future from the perspective of values
inevitably comes up against several objections. First of all, it is sometimes argued
that political life is dictated by interests, not values. Both in Member States and
at the EU level, successful development is more likely to be guaranteed by
situational cleverness and the ability of politicians to determine the most
advantageous solutions in any given situation than by any attempt to present
values as an end in themselves. Of course, specific interests can be well served
by a mention of values – can anyone object to the idea that we should all enjoy
peace, justice and respect for human dignity? Any opposition to such ideas is
unwise and also dangerous. After all, if we hold on to values, we can easily be
injured vis-à-vis the interests of others. As Puriòð has pointed out elsewhere in this
book, ‘other actors do not perform on the basis of rules proposed by idealists’ in
the global and international system.3 It has to be said, however, that this ‘realistic’
view is based on a fundamental misunderstanding. Values and interests are
defined separately as guidelines for human behaviour. Here we might point to
the ideas of the Canadian philosopher Charles Taylor – values are values because
they are not arbitrarily created and cannot be chosen.4 The horizon of values
exists already, and it is assumed to be objective. We cannot choose to value
something from now on. On the other hand, values in a modern society obtain
their specific content only through the intermediation of independently defined
interests and individual decisions. Values which do not resonate with interests
and individual decisions are perceived by people as dogmatic and empty. That
is why the declaration of general values in political documents sometimes
creates the impression of excessive verbosity. The conflict between values and
interests, in other words, is usually a conflict between those values which correspond to interests in the specific situation and those which do not.
Second, one might argue that even if values perform a specific role in political
life, they are nonetheless too abstract and open to interpretation to help in any
analysis of political processes. For instance, the European Union has always
praised concepts such as security, individual rights and economic welfare, but
that says nothing about how exactly these values are to be pursued. Values, in
other words, are too general to be of use in any attempt to comprehend the
conflicting and dynamic processes of Europe in the present day. Problems
with the EU’s Common Security Policy, for example, emerge not from any lack
of unanimity about the value of security, but rather from diversity of understanding as to what that concept really means.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

16

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 16

IVARS IJABS

This objection, however, does not reject the importance of the perspective
of values; instead, it demands diversity and ceaseless updating of that perspective. It leads us to avoid the false assumption that European values create
a harmonised and complete system in which all components are mutually
agreed and arranged in a hierarchical structure. This is simply not true. The
value orientations which have traditionally been decisive in Europe are contradictory – an orientation toward political pluralism conflicts with excessive
nationalism, and the demand for social solidarity has come up against the value
of individual freedom and accomplishment. Indeed, the world view which is
typical of Europe is based specifically on this lack of agreement about value
orientations and on the conflicts and compromises which are the result of the
diversity of opinion.
Conflicts among value orientations have been particularly common during
the last 15 years, when the EU has undergone rapid development. Individual
aspects of this are reviewed elsewhere in this book, with each author focusing
on a different aspect of the EU’s future. Of particular importance here is the
future of the Constitutional Treaty, which is still unclear. There are postulates
in the treaty which are important expressions and continuations of European
political tradition – the Charter of Fundamental Rights, the definition of EU
citizenship, and new rules regarding the relationship among various institutions.
On the other hand, the fact that many citizens rejected the very idea of the
Constitutional Treaty – as manifested most clearly in the ‘no’ vote in France and
the Netherlands in the spring of 2005 – represents a very specific expression of
value orientations and concepts, particularly in terms of the role of the state in
ensuring social solidarity and immigration controls. These are concepts which
can hinder EU integration, but it is absolutely unacceptable to reject them out of
hand as somehow being ‘un-European’. As consideration of the Constitutional
Treaty continues, it is of key importance to think about labour markets and
migration. Protection of labour markets is becoming an increasingly severe
problem in the EU, given that there is an expanding need for the economic
dynamism and competitiveness which have long been of importance in the
world view of Europeans. A similar ‘competition’ among value orientations
has also been seen in the context of the Lisbon Strategy. The political idea of the
strategy focuses on innovation and competitiveness, but it has come up against
a conservative response among political elites in the various nation states, to
say nothing of limited support in the public at large.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 17

The Dimension of Values in European Integration

17

The issues of immigration and labour market liberalisation have also brought
up the matter of the uniqueness of national cultural spaces and preservation
thereof. This is a seminal issue in terms of the self-understanding of Europe
itself. Yes, there has been much movement in the EU toward economic and
political unification, but the preservation of the individual competences of EU
Member States is by no means an unimportant issue when we think about the
Union’s future. Preservation of political and cultural pluralism is still a key
component in the European identity, it exists right alongside such concepts as
solidarity and common values. Of particular importance in this context is the
debate about multi-level governance, because this focuses not only on the relationship between the EU and its Member States, but also on the role of regional
and local governments. Here the issue of European values is very closely linked
to politics in the nation states, to their understanding of what good governance
and democratic participation really mean.
The dimension of values is also important because of the EU Common
Foreign and Security Policy. The former US defence secretary, Donald Rumsfeld, may have been the one who offered the polemical split between ‘old’ and
‘new’ Europe, but it was Europeans themselves who often interpreted this
division as meaning that there is a need to separate ‘true’ Europeans from
others once and for all. Thanks to the conflict over the war in Iraq, Europe has
created an ‘honourable opponent’ against which Europeans often define their
own identity – that opponent is the United States of America. To be sure, antiAmericanism has been around in European Union since the days of Charles de
Gaulle, but the war in Iraq has meant that anti-Americanism is no longer a
privilege of the elite in Europe. The concept has gained considerable support at
all levels of European society.5 In recent times there has been increasing insistence
upon those values which, at least seemingly, separate Europe from the USA. It
is sometimes argued that the USA is a place where politics are not kept separate
from religion, where economic neo-liberalism is the rule, where there are hegemonic ambitions, a denial of the state, mass culture and the death penalty. Any
attempt to define European values as contrary to the United States, however, is
destined to fail. The reasons for such an attempt cannot be based on a unilateral
concept about political and cultural life in America. This point has been addressed by Susan Sontag, who has argued that the USA is not anti-European, it
is rather a ‘neo-European’ culture.6 Many parts of American politics are based
on typically European ideals, they are still closely linked to those ideals. This

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

18

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 18

IVARS IJABS

means that the search for fundamental European values must be based on
more than just politically inspired opposition to a culture which maintains its
fundamental links to the Continent.
Issues related to the EU’s Common Foreign and Security Policy are particularly important in the context of values, and that goes beyond the issue of
‘damaged’ Transatlantic relations. One problematic issue is the ongoing expansion of the European Union, particularly in terms of the controversial matter
of Turkey’s possible accession. For Latvia, the political future of the EU in
Eastern Europe is particularly important, and this is something which must be
assessed on the basis not only of interests, but also of long term value orientations.
It appears that Europe has recently been coming around to the understanding
that in relations with Russia, the focus must be not only on immediate benefits
from co-operation in the area of energy supplies, but also on the future of
democratisation in Russia. It is also true that there are still uncertainties about
the EU Neighbourhood Policy and the development of co-operation with other
countries. Latvia, as an EU Member State, offers solidarity with other former
Soviet republics such as Moldova, Georgia and Ukraine. This is closely linked
to another important value orientation, one which requires durable belief in
the reliance of leaders in those post-Soviet countries upon the ideals of modern
democracies. Involvement in the development of the Common Foreign and
Security Policy requires an understanding not only of immediate challenges
and interests, but also of the more long term perspective. The debate should
therefore include the different views of various countries and regions in the
EU although the process must not lose sight of the guideposts which all of the
EU has in common – trust in human rights, democracy and the rule of law.
In this context, interesting polemics are offered by the aforementioned
Puriòð, who is sceptical about the EU as a defender of ‘universal values’ and
overall ‘harmony of interests.’ Puriòð represents the school of realism in saying
that ‘[..] those who do not experience a similar improvement in welfare think
that the concept of harmonised interests is hypocritical.’7 Such idealism, therefore, probably will not have much of a future in the EU – according to Puriòð,
the Union should instead focus on an inevitable conflict with the impoverished
world which lies outside the ‘West.’ Particularly in Latvia, however, we must
not forget that the rapid integration of our country into the EU would most
probably not have been possible without a certain amount of ‘universalism’
and ‘idealism’ on the part of ‘old’ Europe. Without it there would have been

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 19

The Dimension of Values in European Integration

19

no major support for the development of democracy and the rule of law in our
country. Any attempt to reduce these processes to the national interests of
individual Member States may be interesting from the perspective of analysis,
but it also narrows our understanding of the present and future of Europe in
an excessively hasty way.
The review of values which I propose here is not all-encompassing. My aim is
to offer a brief look at the debates which have occurred in response to questions
about European identity and the values which shape that identity. There are
several considerations which are at play here:
■ The listed values are by no means the exclusive provenance of Europe.
Political pluralism, for instance, is an inviolable component of many
nations which are not in Europe. The same is true of such ideas as the rule
of law and tolerance. Still, the way in which the world understands these
values is unquestionably European in origin – our understanding of
rights comes from Rome, and our understanding of tolerance comes from
17th-century England. Here I list those values which are of decisive importance in European politics and culture, those which are still discussed
when people debate the Continent’s future.
■ The separation of European values into the political, social and cultural
category is conditional. The issue of social solidarity, for instance, inevitably involves political mechanisms which ensure that solidarity. Still, this
separation allows us to classify values on the basis of whether they are in
line with (1) ideas about good political decisions, (2) ideas about the
‘good life’ in society and harmonised relationships among people, and (3)
all-encompassing cultural models of the world view which are typical of
European thinking as a whole.
