1. Political values
Citizenship
Any political power structure has its subjects, but only in Europe was this
relationship first defined by the special and individual status of citizenship.
The word ‘citizen’ has quite seldom been strictly defined in the history of the
world. A citizen submits to the power structure, but is also a participant in
shaping that structure. Ancient Greece was the place where relationships that
would later be of importance in Western politics were established – political
authority is based on the will of the community of citizens. Citizens are free
and equal, and one’s political relations as a citizen are kept clearly apart
from other links of dependency, whether economic, familial or other. Citizens
can take equal part in decision making when it comes to issues which affect
everyone.
Of course, citizenship also involves responsibility. Civic relations between
the state and the individual do not allow blame for public problems to be
assigned to a despotic ruler. Citizens are free to take their own decisions, but
they must then take responsibility for that which has been decided. A wellstructured and free country provides for a community of competent citizens

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 21

The Dimension of Values in European Integration

21

who are interested in the future of their community and can undertake responsibility for it.
Values which are important today are based on this ancient ideal of citizenship in Europe – democracy, individual rights and constitutionalism. True
modern democracy, of course, first developed in the United States, so it cannot
really be termed a European value, but it is European as long as modern democracy is based on the idea of the individual’s political status – citizenship.
The typically European ideal of the citizen, of course, is problematic today.
Citizenship is usually seen as nothing more than a formality, as a passport in
one’s pocket. The individual is often linked to his or her community by nothing
more than instrumental consideration – what private benefits come along with
citizenship in that or another country? Since the Cold War in particular, however, the issue of citizenship has once again become important. Societies in
Europe have become individualised and politically inert. Democracies which
are dominated by elitist and professional politicians cannot integrate public
needs and become alienated from those needs. That is why we often hear demands for a new and more content-based citizenship, one which would ensure
the individual’s autonomy whilst simultaneously renewing the individual’s
active link to the political community in which he or she lives.8
Rule of law
An inviolable component of European political identity is the law as a
systematic collection of norms which, via interpretation, are applied to various
situations. The emphasis on the legal regulation of political power has been of
considerable importance in the European understanding of politics. For the
first time in human history, there is a demand for clear and universally known
boundaries which those who hold political power may not cross. The rule of
law has proven to be of vast importance in the modernisation of Europe. The
development of economics, communications and culture has demanded that
political power be predictable. That is why liberal culture in Europe has
supported freedom – not just freedom to take part in governance but, first and
foremost, freedom from arbitrary and unpredictable interference of political
power structures in human lives. The law creates trust and predictability,
the law is that which creates a socially legitimate status for things which
people do.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

22

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 22

IVARS IJABS

The rule of law, as a typical European political value, is closely linked to
ideas about human autonomy – the ability of humans to decide on their own
activities and to take responsibility for same. The role of the law (as defined,
for instance, by Immanuel Kant)9 is not so much to regulate the behaviour of
each individual as, on the contrary, to provide each individual with a social
arena in which he or she may take free and responsible decisions. The aim of
the law is not to serve as a schoolmarm, it is to allow each individual to take
responsibility for his or her own actions. This is a tradition which leads to the
insistence of Europeans upon the rule of law in international relations, too.
Legally regulated relations among sovereign and equal countries – that is an
idea which comes from the Europe of the modern age, and it is a concept which
continues to dominate in European political thought.
Kant’s vision of a free and equal federation of states, one in which the rule of
law prevails, is one of the guidelines for Europe in this regard. Such a federation
is seen as a guarantee of international security – without rule of law, the world
would return to an order where only the strong prevail in accordance with the
natural order.10 Any other structure of international relations contains the
potential for war. This typically European vision has been particularly debated
in recent times in the context of the war in Iraq and of Europe’s split in response
to that conflict. It is well known that many European politicians insisted on the
rule of law in terms of a United Nations mandate for the war. Not all arguments,
however, are on the side of the Europeans when it comes to this particular
matter. Neo-conservative authors in America such as Kagan have argued that
this ideal has often kept Europe, unlike the United States, from being active in
the international arena. The desire to establish relations among countries
which are based on the rule of law at any cost does not allow Europeans to
defend ideals such as human rights, democracy and freedom to any great effect.
Europeans, according to these authors, behave as though such a legal federation
were already in place, and so they risk finding themselves in ‘perpetual peace’
in the most pitiful sense of that concept. Still, one doubts whether the failures
of Europe’s Common Foreign Policy can really be attributed to the insistence
upon the idea of the rule of law. No, the problem is probably a lack of political
unanimity behind the smokescreen of legal procedures and norms.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 23

The Dimension of Values in European Integration

23

Political pluralism
It is true that two of the most powerful totalitarian regimes in the world
appeared specifically in Europe during the previous century, but from the
broader perspective, it can be said that total concentration of power is not typical
in European political culture. In comparison to other regions in the world,
Europe has almost always held political pluralism to be very important. Here
we are discussing the idea that political power must not be concentrated in
any single location or any single pair of hands. Unlike despotic dictatorships
in the East, Europe has never considered absolute concentration of political
power to be a value, and this is a tradition with very deep roots. Some authors
see the origins of the principle in the ‘arranged diversity’ of ancient Greece.11
Others plump for the duality of secular and spiritual power in Western Europe
during the Middle Ages.12 In the modern age, this approach is manifested in
concepts about the separation of power. These ideas emerged in Europe and
would later become a cornerstone for modern democracies. The concentration
of power in the apparatus of the state is also seen as something that is undesirable. Charles Louis de Montesquieu, while still a supporter of the monarchy,
wrote of the corps intermediaries – the necessary ‘intermediate corporations’ between state and citizen which serve to mitigate and to control political power. This
is an element which is of significant importance in the theoretical European
tradition of the ‘civil society’ – a system in which the capacity for civic selforganisation is seen as a counterweight to the administrative power of the state.
In this context, some authors point to the fact that in European political life,
there is usually maintenance of a long-lasting balance among various contradictory interests without allowing them to develop into an open conflict.13 This
perspective helps to explain the period of the Cold War in Europe. Despite
ideological confrontations and military tensions, it never did turn into a ‘hot’
war. Authors often point out that European states have extensive experience
with wars amongst themselves, and in this they differ from the United States,
which has not seen war on its territory for a very long time. This is experience
which has affected European mentality – Europeans have lost faith in aggressive and voluntary solutions to political problems. Umberto Eco has polemically claimed that if the terrorists of September 11 had blown up not the World
Trade Centre in New York, but Notre Dame in Paris and Big Ben in London,
Europeans, unlike the Americans, would have reacted with ‘[..] horror, pain

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 24

24

IVARS IJABS

and disgust, not a shattered tone and syndrome of depression which are compensated with the instinctive desire to take immediate and inevitable revenge.’14
One can dispute this idea, of course, but there is no doubt that Europe’s longlasting experience with pluralism, conflict, compromise and fragile balance is
both unique and sustainable. This experience probably should not be subjected
to the strict federalisation of the United States, as Puriòð suggests in his paper.
The European Union’s slogan is ‘United in Diversity’, and this is more than just
an ideological slogan. Diversity really is a distinct European value. Problems
in contemporary Europe, it appears, are more caused by the first part of the
slogan – ‘united’.
Representation, parliamentarianism and transparency
Another specific element in European politics is the concept of representation.
This is a basic component of a democratic regime, one which seems self-evident
today. Citizens can nominate their own representatives in support of their own
interests, and that is neither natural nor trivial. This is a specifically European
form of political power. Until recently, it did not exist anywhere outside the
‘old world’. Representation creates a special link among those who are involved
in it – those who are delegated to represent others receive trust, and their activities depend on that trust. European representation speaks to bilateral links
between the representative and those who have chosen the representative. It is
a contract of sorts, and both sides are, at least formally, equal within it. This
structure of political life has not been found outside of Europe – everywhere
else, people have either pursued their interests in politics directly, or they have
not pursued those interests at all.
The concept of representation dates back to the 17th century, and it was by
no means democratic at that time, at least not in the modern sense of the word.
Initially, aristocrats were the only ones to nominate representatives, and later,
in England, an oligarchy did the same. It was in the UK, however, where the
tradition of parliamentarianism first emerged, and it has become enormously
important in European politics. A parliament is more than just a gathering of
representatives, it speaks to a specific way in which those representatives act.
A parliament is a ‘talking shop’, and decisions are based not on influence or
wealth, but on the best and most convincing arguments. Parliamentarianism
in Europe is based on the idea that governance is founded on debate. That is a

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 25

The Dimension of Values in European Integration

25

classical ideal which in some senses is not in line with reality today, but serves
as a regulative idea, which is still of major importance. It is no accident that
European countries typically have parliamentary or semi-presidential regimes,
as opposed to purely presidential ones.
The idea of parliamentarianism also involves the assumption that political
decisions are taken in an open and transparent way. Parliamentary debates offer
a concentrated ‘representation’ of the debates which are taking place among
the public at large. Those who take political decisions, therefore, must be able
to explain to people why they took exactly this, and not another decision. This
is a process which defines the European democratic tradition – one which, it
seems, is not yet fully in place in Latvia. According to this tradition, members
of the public must consistently attack any decision that is accepted in a closed
or secretive way, without any open debate at all
Nationalism and scepticism about it
Nationalism and the associated assumption that political power is rooted
in the will of a sovereign nation are often linked to the Judeo-Christian tradition
in European culture. Ideas about the nation as a special community of solidarity
are typical in nationalism, and they have emerged specifically from this tradition.
These ideas are based on the community’s special and ‘chosen’ mission in the
world, on the territory which it unquestionably deserves (the ‘promised land’,
for instance), etc. In this sense, the origins of European nationalism are deeply
rooted in history, but generally speaking, nationalism is a phenomenon of the
modern world, one that is linked to the modern and sovereign state, the development of democracy, and the problems of democracy, too.
If we review the literature which has been published in Latvia on the topic
of political issues since the restoration of the country’s independence from a
purely quantitative perspective, we see that the national dimension is still the
determinant issue in Latvia. Democracy, human rights, Europe and social issues
are comparatively less important in terms of the quantity of literature about
them. This is understandable to a certain extent, given the specific history of
the Republic of Latvia. On the other hand, nationalism today is usually reviewed
on the basis of considerably greater nuance and equivocation. The role of
nationalism is not denied in the present-day world, but it has long since not
been seen as an unequivocal value in Europe.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

26

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 26

IVARS IJABS

In order to gain a better understanding of nationalism, we must separate out
two fundamental dimensions within it. On the one hand, nationalism is democratic and aimed at public integration. An emphasis on nationality establishes
democratic equality among people, ensuring that politics are independent of
inherited, economic or cultural privileges. In this sense, nationalism allows
people to feel a sense of belonging to a specific community and to accept certain
rights and obligations. On the other hand, nationalism has often proven to be
divisive. National identity is often shaped specifically so as to keep apart from
another nationality or to distinguish oneself in comparison to it. The other
nationality is usually viewed in negative terms. In this sense, nationalism is no
longer integrative. The aim is, instead, to seek the image of an enemy and then
to combat the enemy with undemocratic weapons in hand.
It is specifically because of this latter fact that Europeans treat the idea of
nationalism with considerable caution. Particularly in leftist politics, nationalism
is often suspected of being less than democratic. Since the very beginning, the
EU has seriously opposed the threat of antagonistic nationalism. One of the
founding fathers of the EU, Robert Schuman, said in 1950 that one of the basic
motivations for establishing the European Communities was to overcome
nationalism in favour of that which is supranational.15 This belief is still very
much on the table – national emotions, pride and patriotism do not have to be
prevented,16 but they must be channelled into a new sense of the supranational
community. This can create new identities, loyalties and political participation.
Sometimes this view is defended on the basis of the claim that the identity of
European nations is itself based on artificial constructs which were ‘conjured up’
in the 18th and 19th centuries. This permits for the creation for higher identities –
a European Union, a Baltic Sea region or other identity. Europeans have experience with this kind of thing, and it should be put to use on a critical and
productive basis.
One must respect the problem which Puriòð has indicated – an open and
multicultural identity is less capable than an exclusive identity to promote
‘consolidation of large groups’ and the emergence of a unified and self-confident ‘European nation.’17 We must not forget, however, that from the very
beginning the EU project has been aimed against this negatively formulated
identity (Europeans versus Americans, Russians, Arabs or others). Instead, the
EU has tried to implement a ‘thin’ sense of community which is based on
democracy and human rights.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 27

The Dimension of Values in European Integration

27

Separation of church and state
Europe has traditionally ensured fairly strict separation between secular
and spiritual power, between politics and religion. Politics, in the European
view, are a completely secular process, one that is not expected to achieve
supernatural goals. Political power structures do not have to care for the souls
of citizens, they deal only with the external manifestations of their lives. If a
state thinks about the conscience of its citizens, then it inevitably limits the
freedom of those citizens. This is a fairly definitive belief in modern Europe –
the Continent is radically opposed to any manifestation of a theocracy.
We must note here that this postulate affects not just religion in the traditional
sense of the word, but also various political lessons which, like religion, seek
to create a monopolistic and unique explanation of the events of the world. We
can certainly consider Marxism, which, as numerous researchers have noted,
is quasi-religious in content. Modern Europeans sometimes stress the doctrine
of separation of church and state in contrast to the seeming ‘fundamentalism’
of the Americans. True, this is a norm which is observed quite selectively in
Europe. Religious lessons are mandatory in many European schools, and many
countries have their official state churches. ‘Old’ Europe, however, generally
tends to reject the manifestation of religiosity in the public arena. That does
not mean any rejection of religious belief, but that is a belief which is left up to
the individual and his or her conscience.

2. Social values
Welfare
High appreciation of material benefits is probably not a specifically European
thing. Wealth is much appreciated in all civilisations. Europeans, however,
have a special idea about the forms, manifestations and meaning of welfare in
human lives. First of all, Europe generally has a positive attitude toward the
ordinary and ‘earthly’ life. To be sure, cults of asceticism and poverty have not
been unknown in Europe, but they have never had a dominant influence, as,
for instance, has been the case in Indian culture. Welfare has always been recognised as a positive value, one which creates a foundation for a proper life
among individuals.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

28

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 28

IVARS IJABS

Second, Europe has always been dominated by the view that very unevenly
distributed welfare is not proper welfare. This assumption has often been far
from reality, of course, but the ‘ideal’ European will never feel happy if he has
private wealth but is surrounded by terrible poverty. ‘Social solidarity’, as
defined by modern Socialism, is certainly a fairly recent invention, but the
foundation for such ideas about the social nature of welfare in Europe was
created long before that. For instance, we are accustomed to thinking about
feudal relationships during the Middle Ages in negative terms – we believe
that to have been a terribly undemocratic and irrational system. One of the true
obligations of the feudal aristocracy, however, was to take care of people in the
lower classes, to defend them and to allow them to use resources for free. This
was true specifically because of the aristocratic status of those individuals. In
other words, ‘social solidarity’ and ‘social responsibility’ are not really anything
new. Socialists in the 19th century simply organised an exaggerated and new
type of reaction to the polarisation of welfare which was created by the collapse
of feudal relationships in the wake of the expansion of capitalism.18
For this reason, it is difficult to agree with Puriòð when he argues that the
meaning of ethical considerations and the subject of social justice in the global
economy are ‘an illusion created by post-war welfare’ – something which
Europe should eliminate as soon as possible.19 In essence this is fundamental
social intuition, and its roots are found not in abstract altruism, but in the
understanding that terrible poverty is of a dehumanising nature. Interestingly,
ideas about the meaning of overall social welfare in the lives of individuals
have been rooted in European consciousness far more deeply than one might
expect. Even neo-liberal ‘market theologians’ such as von Hayek and Friedman
do not really object to the spread of public welfare – even for them, this is an
unequivocal value. These authors do, however, try to suggest that this goal will
be achieved more quickly if unlimited free market forces are unleashed.
There are also other aspects to European ideas about welfare. Western
culture is often accused of being ‘materialistic’. True, Europeans like to accuse
Americans of this particular ‘sin’. Sustained stereotypes say that Americans
can no longer be rescued from consumerism. This is an incomplete idea even if
we ignore real differences in the European and the American understanding of
welfare in such areas as environmental concerns. A special attitude toward
material benefits is rooted in European culture and experience, while in
America, this attitude is only expressed, albeit in a fairly vivid way.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 29

The Dimension of Values in European Integration

29

One element is an orientation toward rational accumulation and expansion
of property, roots for which Max Weber sees in Protestant religiosity.20 Fundamental changes in the European economic mentality are the foundation for
this thinking. Work appeared in Northern Europe as a new and determinant
value in the modern age. Of course, people have always worked and managed
things, but physical work for self-sustenance in the antique world and during
the Middle Ages is often seen as something which resembles slavery. In the
modern age, Europeans have come up with the idea that work and enhanced
welfare are key in a good life – things which prove that the individual is living
in accordance with the structure of the world. Hard and skilful work and
economic success are granted the status of moral accomplishments, they prove
that the individual is ‘chosen’. At the same time, however, many authors have
pointed out that the religious and moral justification for economic life has
disappeared in present-day Europe. The property which is owned by an individual has itself become the highest value, it no longer reflects moral achievements. Property is what mostly determines the social existence or non-existence
of the individual. Talk of ‘European materialism’, in other words, is not entirely
unjustified.
Tolerance
Tolerance is a value which is emphasised in the European Union’s normative documents, and it is of key importance in European identity. This is a
specifically European element of mentality, one which encourages people to
distance themselves from all objective differences related to religion, race,
culture, world view, class, etc. The European is asked to see others as nothing
more than people who are similar to the European himself or herself.
There are extensive debates in our time about how we should interpret this
concept of tolerance. Its old meaning – nothing more than indulgence toward
that which is different – is no longer really valid. Many of us may dislike black
people, homosexuals or Muslims, but we tolerate them because of our charity,
love of humanity or ‘European nature’. This classical understanding of tolerance
is clearly insufficient today. Tolerance today demands not just getting along
with those who are different, but also accepting their difference. This is not the
tolerance of a humanitarian majority, it refers to the need to allow others to
define their own identity without insisting on some aspect of ‘normalcy’. In

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 30

30

IVARS IJABS

this sense, tolerance demands not only an equal attitude toward everyone, but
also an elimination of hidden inequality and discrimination, which has been
so common in European society until now. There have been patriarchal ideas
about gender relations, discrimination against representatives of other religions
and races, homophobia, anti-Semitism and the like.
The idea of tolerance today has found its most thorough manifestation in
the concept of so-called political correctness, although this has been expanded
most thoroughly in the United States. Minority groups in Western society which
are different in terms of gender, race, ethnicity, sexual orientation, physical or
mental disability, etc., are stylised as victims of cultural oppression. Being different is seen as a self-sufficient value. Existing cultural traditions are reassessed, because traditional culture, it goes without saying, has always been
dominated by ‘dead white males’, from Plato to Freud. All of this means that
the understanding of culture must be expanded to include the values of
African, Asian and other non-Western cultures.
It is significant that the spread of political correctness is not always viewed
positively in Europe. Despite the fact that the ideas of tolerance are fundamentally important, the transformation of those ideas into a militant ideology is
dangerous for several reasons. First of all, the increasing demands of political
correctness have the potential of limiting freedom of speech, and in this case
they are in violation of some of the basic assumptions of democracy. Some
European philosophers such as Þiþek have claimed, and not without reason,
that ever-present political correctness represents a particular ban against thought
(‘Denkverbot’).21 This ban is aimed against valid attempts to improve society. It
appears that Puriòð criticises political correctness from this perspective, seeing
it as an obstacle against the consolidation of the community of EU citizens.22 It
is also true that political correctness quite often achieves the unintended effect.
This applies to so-called positive discrimination and, in some cases, even greater
segregation. People who proclaim themselves to be victims are increasingly
seeking priorities in the redistribution of resources in welfare states.
Individualism
Individualism is also often cited as one of the fundamental values of modern
Europe. When asked to explain the meaning of the term, however, people offer
a wide range of opinions. Here we must look not at theoretical constructs, but

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 31

The Dimension of Values in European Integration

31

rather at the manifestation of the value of individualism in specific societies.
Individualism is an issue of culturally conditioned mentality. In modern Europe,
people see themselves as the authors of their own biographies. What is more,
the biographies often have to be rewritten several times during the lives of
their authors. Individualism today, however, is more than just mentality. It is
deeply rooted in public institutions, beginning with seemingly universal
human rights, which are construed as the rights of the individual, and ending
with social policy which has, to a large extent, been applied to the individual,
not to the family or a local community.
Individualism is not egotism, it does not refer to selfish cynicism or apathy
toward moral considerations. On the contrary, it means that all moral decisions
can be fully justified in the individual’s own judgments and in the individual’s
autonomy, which is seen as unique. The value of individualism is rooted in the
belief that the individual (and not the family, class or anything else) is the basic
building block of society. Moreover, this individual, under normal circumstances,
is able to take independent decisions on what would be best for him or her.
Individuals do not need the protection of society in living a life of full value.
At the same time, individualism also means that people do not have to interfere into the business of others without any particular need. Individualism
speaks to respect toward the private lives of other people, toward the beliefs
and ideas of a good life which others hold. In this sense, individualism also
involves a critical attitude vis-à-vis oneself, one’s judgments and beliefs. I can
be mistaken in my judgments about other individuals. I must not insist on the
absolute truth of my views even if the majority is on my side. This belief is the
foundation for the specifically European denial of capital punishment.
Individualism, however, also has another side. Alexis de Tocqueville was
the first social scientist, who viewed individualism as the fundamental problem
of modern society, arguing that individualism leads people to lose interest in
public life, to lose links with their fellow individuals, and to become locked up
in the sphere of private consumption.23 This idea reappeared in the late 20th
century, when traditional forms of community – the traditional family, the church
and the nation – were undergoing a serious crisis. Individualism was given a
distinctly egotistical tinge. People have become so individualised that moral,
ecological and social issues have lost all meaning in their eyes. In line with de
Tocqueville, authors often point to the danger that this extreme individualism
can open the door to despotism. If all people think only about themselves and

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 32

32

IVARS IJABS

their comfort, no one will stand for democratic values – after all, those values
do not serve any specific individual. This thought was developed in the 1960s by
MacPherson, who described the so-called possessive individualism in European
political theory.24 According to MacPherson, the European individual in the
modern age is constructed first and foremost as a capitalist private owner –
not only with respect to things and resources, but also in relation to his or her
own body and abilities. Modern individuals are their own capitalists and their
own exploiters. The good life is one in which the individual is as successful as
possible in exploiting himself or herself in pursuit of profits.
This, of course, is a leftist criticism of individualism, and it is not completely
acceptable. This is just a polemic emphasis on the materialistic orientation of
European understanding. There are more nuanced discussions of individualism
today. Until the 1980s, individualism of an ideological and conservative nature
was one of the slogans of so-called neo-liberals. Illustrative of this concept is
something which Margaret Thatcher famously said: ‘There is no such thing as
society, there are only individuals.’ Individualism in the Europe of today, however, does not only mean calculating market egotism. Giddens, for instance,
has written about new forms of solidarity and participation.25 These are seen
in new social movements focused on various issues (feminism, human rights,
peace, ecology, gay rights, anti-globalism, etc.) which are quite popular in developed Western countries, and they do not deny the individual autonomy and
independence of the individual. On the contrary, the movements emphasise
such individualism and take nourishment from it. The decisive thing is the
self-realisation of the individual – something which increasingly requires these
manifestations of public participation and solidarity.
Competition and the market
Another value closely linked to individualism is the productive role of
competition. Among other cultures, Europe is distinguished by the belief that
competition among individuals can serve the public interest if it is honest and
sensibly regulated. To be sure, individuals sometimes compete among themselves on the basis of their own interests and nothing else. Other members of
society, however, gain from such selfish competition when it occurs in such
fields as the economy, the arts, science, or politics. This diversity in interests,
therefore must not be prevented, it must be made constructive. Huizinga, in

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 33

The Dimension of Values in European Integration

33

‘Homo Ludens’, has stressed the role of games and creative competition in
European culture. Regulated competition is presented as a way in which people
in Europe have always settled issues amongst themselves.
The market, of course, is the most important area for competition. In traditional cultures, trade was often seen as unworthy of human beings – after all,
it seemingly creates no added value. It took the European Enlightenment to
award positive status to the open market. The market can harmonise the selfish
interests and abilities of individuals so that their selfish actions serve the interests of society. The market thus establishes links of interest among members
of the public – in order to satisfy my own needs, I am interested in your ability
to satisfy yours. The market causes each person to do exactly that which creates
the greatest public benefit. That is why there are efforts to prevent all artificial
limitations against the free circulation of goods and services in modern economies. Only then can the perfect harmonisation of individual interests and
abilities be ensured. Since the times of Adam Smith, this is an idea which has
been applied at the international level to oppose high customs tariffs, monopolies, etc. Artificial limitations on competition mean opposition to the equal
and just procedures which the free market creates in society.
To be sure, the idea of free market competition has not been without its intensive opponents. Socialists of various types have tried to prove that ceaseless
competition is dangerous to social solidarity. Conservatives, for their part, have
claimed that market principles destroy public morals and the natural hierarchical and organic order which society has accepted for time eternal. There are
many arguments which lead societies to seek protection against the excessive
involvement of market principles in spheres which operate on the basis of other,
non-commercial logic. Examples include culture, social care and education. One
of the greatest achievements of post-war Western Europe is based specifically
in this understanding – a flexible and free market economy which is, nevertheless,
socially responsible. Competition and the market are still seen quite positively in
Europe, but that belief is accompanied by the understanding that the market is
not a cure to all ills. The market economy promises welfare, responsibility and
justice, but if it is to achieve these goals, it must be rooted in specific political
and social institutions. The process demands democratic responsibility among
those who are in power. Political decisions must be transparent, and social policies must be sensible. Otherwise the market can cause very different results –
segregation, authoritarian yearnings and new forms of exclusion.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 34

34

IVARS IJABS

Free time
People are so busy today that it can almost seem problematic that one European value has always focused on the free time of individuals. In other words,
Europe has always attached a great deal of value to the amount of time during
which people can decide on what they will do and not be subject to the demands of reproduction. Over the last century, this value has manifested itself
most thoroughly in the eight-hour working day. That is why Latvia, as a European
country, probably could never implement the ‘tiger leap’ scenario of countries
in South-eastern Asia. That ‘economic miracle’ was achieved largely as a result
of enormous burdens placed on the labour force. Umberto Eco has discussed this
specific aspect of European culture in the context of how well Asian students
do at Western universities. In the future, this may prove to be a serious obstacle
against European (and also American) competitiveness, and this is something
which must be considered in the context of the Lisbon Strategy.
Free time is a value which did not appear in Europe accidentally. It originated
in Ancient Greece with its prevailing ideal of autarchy. A free man is the man
who shapes and tends to himself. The Greek word sholç, from which the word
‘school’ comes, initially referred to free time – people can educate themselves if
they have free time. Ceaseless work in pursuit of material goals means enslaving
oneself. The individual becomes dependent on external and destructive things.
Of course, not everyone can be fully devoted to this moral ideal of caring for
oneself. This is a privilege for aristocrats, and it is a system which is based on
the consistently defined groups which make up the labour force – slaves, women
and foreigners. The individual for Greeks was a person only insofar as he had
these opportunities. During the Middle Ages, too, there was institutionalised
inactivity in the form of cloisters. This is a system which is still in place today,
albeit in different form. Interestingly, this idea of ‘creative indolence’ was still,
quite recently, very important in European academic life. The idea was based on
the conviction that a scientist would do the best work if there were no external
obligations and demands – the scientist must be allowed to do the research
work freely and without being disturbed.
To be sure, the modern era and, particularly, the Reformation have been
quite critical vis-à-vis this idea. The work ethos of capitalism and rationally
organised economies turned indolence into a defect – the mother of all vice.
Europe, however, did not get rid of free time because of these ideas. Free time

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 35

The Dimension of Values in European Integration

35

continued to exist in various forms. The Socialists of the 19th century insisted
on shorter working hours not to promote laziness, but rather so that people
could improve themselves and engage in leisure activities. A guaranteed amount
of free time in developed post-war Western Europe became one of those fundamental social values which are ensured for individuals by the state.
Certainly, that does not mean people always think that self-improvement is
the only way to spend free time. In the latter half of the 20th century, another
problem has been emphasised in this area, beginning with the so-called Frankfurt School.26 Expanded social guarantees increased the amount of free time
that is at the disposal of the individual, but the ability of the individual to
spend that free time freely is quite circumscribed. Free time (such as holidays)
is increasingly administered and ‘managed’ in accordance with the demands
of the cultural industry, the tourism industry, etc. There is less and less selfrealisation and creativity as free time is entrusted to professionals.

3. Cultural values
Christianity
Christianity is one of the most hotly debated aspects of European values.
This is truly a multi-layered issue. There is no question that Christianity appertains to Europe, but there is reason to question whether it is a value in the
same sense as the other values which are listed here. First of all, Christianity is
the root of many different social and cultural phenomena which can be quite
contradictory. Christian values, explanations and Holy Scriptures have been
referenced in Europe by everyone, including absolutists such as Richelieu,
democrats such as the Puritans who were America’s first colonists, and anarchists such as Tolstoy. Second, much of what we praise in Europe today emerged in
opposition to the political, social and cultural monopoly of the Christian
church. Do we need to mention anything other than the ideas of tolerance,
modern science and rationalism? It can be argued with reason that the denial
of Christianity which was typical of Europe during the age of the Enlightenment dialectically included many elements of Christianity within itself. Still,
to reduce the distinctly liberal, post-traditional and secular culture of Europe
to Christian values would mean ignoring the trends which have taken a stand
against the moral hegemony of Christianity over the course of the last three

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 36

36

IVARS IJABS

centuries. Third, Christian faith and ideas about that faith are quite diverse in
the present day. When the pope takes a stand on behalf of including the concept
of Christianity in the preamble of the European Constitutional Treaty, he is
thinking of a very specific form of Christianity – Roman Catholicism or, at least,
ecumenism that is overseen by the Catholics. That is not the Christianity of all of
Europe, of course. There are many other important branches of the Christian faith,
and they represent very different values. The influence of Judaism and Islam
in Europe should also not be underestimated.
This suggests that Christianity in Europe is not a separate value. It shapes
an important horizon for European culture and politics in very many respects,
but if it were to be turned into a politically defensible value which can be propagandised, that would probably not be compatible with other principles on which
modern Europe is based – principles such as secular humanism and tolerance.
Enlightenment
Like Christianity, the Enlightenment is more than a value in European culture.
The Enlightenment was an intellectual movement, one which still has a great
deal to do with European ideas about the relationship between individuals and
their society. The ideas of the Enlightenment, of course, are not the exclusive
property of Europe anymore. Ideas such as human rights and constitutionalism
have become elements of Western civilisation as such. There are a few authors
such as Himmelfarb who argue against the accustomed identification of the
Enlightenment with French and German culture,27 but the fact is that the origins
of the Enlightenment are still closely linked to the European world view.
One of the fundamental beliefs of the Enlightenment is still of decisive
importance in Europe – the idea that humanity can progress thanks to the
rationality of the human being. The ability to study the physical, biological and
social world, to shape and maintain rational political institutions – this ability
leads the human species toward welfare, justice and progress. This progress is
inevitable, and it is the duty of each individual to make his or her own contribution
toward it. That can best be achieved if we reject old and untested biases and
learn to rely only on our own minds. Kant described this as ‘escape from immaturity in which man finds himself for reasons of his own fault.’28 If someone
relies only on the ideas of others, then he or she is fundamentally immature –
such people have never learned to think for themselves.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 37

The Dimension of Values in European Integration

37

This belief is closely linked to another – that rational study of reality in the
form of science can bring humanity toward joy and welfare. Science must
reject moral and religious biases and devote itself entirely to a study of reality
that is based in mathematics and logic. The more we know about the world,
the better we will be able to structure it. There is no denying that despite the
cataclysms of the past century, this faith in human ability is still the defining
factor in the European world view.
The Enlightenment is also the origin of the belief that political institutions
must be harmonised with the aforementioned demands of the human mind
and of human autonomy. Traditional political institutions such as monarchies,
classes and church privileges must be subject to merciless rational evaluation.
The state and its political institutions are not the result of heavenly revelation,
they have been created by human beings through a so-called social contract,
and people themselves can transform them. Accordingly, the state must count
on the individual, it cannot take arbitrary or confusing decisions. The state must
be commensurate with human rationality. People have the right to change the
form of the state if it cannot be justified on the basis of common sense. Individuals have inalienable rights which the state may not violate, and these are
rights which are based in the individual’s autonomy and reason. Individual
rights and constitutionalism are unquestionable. This is the cornerstone for
understanding the politics of the Enlightenment, and this is a process which is
still of great importance in Europe.
The Enlightenment, on the other hand, does not mean democracy in and of
itself. Not everyone is sufficiently enlightened to take rational decisions about
the affairs of state. In the context of present-day Europe, however, another
postulate in the political theory of the Enlightenment is more important. That
is the idea that the individual citizen has the right and even the obligation to
criticise decisions that are taken by those who are in power and to express public
views about them. During and after the Enlightenment, unlike in earlier times,
criticism did not mean disrespect or denial. Criticism now means that individuals
with reason are moving together toward the best and the most rational solution.
In practice, this idea has been implemented in the sense of ‘the public
sphere’ and in the public evaluation of political decisions. In the Europe of the
Enlightenment, there has been a rapid increase in various forums for debate –
clubs, polemical press publications, and forms of political participation. Taken
together, these form a space in which people can freely exchange views about

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 38

38

IVARS IJABS

public and political issues. Public opinion in the modern sense of the concept
first appeared during the Enlightenment. It refers not just to the collection of
views that circulate in society, but also to a critical counterweight to the
authority and power of the state. Much has been written in recent times about
the loss of this critical function of openness – commercialised media have led
public opinion to become commercialised and easily manipulated, too. Critical
debate among rational individuals, thus, is an ideal, not the reality of the public
sphere. It may be, however, that the ideals of the Enlightenment have been so
effective and durable in Europe specifically for that reason – their full achievement
has always been quite infrequent.
Inner life
One of the values of Christian origin in Europe is the importance of the
individual’s inner life – this value still determines Europe’s world view. People
who have grown up in European culture may not feel that inner life is all that
distinct or important. Its meaning is revealed more completely if we encounter
non-Western cultures (Islam, the cultures of the Far East) and their representatives. Since the times of St Augustine, Europe has attached a great deal of
importance to the individual’s inner life. The idea is that the true essence of the
individual is found not in his or her social status or external actions, but, instead,
in the soul, in the interior which is truly accessible only to the individual.29
This is what ensures true harmony between the individual and the world
order – external manifestations are only the consequence of this harmony. This
is what differs the Christian European culture from the culture of Islam, where
external activities, in accordance with religious ideas, are moral achievements
in and of themselves. In European culture, by contrast, the authenticity of the
individual is granted great importance – the individual’s inner truth, belief in
oneself, life in accordance with oneself, etc. This is indicated in the ideal of
self-realisation which is so important in the West today, no matter how foggy
that concept really might be.
Emphasis on the inner self does not mean that external and sentient reality
is denied or scorned. Absolutely not, but one’s inner self is a point of reference
for any evaluation of external and sentient reality. One also finds the idea that
despite the privileged role of the inner life, the human being tends to ‘scatter’
himself or herself among external things, yielding before the temptations and

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 39

The Dimension of Values in European Integration

39

noise of external life. People must occasionally return to their inner selves, their
authentic essence. If one wishes to be authentic, one must be able to distance
oneself from the ‘noise of the world.’
European culture, however, is not particularly focused on introspection, on
ceaseless study of oneself. In this sense, the meditative and ascetic practices of
India are clearly superior. Europe is more characterised by endless tensions
between the ‘inner’ and the ‘external’ individual. There is always problematic
interaction between these two, and this issue has been interpreted in very
different ways during various phases of European history.
This is an idea which is rooted in religious anthropology, and it has had a
serious effect on European culture. Worth mentioning are just a few phenomena –
diaries, the history of the still life, the importance of the conscience in European ethics, the emphasis on unique experience in the art of the period of
Romanticism, etc. The inner life is also a political phenomenon. It is the origin
of our ideas about the relationship which exists between the public and the
private, about the need to respect each individual’s conscience and to ensure
that individual’s right to a private life.
Irony
Irony is one of the values of contemporary Europe, both in human contacts
and in cultural processes overall. Irony does not refer to the disability to treat
things seriously or to twist reality in a conscious way. Instead, it refers to the
ability to distance oneself from one’s own perspective, and to understand that
one’s views are accidental. Europeans understand that things could seem
quite different from another perspective. At the same time, they do not have
access to other perspectives, so they have to make do with the one that they
have, simultaneously not forgetting that it is by no means the only perspective
or even, perhaps, the most complete perspective. Nietzsche wrote about this
typically European ability. He called it ‘the pathos of distance.’ Irony in this
sense involves the ability to distance oneself from seemingly self-evident explanations and evaluations. European mentality always leads one to remember
that everything could be different, and before we express categorical judgments
about others, we must remember where we ourselves are. Irony involves
distrust of simple solutions – the world has become too complex to be viewed
from just one perspective.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 40

40

IVARS IJABS

This irony is often found in thoughts about European identity. On the one
hand, European mentality has long been typified by something which Derrida
calls ‘Euro-centrism’. This is the belief that Europe (which, in fact, is a large
peninsula of the Asian continent) represents all that is universal and humane,
it represents the totality of human progress. The European is the most complete
form of the individual, and others must seek to compare themselves to Europeans if they wish to be civilised, sensible and moral beings. Europe today clearly
wishes to rid itself of this imagined privilege – it is rejected in the names of
European ideals themselves. Europeans are the only ones for whom ‘Eurocentrism’ is a problem – Africans and the Chinese do not know such concepts,
they are uncommon among Americans, too. On the other hand, Europe wants
to find new unity and a common self-understanding today, although it more
often encounters various differences and diversities. Thus the search for a European identity is based on a denial of a Euro-centric identity, and this situation
itself is unquestionably ironic.
Historicity
The portal of the building which houses the National Archives of the
United States bears the text ‘The Past is a Prologue’. This is a statement which
allows us to define a specific form of European difference. In Europe, the past
is something more than a prologue to the future. History is a form through which
European culture explains social, political and cultural phenomena. European
thinking is characterised by the belief that one can understand a process if one
looks at its historical development. Hegel was the first to suggest this approach in Europe. History tells human beings about the logic of the world’s
structure, and it is the job of these human beings to learn to explain it – nothing
more. The European needs history not only because it allows one to learn about
the experience of others and to learn from their mistakes, but also because
history offers a look into the meaning of phenomena and allows us to understand where we are. For illustration, we can mention the important role which
Europeans attach to historical disciplines of science – the history of philosophy
in philosophy and the history of sociological thought in sociology.
The emphasis on history has often served as a justification for accusations
of conservatism. This is not entirely valid – the status of an heir to historical
development sometimes causes one to become immune against new ideas or

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 41

The Dimension of Values in European Integration

41

courageous solutions. At the same time, we cannot really deny that for Europeans, history can also serve as a reason for quite radical change. The Holocaust, for instance, left such deep tracks in German and European mentality
specifically because this massive crime was in complete contradiction to the
idea of European ideals which had emerged for such a long time before – that
the European is humane, rational and supportive of individual rights. History
is more than a collection of memories, it also calls on us to seek out new identities.
The role of history in the search for a European identity can be explained
through practical considerations, as well – history still distinguishes Europe
among all other regions of the world. Modern civilisation, which has spread all
around the world today, found its origins in Europe and nowhere else. It must
be said, therefore, that Europe and its values are in something of the situation
of King Lear. The best daughters – political democracy, modern science and a
capitalist economy – have already departed to become universally recognised
achievements of civilisation. For that reason, history is and will remain Europe’s
strong side.
