TOMS ROSTOKS

Debates about the Future of the European
Constitutional Treaty During a Period of Reflection1
Yet eagerness for a finished constitution may actually hold up progress.2
David Mitrany, 1943

The European Constitutional Treaty has much to do with the future of the
European Union. It would not be proper to view the future of the EU exclusively
in the context of the Constitutional Treaty, however, as it is shaped by many
issues, not just institutional restructuring. Neither can it be said that the
Constitutional Treaty is an unnecessary document, because it would have introduced changes which leaders of EU Member States and governments had
agreed were necessary.
In the late May and early June of 2005, the European Constitutional Treaty
(ECT) was discussed to a greater extent than had ever been the case before.
That was not because of the content of the treaty, but rather because within just
a few days, it was rejected by two EU Member States in referenda. What is
more, the rejection came from countries which were among the six original
founders of the EU. They had never been seen as countries in which ratification
might prove to be a big problem.3 The confusion which followed the French
and Dutch referenda was declared to be the beginning of a period of reflection
at a meeting of the Council of the European Union in June 2005. Leaders called
not only for a discussion about what to do with the ECT, but also for a broader
debate as to what the European Union’s residents expect of it.
The aim in this paper is to assess debates about the future of the ECT which
occurred in EU Member States between June 2005 and the autumn of 2006.
First, a brief description will be offered of the current situation with ratification,
looking also at the foundations for the crisis that has occurred.4 Next there will
be a consideration of initiatives and views proposed by EU Member States and
institutions as to whether Member States should or should not continue to
ratify the ECT. The third section will be devoted to various possible scenarios

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 44

44

TOMS ROSTOKS

on the basis of the aforementioned initiatives and viewpoints, looking particularly at how events might develop in 2007.

1. The existing situation with ratification
and the essence of the problem
Ratification of the ECT was hindered from the very beginning by the fact that
confirmation of the treaty had to occur in 25 Member States and in 25 different
ways. There was much public discussion about those countries which required
a referendum for ratification and those in which ratification was the job for
national parliaments. This is not really a proper comparison, because in many
countries which were assigned to one or another of the aforementioned camps,
the process of ratification was, in fact, far more complex than might have
seemed at first glance.5 Still, the overall division was between countries with
referenda and those with parliamentary ratification (Table 1).
Table 1

Division of countries by method of the ECT ratification6
National referenda

Czech Republic, Denmark, France, Ireland, Luxembourg,
Netherlands, Poland, Portugal, Spain, United Kingdom

Parliamentary vote

Austria, Belgium, Cyprus, Estonia, Finland, Greece, Hungary,
Italy, Latvia, Lithuania, Malta, Slovenia, Slovakia, Sweden

The division which is shown in Table 1 allowed experts to make assumptions
about those countries which might face the greatest difficulties in the ratification process. The content of the treaty was agreed by an intergovernmental
conference, and so there was no real reason to believe that any parliamentary
vote on ratification would be a problem. The main question focused on those
10 countries which were to hold a referendum. Even as the treaty was being
drafted it could be predicted that the biggest difficulties might occur in the
United Kingdom. The Czech Republic and Poland were also mentioned as
potentially problematic countries.7 As the process developed, France and the

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 45

45

Debates about the Future of the European Constitutional Treaty..

Netherlands were added to this list. After the referenda in the two countries
had taken place, also Denmark, where voters had rejected the Maastricht
Treaty, was considered to be a difficult case.
The rejection of the treaty in France and then, a few days later, in the Netherlands was certainly a heavy blow to the ratification process. It did not end, however, and several countries have ratified the ECT since the two referenda
(Table 2).
Table 2

Ratification of the ECT in EU Member States8
Ratification before French referendum Lithuania, Hungary, Slovenia, Spain, Italy,
Austria, Slovakia9, Belgium, Greece, Germany
Ratification rejected

France, Netherlands

Ratification since Dutch referendum

Latvia, Luxembourg, Estonia, Cyprus, Malta,
Finland

No ratification yet

Poland, Czech Republic, Portugal, United
Kingdom, Ireland, Sweden, Denmark

Most of the countries which have not ratified the ECT have simply suspended
any consideration of the issue. This, to a certain extent, contradicts a paragraph
in the treaty which refers to a situation in which one or more countries refuse
to ratify the treaty. The idea is that at the conclusion of the entire ratification
process, the European Council is to meet so that heads of state and government can decide on what to do next. According to this, the ratification process
should proceed, and only afterward, depending on how many EU Member
States have ratified the ECT, should a collective decision be taken on how to
proceed. The authors of the treaty have been praised for their far-sightedness
in providing for the possibility that one or more Member States might not
ratify it, but that is somewhat pointless, as it is very difficult to imagine a
European leader who would attend a meeting of the Council and vote to ratify
the ECT after his or her people have rejected it in a referendum.10 It is only
logical, therefore, that other countries have decided to wait until a solution to
the ECT issue is defined.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

46

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 46

TOMS ROSTOKS

What is the essence of the ratification crisis? The scope of this paper does
not make it possible to cover all problems related to ECT ratification, so reasons
for the dissatisfaction of people in France, the Netherlands and other countries
shall not be reviewed here – that is the subject for other research projects. The
essence of the ratification-related problems involves a dilemma which has
appeared among those countries which wish to continue the ratification
process and those which have not done so and/or do not wish to do so. On the
one hand, 16 EU Member States have ratified the ECT, and it is clear that it will
not be easy to ‘kill’ it if anyone wants to do so. On the other hand, two countries
have rejected the treaty, and ratification in others has been postponed to an
unknown point in the future. During the last 18 months, many people have
said that the ECT is too full of ambition and that it is not really all that important
from the perspective of the EU’s future, but that is not really true. Most political
leaders in the EU Member States admit that rather sooner than later the EU will
have to engage in some institutional restructuring.
We can conclude that this is not a black-and-white issue, because between
the desire to continue the ratification process and the desire to ‘bury’ it, there
is a wide area in which solutions to the problem are being sought. None of the
countries which have not ratified the ECT have indicated that they plan to do
so in the near future. Progress could occur in 2007, but it is becoming increasingly
clear that no simple solution to the ECT ratification crisis is available. This
means an evaluation of the existing situation with respect to proposals from
Member States as to what should be done. The proposals from Member States
and EU institutions will have to be reviewed so as to come up with qualified
assumptions about how the ratification process might develop further.
Let us now turn to the positions taken by EU Member States and institutions vis-à-vis ratification of the ECT, focusing also on proposals that have been
made as to further movement in this regard. These proposals serve as the basis
for the third section of the paper, where possible solutions will be considered.

2. Solutions proposed by Member States and institutions
More than a year has passed since the Council of the European Union declared its period of transition, and so the time has come to look at the thinking
of EU Member States and institutions about the ECT during this process. Only

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 47

Debates about the Future of the European Constitutional Treaty..

47

then can the importance of the treaty in the future visions of the EU really be
assessed. It must be admitted, that countries have used differently the period
of reflection, and the activities have been quite varied. In some cases, the
transition period has been declared to be a period of inaction. The review of
Member States and institutions was structured as follows: first, consideration
was given to what Member States did during the transition period; then the
focus was on the initiatives of EU institutions since the French and Dutch
referenda. EU Member States can be divided up into four categories in terms
of their activities during the proclaimed period of reflection.11
1. Countries which have ratified the ECT and have not displayed any
significant activity during the period of reflection
This group includes Greece,12 Slovenia,13 Cyprus,14 Malta15 and Luxembourg.16 Their investment in the process of ratification and reflection stopped
with ratification, and none of these countries has produced any significant
initiatives as to what should be done about the ECT in the future. Neither have
these countries been active participants in debates about the future of the EU,
and in most cases, they have focused little attention on the provision of public
information about EU issues. These are countries which do not have sufficient
political weight to promote any initiatives in this regard, but they also tend to
be the ones in which there are no domestic debate about EU issues. The view is
that it would be better for the initiative to come from other countries.
2. Countries which have ratified the ECT and have also come forward
with significant initiatives and/or have demonstrated significant activity
during the reflection period
Here we are speaking of Lithuania,17 Hungary,18 Italy,19 Austria,20 Slovakia,21
Belgium,22 Germany,23 Spain,24 Latvia,25 Estonia26 and Finland27 – these are
countries which have ratified the treaty and have offered significant initiatives
vis-à-vis the future of the ECT, or else have engaged in active debates about the
future of the EU. Some of the countries in this group are small, and during the
course of the period of reflection, they have engaged in active discussions
about the future of the EU. Austria is included in this group, because during
the first half of 2006 it was the presiding country in the EU. Belgium’s presence
in the group was dictated by an initiative on closer integration which was

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

48

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 48

TOMS ROSTOKS

proposed by Prime Minister Guy Verhofstadt, as well as by fairly lively debates about the ECT and the future of the EU.
Inclusion of Spain in the first group of countries could be questioned, because
issues concerning the ECT and the future of the EU are not really important
there. Spain, however, is a large country, and its views will be important in
2007, when Germany makes proposals as to how best to resolve the ECT crisis.
That is why the author has chosen to list it in the second group. There was an
election in Italy in the spring of 2006, so not very much information was
focused on the future of the EU and the matter of the ECT. Since the election,
however, the new head of government has been a former president of the
European Commission, Romano Prodi, and this suggests that the country will
play a more active role in the future.
Debates about the ECT have been very weak in Slovakia, but the country is
listed in the second group as a result of the fact that a convincing parliamentary
vote in favour of the ECT still did not complete ratification of the document. A
group of people from Slovakia’s civil society have appealed the ratification
before the country’s Constitutional Court, asking it to rule whether a referendum
should have been organised. This essentially indicates that debates about the
ECT are ongoing in Slovakia. It is unlikely that the Constitutional Court will
issue a ruling before the time when there could be further movement in the EU
with regard to the treaty, and there is also the possibility that the ruling might
be more political than legal in nature.
Germany is the country upon which the greatest hopes are resting, because
it has sufficient political weight to take leadership. What is more, Germany is
presiding in the EU in the first half of 2007. Berlin has been less than generous
with proposals, limiting itself to announcements that it supports further ratification of the treaty. There is no doubt, however, that Germany will make
further proposals during its presidency, not least because Finland, which was
the previous presiding country, has collected information about the views of
Member States on the matter of the ECT.
3. Countries which have not ratified the ECT and have been inactive
during the period of reflection
In countries of the third group, the political elite and/or members of the public
have negative views about the ECT, or else there have been no significant

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 49

Debates about the Future of the European Constitutional Treaty..

49

debates about the future of the treaty and the EU during the period for reflection. Countries in this group include Sweden,28 the Netherlands,29 Poland,30
Portugal31 and the Czech Republic.32 Sweden is in the third group because it
has not ratified the ECT. No proposals have been made as to what to do in
response to the crisis, and there have been no real public debates about the
future of the EU. This is in part because there was an election in Sweden in the
autumn of 2006, and the political agenda was full of other issues. The Swedish
government in office prior to the election supported the ECT and considered it
to be a balanced and useful document. It has been suggested, however, that
Sweden prefers a resolution to specific problems, as opposed to debates about
the EU long-term developmental visions, complete with endless quarrels about
the best institutional structure for the Union.
The Netherlands is a country in which the European Constitutional Treaty
has been stricken from the public agenda. The government feels that no matter
what happens during the period of reflection, it will not change its view on the
ECT. It does not deny that EU institutions will have to be restructured, but because the results of the Dutch referendum were received with such seriousness,
the government has no plan to discuss the current text of the treaty or any
other version thereof. The Dutch are not debating the ECT or the future of the
EU, but the government is trying to learn more about what local residents think
of the EU.
Ratification of the treaty in Poland is made difficult by the fact that Warsaw
considers the ECT to be harmful to the country’s national interests if it is
compared to the Treaty of Nice. That means that the process of ratification
would be fraught even though most Poles have a positive opinion of the EU as such.
During the period of reflection, the Polish government has not demonstrated
any real initiative in terms of debates about the future of the EU, to say nothing
of the ECT. Parties in the governing coalition do not support the treaty.
Initiatives from the civil society and the academic environment have not been
powerful enough to launch any real debate of the ECT and the future of Europe.
Portugal planned a referendum on ECT ratification, but it has now been
postponed for an indeterminate period of time. The Portuguese government
has said that ratification will involve a referendum if EU heads of state and
government take a collective decision to restart the ratification process. Portugal
is in the third group of countries because there have been no major debates about
the ECT and the future of the EU. The period of reflection was interpreted as

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

50

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 50

TOMS ROSTOKS

an opportunity to consider the causes of the crisis. In June 2006, the period was
extended at least until the summer of 2007, because Portugal will take over the
presidency of the EU in the second half of the year. That means that Portugal
may soon shift to the fourth group of countries. The Portuguese government
has generally positive attitude to the ECT, but it does not think it very likely at
all that the document will ever be ratified. Portugal has also questioned the
idea of a Europe of projects.
The Czech Republic is in the third group of countries because it has not ratified
the ECT and has no plan to do so in the foreseeable future. There have been no
major debates about the future of the EU, and there has been no real activity
during the period of reflection. An election was held in the Czech Republic in
2006, and the future of the ECT and the EU was not of much interest during
the campaign season. It has to be noted that no real debate emerged after the
election either, because the 200-seat Czech parliament has split exactly in half,
with 100 seats going to the former governing coalition and 100 to the former
opposition. A temporary government was installed pending a resolution to the
situation. The bottom line is that the ECT is of no real interest to the people of
the Czech Republic at all. Overall views, moreover, tend to skew toward the
negative, and so a referendum would be a fairly certain way of scuttling the
treaty altogether.
4. Countries which have not ratified the ECT but have developed
significant initiatives and/or have engaged
in extensive activities during the period of reflection
Here we refer to the United Kingdom,33 Denmark,34 France35 and Ireland.36
In the United Kingdom, there are negative views as to the ECT both in the
political elite and in the public at large, but during the latter half of 2005, the UK
was the presiding country of the EU, and London introduced its presidency by
announcing that a new debate about the future of the EU was necessary. The
Conservatives who are in opposition and the Labour Party which is in power
agree that the ECT is dead, but the difference between the two parties is that
Labourists have still been talking about the idea that institutional reforms are
needed in the EU. The British government does not support the current
version of the treaty, but it does believe that certain changes in the institutional
architecture of the EU are necessary. This view is based in part on the British
presidency of the EU.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 51

Debates about the Future of the European Constitutional Treaty..

51

Denmark is a country in which comparatively intensive debates have occurred during the reflection period as to the future of the EU. Prime Minister
Anders Fogh Rasmussen has suggested that the best solution to the ECT crisis
would be a ‘mini-Constitution’, one which would address the EU’s most important institutional functions and future challenges. Denmark supports the
idea that the ECT should be implemented in a gradual way, and it supports
the resulting ideas about Europe.
In France, no serious political party feels that another referendum could be
held on the existing version of the ECT. During the period of reflection, there
have been lively debates about the EU and the future of the ECT in France. The
debates suggest that France supports the idea of a Europe of projects, one which
would allow the people of the EU to understand the practical benefits of the
Union’s existence. With respect to the ECT, the view is gradually developing
that either there must be work on a new treaty, or just the first and, perhaps
second part of the current treaty should be ratified.
Ireland, for its part, feels that there is no point in ratifying the ECT until the
situation which emerged in June 2005 is resolved. The government supports
ratification of the treaty, and during the period of reflection, government,
academic and non-governmental institutions have all taken active part in debating the future of the EU.
The European Parliament (EP) and European Commission (EC) are important
political entities at the EU level, and so a review of initiatives, views and
proposals would not be exhaustive if only the Member State positions vis-à-vis
the ECT were reviewed. The EP and EC wish to be important participants in the
development of EU policy, and it is also true that several Member States point
to the two institutions as the required source of proposals on resolving crisis.
The EP’s position on the ECT is clear and unequivocal. In January 2006, the
EP voted to renew the ratification process while also proposing that there be a
series of discussions with members of national parliaments in the various Member
States. The EP supports the current version of the ECT, and in a resolution which
was approved in June 2006, it declared the ECT to be a ‘global compromise.’
The EP disagrees that the treaty should be implemented bit by bit, and it does
not support any proposals to that effect. It is recognised that France and the
Netherlands will not organise a new referendum on the current version of the
ECT, but the EP still feels that the process of ratification must be continued so
that by mid-2007, the treaty has been ratified by at least 20 Member States. In

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

52

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 52

TOMS ROSTOKS

that case 80% of Member States would have ratified the treaty, and heads of
state and government would certainly have to decide on future steps. As noted,
most of the Member States which have not ratified the ECT have no intention
at this time of doing so in the near future, but the EP has called on those countries
to announce their future plans for the period of reflection, as well as when
they might ratify the ECT.
EP support for the current version of the ECT could be explained by virtue
of the fact that its norms would enhance the role of the EP in the legislative
process. It must be said, however, that there is no unanimity in the European
Parliament as to this issue. The relevant resolution was approved, but only by
a vote of 347 in favour, 212 opposed, and 70 abstaining. This indicates that more
than one-third of MEPs either do not have a strict position on the matter, or
they do not support the placement of pressure on the European Council or the
Member States.37 It is not precisely known why so many MEPs voted against
the resolution or abstained, but it may be that they simply did not support the
treaty as such, or that MEPs have no faith that the countries which have not
ratified the treaty will end up doing so at some point in the future.
The European Commission does not have a unified position vis-à-vis the
ECT, and for a long time it avoided making any precise statements about the
future of the treaty. The most important EC contribution during the period of
reflection has been ‘Plan-D’,38 which was launched in October 2005. This plan
is meant not just for the period of reflection, but work on it did begin after the
European Council said in June 2005 that the period must be one of discussion
involving political parties, social partners, parliaments and the civil society.39
There have been various responses to ‘Plan-D’, because some people see it as
an opportunity to take part in debates about the future of Europe, while others
take it as yet another example of the EC’s bureaucratic approach to life. There
have also been requests for the broader involvement of the EU’s residents in
the Union’s political processes from the academic environment. Scholars have
called for the establishment of a community of citizens or a new and democratic
convention, but these initiatives have not won widespread support. ‘Plan-D’,
moreover, can only partially be seen as an attempted investment in resolving
the ECT crisis.
During the period of reflection, the EC has not taken a unified position on
ratification of the ECL. The president of the EC, José Manuel Barroso, has
suggested that he supports implementation of certain elements of the treaty if

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 53

Debates about the Future of the European Constitutional Treaty..

53

ratification of the full document proves to be impossible. In the latter half of
2006, however, the EC’s overall views about the ECT slowly became visible.
The commissioner for communications, Margot Wallström, has said that the
Commission wants the solution to the ECT issue to be based on the existing
treaty and that the first and second parts of the treaty should remain unchanged. Commissioner Wallström has particularly emphasised that there
should be no new negotiations about issues which proved to be particularly
difficult to agree on during the intergovernmental conference.40 This applies to
the membership of the EC and the distribution of votes in the Council of Ministers. It has to be noted that the EC position is not in line with that taken by
certain French politicians and EU Member States, but it is quite similar to the
views which have been expressed by Germany. Assuming that referenda
would be organised on a new version of the treaty not just in France and the
Netherlands, but also in the other remaining EU Member States, the focus now
must be on how to make the current version of the ECT more attractive in the
eyes of EU residents. One proposal is to address issues which are significant to
Europeans – the fight against climate change, for instance. Still, it is too early
to talk of any specific activities in this regard.

3. What to do with the ‘comatose’ ETC?41
As noted before, 2007 is going to be an interesting year for the EU and its
Member States, because Germany will take the presidency of the Union in the
first half of the year, and a presidential election will be held in France.42 The
issue of the ECT and the difficulties which have occurred in ratifying it lead one
to think about the structure of EU governance. If Germany comes up with new
ideas on the resolution of crisis, it will still be true that the governance of the
EU is, to a certain extent, accidental. It is just a coincidence that Germany is the
presiding country ‘at the right moment.’ We can only imagine the consequences
if the presidency was held by a country which has not ratified the ECT and
does not intend to do so, or by a country that has tried to ratify the treaty but
has failed in the attempt. What about the presidency of a country which
merely does not have enough political weight? One must not exaggerate the
ability of the presiding country of the EU to influence the Union’s political
agenda, but leadership by a country which does not want the ECT ratification

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

54

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 54

TOMS ROSTOKS

process to move further at all would destroy the treaty without much effort.
During its presidency, for instance, Britain tried to turn the debate about the
ECT into a discussion about the EU future. A British member of the European
Parliament, Andrew Duff, has claimed that during its presidency, the UK
did absolutely nothing to resolve the ‘constitutional crisis.’43 It can be argued
that the difficulty in ratifying the ECT makes clear, in and of itself, that there
must be institutional reforms in the EU so as to overcome the refusal of
individual Member States to deal with issues that are unpleasant or uninteresting to them.
Two major positions have emerged in the context on importance of future
EU institutional restructuring. One vision stresses the significance of institutional restructuring although it is not a unified whole, of course, within it there
are lots of different views about what kinds of restructuring are needed, how
quickly this should happen and how extensive the restructuring should be.
Valéry Giscard D’Estaing, the former president of France who chaired the EU
Convention, feels that Europe must first reform its institutions. He argues that
unless institutional shortcomings are reversed, any attempt to focus on policy
will fail. From this perspective, the ECT is a key to further development of the
EU – without it, practical issues cannot be handled at the level of overall
policy.
The opposite end of the spectrum of views is represented by British Prime
Minister Tony Blair, who would prefer to neglect the ECT and focus on practical
issues instead. This was made very clear when the prime minister appeared
before the European Parliament when Britain took over the EU’s presidency.44
Blair and those who agree with him argue that EU Member States should not
attempt to define the ‘only proper’ institutional framework for the Union.
Instead, they should focus on specific projects which allow people to believe
that they really need the EU, ones which make a significant investment in the
betterment of human welfare. It appears that the problems related to ECT
ratification have also forced French President Jacques Chirac to support the
idea of ‘a Europe of projects’.45
Researchers have proposed various ECT crisis-resolving scenarios, and these
largely depend on each researcher’s view as to what is and what is not possible
in the existing situation. Similar alternatives are proposed in many scenarios,
but the ideas of researchers as to the most likely result of the process are very
divergent. Some believe that the ECT is ‘dead’, others think that ratification

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 55

Debates about the Future of the European Constitutional Treaty..

55

both should be and can be continued. Still others think that none of the proposed solutions is possible in political or legal terms. They question whether
the crisis can be resolved at all. This suggests that the ECT crisis has not been
reviewed in isolation from the overall development of the EU, and that means
that the crisis might ‘overflow’ its boundaries and threaten the future of the
EU as a political union. Katinka Barysch has listed three possible scenarios for
resolving the ECT ‘problem’, while other authors have drafted more detailed
scenarios. Sebastian Kurpas and Justus Schönlau, for instance, have defined
five possible ways in which the ECT crisis can be resolved.
This author has his own views as to the future of the ECT and how it might
affect the future of the EU. The following scenarios are based on a sampling of
research.
1. The ratification process continues
This position is taken by most of the countries which have already ratified
the ECT. Ratification, indeed, continued even after the referenda in the Netherlands and France. Five Member States have ratified the treaty since June 2005,
but there are others which certainly are not prepared to do so. This first scenario
speaks to a continuation in the ratification process until at least 80% of EU
Member States have ratified the treaty. In that case the European Council would
have to take a decision on what to do with the treaty and the Member States
which have not ratified it.
There are several problems with this alternative. First of all, it seems very
unlikely in purely political terms right now that 80% of the EU Member States
will ratify the ECT. Second, a decision by the European Council on the future
of the treaty would have to be taken anonymously, and any one country could
veto such a decision. Even if governments were prepared to accept the ECT
unanimously, as was the case when the treaty was signed at Rome in the autumn
of 2004, there would still be a question as to the referendum results in France
and the Netherlands. It is entirely possible, too, that some other Member State
might still hold a referendum in which voters reject the ECT. Third, there would
be the question of the legal consequences of such a unanimous decision, because the ‘no’ vote in France had both political and legal consequences. The
legal issue could be handled via another referendum, but it is politically impossible to organise a second vote on the exact text that has once been rejected.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

56

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 56

TOMS ROSTOKS

Among France’s leading politicians, none has even suggested that a second
referendum might be organised.
The aforementioned difficulties do not, however, mean that the possibility
of further ECT ratification can be totally excluded. EU leaders have agreed to
extend the period for ratification and agreed that in the spring of 2007 Germany will make new proposals vis-à-vis the destiny of the treaty, so at least
theoretically it is possible that the ECT will eventually be ratified by all countries,
albeit, possibly, with small changes.46 True, French and Dutch politicians have
demonstrated no particular enthusiasm as to further ratification of the current
text of the treaty, but proposals have been made to supplement the text with a
social protocol. That would be aimed at soothing down voters, because many
people in France and the Netherlands voted against the treaty in the belief that
it would threaten their social systems. According to Kurpas and Schönlau, an
additional protocol would not resolve all of the relevant problems, and it might
create new ones. If the social protocol were simply a declarative document, it
might be less than convincing. If it included legal requirements, it would have
to be ratified by those countries which have already ratified the ECT. That
would create additional complications. The inclusion of a social protocol, moreover, might create even greater scepticism in the United Kingdom, where the
ECT is already seen as a product aimed at ensuring the survival of the persistent
social systems which prevail in France and Germany. This would be unacceptable to British voters. In short, most of the surveyed authors feel that further
ratification of the treaty is quite unlikely, if not impossible.
As noted before, the idea of a ‘Europe of projects’ has also arisen in the context of further ratification of the ECT or the implementation of just some of its
parts. Various versions of this idea have been presented in France, the United
Kingdom and the European Commission. The idea is that the EU is used as a
framework for intergovernmental projects both during the period of reflection
and afterward so as to renew voter trust in the idea that the EU is necessary
and that its existence provides true benefits. A Europe of projects is an attractive
concept, but then one has to ask whether the development of the EU has not
always been based on projects. Major integration projects implemented by EU
Member States include things such as the unified market, enlargement of the
EU and introduction of the euro. These have been quite successful, but perhaps they have been too successful. The idea of a Europe of projects is too
general at this time, because there are no plans as to which projects might be

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 57

Debates about the Future of the European Constitutional Treaty..

57

pursued, and it is not very likely that Member States would find it easy to agree
on specific projects or priorities.47 There is also no guarantee that successful
projects in the area of further integration would encourage people to vote in
favour of the ECT or a similar document in the future.
2. The ECT is ‘buried’
It is clear that this would be the preference of those countries in which
ratification of the ECT would be hard or impossible in the current political
situation.48 Countries in this group include the United Kingdom, Poland, the
Czech Republic, the Netherlands and Sweden. These are countries which neither
want ratification of the existing text to continue, nor a new version of the ECT,
arguing that the EU has other problems to resolve instead. When it comes to
innovations in the ECT, these countries feel that most can be introduced
through existing treaties. It can be asked, of course, how long the EU will be able
to operate on the basis of its existing treaties and how effective such operations
might be. Kurpas and Schönlau, for instance, argue that the EU can continue
to operate on the basis of the Treaty of Nice for a while longer. That would be a
forced scenario, but if Member States cannot agree on how to move forward, the
EU will have to continue to work on the basis of existing treaties. If none of the
large EU Member States takes the leadership role in offering a widely accepted
way of moving forward, then changes in the current institutional structure
might occur only as the result of a serious political crisis – one which would force
heads of state to reach agreement on a common position.
The Common Foreign and Security Policy is sometimes cited as a reason why
ratification of the ECT should continue, because the treaty contains several key
innovations with respect to the CFSP. Some researchers have taken a closer
look at the further development of the CFSP even if the ECT is rejected, and
have concluded that this would not be too serious a blow against the policy.
Karen Smith has analysed the most important elements in terms of foreign
policy development in the ECT. Among them is the creation of an EU foreign
minister and the establishment of a European External Action Service. The EU
would become a subject of international law, and the treaty also contains an
article on solidarity.49 The EU’s Petersberg missions would be expanded to
include disarmament operations, military consultations and assistance among
Member States. There would be work on conflict prevention and post-conflict

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 58

58

TOMS ROSTOKS

activities. A Defence Agency would be established to enhance co-operation in
the field of defence. Smith argues that of all of these reforms, only the first three
are not being introduced already, adding that the foreign minister, the External
Action Service and the idea of the EU becoming a subject of international law –
these are not urgent and, especially in the case of the latter reform, particularly
important issues.50 This means that political will, not formal rules in a treaty, are
needed for enhanced co-operation in the field of foreign and security policy.
3. Dividing up the ECT into component parts51
This scenario speaks to the implementation of only those elements of the
Constitutional Treaty which would not require a referendum. The idea is supported by those countries which feel that the ECT is dead, but that changes must
nevertheless be made to existing EU treaties. Some fragments of the ECT must,
therefore, be ‘rescued’. Barysch argues that France is among the countries
which hold this view, noting that the current French president and the leading
candidates in the presidential election of 2007 all support the introduction of
just certain elements of the treaty. Barysch believes that European Commission
President Barroso also supports this approach.52
Kurpas and Schönlau have noted that this approach is attracting increasing
support not just among Member States, but also in various EU institutions. If
Barysch claims that Barroso defends the idea, then Kurpas and Schönlau believe
that there are also several members of the European Commission who do the
same. Javier Solana, the European Union’s high representative on matters of
foreign and security policy, might also support the introduction of those parts
of the treaty which would lead to institutional restructuring so as to enhance the
EU’s capabilities in the fields which he oversees. True, this approach is difficult
in that countries would certainly have problems in defining those elements of
the ECT which are the most important and should be introduced first. Neither
would it be easy to define those which could be implemented through the
existing legislative basis of the EU’s operations without organising new referenda.
4. Launching new negotiations and starting from scratch
Authors whose works were studied for the purpose of this paper almost
completely rejected the possibility that this might happen, because the proposal

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 59

Debates about the Future of the European Constitutional Treaty..

59

to draft a completely new treaty has come from very diverse political actors
who would find it difficult to reach agreement in practice.53 Several major
problems would emerge if work began on a new treaty. It would certainly not
be a good idea to draft a new political document which is similar to the existing
one, because the result of national votes would probably not change. If a new
and different treaty were to be created, there would be disputes about the
institutional framework within which it would be assembled – an intergovernmental conference or a new convention? Experience with the ECT suggests
that Member States would not be thrilled about involving themselves in this
kind of project again, and they would not want to invest time and resources if
there were no certainty about the results. It is most likely that Member States at
this time are not prepared for a massive and very ambiguous project of this time.
5. Ratification of the first and (perhaps) second part of the ECT
This is a proposed compromise aimed at satisfying both those who want to
continue ratification of the existing text and those who want to implement just
certain parts of the Constitutional Treaty. This option, too, is not without some
serious problems. First of all, it would be hard to avoid referenda in quite a
few EU Member States, and political leaders would be displeased about the
possibility that even an abridged version of the ECT might be rejected once
again. Second, there could be legal problems. If only a few Member States were
to ratify one part of the ECT, there could be questions about the application of
various other sources of law. It is not known whether it is even possible to take
the first or first and second part of the ECT while ignoring the rest of the document. Neither is it clear how countries which have ratified the entire ECT would
form relations with countries which have ratified only a few parts thereof. It
must be noted that existing agreements would remain in force, at least in the
second group of countries as defined above. Kurpas and Schönlau argue that
this would be a very complicated scenario in legal terms, but it would not be
impossible.
6. A crisis which spells the end of the political union
This less concerns the ECT as such and more the way in which the crisis
affects the future of the EU as a political unit. Here the starting point is the

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

60

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 60

TOMS ROSTOKS

same as when considering the scenario of ‘burying’ the ECT, but the consequences are quite different. The ‘burial’ scenario may sound pessimistic, but it
is in fact quite optimistic if compared to the possibility that the EU might fall
apart altogether. The point is this: since the French and Dutch referenda, the ECT
has been ‘dead’, but this is just the beginning, because the failure of the treaty
has a long-term influence on other aspects of the European project. As Charles
Grant has pointed out, rejection of the ECT means that treaty-based integration
is coming to an end. Deepening of integration could be halted for a long time
to come. Certain Member States could try to implement models of deeper integration, and the influence on overall EU integration would be deleterious, as
that would truly mean a Europe of two speeds. Without necessary institutional
restructuring, the EU may experience an institutional crisis in a few years’
time, one which leads to Member States rejecting the existing achievements of
integration. The EU would then become nothing more than a free market zone
with weak political institutions.54 This pessimistic view might not apply to ‘core
Europe’, but it would certainly apply to the EU as a whole.
Enlargement might be another victim of the failure of the ECT. If the deepening
of integration grinds to a halt, enlargement would also probably stop. Member
States have traditionally insisted that the deepening of integration must not
suffer damage as a result of enlargement. The next-to-last round of EU enlargement was, to a certain extent, the cause of the French and Dutch rejection of
the ECT, and so except for the admission of Bulgaria and Romania at the beginning of 2007, the EU, under this scenario, would halt all further enlargement
until the issue of deepening integration is resolved. Grant argues that deeper
integration is unlikely in the near future and that quite the opposite might
occur instead, and this means that enlargement would stop for an indeterminate
period of time.55 Indeed, the accession of Romania and Bulgaria remained
under the question mark until very late in the autumn of 2006. Negotiations
with Turkey could be launched only because some EU Member States believed
that the negotiations would never end. France has decided that it will hold a
referendum on the admission of any new Member States. It is quite possible that
Austria would insist on a referendum if membership were offered to Turkey.
Other EU Member States are also not too delighted about the possibility of
Turkey’s accession to the EU.
This is a scenario, of course, which goes far beyond the ratification of the
ECT as such, but it is necessary to think about this possibility even if it does

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 61

Debates about the Future of the European Constitutional Treaty..

61

seem rather apocalyptic. In the scenario of ‘burying’ the ECT, the overall mood
remains optimistic, because the EU can, after all, continue to move forward on
the basis of its existing treaties, and it can achieve significant progress through
specific projects, thus restoring the faith of Europeans in EU integration. In this
scenario, however, ‘burying’ the ECT means disintegration and a damaging
influence on major EU projects such as further enlargement.
7. Attempts at more intensive integration
This scenario is based on the initiative of Belgian Prime Minister Guy Verhofstadt, who has spoken of closer co-operation among the ‘core countries’ of the
EU. That does not mean that this is the only possible initiative, however. Verhofstadt’s ideas are just an example of how some countries may try to overcome
the crisis at hand by establishing closer co-operation. It is very important that
the ECT can serve as a foundation for such closer collaboration, but if we look
at which countries have ratified the treaty and which have not, we must find
that it is unlikely that the ECT will really serve as a cornerstone for closer links
among certain countries. First of all, the group of countries which have ratified
the treaty is far too diverse. Second, any attempt at more intensive integration
means co-operation which goes beyond the goals that were defined in the ECT.
Verhofstadt, in his ‘United States of Europe: A Manifesto for a New Europe’,
has not defined the way in which problems related to ECT ratification could or
should be resolved,56 but he does feel that the EU should now focus on several
goals. First, Europe must establish a common social and economic strategy
and administrative system for same. Second, there must be greater investments
in new technologies – an area in which the EU is far behind the United States
and Japan. Third, EU Member States must enhance co-operation on judicial and
internal affairs so that the EU can deal more effectively with organised crime.
Fourth, the EU must find one voice in foreign policy. A diplomatic service must
be established, and resources must be brought together in pursuit of common
foreign policy goals. Fifth, the EU must establish an army so that Europe becomes a powerful player in the defence arena. Essentially these proposals by
the Belgian prime minister go far beyond the brief of the ECT as such, and so
the question has less to do with ECT ratification but more with enhanced cooperation among some EU Member States. Verhofstadt has indicated that the
countries of the euro zone should form the core of this group.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 62

62

TOMS ROSTOKS

There has been intensive debate about the prime minister’s ideas. Scholars
and politicians agree that his intentions are good, but impossible to pursue in
practical terms. Verhofstadt’s proposals are his own, individual initiative, and
they have even been criticised by members of the Belgian Cabinet of Ministers
which he chairs. Critics point to problems in the creation of ‘core Europe’. EU
enlargement has demanded great effort, and new Member States would certainly object to any attempt to shunt them aside. Second, the five priorities that
have been defined by Verhofstadt have also attracted critics who say that they
may seem attractive at the level of declarations, but it is by no means clear how
the current situation could be resolved on the basis of the five priorities. Verhofstadt, say these critics, has chosen to ignore the differences of opinion among
EU Member States, as well as the many different issues which prevail among
those countries.57

Summary
The European Constitutional Treaty and the things which have happened
with it lead one to reflection and many conclusions, all of which cannot be
included in an article of this scope. Here are a few of the major conclusions:
First of all, the ECT is closely linked to the future of the EU. This is the fundamental assumption and conclusion in this paper. The link differs from one
reviewed scenario to another, but it is hard to imagine that the future of the EU
will not be affected if institutional reforms are not conducted. True, ratification
of the ECT in its current form or some other form would not mean that EU
Member States, whether together or separately, would be able to cope with the
problems that are upsetting to EU residents. Institutional solutions, of course,
are just one prerequisite for dealing with other challenges. They certainly do not
guarantee that all problems will be resolved.
Second, is it most unlikely that the current version of the ECT is ever going
to be ratified. Approval of a ‘mini-Constitution’’ is not out of the question, because no EU Member State is opposed to integration as such, and all Member
States admit that institutional restructuring will be necessary sooner or later –
even if the EU does not suffer from the fact that this has not happened yet.58
Ten Member States have not yet ratified the ECT, but approval of a modified
version of the treaty is not impossible.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 63

Debates about the Future of the European Constitutional Treaty..

63

Third, the German presidency of the EU represents the best chance to reach
consensus on the ECT issue, but even Berlin’s active participation will not
guarantee success, because much depends on the countries which have not
ratified the treaty. If the view that the EU can continue to operate on the basis of
existing treaties takes the upper hand and that it has more important problems
to resolve, then institutional reforms may be postponed for a long time to come.
Finally, this may be the last time that ratification of an intergovernmental
agreement in the EU is conducted through a national referendum, at least in
some countries. One can understand the unwillingness of some countries to
move forward with ratification under the current circumstances, but agreement
was reached on the ECT, and all of the EU Member States did sign the treaty in
Rome in 2004. It is unlikely that intergovernmental agreements which have
been thrashed out in negotiations among 27 Member States will ever be subject
to the vagaries of national referenda in the future. If national votes are taken,
they will certainly be organised in a much different way – perhaps, for instance,
organising a referendum in all Member States simultaneously.
