KRISTÎNE KRÛMA

The EU Constitutional Treaty Before Its Rejection:
How Many Hurdles Against the European Dream?
Introduction
The European dream is usually related to such concepts as integration,
cultural diversity, quality of life, balance between work and leisure, human
rights, and a stable world in which peace prevails.1 At the level of rhetoric, the
European Union is considered to be sui generis in this context, and it is believed
that it must pursue the aforementioned dream. Once pursuit of the dream moves
from theory to practice, however, news reports are full of information about
battles over the EU’s voting system, the number of votes on the European
Council, a reduction in the number of European Commissioners, the transfer
or preservation of competence in the defence and foreign affairs sector, and
alike. All of this suggests that the EU Member States which are supposedly
pursuing the European dream do not have a unified vision of the EU’s further
development, and they also lack any real desire to undertake joint responsibility
for the development of the EU in the interests of everybody, as opposed to the
interests of national agendas.
The Treaty Establishing a Constitution for Europe (TEC) was invested with
great expectations to the effect that it would be a document which would define
new frameworks of European co-operation and define goals for the EU. While
the Treaty was drafted, it attracted the attention of many researchers who were
discussing what the EU is and what it should be. From the moment that the
European Convention was summoned to write the TEC until the moment that
voters in France and the Netherlands turned it down, views were expressed
which, directly or indirectly, predicted its doom. This paper is not meant to
offer an all-encompassing presentation of that debate, but it will consider the
main aspects of the TEC on the basis of the fundamental principles of EU law.
These principles represent the common values of Europe in the legal sense of

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

70

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 70

KRISTÎNE KRÛMA

the term, even if they have never been completely formulated. After analysis
of the TEC, the author will propose a few recommendations as to what Latvia
should do during the ongoing period of reflection about the Treaty.

1. The methodology for European constitutionalisation
1.1. The context for European constitutionalisation
The debate about the constitutionalisation of the European Union began at
least 40 years ago, when the European Court of Justice delivered two rulings
in which it defined the principle of supremacy of EU law and the principle of
direct effect. The Court also declared that the EU is an autonomous system of
laws. This means that the doctrine of constitutionalisation has existed for
several decades now in parallel to EU law as ‘integration law.’ This can largely
be seen as the development of constitutional principles as promoted by the
Court and scholars.
Current debates about the EU must be viewed in two overlapping contexts.
First there are debates about the importance of the State in the future and
about the need to view the State more thoroughly in the context of globalisation.
Power is no longer entirely in the hands of individual States today. All nations
are affected by international economic processes, global markets, transnational
corporations, international crime, and other related processes. All countries, in
other words, are dependent upon processes beyond their own control. The
second debate focuses on the future of the EU as such. It has been suggested
that the EU is the most appropriate integration model for European countries
at a time of globalisation, but it has also been admitted that the EU has not
completed its own establishment. The EU must find itself in the context of
global processes, and it must become a new type of international structure.
This is a debate which has to do with the search for European identity and
commonality so that the EU can stand up against various challenges. The
process can be defined both in positive and in negative terms: concepts such as
European welfare, European democracy and European governance on the one
hand, and the idea that Europe is not authoritarian, Europe is not impoverished
and Europe is not violent on the other.2
From the perspective of international law, the EU is nothing unique, and it does
not exist outside the framework of international law. The effective functioning

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 71

The EU Constitutional Treaty Before Its Rejection..

71

of the EU under conditions of closer integration, however, requires unprecedented
ideas to find answers in the two aforementioned areas of debate. That is because solutions must be defined for two major problems: (1) conceptually
separating the constitution3 from the state; (2) transforming the EU’s founding
treaties into a Constitution. Here it has to be said that it is difficult to identify
Europe’s common values by simply projecting national concepts onto the
context of the Union. Doctrine offers the viewpoint that analogy and transfer
are undesirable or even dangerous here, because the Union is not a State.4 The
EU is not based on unified sovereignty, and it is shaped simultaneously by
numerous actors. During the Cold War, rhetoric suggested that the basic
constitutional principles of the EU included promoting economic welfare,
avoiding war, and contrasting the EU against the communist territories to the
East and, possibly, even against the United States. This rhetoric is no longer valid.
An argument which opposes the doctrinal idea of the incompatibility of the
national in the transnational is that the EU is based on international law, where
general principles of law are based on the practice of nations in their mutual
relationships. The fundamental principles of the EU, therefore can have no
source other than principles which Member States have accepted on a collective
basis. Accepting one of the two approaches would require a fundamental decision on the EU as a subject, i.e., the extent to which we see it as linked with or
independent of its Member States, the way in which we view the competence
of these two entities with Kompetenz Kompetenz. The two approaches co-exist in
parallel at this time. The EU absorbs the principles of States, but it also creates
special content for those principles.
When countries join the EU, they transfer some of their sovereignty to the
Union. This refers to areas in which Member States can no longer approve independent regulations or take other steps without EU authorisation.5 The EU
then continues to develop co-operation with its Member States, and that is the
area in which constitutional regulations are needed. It can be argued that the
EU already has certain constitutional manifestations. Researchers of EU law
have used various methods to identify these. It is sometimes suggested that
the constitutional law of the EU is represented by all of its founding Treaties
and it seems that the authors of the European Constitutional Treaty have thought
along these lines. German scholars in particular, however, have encouraged
selection of those issues which really are constitutional or represent the set of
primary law, i.e., so called ‘Inbegriff des Primärrech.’ 6 These, then, must be

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

72

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 72

KRISTÎNE KRÛMA

supported in the doctrine of principles, i.e., the most important legal norms in
the legal structure of Europe must be exposed in a systemic way.7 Principles
which are an important source of Western law define the key elements of the
legal order and serve as a ‘framework of orientation.’8 This means that principles
can be used to shape European identity and a common understanding of the
community. These are not principles of primary law, but they are fundamental
principles of the constitutional ranking. The entire EU is based upon these,
and this eliminates fragmentation of the system.
1.2. Fundamental constitutional principles of the EU
Article 6 of the Treaty of the European Union and Article 2 of the European
Constitutional Treaty both make reference to common principles and values of
Member States. Principles at the EU level operate at the level of common denominators, given the special nature of the EU. In this they differ from identical
principles in the Member States.
In the context of the EU, five major principles can be defined at this time:
The principle of integration ensures the dynamics of the European Community and is supranational by nature.9 There is doctrinal reason to defend
the view that the integration principle does not in and of itself mean that the
final goal is the vision of federalists about establishing a supranational state. The
essence of integration rests largely in the fact that there is a need for functional
loosening of nation states which, in the name of effectiveness, transfer some of
their functions to the EU.10 The supremacy or supranational principle is in
place when these functions are transferred, and the principle helps the EU to
increase its influence vis-à-vis decisions that are taken by national governments.
The supranational concept is replaced in treaties with the word ‘common’,
which refers to the interests of all Member States while avoiding any impression
that the aim of the EU is to destroy nations. In practice, this principle is implemented with the help of comitology and diplomacy. Comitology allows
national experts to oversee the work of the European Commission. Diplomacy,
in turn, is the dominant method in the work of the Committee of Permanent
Representatives and in that of the European Council of Ministers. It is only in
the sense of the TEC that supremacy means that decisions which are taken in
spite of a Member State’s objections become mandatory for that Member State
and are superior to the Member State’s own laws. This means that the principle

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 73

The EU Constitutional Treaty Before Its Rejection..

73

of integration at this time is moderate and dual, including elements of multilateral diplomacy and supranationality. What is more, the implementation of the
principle is limited by the principle of subsidiarity, which speaks to an evaluation
of effectiveness before an issue is handed over to the supervision of the EU.
The principle of freedom11 covers several specific freedoms which can be
described as ‘respect for human rights, fundamental freedoms and the rule of
law.’ An individual’s freedom promotes an individualised understanding of
society and the law.12 At the EU level, individualisation is manifested through:
(1) the ability of individuals to make reference to the norms of EU law in a
national court proceedings (the principle of direct effect of derived acts); (2) ensuring fundamental rights; (3) awarding constitutional functions to private
rights, including the individual’s economic and contractual rights, which is
still the main cornerstone of EU operations.13 All in all, the EU offers greater
opportunities for manifestations of individual freedom than do individual
Member States.
Observing human rights is a precondition for any country which wishes to
become an EU Member State. If this requirement is violated, the country’s
participation in the EU can be limited.14 One problem for Europe relates to limits
on individual freedom at the level of individual countries in which the model
of the welfare state dominates. The principle of freedom is undergoing transformation at this time, because the EU is no longer a union just of ‘market citizens’ – it is a union of EU citizens, too. This means that the principle must be
strengthened outside of the economic framework. This is seen in the latest
secondary acts which are based on citizenship, not on economic activity. The
case-law of the ECJ must also be mentioned, because it grants EU rights on the
basis of citizenship. The ECJ, for instance, strengthens the principle of minimal
financial solidarity among Member States, one which speaks to the payment
of social benefits to citizens of other Member States, irrespective of whether
they have contributed to the relevant national budget in the form of taxes. The
pillar of Justice, Freedom and Security, too, has changed in terms of content –
it is now the competence of the EU, not exclusively of Member States.
The principle of the rule of law states that the culture of law has a central
role in European integration, because it ensures stability and the rule of law.
Because the EU was established on the basis of international law, effectiveness
related to the creation and application of norms, as well as to the implementation
and sanctions, is of particular importance. One can agree with the idea that the

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

74

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 74

KRISTÎNE KRÛMA

larger and freer the EU, the more it must be based on the law.15 The Treaty
makes this clear by awarding functions of constitutional review to the Court –
something that is by no means all that common in the practice of international
organisations.16 National courts are also of key importance in ensuring that
the principle of the rule of law is observed. The principle consists of such
concepts in EU law as direct applicability, homogeneous interpretation and
supremacy. Several problems have appeared in this regard, not least the lack
of legal controls in issues related to the second and third pillar. It is also true
that individuals have limited rights to dispute the Community’s legislation,
and Member State courts have been passive in terms of co-operation with the
European Court of Justice.
The principle of democracy – here it is crucial to differentiate between
political science and law, as well as to think about the essence of the principle
in States and in a supranational organisations. Democracy is usually considered
to be a political concept. There was a shift in thinking in the early 1990s, when
two aspects of the legal principles of democracy were first studied in relation
to the EU. One was based on civil rights and EU citizenship, while the other
focused on the institutional aspects of the EU’s organisational structure.17 The
treaties of the European Union create foundations for both directions of
research.18 The European Court of Human Rights, too, has declared that the
institutional structure of the EU has reached a level at which it can be evaluated
in terms of how it corresponds to the principle of democracy.19 The EU faces
challenges in his context. The foundation for the principle at the national level
is equality among citizens, but at the EU level, the principle must ensure that
the diversity of Member States is observed.20 One of the slogans of the EU,
‘United in Diversity’, is the cornerstone for the principle of democracy irrespective of whether it affects EU activities in the context of EU citizenship or
EU institutions.
The principle of solidarity is reflected in the Treaty of the European Community and in Article 2 of the Treaty of the European Union, which lists the
duties of the European Community and the EU – those which all Member
States agree to implement. There is also Article 10 of the ECT, which obliges
Member States to do everything that is necessary for them to comply with
their duties.21 Article 10 also bans any steps on the part of Member States that
would hinder or threaten the ability to achieve the goals of the Treaty. The
norm has also been described as the principle of loyalty, and this is a concept

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 75

The EU Constitutional Treaty Before Its Rejection..

75

which relates to the principle of solidarity. Although this latter principle is of
fundamental importance for the future of the EU, its implementation or even
recognition is a major problem. Disputes about the distribution of votes on the
Council, the future of EU financing and the vision of the EU as such – these
vividly illustrate difficulties with the principle of solidarity. The main problem
in this context is how to shape a constitutional process which mobilises
European public opinion in a new, legitimate and non-national nation which is
based on loyalty and solidarity vis-à-vis the EU.22
All of this shows that the principles of the EU are not static. That is because of
the dynamic and incremental nature of the project that is the European Union.
This means that the fundamental issue is whether the constitution and the process of constitutionalisation, in the statal sense, is appropriate to the EU at its
current stage of development. Constitutionalism as such is a conservative method
of political theory, because it is meant to strengthen the State as a permanent
subject of international law, defining the basic elements of its activities. The EU,
however, remains dynamic, and that requires responses to new needs and challenges with corresponding progress.23 It is necessary to consider, therefore, whether
this dynamic process of development really requires a legally finalised structure –
if it is even possible to achieve the creation of such a structure ever.24

2. Creating the Constitution
During 50 years of step-by-step integration, the European Union came to
resemble a patchwork blanket, one with many different initiatives which were
truly understood only by experts. A new approach was needed to deal with
various democratic challenges.25 The definition of a new approach began in
the Laeken Declaration, which ordered the European Convention to define
overall duties that would be included in the text of the treaties. This section
will take a critical look at the way in which the new treaty was prepared and
the results of that process from the perspective of time, place and form.
2.1. The Laeken challenges
The need to review the basic treaties of the European Union was based on
several circumstances. For one thing, future enlargement was creating questions
about the effective governance of the EU. Second, there was the heritage of the

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 76

76

KRISTÎNE KRÛMA

Amsterdam and Nice Treaties, with intergovernmental conferences proving to
be unable to simplify the basic treaties which, therefore, remained chaotic.26
Third, political debates about the process of enlargement renewed discussions
as to the future of Europe. European decision makers had no unanimity of
opinion on these matters, and that remains true today. Tony Blair argued that
the EU must be a ‘superpower, but not a super-state’, Jacques Chirac was
plumping for ‘a unified Europe of nations, not the United States of Europe’,
and Joschka Fischer supported the idea of a ‘European federation.’27 Guy
Verhofstadt, for his part, spoke of the United States of Europe.28 These are
important issues not just in the context of the EU, but also in relation to other
international organisations which have seen their role in international relations
increasing substantially since the end of the Second World War.29 One can ask
whether States, which as the main subjects of international law, are satisfied
with these developments. One might also wonder what they think about the
future of the role of international organisations.
The mandate for the European Convention was specified in the European
Council’s Laeken Declaration of 2001, and a number of problems were identified. The first was the need to bring EU institutions closer to the EU’s citizens
by improving the distribution of competences, simplifying EU legal acts, and
promoting democracy, transparency and effectiveness. Next there was the role
of Europe in a globalised world. The Convention was also asked to simplify
the EU’s basic treaties without amending their content to any particular
degree, to review the structure of pillars, and to decide on what to do with the
European Union Charter of Fundamental Rights30 and the European Convention on the Protection of Human Rights and Fundamental Freedoms.31
We can conclude, therefore that the mandate issued by the Council to the
Convention was moderate. The main issue was still the functioning of the
internal market and the common currency. The Convention, however, developed a far more ambitious project. Its failure can be attributed to three factors –
a bad place, a bad time, and a bad form.
2.2. A bad place
The Convention which drafted the new Constitution had 105 full members
and 102 deputy members. The project was managed by a powerful Presidium
of 13 individuals and of limited membership. This is not a structure which is

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 77

The EU Constitutional Treaty Before Its Rejection..

77

envisaged in EU Treaties, and it was first used to draft the Charter on Fundamental Rights. Even then the model of the Convention was criticised as being
inappropriate for the drafting of legal documents. The Convention proposed a
democratic process for the development of new projects, but on the other
hand, the diverse membership of the Convention and the diverse interests of
its participants could not lead to the creation of a high-quality document and
achieve the necessary legal compromises in a short period of time. The EU had
been seeking an ideal model for itself ever since the amending of the Maastricht
Treaty – amendments and entirely new documents had been prepared. Attempts
to democratise the system created ever more critical problems.
Observers of how the Constitution was drafted say that from the spring of
2003, the Presidium of the Convention held a monopoly on the process.32 The
European Commission was minimally involved in the process. Amendments
were often hasty, and often they were written by a single person – the president
of the Convention. The preamble to the Constitution was an example of this.
The Laeken mandate, in short, was exceeded.33 The Convention was unable
to consider national political realities and public moods, perhaps because it
was so large or because the Presidium had such extensive ambitions. If Member
States are the source of the EU’s competence in pursuit of common goals, they
should have had oversight over the drafting of the Constitution. Instead, the
work of the Convention was dominated by narrow groups which surrounded
the Presidium, and this led to the emergence of spontaneous and contradictory
ideas as to the content of the new treaty. The idea that the Convention was a
democratic institution for preparing the relevant document, in short, is a myth.
That is proven by the inability or disinterest of the Convention in conducting a
serious review of existing Treaties, instead flirting with fundamental terms
without offering, at the end of the day, any thorough definition of any of them.
2.3. A bad time
It can also be argued that the timing of the process led to the failure of the
document – the Constitution was drafted immediately after the EU’s largest
round of enlargement. As noted above, the project that is the European Union
is an incremental project. Several initiatives in the past have failed because of
their timing. Examples include the European Defence Community initiative which
was presented by France in 1950, the plans for European Political Co-operation

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 78

78

KRISTÎNE KRÛMA

in 1970, and the disagreement on implementing a qualified majority vote in place
of a unanimous vote in 1965, when France pursued its ‘empty chair’ policy
during the seven months before the Luxembourg agreement was concluded.
Since the beginning of the EU’s establishment in the shape of three Communities, fundamental changes occurred only when the Maastricht Treaty was
approved in 1992 and the EU established new pillars considerably extending its
activities. The move toward Maastricht began in 1986, when the Unified Europe
act was approved. Subsequent changes occurred very rapidly. Austria, Finland
and Sweden joined the EU in 1995, the Amsterdam Treaty was agreed in 1997,
the Nice Treaty followed in 2001, and 10 new Member States, most from Central
and Eastern Europe, were admitted in 2004. The Amsterdam and Nice treaties
were both disappointing in terms of the doctrinal hopes that had been invested
in them. The major enlargement of 2004 also showed that the EU was by no
means prepared for the admission of new Member States at the institutional
and psychological level in the old Member States. This was confirmed by public
opinion during the pre-accession period. Fully 68% of surveyed EU residents
agreed with the idea that the reunification of Europe was understandable in
historical and geographic terms, but the actual idea of reunification in 2002 was
supported only by 30% of the EU’s citizens.34 Moreover, 39% of respondents in
a 2003 survey said that the EU was not prepared for further enlargement.35 The
failure of the EU to prepare for enlargement was also demonstrated through
the fact that only three Member States did not impose transition periods on the
free movement of citizens from the new entrants – the UK, Ireland and Sweden.
There had to be very convincing arguments, in other words, as to why people
in the EU should support a new basic document with ambitious title for the
EU. Neither the Convention nor the EU’s Member State governments ever
provided such arguments.
2.4. A bad form
In international law, the title of a treaty is of no importance. There are
organisations which call their founding treaty a constitution – the World Health
Organisation is one example. In essence, however, these are international
treaties which do not grant the relevant organisation any special authority. The
European Constitutional Treaty is an international treaty of the same sort. Its
name is based on previous examples – the Treaty on Establishing the European

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 79

The EU Constitutional Treaty Before Its Rejection..

79

Communities and the Treaty on Establishing the European Union. Here we have
to ask whether the TEC differs in some sense from an international treaty.
A closer look at the TEC suggests that it is rather an international treaty
than a proper constitution. Valéry Giscard d’Estaing was the one who chose
the term ‘European Constitution’, as opposed to ‘Constitution of the European
Union’. He may have been embittered by the fact that his proposal to change
the name of the European Union to ‘United Europe’ did not win support.36 The
random selection of a name and the inappropriate content of the document
meant that the addressee of the plan was lost.37 The TEC brought together
both terms – ‘Constitution’ and ‘treaty’, and that typified the new project.
There were those who felt that this dualism represented an understanding of
political realities on the part of the Convention, while others felt that it represented the failure of both the Convention and the TEC.38
A documented constitution can speak to a great deal or not very much at
all. The TEC spoke to a great deal in that it documented an institutional break
in the process and defined a new normative approach which related to a constitutional aspect in creation for the political community to establish legitimacy.
A Constitution does not provide for much if it is seen as an event, not as a
process. The constitutional issue cannot guarantee that in the post-approval
phase it will cover all of the necessary involvement on the part of the public so
as to award the constitution its sufficient depth and breadth in the contextual
sense.39 This is certainly true when it comes to the TEC. In the context of form,
it must also be noted that if one can agree with the idea that the best constitution
is a short constitution,40 then the 300 pages of the TEC also represented a failure.
It can be concluded, in other words, that from the very beginning, the
Convention faced a complex situation. There had to be significant political
impulses to convince the citizens of the EU to approve the document. These
could have been found in the text of the Constitution if they had addressed
citizens or other external circumstances. Let us now turn to the specific
elements of the document’s content.

3. The content of the Constitution
The draft TEC consists of four parts – the main constitutional principles,
the fundamental rights, the existing treaties which define the competencies of

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 80

80

KRISTÎNE KRÛMA

the EU, and the concluding terms of the treaty. From the perspective of this
analysis, the constitutional principles are reviewed on the basis of a methodology other than that of the TEC itself. Because the existing Treaty of the European Community was amended to a very minimal degree, that section will not
be analysed. Fundamental rights will be placed into the context of the methodologically selected principles, as will the concluding terms.
3.1. The principle of integration
If we review the TEC in the context of the principle of integration, we must
pose several questions. What are the values on the basis of which and among
whom integration occurs? How close is the integration that is anticipated? What
is the legal foundation for integration? What are the prospects for integration?
The TEC offers no answer to the question of why the peoples of Europe
should require such a document and this type of co-operation. If the job of the
Convention was to prepare a document for the future of Europe, then the
points of reference were very important to define goals and to prevent any
repeated errors. The Preamble of the TEC, in comparison to European history,
is very optimistic or even improper. It speaks to the cultural, religious and
humanist heritage of Europe, not that of the EU Member States, arguing that
this heritage is the origin of fundamental rights and freedoms, as well as
democracy. One wonders whether this is really a good way of describing the
colonialism which was pursued by several European countries or the wars
which have taken place in Europe for many centuries in the search for an ideal
balance of power. The next section of the Preamble, too, refers to the confusing
‘bitter experiences’ which preceded Europe’s reunification to continue along
the path of civilisation, progress and prosperity. That does not speak to a large
part of the new EU Member States, as they did not take part in that process
until the Cold War was over. The Preamble continues to be improper, because it
claims that Europe remains proud of its history. Surely most European countries
are not proud of all of the pages of their history – colonialism, collaboration
during the Second World War could serve as examples. These points of reference
are followed by the definition of the idea ‘United in diversity’ and confirmation
of the relationship continuity between existing EU Treaties and the TEC.
At the end of the Preamble, delegates thank the Convention for its work and
offer a Constitution on behalf of the ‘citizens and States of Europe.’ Similar text

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 81

The EU Constitutional Treaty Before Its Rejection..

81

is found in Article I-1. The reader may once again wonder about the real goals
of this project. If Europe is viewed in such a positive light, if the argument is
that Europeans must be proud, must recognise progress, prosperity, civilisation
and the like, then why does anything have to be changed on the basis of the
TEC? The goals of unification can relate to the need to deal with problems in
new frameworks, redistribution of prosperity among otherwise satisfied European countries.
Constitutions usually express the will of citizens, while the will of States is
usually expressed in international treaties. Clarity about this is provided in
Article I-1 of the TEC, which says that the Constitution ‘establishes the European
Union, on which the Member States confer competences to attain objectives
they have in common.’ Thus the TEC offers a vision of States which engage in
co-operation but are otherwise not linked to one another. This is something of
a marriage of convenience.41 The central role in this ‘marriage’ is played by
Member States, not by the will of the people. The second sentence in Article I-1
states that the duty of the European Union is to co-ordinate the policies by
which the Member States aim to achieve these objectives, and that it shall exercise
on a Community basis the competences they confer on it. This means that as
far as the TEC is concerned, the EU does not have any competencies in and of
itself – those are conferred to it. Article I-1, in other words, confirms that the
‘Constitution’ is not a constitution in the traditional sense of the word.
Constitutions signify authority in and of themselves stemming from people,
not indirectly, as is the case with this document via delegation from the
Member States.
Despite these criticisms, it also has to be said that the TEC makes a positive
contribution in terms of ensuring that the de facto existing legal personality of
the EU in the Treaty is confirmed de jure. This means a de jure end to a debate
which has swirled around legal personality of EC and EU for the past 10 years
and more – a problem which has made it complicated to apply EU law (ratification and application of mixed agreements, for instance).
EU integration has become more concrete thanks to a strict listing of spheres
of competence in the TEC. TEC provides for three types of competence – exclusive, shared and supporting (complementary) competence, with a detailed
list of areas of co-operation which fall under each of the types of competences.
In addition, there is also an indication of special co-operation in areas of
economic and employment-related issues, where EU Member States are obliged

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 82

82

KRISTÎNE KRÛMA

to co-ordinate their policies with the EU. The EU is also entitled to undertake
the initiative in co-ordinating social policy. Particular competence is awarded
to the EU when it comes to the common foreign and security policy.42
New posts could also serve as an additional impulse for integration –
President of the European Council and Foreign Minister of the EU. Both posts
entail fundamentally important functions, but the implementation will very
much depend on the people who take the posts and on the vision of these
people vis-à-vis Europe.
The conclusion is that with a few exceptions, the principle of integration
has been moved towards intergovernmental co-operation, as opposed to cooperation among the citizens and the Union itself. Member States will be the
main players in implementing EU policies and in defining limits to those policies. It can be argued that the principle of integration would have become
more clear, but it would also have lost meaning if the new Constitution had
been approved.
3.2. The principle of freedom
In the context of the principle of freedom, the key issue is the discussion of
fundamental rights in the Treaty. These are emphasised as one of the main
values of the EU in Article I-2 of the TEC, but the fact is that regulation of these
rights in the Treaty is not at an acceptable level. First of all, Articles related to
EU citizenship have not been amended at all. There have been many doctrinal
proposals about how the status of EU citizenship should be strengthened. The
European Court of Justice has developed a significant body of case law in this
regard. Some of the proposals have perhaps been too radical for the contemporary political context. This can be said of the idea that EU citizenship should
be awarded to permanent residents of the Union who are citizens of other
countries or the proposal that parliamentary voting rights be awarded to all
EU citizens in any Member State in which they they are residing.43 At the same
time, however, the failure of the TEC to define EU citizenship as a fundamental status of the Member States citizens or to state the importance of citizens in
the EU sends a signal that at the level of Member States, the debate has stopped at the old concept of ‘market citizens’.
Secondly, the overall regulation of fundamental rights is clumsy. Article I-9 of
the TEC, which speaks to fundamental rights, does not offer any clear statement

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 83

The EU Constitutional Treaty Before Its Rejection..

83

as to the future of these rights in the EU. A Charter of Fundamental Rights was
implemented as a part of the TEC, but on the basis of insistence on the part of
Great Britain, the Treaty states that the EU shall ‘recognise’ rights, freedoms
and principles which are defined in the Charter. Here one might ask with good
reason why a special Article in the TEC is needed if the Charter of Fundamental Rights is a part thereof and what exactly it means to ‘recognise’ the
Charter. One interpretation is that the Charter is recognised only on the basis
of its content, but it has not been incorporated into EU law. From the point of
view of legal technique another question arises, i.e., whether the Charter should
not be reviewed. It was written as a declarative document, one that is not
legally binding, but now it has been incorporated into the treaty itself.
Because the Charter of Fundamental Rights was drafted as a document
which codifies those rights that are protected in the EU in one or another way,
the third part of Article I-9 is even more confusing. It basically repeats a
formulation from previous EU Treaties – that ‘fundamental rights, as guaranteed by the European Convention for the Protection of Human Rights and
Fundamental Freedoms and as they result from the constitutional traditions
common to the Member States, shall constitute general principles of the Union’s
law.’ Here one must ask whether this means that the norms of the Charter of
Fundamental Rights are to be applied as a part of general principles of law, as
opposed to norms of a constitutional nature.
From the perspective of basic EU Treaties, the second part of Article I-9 is
also peculiar. It states that ‘the Union shall accede to the European Convention
for the Protection of Human Rights and Fundamental Freedoms.’ Why do
such political promises have to be part of a fundamental treaty? If the aim was
to ensure that this promise is kept, its presence in a long-term fundamental
treaty seems untraditional and unnecessary.
This means that the status quo remains in place insofar as the principle of
freedom is concerned. That will change only if the EU’s institutions become more
active in the context of this Article of the TEC and the Charter of Fundamental
Rights. This work has begun to a certain extent, because recent directives have
made reference to the Charter. The Fundamental Rights Agency has been
established, and in recent rulings, the European Court of Justice has made
reference to the Charter as a source of fundamental rights.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 84

84

KRISTÎNE KRÛMA

3.3. The principle of the rule of law
There have been several obstacles against implementation of the principle
of the rule of law. These include legislative processes, the diversity of secondary legal
acts, and dilemma of supremacy and co-operation between the Community
and the courts of Member States. The TEC seeks to address all of these issues.
Legislative processes would be based on three major principles – conferral,
subsidiarity and proportionality. Also of importance would be the new Articles
of the Constitution in which spheres of competence are defined very precisely.44
The principle of conferral would be of the greatest importance in determining
the frameworks for EU competence. The principle means that the EU can act
freely only in those areas of competence which have been assigned to it in the
Treaty. Changes therein would require amendments to the Treaty. Concerns
have been expressed about the idea that if this principle is observed strictly,
that would hinder the use of implied powers, as defined in Article I-18 of the
TEC. Unanimous agreement among Member States would be needed before
those powers could be used, but it is not clear whether this decision would
expand EU competencies to other areas that are not defined in the Treaty. On
the one hand, this confirms how important the principle of the rule of law is
for the EU. On the other hand, it also makes the EU’s everyday work more
difficult, because unanimity in an enlarged European Union will not be all that
easy to achieve.
Unfortunately, the draft TEC has not amended the principle of subsidiarity.
The main problems in applying this principle in the EU remain in place. It is not
always a legal question as to whether a more effective result can be achieved if
Member States act separately or if the EU should work together. In addition to
the European Court of Justice which had to deal with this issue, the TEC envisages that national parliaments should be the main institution in making sure
that the principle is observed. This can be seen as a value-added to democratic
principle. However, it can also hinder the effectiveness of adoption of secondary
acts, what is of importance in strengthening the rule of law.
The inclusion of principle of primacy in the Treaty is questionable. The text
in English is not clear in that the word ‘primacy’ is used in place of the more
common concept of supremacy. Primacy, as defined in the TEC in Article I-6,
would apply in those areas of competence which are assigned to the Union in
the Constitution. The question here is who will supervise the application of

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 85

The EU Constitutional Treaty Before Its Rejection..

85

the principle: the European Court of Justice or the constitutional courts of the
various Member States.
The biggest innovation in the TEC also creates the greatest lack of clarity.
The TEC proposes an entirely new system of EU legal acts. One of the tasks for
the Convention was to simplify the existing structure, which is comparatively
complicated. There is no universal agreement as to whether the Convention
achieved this. The Constitution introduces new types of acts in some cases and
preserves the old ones in others. The draft TEC introduced European laws,
European framework laws, European regulations and European decisions and
other acts. The first two are comparable to the derived acts that are in force
today, but European regulations and European decisions would establish a
new set of legal acts which are non-legislative as they would not be issued by
legislative institutions. The legal nature of these acts would be different. The
Constitution also provides for additional derived acts, which are described as
‘implementing acts.’ These would be approved when the implementation of
legal acts requires uniform conditions. The TEC also speaks to special rules
vis-à-vis acts which relate to foreign, security and defence policy, as well as to
various aspects of freedom, security and justice. For instance, legislative acts
related to the Common Foreign and Security Policy would not be permitted.
It is hard to predict how much confusion this new system of legal acts
would create if it were approved, but in its present form, at least, it does create
a series of questions about the consequences of redefinition of the acts.
Finally, in the context of the principle of the rule of law a central institutional
role is performed by the European Court of Justice, which would also have to
deal with reforms. In addition to existing European Court of Justice and the
Court of First Instance, to be re-named as the High Court, the TEC also provides
for the establishment of special courts to deal with specific issues such as
industrial property. The fragmentation would be a good thing in that it would
speed up the court process, but there would have to be careful consideration
about ensuring continuity of case-law in homogeneous interpretation of EU law.
3.4. The principle of democracy
As noted before, the TEC did not review existing rules as to European Union
citizenship as such. Let us, therefore, look primarily at co-operation among
institutions and the decision-making, referring also to civic initiative, which is
addressed in the draft Constitution.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

86

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 86

KRISTÎNE KRÛMA

The TEC contains several changes to the context of decision-making which
seek to improve and democratise the process. For instance, it expands the
range of issues in which decisions require a qualified majority, as opposed to
unanimity; the structure of pillars would be eliminated, and the co-decision
procedure would be applied to 43 new spheres which means greater involvement of the European Parliament in the legislative process. This would affect
the balance of power between the EU and its Member States in sensitive areas
such as criminal law, immigration, asylum, foreign policy, defence, intellectual
property and taxation. The reforms related to legislative procedures are commendable by some. One can praise the aforementioned procedure in applying
the principle of subsidiarity in the context of promoting democracy. In this
paper, it is not possible to analyse the new legislative and budgetary procedures
in detail, but it can be concluded that the TEC would significantly expand the
role of the European Parliament in the legislative process.
The TEC tries to make the work of institutions more effective in response to
the enlargement of the European Union. The greatest debates among Member
States related to the Council of Ministers and the process of qualified majority
voting. Article I-25 speaks to a complex procedure for calculation of the qualified majority, but this is not of decisive importance from the perspective of
Europe’s future, providing that a certain balance will be preserved. The debate
rather represented the efforts of Member States to maintain as much influence
as possible in a system in which decisions are no longer taken unanimously.
From the organisational perspective, the Council would be divided up into a
Foreign Affairs Council and a General Affairs Council. The suggestion by the
Convention that there also should be a Legislative Council did not win the
support of Member States. The final draft of the TEC did not contain the idea
which is welcome result.
Some Member States were particularly upset about the proposal to reduce
the number of European Commissioners so as only two-thirds of Member
States would have their own Commissioner. This innovation could be seen in
the context of democracy and integration, because a reduction in the number
of Commissioners would indicate a certain amount of maturity in European
integration. It would also make the process of decision making more efficient,
particularly because Commissioners are not allowed in any event to defend
the interests of their countries of origin. From the perspective of democracy,
reforms of the European Commission would not be a problem.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 87

The EU Constitutional Treaty Before Its Rejection..

87

Finally, one must speak of the citizen initiative, which provided that one
million citizens from a ‘significant’ number of Member States could demand
that the European Commission draft a secondary act. The TEC promises that
the specifics of this would be defined in a special EU law. This means that to
date it is not clear whether the Commission would be obliged to forward any
such proposal to other institutions and to what extent the other institutions
would find it easy to reject the proposals. Some reseachers have called this
utter populism, because the Commission would have to react to initiatives
proposed by less than one-third of 1% of the EU’s citizens.45 From the perspective of the principle of democracy, however, this is a commendable initiative,
because it speaks to direct democracy in the EU.
The Constitution clearly would improve harmony among the EU’s areas of
competence and the effectiveness of those areas. This is because it would expand majority voting into the areas of freedom, security and justice, providing
also for jurisdiction of the Court, and it would give the Union authority in the
context of the CFSP. The Constitution also has a section on democracy which
calls for greater openness and dialogue. It must be said, however, that the
draft Treaty does not offer EU citizens any substantial new rights to be part in
European processes. Therefore, EU did not take the chance to speak to the people
of Europe and particularly to the EU’s citizens – it was too busy debating the
distribution of votes on the European Council, and that has more to do with
the influence of Member States, not their citizens. This means that Europe did
not draw closer to its citizens, and Member States retained their status as strong
intermediaries between the two sides. Europe does not have its own society,
and the Constitution does not address this problem.46
3.5. The principle of solidarity
The principle of solidarity in the TEC can be judged from various perspectives.
First of all, the new Treaty represents an attempt to create the impression of
solidarity and unity by, for instance, eliminating the EU’s pillarisation,47 restructuring legal acts, reducing the number of European Commissioners, establishing
the posts of foreign minister and Council president. The way in which the TEC
was written in and of itself also demonstrated a certain level of unity in the EU.
Secondly, the principle of solidarity is enshrined in Article I-5, which speaks
to the ‘principle of sincere co-operation’ in relations between the EU and its

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

88

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 88

KRISTÎNE KRÛMA

Member States. This is analogous to the principle of loyalty present in current
European Community Treaty – a principle which is assigned a significant role
by the European Court of Justice in its case-law. Article I-43, moreover, speaks
to solidarity among Member States in response to terrorist attacks.
Several other Articles of the Constitution, however, suggest that the principle of
solidarity must be reviewed from a more critical perspective. Article I-44, for
instance, speaks to enhanced co-operation in non-exclusive areas of competence,
making use of Union institutions to further the process of integration. In this,
the TEC would legitimate the principle of a ‘Europe of different speeds’. There
is reason to claim that this would have an effect on the principle of solidarity,
creating tensions between older and newer or richer and poorer Member States.
It might even promote disintegration and a lack of transparency in the EU.48
The principle of solidarity could also be weakened by the Article I-60 in the
Constitution which would allow Member States to withdraw from the EU on
voluntary basis. This could turn the EU into a hostage if a Member State were
to threaten withdrawal so as to oppose a decision that is unfavourable to it.49
This would afford greater legitimacy and finality to situations similar to one
when France practiced ‘empty chair’ policy of the 1960s. International practice
shows that organisations avoid such norms in their founding treaties so as to
ensure their survival from the very beginning and to send out signals about their
stability. The Charter of the United Nations does not provide the right to withdraw for Member States. However, the League of Nations did. The norm in the
TEC would signal that the European Union becomes less integrated. Critical
doctrine holds that this nominally constitutive document would in fact be used
to de-constitute the EU.50 There is good reason to make this claim, given that
international customary law allow States to withdraw from a treaty under certain circumstances, even if the treaty itself does not speak to such a possibility.
In summary, it can be concluded that the Convention and the Intergovernmental Conference did not manage to sketch out a stable future for the EU
within framework of principles outlined. It may be that this was because of an
excessive focus on technical issues such as exact allocation of votes, and ‘checklist’ approach to other important issues such as fundamental rights. Another
problem may have been the conflict over general vision for Europe between those
who see Europe as a union of States and those who see it as a union of individuals.
At the same time, however, the TEC is a document which is convenient for
everyone. Euro-optimists can point to the awarding of the legal personality to

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 89

The EU Constitutional Treaty Before Its Rejection..

89

the EU or to the strengthening of the principle of supremacy in the Treaty.
Defenders of democracy can delight at the stronger role for the European
Parliament. Human rights activists will be pleased at the inclusion of the Charter
of Fundamental Rights in the TEC. Those who enjoy confidentiality can argue
that governance is not of prominent importance in the draft Constitution. Those
who are no fans of European integration can also point to the TEC, because it
does not speak to any closer integration or to any more federative approach.
Inevitably, one should ask whether Europe really needs this document. There
is no question that it is impossible at this time to create a utopian basic treaty
for unified Europe, but the question is whether the TEC offers something
substantively better than the previous treaties? It could be argued that the TEC
offers a good starting point for debate. Responsibility for the result must be
undertaken by those Member State governments and EU institutions which
insisted that it is wrong for the Intergovernmental Conference to continue to
debate about the text of the Treaty after it was approved by consensus at the
Convention.51 It is against the background of these results that one must look
at prospects for the future and at those issues and actions that are of key
importance to Latvia.

4. Key issues for Latvia
Debates about the TEC after its rejection in France and the Netherlands
began in June 2005, but the issue really returned to the agenda of the European
Council in the first half of 2007, when Germany took over the EU’s rotating
presidency. At the end of the Finnish presidency, in December 2006, a new
deadline for decisions was set – 2009, when European Parliament elections
will be held, a new European Commission will be formed, and Croatia will
perhaps be admitted to the EU. Until then, Germany is supposed to come up
with a plan of action, but that is a fairly complicated task. There are politicians
in Europe who claim that the EU is in crisis mode, while others say that there
is no reason to be nervous about what is happening.52 Similarly, proposals made
by EU Member States as to what should be done are quite diverse. France is
speaking of a strategic dead end, suggesting that a ‘mini-treaty’ be approved.
Finland ratified the existing version of the TEC in December 2006.53 Several other
Member States, as well as the European Commission, have called for limitations

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

90

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 90

KRISTÎNE KRÛMA

on the reform process, arguing that it should be based only on institutional reforms. Experts, meanwhile, have been calling for political reforms, and they have
also spoken about the sustainability of the idea of a Europe of different speeds.
If we think about Latvia’s position in this context, it is clear that we must
first consider the positions that are being taken by other Member States. Any
process of consultations or negotiations, after all, requires a certain amount of
compromise. This does not, however, mean that we should not shape our own
vision as to the future of the European Union so that we can also develop
consistent positions vis-à-vis other issues. This is confirmed not just by the
positions taken by various political forces in Latvia with respect to the TEC,
but even by the fact that some political parties have changed their thinking
about the Treaty very quickly, indeed.54 The latest debate has been launched
by the Foreign Ministry, which has defined Latvia’s national strategy vis-à-vis
the EU.55 Formulation of a constructive position and debates about the future
would shape a qualitatively new phase in Latvia’s vision, because the European
Union has so far been observed mostly through the prism of funding which it
makes available.
As noted in this article, one of the main questions is whether Europe really
needs a Constitution at all. If the TEC should ensure stability and finality of
the process by answering all the constitutive questions which exist, then the
EU is not anywhere close to defining those answers, and any definition for the
sake of a definition would bring the natural evolution of the EU to a halt. The
EU is presently a set of diplomacy and certain democratic principles, and some
researchers have suggested that a new ‘European Enlightenment’ is needed
before it can decide in which direction to go.56 This is a premature idea at this
time, however, because individuals and Member States must first become
accustomed to the changes that have occurred in recent times, they must come
up with more precise understandings of the functional problems which exist
in an enlarged Europe.
The EU is often accused of a democratic deficit, and this complaint is made
by Member States both large and small, as well as by residents of the Union. This
is because the EU holds primacy in a whole range of areas – areas which used
to be the provenance of individual States. Indeed, the EU gains its legitimacy
from States, not individuals, and it is and remains dependent upon them. The
body of EU citizens does not really have any collective identity at this time,
and there is nothing which institutional reforms alone can do about this fact.57

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 91

The EU Constitutional Treaty Before Its Rejection..

91

An utter parliamentisation of the EU to make it similar to a State could be
problematic, too, because links with Member States would diminish. The EU
is needed both by States and individuals, however, because there are problems
which should be resolved outside the narrow confines of individual countries.
In this sense, the EU must be preserved as a supranational structure. Further
development must be based on its special nature as such a structure, and there
is no need to attempt copying national models instead.58 This means that one
scenario is preservation of the status quo, at least until a collective identity
emerges among EU citizens or integration achieves a new qualititative stage.
Of greater complexity is the question of shaping a common political culture
and identity through the use of democratic citizenship. First, the integration is
becoming more and more intensive, because some of the spheres of competence
of Member States can no longer be split apart as easily as was once the case.
Therefore, the EU has a natural need to expand its range of competences. This
promotes the more rapid amending of EU treaties and creates the question of
whether the EU could have avoided the developments which occurred after
the Maastricht Treaty. Second, external circumstances are facilitating this closer
integration – circumstances which are threatening the traditional model of
European welfare. National economies today are dependent on global processes, and the international economy is no longer based on bilateral or multilateral agreements among States.59 Third, the reaction of EU residents is quite
opposite, because at the national level, they encounter the unfavourable consequences of these processes, and they do not feel that the EU has made much
of a contribution toward resolving their problems. It is, at the same time, difficult
for the EU to deal with various problems if Member States only think about
their domestic agenda.
This Catch-22 situation can be escaped only if thinking changes fundamentally. Traditionally, State has been seen as the highest achievable structure for
people who are linked by solidarity.60 Symbiosis between the State and democracy emerged on the basis of sovereignty, but globalisation is threatening this
structure now. International law is becoming of ever greater importance as a
result of these processes. International law is becoming more ethical, and
organisations are becoming more autonomous. However, the basic units of
these processes – people – appear to be forgotten. States and organisations as
such are not capable of thought, only people do. That means that changes will
very much depend on the readiness of people to take a critical look at what is

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

92

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 92

KRISTÎNE KRÛMA

happening and to change their thinking. The question is what exactly the EU
offers to individuals and what the State offers them.
The process of EU integration has not been constitutional, it has in most
senses been functional, and there is reason for this. A global or regional federation is highly unlikely, but there can be successful and close co-operation
among existing democracies. Of particular importance in this context are the
protection of fundamental rights and the rule of law, i.e., those principles upon
which the EU’s legal structure is based. In developing positions, therefore, the
Latvian government should consider to what extent the project, the decisions
of the EU and the position taken by Latvia are in line with these principles, the
extent to which they strengthen those principles.
From the legal perspective, it is of key importance that changes of public or
national significance be reflected adequately in the fundamental documents of
the State if residents are to accept those changes. Latvia has always argued
that accession to the EU launched an entirely new phase in the country’s life,
both in domestic and in foreign policy terms. The same was argued when
Latvia restored its de facto independence. Latvia confirmed the process by
acceding to various human rights documents and by enshrining these in the
Latvia’s Constitution. EU law, too, was drafted with the goal of ensuring better
lives for the people of Europe. Therefore, it would be commendable if these
significant changes were to be reflected in the Latvian Constitution, too, thus
consolidating society in pursuit of a common European identity and a democratic system of EU citizenship. The question is whether Latvia, in the sense of
politicians, of the public at large, and of individuals, has redefined the fundamental principles of the nation which were prepared in the 1920s, and whether
it is prepared to reflect these redefined principles in fundamental law, as has
been done in most European countries.61 Are the international processes in which
Latvia has been involved since the de facto restoration of its independence
reflected adequately in the Constitution? It could be argued that amending the
basic law and involving the citizenry in this process would help in contributing
toward an EU identity. The EU is to a large extent invisible for people. Approximately 20% of Europeans and fewer than 10% of Britons know the
number of Member States joined in the EU. In a 2006 survey, it was found that
10% of Europeans had a view that Poland was preparing to join the EU, and
9% still thought that Hungary was a candidate country.62 In Latvia, only 16%
of respondents in a 2004 survey thought that they were well informed about

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 93

The EU Constitutional Treaty Before Its Rejection..

93

the EU Constitution.63 These statistics make it clear that it is not only TEC but
also any amendments to it in the context of an enlarged Europe cannot be of
revolutionary importance unless the process is successfully presented to the
citizens on whose behalf the EU was created in the first place, the citizens in
whose interests the EU Constitution is being discussed.
There are already barriers which the EU must overcome in order to ensure
its ability to function in the future. Another important job is not to miss the
starter’s pistol and then sprint forward as a unified whole with good faith, as a
Union which clearly wants to achieve good result at the finish line.

IVETA REINHOLDE

Multi-Level Governance in the EU:
How Many Floors Will the Building Have?
Introduction
It was back in the 1980s that academics and practitioners discovered that
the amount of work which EU Member States had to do in relation to the
Union had increased dramatically. Governments learned something else that
was no less surprising – they no longer had exclusive control over their countries.
Governments suddenly woke up to find themselves in a unified system which
they were supposed to accept and within which they had to deal with other
players and participants. This unique system was called many different things –
most often it was known as multi-level governance.1 This poses several important
questions. First of all, this is a system of multi-related levels. Second, it is a
system which involves both the governance of the EU and the governments of
the Member States. Third, there is communication and all levels of governance
are committed to mutual co-operation.
The purpose of this article is to review the future of Europe through the
prism of multi-level governance, focusing particularly on the view from
Latvia. It is clear that multi-level governance as such exists, but it is not clear
how it is to be organised and implemented. The EU is at a crossroads in terms
of governance, and this article will consider the historical development of
multi-level governance, the various types of governance which exist, and the
relevant administrative values which prevail in Europe.

Defining and interpreting multi-level governance
Multi-level governance began to develop in the EU along with reforms in
structural policies in the late 1980s. Once Greece, Portugal and Spain joined
the EU, there were extensive debates about how best to offer assistance to the

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

98

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 98

IVETA REINHOLDE

three countries so that they might more quickly reach the average EU level in
their economies. The European Commission proposed a system in which
Structural Fund finances would be administered via the national, regional and
local institutions of the three countries, with the European Commission taking
part as a supranational participant.2 This unique model of partnership in
administering the Structural Funds enabled several administrative levels to
work together – something that was unprecedented at the time. The regional
and local levels of administration had typically worked at the domestic level,
but this model meant that they would transcend their national borders.
In the 1990s, the concept of multi-level governance was, for the first time,
defined as the overall decision-making model in the European Union with a
great many participants. Multi-level governance, as a unique phenomenon, has
also been called in the another way – polycentric governance, multi-perspective
governance, fragmentation, consortium, etc.3 This diversity in terminology
indicates that no real agreement has been reached on the content of the term
both among academics and politicians alike. This means that Member States
can choose any of the aforementioned definitions and then fill it up with
specific content and understanding.
In 1993, Marks explained multi-level governance as ‘a system of continuous
negotiation among nested governments at several territorial tiers.’4 This marked
out one of the most important characteristics of multi-level governance – negotiations among the various administrative levels. Marks also assumed that
all levels were involved in a network of mutual co-operation, because the role
of the regional and the local level was on the rise in EU decision-making. This
was true despite the fact that the EU had traditionally been seen as an arena
for two major players in decision making processes – the supranational and
the national level. The increasing role of the regional and the local level indicates
that in the more distant future, there could be talk of a unified administrative
system in the EU, although that seems to be rather a utopian idea at this time.
Criticisms of multi-level governance are based on the fact that it overemphasises
the importance and role of the transnational level, thus swamping the other
levels.5 In effect, structural reforms in Europe had ambiguous results – co-operation
between the European Commission and the regional level ignored the national
level. The positive aspect was that regional interests were taken into account,
to be sure, but this was an unfavourable situation for the national level which,
to a certain extent, lost control over the regional level.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Multi-Level Governance in the EU: How Many Floors Will the Building Have?

Theoretically, there are two opposing models for multi-level governance. In
one, it is assumed that power expands into a limited number of levels and
authorities. In the other, the assumption is that multi-level governance is based
on functionally specialised authority with shifting and overlapping duties.6
Table 1
