S o u r c e: Marks, G. and Hooghes, L. Types of Multi-Level Governance. European Integration
Online Papers, Vol. 5, No. 11, 2001, Table 1. http://eiop.or.at/2001-011a.htm

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

100

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 100

IVETA REINHOLDE

The first type of governance involves a limited number of players and a
limited number of administrative levels. The limited number of players poses
the question of the capacities and influence of same. If local governments, as
the lowest level in a multi-level system of governance, become involved in the
process, then they must be sufficiently large and powerful. Marks and Hooghes
have emphasised that in multi-level governance, each player must be large
enough to fulfil a diversity of duties.7 True, it is not clear how large a player
must be in numerical terms, but it is quite unlikely that a local government
with 1,000 residents will ever be an influential player. A limited number of
players with exclusive authority and power means that local governments
which are at a certain level are only responsible for their own administrative
territory. Therefore in this type of governance, the exclusive authority that is
granted to each player means that players do not interfere in each other’s areas
of competence. This type of governance has a limited number of levels, holding
on to the accustomed understanding of the local, regional, national and supranational level, as illustrated in Figure 1. Functions of any other administrative
level is immediately questioned. According to Marks and Hooghes, the first
type of governance represents existing understanding of governance in
Europe, where power and policy influence are divided up among various levels
of governance.8 Moreover, each level is more or less unchanging in its structure
and its functions, which means infrequent reforms and gradual division of
power.
The second type of governance is completely opposite to the first. In this
case there are many levels and many players, and their power is not linked to
a specific territory. Actors are not just the official levels of power – the national,
regional or local level. There are also local community organisations, nongovernmental organisations, volunteer and charitable organisations, and organisations of various sizes which offer public benefits. This diversity of actors
with different interests and abilities to influence policy shows that the second
type of governance is more similar to the model of governance which exists in
the United States, not the European Union. The point is that people do not
encounter the government or the public sector as such. Instead, they deal with
various organisations which serve the public need, each of which can take
decisions in its specific area.9 This type of governance tends to break down the
traditionally accepted hierarchies among levels, and so a local problem can
become a transnational problem in the blink of an eye.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 101

Multi-Level Governance in the EU: How Many Floors Will the Building Have?

101

Marks and Hooghes argue that the EU is a place where the two types of
governance exist simultaneously. There are no policies which are purely
national, even though the development of multi-level governance in the EU
began with the first type of multi-level governance. Over the course of time it
absorbed certain elements of the second type of multi-level governance. In
practice, the first type is usually applied to the supranational, national and,
partly, the regional level, where the division of power is simple. The second type
of governance is more common at the regional and local level of governance,
where local governments co-operate actively with local inhabitants, public
organisations and interest groups in dealing with issues that are of a local
nature. The distribution of these two types of governance can also be reviewed
on a different basis – some operations in the EU are largely the competence of
a single level (i.e., the first type of governance), but there are somewhat independent EU agencies, and they, along with different systems of governance in
the three major policy pillars, more resemble the second type.10
Irrespective of the theoretical development of multi-level governance, several
critical aspects of a vertical and horizontal nature must be considered. When
multi-level governance is viewed from the vertical perspective, one of the main
problems is that policies introduced at various levels and elements of those
policies may be incompatible. When policies are introduced at each level, are
the overall goals of the policy taken into account, or are just the views of the
individual level considered? The horizontal perspective, for its part, is essential
in identifying forms of co-operation among institutions at one level but in different Member States. Finally, multi-level governance, at its very deepest foundations, speaks to co-operation among the public, private and non-governmental
sector. Thus, faultless multi-level governance, whether viewed from the vertical
or the horizontal perspective, must involve the private and the non-governmental sector, and this is a goal which should be pursued in the ongoing
development of governance.
To return to the origins of multi-level governance, we must consider links
between those origins and the increasing role of the regional level in governance processes. In evaluating its partnership and communications model in
the late 1980s, the European Commission did not anticipate the consequences
of its decisions. Regions were unable to communicate with the EC directly and
without the involvement of the national level, and this created a debate about
the division of power, as well as about communications between national and

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

102

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 102

IVETA REINHOLDE

regional levels. From the policy perspective, ongoing consultations are of
importance between the two levels if decisions mandatory for both sides are
being considered or these may be consultations between the two levels without specific results. We must also consider the extent to which the regional
level is represented in national parliaments and how much it participates in
the elaboration of national policy.11
The European Commission has an established principle of partnership
between itself and regional administrative institutions. This has allowed regions
to avoid the national level when the EC awards financing, and it also has institutionalised the representation of regions. The Committee of Regions has been
established, and regions can open up offices in Brussels. This means that the
regional level is involved in EU policy development – an area which used to
involve only the supranational and the national level. The Maastricht Treaty,
moreover, expanded the competence of the Committee of Regions to say that it
can provide consultations in all policy debates which could have an effect on
social and economic cohesion in Europe.12 In practice, the principle of partnership is seen very well in the European Commission’s INTERREG programme,
which seeks to promote trans-border and regional co-operation. The Committee of Regions conducted a study of local and regional participation in EU
policy development processes, and it quoted a German researcher who claimed
that at the moment of establishment, the European Union suffered from
‘federal blindness’, because it ignored the regional and the local level.13 The
partnership principle established by the European Commission has ‘healed’
Europe to a certain degree.
Bache and Flinders have written about changes at the supranational level,
arguing that multi-level governance must be viewed in the context of the integration process which resulted in the Unified Europe Act of 1987. It defined
the fundamental way in which the EU differs from other international organisations specifically from the perspective of governance. Qualified majority
voting was introduced in place of unanimous votes, which indicates that the
governance of the EU began to involve characteristics typical of domestic policy
making.14 These were strengthened even further in the European Constitutional
Treaty. This has changed perceptions as to what the EU really is. From the
perspective of multi-level governance, the EU must be seen as a more or less
unified system which works on the basis of unified rules and irrespective of
the geographic location of the various elements in the system.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 103

Multi-Level Governance in the EU: How Many Floors Will the Building Have?

103

Each of the types of governance can be described with a different word –
‘unchanging’ in the case of the first, and ‘flexible’ in the case of the second. The
reason for this is that in the first type of governance, each level can have its
own executive authorities, as well as elected legislators. The process of changes
is more complicated and poses questions about the division of power among the
levels, as well as the institutional structure which ensures the implementation
of decisions. Changes in the first type of governance must be viewed along the
scale of ‘centralisation-decentralisation’. The second type of governance, with
its flexible approach toward the involvement of various players in policy determination and implementation, can create a situation in which changes lead
to the emergence of entirely new levels of governance.
At the end of the day, inhabitants are of importance in multi-level governance. Marks and Hooghes argue that the first type of governance, in which
levels of competence do not change, is much more closely linked to people,
who live in specific territories or may even identify themselves with a specific
local government, region or country.15 There is a lack of such identification in
the second type of governance. Moreover, such identification is possible if the
resident feels and sees that the relevant level of governance is dealing specifically with his or her problems. In that case, the individual feels more closely
linked to the local government, then to the state, and only then, finally, to the EU.
Even though the aforementioned types of multi-level governance are essentially theoretical, in practice sometimes the two are combined. We must
consider both the strengths of multi-level governance and the obstacles which
it creates against development. First of all, it means co-operation and co-ordination among all levels of governance. Second, multi-level governance must be
seen as a mechanism which has changed the policy development process of
the EU, not as one which has changed the authority of Member States or
affected their sovereignty. Third, the increasing abilities and capacities of the
regional level to communicate with the supranational level make it easier to
develop effective regional policies and to relate to regional problems before
they turn into national or supranational problems.
Obstacles against development created by multi-level governance have
broader political context. Member States are politically unable to reach agreement on the future of Europe, and that will affect the future of multi-level
governance, too. First of all, multi-level governance means greater alienation
between the system and the residents of the EU. The local level usually

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 104

104

IVETA REINHOLDE

communicates with people, but the system does not. That means that the question of ‘What is Brussels doing?’ will remain on the agenda for a long time to
come. This alienation can create threats against the legitimacy not only of multilevel governance, but also of the European project as such.
Second, even if the two types of governance have merged, the problem of
‘unchanging versus flexible’ will be a real test for the development of multilevel governance, because it will be hard to change accustomed, traditional
and strict distributions of functions among existing levels or to allow new
levels to emerge.
Third, in focusing on administrative issues, we must note that different
Member States have different understandings vis-à-vis the role and importance
of administration in national policy development, and that may be a serious
hindrance against the further development of multi-level governance.
Fourth, the regional level’s ability to communicate with the supranational
level is a positive thing, but the process of communications causes problems,
because of the dual situation it creates. On the one hand, the regional level
seeks to ensure that its interests are represented, but on the other hand, it does
so independent of the state of which it is part. As a result, the interests of a
region might be opposite to national interests.

Europe’s administrative values
Although governance is perceived as a system in which government institutions co-operate with the private and non-governmental sector, the foundation
for governance will always be national institutions of governance – the administrative structure of the state, which handles most duties and obligations
related to governance. The EU’s acquis contains no norms to regulate the work
of Member State administrations, but over the course of time common understanding has emerged as to how administrations should function. Defined
overall principles observed in all EU Member States have been introduced by
all Member State administrations, irrespective of the prevailing administrative
model in each country. The concept of a European Administrative Space (EAS)
is sometimes used when discussing the administrations of EU Member States.
The borders of the EAS are identical to those of the EU. The EAS consists of
principles which reflect Europe’s administrative traditions and values. In the

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 105

Multi-Level Governance in the EU: How Many Floors Will the Building Have?

105

context of multi-level governance, the EAS reflects the common administrative values which are held at all levels of governance. These make it possible
for the various levels to work together. The EAS must also be seen as more than
just a phenomenon of principles. It is also a factor which links institutions in
various Member States and promotes the confluence of different administrative
cultures. Interestingly, the concept of the EAS is based on a unified understanding of the European economic and social space, as well as of the space in
which there is juridical co-operation in implementing EU laws.16 Administrative
values, therefore, cannot be separated from other values which underpin Europe.
The EAS is essentially based on four major principles and numerous derived ones. The major principles are reliability, openness, accountability and
effectiveness,17 while the derived ones are trustworthiness, predictability, transparency, utility, technical and leadership competence, capacity of organisations,
and participation of the society.18
Reliability in the context of the EAS must be viewed through the prism of
administrative law. The work of administrations must be legally specific,
predictable and prospective. Administrations are based on the rule of law, and
the rule of law marks out the legal boundaries for their operations. Justice is
not just a political value. In the context of multi-level governance it is one of the
most important administrative values, because not only political power must
be predictable and prospective. The same applies to administrations which, in
most cases, implement political decisions and ensure the implementation of
normative acts. The principle of reliability and predictability also covers issues
such as the professionalism and political neutrality of administrations. Political neutrality is usually interpreted as a system in which employees of the
administration do not become involved in party politics. The concept is somewhat different, however, when we look at multi-level governance, which
involves co-operation and communications among the various levels and with
interest groups, the private sector and various associations. In that case, political neutrality must be linked to systems which make it possible to hear different
views. The predictability of administrations also refers to the question of commensurability when decisions are taken – the extent to which administrative
activities will limit individual rights and serve the interests of the public.19
Reliability refers to stable and predictable procedures. It has to be admitted
that when administrations insist on procedure, they are sometimes said to be
too bureaucratic. This concept refers to complicated and time-consuming

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

106

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 106

IVETA REINHOLDE

procedures, impolite attitudes vis-à-vis local residents, or even alienation
between the administration and the public at large. The fact is, however, that
predictable and specific procedures ensure precise application of the law without the consideration of outside interests. They also ensure objectivity and
respect for human beings – something which can be seen as an administrative
value in and of itself.20
Openness and transparency allow administrations to avoid corruption and
poor administrative activities, and also promote public involvement in the
taking of administrative decisions. The public is involved in administration
not just through the election of a parliament which takes political decisions on
behalf of the public, but also by overseeing administrative activities with the
help of ombudsman institutions and the courts. These help every resident to
understand the logic behind administrative processes.
Accountability has to do with the responsibilities of an administration before the law and before society. Anyone can petition the courts so as to appeal
an administrative decision, and this is an important counterweight to administrative power. Such oversight ensures that institutions do their work
within the framework of the law and in pursuit of specific goals. This is of key
importance in ensuring public and private interests alike.
Effectiveness is an administrative value which means that the administration
works within the framework of available resources while ensuring that all
goals are achieved.21 This is a difficult principle to measure, because the public
interest cannot always be served with minimal economic resources which, in
turn, might cause a significant conflict of values. People expect administrations
to be economically effective, but the European ‘welfare state’ model cannot
exist without a numerically large and extensive administration, one which
consumes a significant amount of resources. The clash of values in which the
administrative value of effectiveness comes up against the social value of
welfare is known as an ‘income crisis’, ‘fiscal crisis’, ‘crisis of an overloaded
state’, or ‘crisis of legitimacy.’22 The key fact is that the state can no longer handle
all of its public functions, and so more or less extensive reforms of governance
and administration are needed in Europe. What is more, Europe will finally be
forced to formulate its own administrative policy.
Even more, EU Member States all established their administrative systems
on the basis of ideas presented by the father of bureaucracy, Max Weber, about
the ‘ideal’ administration, and this is true despite the fact that different

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 107

Multi-Level Governance in the EU: How Many Floors Will the Building Have?

107

Member States have different principles whereby they organise administration.
Weber defined an ‘ideal’ administration as one which is legally regulated, administratively regulated, formal, politically neutral and well educated. The
origins of a modern administrative system can be found in Prussia, where
administrators were known as ‘servants of the state’, which allowed them to
identify themselves with the state.23 Administrators in European Union Member
States today unambiguously identify themselves with their countries, but it is
hard to know with what the employees of the supranational level identify
themselves – their country of origin or the EU.
The development of multi-level governance has created new administrative
challenges. Administrations have to accept new values such as partnership
and communication, which have not been treated sufficiently seriously before
now. Communication will be important not only among the levels of governance, but also with players in the private sector, non-governmental organisations and the public at large.

The model of governance which has been officially proposed
by the European Union
The question of the EU’s legitimacy must be viewed in the context of the
EU administrative apparatus – one which the residents of all Member States
tend to see as excessively complicated and bureaucratic. The EU proposed
institutional reforms in an attempt to react to negative attitudes vis-à-vis ‘the
Brussels bureaucracy.’ In 2001, it published a White Paper, ‘European Governance’ which sketched out various aspects of governance reform in the EU, along
with principles of good governance. The European Commission defined reforms
of governance as a strategic goal back in 2000. The following principles were
selected by the EC as the most important ones in terms of good governance:
openness, participation, accountability, effectiveness and coherence.24 Interestingly, the principles cited in the White Paper are very similar to those in the
EAS, even though the EAS is more or less seen as an academic construct, as
opposed to an example of actual administrative practice. The principle of
openness states clearly that EU institutions must work openly, that they must
join together with Member State institutions in explaining the EU’s activities
to the public.25 The principle of participation is based on the assumption that

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

108

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 108

IVETA REINHOLDE

the public must take part in all phases of policy development. Interestingly,
the European Commission feels that the correct understanding of this principle
depends on the administrative structures of Member States – structures which
certainly must involve the public in shaping and implementing EU-level
policy.26 The principle of accountability speaks to the European Commission’s
belief that the competences and responsibilities of each institution must be
defined very clearly in EU policy development, and that applies both to
national and to EU institutions. The principle of effectiveness involves two
aspects – introduction of effective and timely policy, and making sure that this
is done at the right level of governance. The principle of coherence clearly says
that regional and local governments must be more closely involved in the development of EU policy.27 On the basis of these principles, one can sketch out an
ideal model of governance, one in which all institutions act in a way which the
public understands, a way that is effective in ensuring the participation of people
in designing policy and approving decisions. The result of this is that the activities of institutions at various levels are agreed and understandable.
The European Commission is planning several interrelated activities on the
basis of the aforementioned principles – redefining the roles and responsibilities
of institutions, attracting the experience and skills of regional and local actors
in implementing policy, and defining the rules whereby EU regulatory agencies
are established.28 There is a question, though – in defining these activities, has
the EU not once again covered up the true essence of administrative governance
with the blanket of ‘drawing closer to the public?’ The White Paper does not
define the model of governance which the EU is pursuing. Essentially this
means that the White Paper legitimates the existing and traditional multi-level
model of governance, with a limited number of levels of governance. The model
can be changed, but it is quite far from the ideal system that is addressed in the
White Paper.
Partnership, participation and the existence of formal and informal networks – this is more than just a tribute to modern values and understandings
about public participation in politics. Participation and communication among
many different players are values which will have everything to do with the
future of multi-level governance. In designing policy, the EU has always used
the so-called linear model for implementing policy. Decisions are taken, and
institutions must implement those decisions. If that fails, or if the implementation proves to be unsuccessful, the next step is usually to enhance the capacity

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 109

Multi-Level Governance in the EU: How Many Floors Will the Building Have?

109

of the relevant institutions or to talk about a lack of political will in implementing decisions. The current situation seems favourable for introduction of
a non-linear or interactive model which involves many different players in the
formulation and implementation of decisions.29 This reduces the likelihood of
failures. In the context of the White Paper, the words ‘better involvement’ refer
not just to public information, but also to the introduction of activities that are
important if multi-level governance is truly to be developed further. In other
words, the European Commission promises to establish systematic dialogue
with the national and European associations of regional and local governments so as to engage in consultations on policy from the early stages of policy
development. The Committee of Regions, for its part, is to popularise best
practice at the local and regional level in terms of how to become involved in
the taking of EU-related decisions at the national level. Member States must
improve the involvement of the local and the regional level in EU policy development.30 This means that the EU may expect the emergence of a double
system of communications, one in which the European Commission can
communicate directly with the regional and the local level and the national
level can do the same. This approach would reduce the extent to which the
national level is ignored – the problem which occurred when the EC began to
engage in direct communications with the regions.
Which model of governance is proposed in the European Constitutional
Treaty (the European Constitution)? It speaks to a specific algorithm of multilevel governance – there are commonly defined goals, and the national level
(i.e., the Member States), transfer the responsibility for pursuing those goals to
the EU (the supranational level). The supranational level undertakes the role
of co-ordination among Member States so that they, too, seek to pursue the
defined goals. The relationship between the supranational and the national
level must be viewed through the prism of authority – the things which each
level is permitted to do independently, as well as those areas in which the two
levels are interrelated. The European Constitution divides all authority into
three types – exclusive, shared and supporting authority. Exclusive authority
rests with the supranational level, shared authority is divided up between the
two levels, and supporting authority means that the supranational level offers
necessary support to the national level. According to the European Constitution,
exclusive authority would apply to the customs union, competition, monetary
policy in those Member States which use the euro, preservation of the biological

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

110

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 110

IVETA REINHOLDE

resources of the sea, and common trade policy. Shared authority would refer
to the internal market, economic, social and territorial cohesion, agriculture,
fishing (except preservation of the biological resources of the sea), the environment, protection of consumer rights, transport, energy supplies, European
communications networks, the space of freedom, security and the rule of law,
social policy, and protection of public health, as referred to Part III of the European Constitution.31 Supporting authority applies to health care, industry,
culture, tourism, education, youth, sports, civil defence and administrative cooperation.32
This distribution of authority points to two important trends. First of all,
there are few areas in which the supranational level does not intervene at all.
Second, the areas in which there is supporting authority are weaker, to a
certain extent, than those in which there is exclusive authority. Administrative
co-operation, for instance, is a supporting authority which cannot be ignored
in the context of the European Constitution. The classical principle of the EU is
not to intervene in the administrative affairs of Member States, and that is seen
here, as well. The EU’s acquis says nothing about administrative operations,
and this is an area of supporting authority, but the ability of the EU to achieve
its goals does depend on how well each level of governance and all of the
levels of governance together do their work. It must be concluded that the EU
has its head in the sand to some extent in this regard – it spitefully refuses to
recognise the fact that relations between the national and the supranational
level have long since passed the place where only support and co-ordination
are in place, that the division of authority and competence that is enshrined in
the European Constitution could just as well be applied to any other country
and could be found in any other country’s constitution. When it comes to implementing EU law, the EU promises to support exchanges of civil servants
and training programmes that would help to ensure the confluence of administrative structures in the various Member States. This also shows that the
hiding of the EU’s head in the sand hides an interest in influencing the way in
which Member State administrations do their work.
When it comes to the governance principles that are enshrined in the European Constitution, the distribution of competence and the strengthening of the
principle of subsidiarity in the text are of importance. The supranational level
is given authority which is based on the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality. In other words, the supranational level promises to carry out functions

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 111

Multi-Level Governance in the EU: How Many Floors Will the Building Have?

111

which other levels of governance cannot handle for one reason or another or
which would not achieve defined goals if they were handled by other levels.
Undoubtedly, the European Constitution presents an entirely new interpretation of the principle of subsidiarity, because it offers greater opportunities for
the regional and the local level to participate in the shaping of EU policy. On
the one hand, this recognises the national identity and the political and constitutional structure of Member States (a structure which includes local governments), but on the other hand the Member States (i.e., the national level) are
still responsible for doing the things that are necessary in order to ensure that
defined goals are achieved. From this perspective, the European Constitution
recognises the regional and local level of governance, but it shies away from
clearly defining the relationship among the supranational, regional and local
level.

Latvia’s interests in multi-level governance
Latvia’s position vis-à-vis its administrative system was simple before the
country joined the EU. The goal of joining the EU defined the things which the
government did in the area of administrative reforms. The government of
Prime Minister Guntars Krasts (August 1997 – November 1998) was the first to
recognise the influence of European integration on administrative reforms
that had until then been considered to be an exclusively domestic issue. Administrative reforms were linked to successes in foreign policy, and they
became one of the reasons for foreign policy success insofar as integration into
the EU was concerned. The Krasts government declared that Latvia’s institutions must make active use of ‘the advantages and opportunities which Latvia
receives as an associated state of the European Union.’33 The government
stated clearly that only by enhancing administrative capacity could it make
sure that norms would be approximated and laws would be implemented.34
This makes it clear that the government understood the extent to which the
administrative apparatus could stand in the way of achieving the goal of EU
membership.
In July 1997, the European Commission released its ‘AGENDA 2000’ report,
and it was very frank about administrative problems in Latvia which would
make it difficult for Latvia to introduce the acquis. This could have led to

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

112

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 112

IVETA REINHOLDE

domestic policy discussions about implementation of the acquis and the model
of governance that would be needed in order to represent Latvia’s interests in
the EU. There was no such debate, however, neither then nor in subsequent
years. Latvia resembled a hard-working schoolchild, doing its pre-accession
homework and improving its administrative system only to the extent that the
EU instructed it to do so.
During the accession process, the European Commission spoke to the principles of the EAS – professionalism, objectivity, openness, transparency, accountability, utility and effectiveness – in its annual reports on Latvia’s readiness for
EU membership. Any EU concerns about Latvia’s administrative capacity had
to be reviewed in the context of Latvia’s administrative ability to absorb the
acquis, because delays in administrative reforms made that more difficult.35
Even at the moment of accession Latvia still had not defined the desirable administrative system, to say nothing about any views as to multi-level governance
in Europe. Latvia joined the EU in May 2004, and the situation basically has
not changed since then, not even in the context of the ‘Latvia in the European
Union’ strategy,36 and not even when the Foreign Ministry began consultations
with its social partners.
It is worth asking which aspects of multi-level governance are important to
Latvia. We must first talk about the country’s ability to define its interests and
then to move forward at all levels of governance in relation to those interests.
Second, we must review the aspect of decentralisation, where relationships
and distribution of functions between the national level and local governments are among the most important issues of all. Third, we must focus on the
mechanism of communications between the central government and local
governments.
The most important issue – that of administrative capacity – has been on
the agenda in Latvia since the early 1990s, but the emphases have changed
over the course of time. Capacity in dealing with issues of domestic policy was
needed at the beginning of the 1990s, but by the end of the decade, administrative capacity was a prerequisite for achieving foreign policy goals. Although
the issue of administrative capacity has been addressed in many different
documents, no improvements were achieved for a long time due to the absence
of clearly defined criteria which would help in determining whether the
capacity was sufficient. One or two criteria should be identified to ascertain
that there is enough capacity to define interests.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 113

Multi-Level Governance in the EU: How Many Floors Will the Building Have?

113

The second issue – decentralisation – is very important for Latvia. The
‘Latvia in the European Union’ strategy has stressed the principle of subsidiarity,
which means that decisions must be taken at the lowest feasible level of governance. No matter which type of multi-level governance is implemented, Latvia
must think about the capacity of the local level of governance. An urgent
necessity is to complete administrative territorial reforms, which have been on
the agenda for many years but have not been completed to this very day. In the
context of the future of Europe, administrative territorial reform is not just an
issue of merging parishes into larger amalgamated territories. It has to do with
the capacity of the local level to take part in a multi-level mechanism of governance. It was once said that the EU suffered from ‘federal blindness’, and in that
case Latvia suffers from ‘regional blindness’ – it simply cannot deal with the
issue of regional reforms without any rational explanation for this failure.
The Latvian Association of Local Self-Governments (LPS) believes that there
must be greater decentralisation of power and that local governments must be
entrusted with greater responsibility for the taking of decisions. This would
allow the central level to devote greater resources to the representation of
Latvia’s interests.37 The LPS also believes that development of the national level
is very important in this regard. Latvia must take advantage of the opportunities
that are afforded by the EU in terms of funding for regional development.38
Interestingly, when the LPS and the Foreign Ministry discussed this issue,
there was neither talk about administrative territorial or regional reform, nor
about the capacity of local governments to handle decentralised power. Multilevel governance involves communications between regional and local governments, as well as with the supranational level, which means that the capacity
of the local and regional governance system is very important in terms of the
quality of those communications.
The third, a very important issue for Latvia is communication between the
local and regional level on the one hand and with the supranational level on
the other. The LPS opened an office in Brussels in November 2005, and its goal
is to ensure that the interests of Latvia’s local governments are observed in the
EU’s decision making process. This confirms the existence of the aforementioned type of communications. The city of Rîga and the Rîga Developmental
District Council opened their Brussels office even earlier, in 2004. This is a classic example of multi-level governance with direct communication between the
supranational level and the local level and without the involvement of the

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

114

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 114

IVETA REINHOLDE

national level. This raises the question of whether there has been sufficient
communication between the national and local level to formulate national
interests. When it comes to consultations and agreement on national positions
with the LPS as the local government representative of Latvia’s interests in the
EU, the process must be organised by the institution which handles the agenda
of the relevant EU institution.39 This means that the national level communicates with the local level on how Latvia’s interests are to be represented. It is hard
to say how successful this communications process is. Latvia has typically
done poorly in co-operating with other participants in multi-level governance.
National and local institutions seldom work with local community organisations,
non-governmental organisations, or the private sector.
The key aspects of multi-level governance in Latvia can be reviewed from
the perspective of each level’s strengths and weaknesses. At the national level,
the strength is the ability to co-operate and communicate with the supranational
level. These skills have been developed ever since Latvia first indicated its
desire to join the EU. The strength is also the system whereby official national
positions are designed – it has been implemented, and it can be improved if
necessary. Labour shortages have affected the administrative structure, and so
there is a theoretical need to enhance capacity at the national level. Knowledge
and skills must be improved not just at the central level, but also at other
levels, as well as among NGO activists who work on EU issues. It is quite difficult today to differentiate between those issues which are related to the EU
and those which are not. One can always ask about the level to which administrative capacity has to be raised, and when that capacity will be sufficient.
At the regional level, Latvia basically has no strengths, because it really
does not have a regional level in the first place. Latvia is divided up into 33
regional local government territories – 26 districts and seven major cities. One
can equally claim that the country’s five planning regions are what should be
considered to be the regional level. There are also ‘five statistical regions’ with
borders that are different than those of the planning region.40 No matter what
the regions are called, however, it cannot be said that they are really the regional level of multi-level governance. The local level of governance is fragmentary in Latvia, and individual local governments cannot be serious players,
both because they are usually too small and because they lack the necessary
skills.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Given all of this, the ‘Latvia in the European Union’ strategy should offer a
view as to how governance can develop further. The view of governance that
is presently in the strategy is very similar to that which was identified in the
EU’s White Paper on European governance and in the European Constitution.
Latvia is not trying to come up with its own views about the development of
local governance, it is merely expressing its support for the processes which
are underway. Latvia agrees that there must be new common policies which will
strengthen the EU. Each policy of this type moves the EU closer to becoming a
state. The second important element in the strategy is that Latvia accepts the
generally known idea that the EU’s decision making mechanism is not effective
and requires further reform. As Foreign Minister Artis Pabriks has put it, ‘Talk
of European bureaucracy is not just political folklore. The development of the
EU has actually been hindered by the EU’s complicated structure and the
sometimes unclear distribution of responsibility among various levels of administrative and political decision making and the relevant institutions.’41 The
strategy and the minister’s statement both make it very clear that Latvia is
concerned about the administrative work of the EU, but the fact is that Latvia’s

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

116

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 116

IVETA REINHOLDE

administrative system is a part of that apparatus. Accordingly, if the claim is
made that ‘it is in Latvia’s interests to reduce administrative obstacles in the
EU,’42 then thought must also be given to the elimination of obstacles in
Latvia’s own administrative system.
Latvia expressed automatic support for the model enshrined in the European Constitution by ratifying the treaty. That means accepting the principle
of subsidiarity, as well as the model of governance that is proposed in the
Constitution. It requires more active consultations with the public, but given
that the target audience for the strategy is made up of the Latvian population,
a clearly positive fact is that the strategy emphasises regional development
and admits to the need to complete administrative territorial reforms. This
does not mean that the reforms really will be completed, however, because the
issue has been addressed in a great many strategic documents in the past.
Multi-level governance can be described as a partnership among various
players. Latvia’s interests will be served by a flexible approach in shaping
partnerships with local governments in other countries and with the private
sector, depending on the issue at hand. Partnership means being active and
flexible, it means demonstrating initiative and defending one’s priorities actively. Partnership is also important within the state, and a fundamental aspect
here is consultations with non-governmental organisations as to what Latvia’s
interests really are. Finally, partnership involves a discussion with the public,
because Latvia will effectively implement multi-level governance only if the
public truly believes that administrators and politicians represent and shape
Latvia’s interests in the EU.

Scenarios for developing multi-level governance
Several scenarios as to the further development of multi-level governance
can be proposed, given the existing problems and future challenges related to
same.
The first scenario is simple – the existing distribution of competence and
power among levels of governance is preserved. Over the next three to five
years, indeed, this is a very likely possibility, both because of the failure of the
European Constitution and the fact that reforms in the EU institutions of
governance are proceeding very slowly. It is also true that the EU has always

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 117

Multi-Level Governance in the EU: How Many Floors Will the Building Have?

117

emphasised policies and their content, not the institutions which implement
the policies at various levels. This status quo scenario will remain realistic until
political agreement is reached on what to do with the European Constitution.
Once such agreement is achieved, the focus will turn to the next difficult issue –
the system of governance and how it should be organised. Because the EU
does not have any right to intervene in the administrative functions of Member
States, the matter of multi-level governance may not appear on the agenda
even if a decision is taken on the European Constitution. That would mean
that the multi-level governance system would develop as it has done until
now – reacting to existing practice and to challenges created by political change.
This scenario would be advantageous to Latvia in that the status quo would
allow the government to ignore domestic administrative problems such as
regional and administrative territorial reform, as well as the involvement of
the local community in policy development. If those problems are not addressed, however, Latvia will find it more difficult to formulate and represent
its interests in the long term.
The second possible scenario is provocative, but its possibility cannot be
denied. This scenario is based on the assumption that mutual agreement among
administrative structures in the Member States can create a unified model of
governance in the EU, with multi-level governance operating as a ‘regulatory
state.’43 This scenario is dominated by a process of policy development which
is routine and technically smooth. The functions and competences of the various
levels would remain virtually unchanged, but there would be networks of
various regulatory institutions among the Member States, their aim being to
ensure the equal implementation of the acquis throughout the EU. If this is
ensured in tandem with the values of the EAS in all Member States, that will
promote the development of a technologically unified system of governance.
This scenario is partially based on the first theoretical type of governance – the
one which is dominated by a lack of change, a hierarchy among the levels of
governance, and a promotion of the ideas of federalism. It must also be taken
into account, however, that the development of routine in the specification of
policy will occur very slowly, and it will take a long time for a unified model of
governance to emerge. The result of this scenario in the distant future will be a
European state. The father of bureaucracy, Max Weber said ‘great modern state
is absolutely dependent upon bureaucratic basis.’44 Before this can happen, the
administrations of the Member States will have to admit that they are sufficiently

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

118

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 118

IVETA REINHOLDE

identical. Politicians will have to admit the same. This could be a difficult decision,
because the structure of national administration is dependent on the political
structure which prevails in the country. Second, administrative apparatuses
are considered to be a partial symbol of sovereignty. This eliminates the possibility that multi-level governance could transform into the administrative
apparatus of a unified state. The only alternative is to create multi-level governance as the administrative apparatus of a federal state. The classical signs of
this have already been seen in practice. Power is distributed between two levels
(the EU and its Member States), with co-operation, authority and resources
regulated by EU treaties. EU law stands above the laws of Member States,
because qualified majority voting allows the EU to take decisions even if not
all Member States agree – decisions which all Member States must implement.
This, scenario, too, would allow Latvia to avoid a search for ways of resolving
problems with regional reform. If a federation were established, Latvia would
be a state in that federation. To become a powerful state, however, it would
have to establish a powerful mechanism for formulating state interests, and it
would have to work together far more actively with local governments. This
raises the question of the form of co-operation between the national level as
one party and the regional and local level as the other.
The third could, to a certain extent, be called a ‘reform’ scenario. It is based
on the assumption that nearly all of the reforms identified in the White Paper
‘European Governance’ will be introduced in response to political challenges and
to the alienation of many EU residents and that new reforms will be planned.
The pace of reform would increase. No matter how well planned and implemented, however, reforms are not a solution if their goal is not clearly identified. The scenario of reform would make sure that the EU is not accused of
inactivity, but it would also extend the period of reflection on Europe’s future.
Latvia’s advantage in this case is similar to that which it would experience
under the first scenario. It could avoid work on its internal administrative
problems. It is hard to say which of the scenarios would be best for Latvia and
Europe, however, because the development of multi-level governance fully
depends on political decisions vis-à-vis the future of Europe. The aforementioned scenarios, moreover, can overlap or change under the influence of varying
political circumstances.
To return to the two theoretically constructed types of multi-level governance, one must say that they define the way in which multi-level governance

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 119

Multi-Level Governance in the EU: How Many Floors Will the Building Have?

119

should be developed. In practice, the two types exist already, and they are
both used to some extent, but the fact is that there are still many chances to
develop and improve the system. Multi-level governance, moreover, is not a
mechanism which is created and then left to its own devices. It is a mechanism
which should help Europe to achieve its political and economic goals. The main
question as to the development of multi-level governance is not the number of
levels, but rather the content of the process. If one draws parallels to the building
of a home, one can say that it is possible to build an architecturally perfect
structure which is entirely inappropriate for people’s lives.

achieve results more effectively. Generally speaking, however, the link between the term
‘governance’ and the term ‘administration’ is very powerful. When one speaks of governance, one often stresses that the cornerstone for governance is the administrative aspect.

ANDREJS JAKOBSONS

The Development of European Economic Integration:
from Coal and Steel to a Single Economic Area
and Toward the Challenges of the Future
Now here, you see, it takes
all the running you can do to keep in the same place.
If you want to get somewhere else,
You must run at least twice as fast as that.
Lewis Carroll. ‘Through the Looking-Glass,
and What Alice Found There’

Introduction
The basic issue in this paper is the evolution of economic integration in
Europe since the end of the Second World War and the relationship between
this integration and the European system of values. I will try to consider the main
problems which may be faced by the European economy in the near future,
taking into account the global trends of economic development. In the first
part of the paper, let us take a brief look at the origins of the European Union
and the importance of economic aspects in this process. Then we shall turn to
the achievements that have been made during this period, both from the
perspective of integration and from that of economic development. Finally, we
shall assess trends in global economic development since the origins of the EU,
focusing on the way in which they have influenced economic development of
the Union. In conclusion, we shall try to characterise the main problems which
the EU economy might encounter in the near and more distant future, as well
as possible solutions to those problems. Let us also look at Latvia’s situation in
this context, judging the extent to which economic development problems in
the EU might also be important in terms of Latvia’s development.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 123

The Development of European Economic Integration: from Coal and Steel..

123

We all know that the roots of the European Union rest in economic considerations. During the initial phase of development, these factors encouraged
individual countries to work on mutual co-operation despite the serious
historical conflicts which were the result of two world wars. We can say that
the economic interests of European countries largely led to the further process
of integration – one that has considerably surpassed the initially defined economic goals. Since the Paris and Rome treaties, economic integration in Europe
has gradually moved to the back burner, and today it is just one of several
components of integration.
Is this because the job is finished? Of course not, but one must admit that
quite a bit has been achieved in the area of economic integration, at least in
comparison to other areas. We can speak of free trade, the movement toward
economic and monetary union (EMU), freer movement of labour in the EU, etc.
At the same time, we must remember that economic policy in the individual
Member States still relates to key political instruments, and the use of those
instruments may, in certain cases, hinder Europe’s ability to pursue its common goals. An increasingly important issue is whether a single economic area
really is the optimum goal. Would this ensure that Europe’s initial values are
developed in the context of the globalised economy of today? Even more, have
present-day global economic development trends created a situation in which
Europe will once again have to prove that it has not lost competitiveness and
is ready not only to influence processes within the EU, but also to preserve a
key role at the global level?

Economic integration as a motivation for European unification
after the Second World War
As was noted in the introduction, economic motivations were the main
reason why the major powers of Europe engaged in purely pragmatic considerations after the Second World War and managed to start talking about
closer co-operation. Despite significant achievements in the field of culture
and economics in the past, Europe’s actual situation was far less than enviable –
economies were destroyed, infrastructures were gone, and co-operation was
hindered by memories of the war which had just ended. Leading European
politicians, however, were quick to understand the need for closer co-operation.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

124

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 124

ANDREJS JAKOBSONS

Speaking at Zurich University in September 1946, Winston Churchill said that
a partnership between Germany and France must be the first step toward renewing the ‘European family.’ In 1948, the Benelux countries formed a customs
union which largely served as a model for the further economic integration of
Europe. The main event in this regard, however, was the Schuman Declaration,
which was essentially based on the purely pragmatic need for Germany and
France to co-operate in the production of coal and steel. A joint governing institution had to be established. The Schuman Declaration, which was issued in 1950,
included the idea that this co-operation could create foundations for Europe as
a federative state. Movement toward a unified Europe, in other words, began on the
basis of the need to work together in the economic arena. Economic integration,
indeed, was the only truly realistic path toward political unity in the future.
The next important step toward European unification was taken in 1951,
when the Treaty of Paris on the establishment of the European Coal and Steel
Community was signed. The most important economic principles in this agreement focused on economic growth, facilitation of employment, and enhanced
standards of living in Member States. It must be noted that despite the fact that
these goals were largely related to problems which seemed to be of importance
in the post-war context, European priorities today are still very much linked to
these particular subjects.
The Treaty of Rome of 1957 established the European Economic Community,
and that was the logical next step in bringing European countries together.
The treaty defined the fundamental principles for the development of Europe.
It officially defined the principle of ‘four freedoms’ – the free movement of
goods, services, capital and persons in the newly established economic space.
The Treaty of Rome also spoke to co-ordination of economic policy among
Member States, and this was a very important step in recognising mutual links
among European economies. The treaty also defined fundamental developmental
principles such as harmonised development of regions, balanced economic
growth, and enhanced improvements to standards of living.These ideas and
goals were not conceptually new in and of themselves, but the innovation in this
case related to the fact that a group of countries, as opposed to a single nation
state, agreed to pursue the relevant goals. From the institutional perspective,
Europe implemented the innovative process of creating supranational institutions which were assigned the task of achieving the goals. Once the process
was started, it created conflicts on far more than one occasion between the

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 125

The Development of European Economic Integration: from Coal and Steel..

125

economic interests of Europe and those of individual Member States, and between the ways in which these interests were pursued. Nevertheless, this was
unquestionably a key step toward the launch of European economic integration.

Implementation of Europe’s fundamental economic values:
an evaluation
Most of the values which were defined for Europe in the 1950s remain of
importance today although the situation today represents a massive step forward in comparison to the Europe which was ravaged by war. Most European
countries now are among the world’s wealthiest nations. The economic goals
which were formulated in the Treaty of Paris and the Treaty of Rome were
dynamic, because the main desire in the post-war period was to ensure proper
living conditions. Today, by contrast, this aim has been affected by the process
of globalisation as the main goal is not to fall behind other regions of the world –
regions which are developing even more dynamically in terms of economic
growth. In this context, we can conclude that the European economic values
which were defined in the treaties of the 1950s remain important in the presentday context, even if their interpretation has often changed.
European countries have taken great strides toward the establishment of a
single economic area, but we must also admit that progress has not always
been sufficiently fast. The process of economic integration in Europe is far
from finished, although the initial goals of economic integration are often seen
as self-evident. There have been certain changes in the way in which steps are
taken toward further European integration. The Maastricht Treaty, for instance,
was a key step toward political integration in Europe, even though it also spoke
to fundamentally important aspects of economic integration. In this context, it
is likely that the Maastricht Treaty will be remembered mostly because the
word ‘economic’ was removed from the name of the European Economic
Community. Did that mean that economic issues were no longer a priority in
Europe, that debates about the issue had been exhausted? On the contrary –
economic integration in Europe is not a completed process, it is a process which
faces ever new challenges, particularly in terms of the integration of new EU
Member States. As was noted previously, however, Europe is prepared to debate
more than just issues related to economic integration.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

126

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 126

ANDREJS JAKOBSONS

Europe is forced to deal with ever new challenges in the economic arena. In
the future, European development will be based more extensively on the ability
of Europe, as a single economic area, to compete in the global market. Issues
which relate to the ability of this single economic area to compete with other
important economic entities in the world will come to the fore. Competitiveness
will become the most important prerequisite for achieving the values that were
defined in the 1950s – growth, employment and enhanced standards of living.
There will be a need for increasingly co-ordinated policies in pursuit of these
goals – policies which relate to the training and retention of an educated and
competitive labour force. This has to do with issues such as education, employment and also migration. Europe must be prepared to implement co-ordinated
policies in these areas.
Despite the fact that the Treaty of Rome was signed half a century ago, some
of its principles have not yet been fully implemented. That is partially because
of EU enlargement and other objective circumstances, but it is clear that it is
too early to speak of the completion of economic integration in Europe. Even
more – it will be possible to continue with economic integration and enhancement of competitiveness only if Europe manages to implement unified policies
which are focused on the future. The EU is still far behind the United States in
terms of the integration of various economic processes, and it often suffers from
the fact that laws differ in various Member States. These factors may create the
greatest risk for Europe as a single economic area in terms of its competitiveness
in the global market. This is all the more true given that the main global
competitors have already resolved problems of this nature by creating prerequisites for more effective co-operation within their individual economies.
When it comes to the ability of Europe to maintain its leading role in the
global economy, it has to be said that despite the fact that Europe has taken
great strides in terms of material welfare since the Second World War, still
there are threats which may cause changes in that situation over a longer
period of time. Table 1 shows differences in economic growth rates between
the EU and certain other areas of the world – ones which will probably play a
more and more significant role in the global economy in future years.
This table makes it quite clear that Europe’s economic development has
slowed down in the 21st century. Rapid economic growth in India and China
is fostered by the low average income level in those countries in comparison to
that in the EU, but it is, nevertheless, clear that these major countries are

becoming increasingly important competitors in the global market. Europe’s
situation might be even further complicated by several other key factors,
which will be discussed in the next chapters. Economic growth indicators in
the EU are fairly positive, true, but future changes in the global economy must
also be taken into account.

Future challenges for Europe
Economic integration in Europe is a long-term process which began more
than 50 years ago. There have, of course, been massive changes in the European
and global economy during that period in time. In this section, therefore, let us
turn to the key challenges which Europe will face in the future, looking, too, at
their effects and possible solutions. Not all of these challenges are of a purely
economic nature, but they will be reviewed from the perspective of economic
development. Such long-term issues as aging of populations, to be sure, are of
fundamental economic importance.
1. Population aging
Europe has historically had to deal with problems of a demographic nature.
During the 20th century, these difficulties were primarily caused by two world

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

128

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 128

ANDREJS JAKOBSONS

wars which led to a significant reduction in the working age population.
Emigration and increased mortality both played a role in this. The issue which
I would like to emphasise in this paper, however, has to do with significant
declines in fertility rates in Europe. This will have consequences over the long
term, and not just demographic consequences either. The most important consequences have to do with the fact that if the current intensity of fertility and
mortality is preserved, the average age of the people of Europe will continue to
increase, thus reducing the proportion of working-age people in each population.
The issue of aging populations has long been considered in Europe on the
basis of more than just demographic issues. Forecasts about changes in population numbers conjure up ideas about what the people of Europe might expect
in the nearer or more distant future. The United Nations, for instance, have
issued population projections which suggest that over the next 50 years, the
population of Europe may decline by some 10%.2 What is more, we must remember that in the near future, Europe will experience lower fertility rates than
those in many other parts of the world. It is expected that by 2050, the proportion
of working-age people in Europe will be at a level of 57.4% of all residents,
while elsewhere in the world it is forecast to be at a level of 63.7% (Table 2). In
that case, the demographic burden in Europe will be higher than elsewhere in
the world, and that may exacerbate the problem of labour shortages. It must also
be recognised that rapid population increases in most of the world’s countries
mean that the situation in Europe is quite unique.
We can, of course, be optimists about this issue and say that Europe has dealt
successfully with more than one situation in which population numbers have
dropped significantly in a short period of time – once again, we can consider
the two world wars of the 20th century. The global situation, however, has
changed in this context, too. Approximately a century ago, economic policies
in Europe were minimally influenced by countries such as China and India. In
the early 21st century, by contrast, these are two countries which are increasing
their importance in the global economy at a very rapid pace indeed. Foreign
trade policies are becoming more and more liberal, transport costs are, generally
speaking, declining, and the export of goods and services is becoming increasingly important. All of these factors persistanly force Europe to deal with
countries which, quite recently, could not compete to any significant degree
with the world’s economically developed countries.

Table 2 offers a more precise look at the European problem of aging populations. The median age of the European population in 2050 will be 47.1 years,
for instance, which will be nearly 10 years higher than the world average. The
proportion of population aged 65 or older in Europe in 2050, moreover, will be
at a level of 27.6% — far above the indicator elsewhere in the world..
The worsening demographic situation in Europe must also be assessed from
the perspective of the financial status of countries. Solidarity of generations is
a principle which is put to widespread use in many countries to ensure
people’s old age pensions. If adequate attention is not paid to long-term developmental trends, however, there is the risk that economic growth rates and
lowering proportions of working age population will endanger the overall
financial situation of the relevant country. In other words, the principle of solidarity of generations may fall under threat because of a significant reduction
in the number of working-age people. In the case of the EU, these are problems
which are left largely under the supervision of individual Member States,
because there is no unified approach to pensions and social aid. Still, the problem
may come to the surface if Europe finds itself encountering unexpected problems
of an economic nature. It is very important, too, to remember that the retirement
age may well be increased in various countries as the life expectancy of
Europeans grows longer.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 130

130

ANDREJS JAKOBSONS

The situation in Latvia is not particularly clear in this regard. Table 2 shows
that Latvia’s demographic indicators are such that Latvia can expect even
more serious problems than the average country in Europe. Though Latvia’s
pension system was designed relatively recently, its authors took into account
the anticipated drop in the number of working-age people in the country. This
will become important when those children who were born in the 1990s enter
the labour market, because they are considerably fewer in number than those
who were born in the two previous decades. Rapid economic growth has so
far supported Latvia’s social budget, but the authors of the country’s pension
plan did not take into account the fact that Latvia would be joining the
European Union and that the EU labour market would be open to Latvia’s
residents. From the perspective of the social budget, this is a worrisome situation,
because of unpredictable drops in tax income which have no effect on the
number of people who are retired or retiring. The situation might be improved
to a certain extent if Latvia were to open up its own labour market. That could
bring in additional tax income to compensate for income lost from those who
have emigrated. In that case, however, Latvia, as a potential place of employment, will face serious competition from other EU Member States, and it might
well face domestic policy consequences as a result of an excessively hasty
opening up of the labour market.

2. Europe’s competitiveness
in the global market
As noted above, the origins of the European Union in the wake of the
Second World War were closely linked to the need to establish closer economic
co-operation. The relevant issues were important enough to convince recent
enemies on the battlefield – Germany and France – that closer economic cooperation would be mutually advantageous. In subsequent years, there were
fewer and fewer questions about closer economic co-operation, and that facilitated the further development of the EU. However, the issue of economic
integration, which was initially the primary motivation for closer ties, has
gradually been pushed to the back burner since the beginning of the process.
Doubts about the need for economic integration dissipated as foreign trade
volumes increased, but they were replaced by doubts about other issues.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 131

The Development of European Economic Integration: from Coal and Steel..

131

In this context, it is important to consider changes that have occurred in the
global economy over the last 50 years and to think about whether the fundamental principles of the Paris and Rome treaties are still of key importance in
Europe now and in the future. At the close of the Second World War, Europe’s
economy was devastated, and the renewal of industry and trade was a key
priority. The issue of global competitiveness was not really on the table at that
time, because foreign trade growth was not yet one of the most important
driving engines for economic development. In subsequent years, however, the
European economy developed successfully, and participation in international
trade and the need to strengthen external competitiveness became more and
more important. However, we must remember the fundamental changes in
the global economy which occurred during the same time period. In the mid-20th
century, the main competition for European countries in the world of foreign
trade came from the USA, but by the end of the 20th century, Japan was one of
the main players in the world of international commerce. Furthermore, economic
development trends in the 20th century were focused on market liberalisation,
because countries wished to gain greater income from foreign trade by putting
their competitive advantages to work. The General Agreement on Tariffs and
Trade (GATT) and, later, the World Trade Organisation worked to promote
economic integration in the world and to reduce trade barriers. It must certainly be admitted that this work is far from complete even today, but increasing
income in European countries, trade liberalisation, and the easier flow of
goods, services and workers among countries – all of this created a situation in
which countries which had been among the most important players in the
global economy of the 20th century found themselves at the risk of losing their
leading positions. Low labour costs became a key factor in the decision of
businesses from the world’s developed countries to move their manufacturing
facilities to places where costs are lower, where there are fewer limitations
related to environmental protection, etc.
In a situation of this type, countries which can offer lower labour costs and
larger labour forces play an increasingly important role in the global economy.
In Europe, these trends have led to the process of deindustrialisation, and the
result is that approximately two-thirds of added value in Europe today are
based on the service sector. China and India are among the most important
competitors – their role has expanded over the last 20 years and will continue
to expand in future years as a result of lower manufacturing costs. All of this

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 132

132

ANDREJS JAKOBSONS

means that countries which are at a comparatively lower level of economic
development can develop their own industries more quickly and take part in
foreign trade so as to earn revenues that are not available in the domestic
market.
Europeans may be quite surprised at the rapid economic growth of China
and India, but specialists in the area of economic history point out that right
up until the Industrial Revolution in Europe, Asian countries played the leading
role in the global economy. The situation underwent a radical change only at
the end of the 19th century, when the leading role was taken over by the British
Empire and the United States. For most of the 20th century, dominance in the
global economy was enjoyed by the United States, European countries, as well
as the Soviet Union and Japan. It must be noted, however, that the most important trends during this period had to do with globalisation and a rapid
increase in the importance of foreign trade in the global economy. Countries
with lower income levels have been adapting to this situation more and more
successfully in recent times. Removal of trade barriers has stimulated this
process to a very great degree, and many barriers have been lifted by the
GATT and the WTO. The next table shows the proportions of the world’s most
important economic players in the global economy over the last 25 years.

As can be seen in Table 3, the European Union has maintained an almost

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

133

role of the United States has continued to increase. In the context of what has
happened in the last decades, the dynamic development of certain Asian countries,
of course, must be particularly re-emphasised. The power of the Chinese economy
continues to increase very rapidly. Referring to the Lewis Carroll’s quote at the
beginning of this paper, one can conclude that Europe has run quickly in the
last 25 years, but it will have to run even faster if it wants to maintain its leading
role in the global economy in the future, too.
Given the aforementioned changes in the development of the global economy, it is important to determine whether Europe is prepared to compete with
other countries in the global marketplace as Europe has recently encountered
problems related to its global competitiveness. Clearly, the European economy
has not been able to compete with many other parts of the world in terms of
labour costs — that has been true for quite some time now. Still, there are
several European countries which spitefully maintain their high ranking on
lists of the world’s most competitive countries. Finland, Sweden and Denmark
can be mentioned as vivid examples — high labour costs do not keep them from
finding themselves high up on those lists. Europeans, apparently, will have to
come to an understanding of why this is true if they are to formulate the desirable direction for Europepe’s economic development. Labour costs are just
one factor in the modern-day global economy, labour productivity factors are
becoming more and more important. The question may instead be this: How
to ensure that European countries have developed the most important prerequisites for a high level of competitiveness? These prerequisites include a
high quality of education that is universally available to everyone, the use of
innovation in production, and the use of high technologies.
Apparently, the most unpleasant aspect of competitiveness in Europe at
this time is that those European countries which are a step ahead of others in
this regard may set an unachievable goal for other Member States, thus turning
the ideal of a unified Europe into a formality or a dream. The World Economic
Forum has released ratings of the competitiveness of various countries in the
world which describe this situation quite well.5 In 2006, the 15 ‘old’ countries
of the European Union were ranked between second place (Finland) and 47th
place (Greece). This clearly illustrates the heterogeneous composition of Europe
from the perspective of global competitiveness. The new Member States do
not lag far behind the others in this regard. Poland, which joined the EU in
2004, is in 48th place on the list – lowest among the new EU Member States.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

the study, somewhat break the European mould by appearing on the list in
68th and 72nd place respectively. The next table shows changes in the position
