alone are premature but it is clear that from the perspective of global competitiveness, India and China still have vast untapped development potential.
From the perspective of global competitiveness, the EU will have to cope with
the fact that the competing countries and regions which are creating the greatest
concerns at this time will probably become even more serious competitors in
the future, and not just because of lower costs. Anyone who looks at the indicators of global competitiveness cannot fail to notice the fundamental progress
that has been achieved by numerous countries in Asia. In the future, they will
become serious competitors for European countries, not just because of lower
production costs, but also because of their vast potential in terms of labour
resources and increased educational levels.

135

3. The Lisbon Strategy as a possible solution
The situation is not very encouraging even if potential influence of external
factors on European economic development are ignored. Within Europe itself,
there are still quite a few unresolved questions which have to do with the
rational use of the region’s limited labour supply. One of the most widely
discussed topics concerns the free movement of labour. This is a problem
which has not yet been fully resolved since (or because of) the enlargement of
the EU. Barriers against more optimal structuring of production and economic
integration are still in place as a result of this. It is possible that the existing
limitations and periods of transition keep competitive European companies
from finding sufficient workers to compete successfully at the international
level – and that is happening in what is supposedly a unified European economic area!
The economy of the EU has developed in accordance with the economic
priorities which were defined in the treaties of Paris and Rome, and there have
been significant successes in this regard. Despite this, however, it has to be
noted that external economic circumstances have changed substantially during
the ensuing period of time, and there were quite a few economic indicators at
the end of the 20th century which suggested that Europe’s competitiveness
was declining. One was the lower rate of economic growth in comparison to
the main global competitor – the United States. The rate of labour productivity
also increased sluggishly. The demographic aspect may be of great importance
in ensuring long-term economic development. The Lisbon Strategy, thus, may
be interpreted as Europe’s response to the global challenges which have been
reviewed in this paper and which are becoming more and more important for
Europe at this time. To a certain extent, the Lisbon Strategy also indicates that
Europe has comprehended its long term problems and is starting to seek ways
of resolving them.
The stated aim of the Lisbon Strategy is to create (or recreate) Europe as the
world’s most competitive economic space, this despite the comparatively unfavourable trends of the late 20th century. One of the main priorities in the
strategy relates to labour availability and the qualifications of the labour force.
It is certain that the level of people’s income in European countries and the
existence of employee organisations mean that low labour costs will not be an
advantage at the global level in the foreseeable future. Moreover, in purely

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

136

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 136

ANDREJS JAKOBSONS

quantitative terms, the European population cannot compete with countries
such as China and India, therefore the strategy, is focused on the more efficient
use of Europe’s labour resources, as well as on increasing the education and
skills of that labour force. Another key issue is financing for research and
science. Given high labour costs in Europe, such spending is necessary so that
the region can compete on the basis of products and services with a particularly high level of added value.
Achieving the goals of the Lisbon Strategy may prove to be a major challenge
for the EU’s Member States, but it must also be admitted that this represents
an opportunity to move in the only strategically correct direction from the perspective of developing Europe’s global competitiveness. Sadly, progress in this
regard has not been encouraging. Overall employment indicators and other
indicators related to the Lisbon Strategy did not change sufficiently by the
year 2005 to allow anyone to say that the strategy was being pursued very
successfully. The overall level of employment in the EU rose from 63.4% in
2000 to 65.2% in 2005.6 This progress does not seem to be sufficient to reach a
level of 70% by 2010. Comparatively more rapid progress has been made in the
employment of women and older people, but there are doubts as to whether
the goals will be achieved with much believability. Another goal is to spend
3% of GDP on science and research, but actual spending is not increasing sufficiently quickly to achieve this.
If we compare Latvia’s indicators to the EU average and to the goals that
are defined in the Lisbon Strategy, it is very important, first of all, to consider
historical aspects, which by no means are homogeneous in all of Europe. It has
been repeated over and over that Latvia’s strengths in comparison to other
Member States include the comparatively high level of women in the labour
market (in 2005, Latvia was close to the goal of 60%) and a high level of overall
education in the country. On the other hand, there are also serious problems –
a low level of research spending and difficulties in increasing the overall level
of employment. Latvia has undergone rapid economic growth over the last
several years, and, hopefully, this will serve as a positive factor in ensuring
greater investments in science and research. In this area in particular, Latvia is
still far below the EU average, and to a certain extent this contradicts the
comparatively high level of education in the country.
From the macroeconomic perspective, Latvia’s competitiveness in external
markets is a critically important issue from the short and long term perspective

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 137

The Development of European Economic Integration: from Coal and Steel..

137

alike. The country’s development model is based on rapid growth mostly in
those sectors which are focused on the domestic market. This is not a sustainable
model. The Latvia’s negative savings – investment balance over the last few
years has been compensated largely by the inflow of external financial resources into the country, and that has created a situation in which private
sector companies and individuals are borrowing more and more money from
abroad, thus becoming more indebted. Internal market development cannot
maintain a high growth rate for too long, particularly in a country such as
Latvia – one which has an economy that is both small and open. Similar developmental problems have been encountered by other countries which have
experienced rapid growth in specific sectors of their national economies. Economic imbalance causes the risk of diminishing the relevant country’s competitiveness in the external market for a long time in the future. Latvia must think
about this risk, because growth only in sectors related to the domestic market,
along with a high rate of inflation under the circumstances of a fixed currency
exchange rate can create fundamental long-term problems with the country’s
external competitiveness.

Conclusions
The integration of European countries which began in the mid-20th century
has been driven by internal and external factors alike. Initially, the emergence
of the EU was linked closely to the economic interests of its Member States.
Countries were helped to overcome the post-war economic disintegration of
the continent. The need to merge their limited resources so as to ensure economic growth proved to be a very important driving force, one which allowed
countries to overcome political disagreements. Results achieved during the
first 50 years after the launch of integration have been very significant. In
terms of GDP, the EU economy is the largest in the world, even beating the
USA. Economic development trends in the world, however, have endangered
Europe’s leading role in the global economy which may fall under threat in the
future. The most important short term indicators point to declining economic
growth rates, a decline in labour productivity growth to the point where it has
fallen below the level in competing regions, and unresolved issues related to
stimulating employment levels. In the long term, the demographic problem

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

138

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 138

ANDREJS JAKOBSONS

may prove to be the one which hinders economic growth to the greatest degree.
One can already predict a substantial decline in the number of European
working age population.
Assuming that Europe wish to retain its leading position in the world economy, solutions to the aforementioned problems can be sought in the following
directions:
1) Europe continues attempts toward science-intensive production which is
focused on high technologies so as to preserve its leadership in the global
economy despite the problem of labour shortages (a highly qualified
labour force, lower employment levels in the future);
2) Europe partially compensates for the labour shortage and the increasing
labour costs by liberalising immigration, by competing with developing
countries, and by simultaneously protecting its advantages in scienceintensive production (a labour force of both high and low qualifications,
moderate employment levels);
3) Europe preserves the existing model of development, adapting it to the
expected demographic trends – promoting employment of the elderly
and ensuring higher participation in the labour market among all age
and gender groups (a highly qualified labour force, attempts to achieve
higher employment rates in the future based on higher participation in
the labour market).
The approach taken by the EU has not yet yielded clearly positive results.
The Lisbon Strategy, which dates back to 2000, is aimed at increasing employment rates and at developing science and research through greater investments
in that particular area. This is a strategically correct direction as it reflects one
of the main possible trends for development, but progress, sadly, has been
insufficient. Europe is not much closer to any long-term solution to its problems.
Moreover, even successful implementation of the Lisbon Strategy raises serious
concerns as to whether that will be enough in the long term. As economic
growth continues, the demand for workers will only increase in Europe. There
are many EU Member States which have high levels of economic growth and
which are encountering labour shortages – Ireland, Spain and the UK are all
recent examples. Many countries are importing workers both within the EU
and outside of it. This situation is not in contradiction to the goals of economic
development, but it has to be admitted that this scenario for development
involves certain risks. The inflow of labour is usually aimed at filling jobs

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 139

The Development of European Economic Integration: from Coal and Steel..

139

which are, for one reason or another, not attractive to local workers. This
approach may create a dual labour market resulting in greater social tension
which certainly does not facilitate the emergence of a single economic area in
Europe.
Europe’s most important long term challenges have to do with the continent’s
ability to ensure sustainable economic development based on the ideals of a
single economic area, also taking into account the demographic problems
which may well have a deleterious effect on Europe’s leading role in the global
economy. Under circumstances of globalisation, Europe’s role may be seriously
threatened unless steps are taken to enhance the continent’s own competitiveness.
Development in Europe is possible only if its economy is knowledge-based
but even then unfavourable demographic rate may threaten its future competitiveness.
Another important factor is the way in which economic decisions are taken
in Europe. Economic policies have historically been based on common values,
but many economic decisions are still taken at the national level. The political
situation in individual Member States often determines whether decisions are
in line with the common interests and values of European countries. It can be
expected that this situation will remain in place for the next few years, and the
supporters of common European initiatives will have to face opposition on the
part of individual Member States in those cases when the EU’s common economic interests run afoul of the interests of those Member States.
Latvia’s development in this context must be evaluated from a similar perspective. Despite rapid economic growth since accession to the EU, there are
still problems which can threaten long term development (demographics, difficulties in enhancing competitiveness). Indeed, Latvia’s situation may be even
more complex, because as it implements changes which are aimed at long
term economic development, it must also deal with domestic policy problems
which do not always enable the adoption of the most justified solutions therefore, it is very important to hold a widespread debate about economic development options. Latvia’s approach must be one which the population supports.
Given information about economic growth in Latvia over the last few years,
we can conclude that during this period in time, we have satisfied the need to
run very fast, indeed. Structural development trends in the economy, however,
do not ensure that Latvia is really ‘running’ in the right direction or that it will
have the strength to keep running for a long time to come. There are significant

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 140

140

ANDREJS JAKOBSONS

long term problems which can have a serious influence on Latvia’s rapid
growth in the future.

DAUNIS AUERS

Friend of Foe?
Latvia and Europe’s Common Agricultural Policy
Latvia’s accession to the European Union (EU) was generally a very benign
process. The Latvian political elite was largely unified in its aspiration to join
the European Union as part of a broader programme of ‘returning to the west’,
which also encompassed accession to NATO, the Council of Europe, and a
host of other ‘western’ organisations. However, this elite cohesion, coupled with
a weak, fragmented, and largely discredited eurosceptic opposition, meant that
there was relatively little qualitative debate on the pros and cons of EU accession.
The partial exception was the European Common Agricultural Policy (CAP).
Eurosceptics claimed that accession would lead to the collapse of traditional
Latvian farming practices (indeed, the perceived negative impact on agriculture
was named as the most important reason to vote ‘No’ to accession by almost
60% of people who called themselves eurosceptic).1 In contrast, ‘Yes’ campaigners
emphasised the clear economic benefits of EU membership to the farming sector.
As Jânis Lapðe, the policy advisor to Minister of Agriculture, wrote in 2003, about
the level of CAP funding that Latvia was guaranteed after accession: ‘The Latvian
countryside has never seen this much money!’2
However, the 2004 enlargement coincided with, and even partially caused,
a Europe-wide restructuring and rethinking of the multi-faceted relationship
between the European Union, the Member States, citizens, and agricultural
policy. In addition to the accession of eight post-communist states with large,
only partially modernised agricultural sectors, increasing environmental,
trade liberalisation and public pressures are changing the nature of farming,
and the shape of agricultural and rural communities. While these challenges
are not new, the British Minister of Agriculture has called their ‘scale and pace
unprecedented.’3

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 142

142

DAUNIS AUERS

These forces and resulting debates accurately reflect the fact that agriculture,
both in Latvia and elsewhere in Europe, is fundamentally different to other
sectors of the economy. It is laden with values and national cultural heritage.
For example, images of the Latvian countryside were used extensively by the
‘Yes’ campaign in the 2003 Latvian referendum on accession to the European
Union. One widely disseminated advertisement pictured a beautifully restored
traditional Latvian farmhouse kitchen with a modern laptop angled on a table
beneath an open window looking out onto an idealistic, well-tended small
Latvian farmyard. The implication was almost clumsy in its obviousness –
accession to the European Union will lead to a regeneration and modernisation
of the Latvian countryside, but, crucially, without any loss of tradition.
This reflects the special place that agriculture takes in the Latvian psyche.
As a recently modernised, geographically small nation, the overwhelming
majority of Latvians have extensive roots in the countryside, be it relatives, a
family farm, or a summer house. Indeed, these connections played an important
role during the Soviet era, with these rural contacts supplementing the relatively
meager selection of food products available in urban shops and markets. Indeed, Rîga still empties out during summer weekends, and becomes a virtual
ghost-town during the midsummer Jâòi festival (which itself is a celebration of
‘Latvianness’, Latvian values, and Latvian’s relationship with the countryside,
as much as an ancient pagan celebration).
However, the special place of agriculture in the European psyche has made
agricultural policy rather difficult to reform. Nevertheless, the CAP is likely to
see radical changes over the next decade. This article considers the factors
driving these reforms, the likely direction of the reforms, and it attempts to
locate Latvia’s position in the debate through a values and interests based approach. The article first considers the current structure of CAP, and then the
main elements of the current European debate, which is focused around
national interests as well as both national and European values. It then attempts to identify a future model of European agricultural policy that both
serves Latvia’s interests and is based on core values.

Agriculture and the European Union
The second half of the twentieth century has seen the rapid enlargement of
the European Community/European Union,4 from initially encompassing six

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 143

Friend of Foe? Latvia and Europe’s Common Agricultural Policy

143

states in 1957, to fifteen by the end of the century, and twenty-seven by 2007.
Each enlargement has, naturally, also entailed the entry of the new Member
States into the CAP. Thus for many years the CAP has shaped European food
production and the rural landscape. Indeed, agricultural policy was the first
major European Community spending policy, and still consumes around 45%
of the total annual budget. Thus while the inadequacies of the still-evolving
Common Foreign and Security Policy, or virulent trade disputes with China or
the United States of America tend to hog European media headlines, agriculture
still dominates the European budget.
The disproportionate centrality of agriculture in the European polity in the
modern era is largely a relic from the past – the result of a congruence of several
unique economic and political considerations in the 1950s. First, the memory
of food shortages during and after the Second World War meant that food
production was both an area of concern, and priority, for a great many European politicians. Second, agriculture was central to the European economies at
this time, employing about 20% of the total workforce and producing around
12% of the EU-6 Gross National Product. Third, food prices are an important
component of individual spending, and thus industrial wage costs. The CAP
was thus seen as an instrument to equalise food prices between the Member
States, a core element of creating a true common market. Fourth, agriculture
was a key compromise between France and Germany. France feared that Germany would substantially benefit from the creation of the European Economic
Community because of its larger, more productive and efficient industrial
sector. Thus creating a system whereby the benefits of the common market
would be partly redistributed via the CAP was the price that Germany had to
pay for the creation of the European Economic Community.5
Article 39 of the 1957 Treaty of Rome establishing the European Community
listed five key objectives for the CAP:6
■ increase agricultural productivity,
■ ensure a fair standard of living for the agricultural community,
■ stabilise markets,
■ provide certainty of food supply,
■ ensure that supplies reach consumers at reasonable prices.
The central policy to achieve these aims was the introduction of a community-wide target price for most farm products. The two primary mechanisms
adopted to maintain this target price were import levies and intervention

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

144

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 144

DAUNIS AUERS

prices. Import levies ensured that products entering the community market
would not undercut the prices of domestic producers. Intervention prices,
decided annually by the ministers of agriculture, committed the community to
purchase and store produce in order to artificially reduce supply and thus
maintain the price of the product. On the plus side, this encouraged European
farmers to increase output, eventually making Europe self-sufficient in food.
Thus, the CAP met a number of its key objectives. However, these policies also
led to huge food production surpluses which were dealt with by subsidised
product storage (butter mountains and olive oil lakes), exported surpluses, or
even destroyed agricultural products, an ethically dubious practice in an era
when parts of the world were suffering from extreme hunger and starvation.
Import levies also arosed controversy for failing the worlds least developed
countries for whom agricultural produce is one of the few products that they
can produce. And intervention prices artificially raised foodstuff prices for
consumers. Moreover, the major beneficiaries have been the larger, more industrialised, efficient farmers, rather than the idealised European smallholder.
Finally, by the 1970s and 1980s the CAP was absorbing about two-thirds of the
community budget, and thus limiting Community actions in other areas.
As a result of these problems, proposals to radically reshape the CAP have
been raging almost as long as the policy has existed. However, beginning with
the Mansholt plan in 1968, substantial reforms to the CAP were consistently
watered down by politicians, largely because of the extensive domestic and
transnational political influence of the farming lobby (which has a strong
financial incentive to maintain the current financing system).7 Although a
number of reforming measures were carried out (milk quotas were introduced
in 1984, and agricultural land ‘set aside’ in 1988), they were rather piecemeal
and did little to change the essence of CAP.
This changed in the 1990s for two major reasons. First, the enlargement of
the European Union to the post-communist countries of east-central Europe
edged ever nearer. It was clear that the CAP had to be reformed prior to this
accession, to avoid the Union budget being bankrupted. Second, Europe fell
under increasing World Trade Organisation external pressure to reform its
agricultural sector in order to allow for a further liberalisation of world trade
through first the Uruguay round of negotiations, and latterly the Doha round.
The first reforms introduced under these dual pressures were named after
the then Irish European Commissioner for Agriculture, Ray MacSharry. The

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 145

Friend of Foe? Latvia and Europe’s Common Agricultural Policy

145

‘MacSharry Reforms’ cut guaranteed prices for a number of agricultural goods,
took land out of production (to reduce surpluses), and introduced direct
payments to farmers.8 This was the first stage in decoupling payments to
farmers from production levels. It also introduced several rural development
measures, particularly to encourage environmentally sound farming. The 1999
‘Agenda 2000’ reforms (also known as Fischler I, after MacSharry’s successor
as Commissioner for Agriculture, Franz Fischler), continued the process begun
by MacSharry, and extended the use of direct payments in place of production
subsidies. It also restructured the CAP into two core pillars of agricultural expenditure. Pillar 1 covers direct payments to farmers and continuing marketrelated subsidies. Pillar 2 funds the rural development and agri-environmental
dimensions of the CAP. The most recent reforms were agreed by Agricultural
Ministers on 26th June 2003 (Fischler II), and entered into force in 2004 and
2005, with transitional periods available until 2007.9 It further reduced market
supports and ensured that direct payments to farmers, as decided at the domestic level (Single Farm Payments – SFP), would almost wholly replace price
supports. However, it also added the ‘cross-compliance’ dimension, which
made payments dependant on good farming practice. Thus Fischler II largely
completed the gradual shift in CAP financing from price support to direct
payment, and also granted Member States a greater say in how CAP funds are
spent in their countries.
Despite these reforms, CAP still has several central, controversial measures.
While the Union does have preferential trade agreements, including granting
the least developed nations in the world free access to the European market, the
EU is still essentially a protected agricultural market. Moreover, the current
CAP is still the biggest part of the EU budget, and remains expensive to the
consumer. Economists estimate that CAP costs the average European nuclear
family of four people 950 euro a year, and is the equivalent of an additional
value-added-tax on food of 15%.10 Indeed, it has been argued that the CAP
system of import tariffs and subsidies and direct payments to farmers leads to
EU consumers paying 80–100% more for their foodstuffs than could be expected
in a market-based regime.11 However, it has also ensured regular supplies of
food and a decent standard of living for farmers, as well as an aesthetically
pleasing agricultural landscape.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 146

146

DAUNIS AUERS

The future direction of CAP:
the contemporary European debate
The reforms discussed above have done little to mute the debate on the
future of CAP. Indeed, there will be a mid-term review of current CAP policies
in 2008-2009, and this is increasingly being seen as an opportunity to make
further, more substantial reforms to the CAP, as the recent reforms are increasingly regarded as unsustainable in the long-term. This is for two reasons.12
First, CAP is still incompatible with a further liberalisation of global trade (and
was a major factor behind the failure of the World Trade Organizations Doha
trade round). Second, the current CAP model remains open to continuing abuse
and corruption. However, while every Member State of the European Union is
aware of the need for reform, there is little agreement on the shape of the
future CAP. This section will bring together the main elements of this contemporary European debate. It will first discuss the trends that are driving the
need to further reform agriculture, and then focus on the potential direction of
future reforms.
Four contemporary trends are driving the need to change the existing CAP:
changing consumer preferences, trade liberalisation, rural depopulation, and a
resulting new vision of the role of farmers. First, a series of high-profile food
crises (‘mad cow’ disease, ‘foot and mouth’ disease, and avian flu to name but
three examples) have shaken consumer confidence in the food that they eat,
and has thus partly shaped perceptions on how CAP should be reformed. This
has manifested itself in growing European support for more environmentally
friendly agriculture, as well as increased public concern for animal welfare and
food security (particularly the food-chain aspect: ‘from farm to fork’). This can,
perhaps, be seen as a post-modern change in focus from the quantity, to the
quality, of food. This is enacted through less intensive farming, with more
focus on organic food products that have not utilised synthetic pesticides and
fertilisers in their production. Certainly, organic food is the fastest growing
part of the agricultural market, having grown at around 30% a year since 1998
and, because it is less efficient than more intensive farming (in terms of production), it also contributes to decreasing production surpluses.13
Second, Franz Fischler, the former European Commissioner for agriculture,
recently identified globalisation and particularly the increasing liberalisation
of trade as a key factor driving, perhaps even forcing, changes in the CAP.14

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 147

Friend of Foe? Latvia and Europe’s Common Agricultural Policy

147

Globalisation has already shaped the first three significant waves of reform to
CAP by forcing the EU to reassess its position on agriculture in order to forward the trade liberalisation regime of the World Trade Organisation. Moreover,
there is a clear long-term trend for increasingly opening up global markets,
and especially agricultural markets, to global competition. This is not just for
neo-liberal economic efficiency (with production increasingly moving towards
the more competitive regions of the world), but also for the humanitarian and
moral dimension of opening up agricultural markets for trade from the least
developed nations.
Third, rural depopulation has long been a feature of the European countryside. However, it is particularly acute in the new Member States. For example,
in the first year and a half of accession to the EU, about 100,000 Latvians
migrated to Ireland and the United Kingdom.15 Rural regions have borne the
burnt of this labour flight all across the new Member States, both in terms of
rural worker migration abroad, and in terms of internal migration to the cities
to take up the jobs left behind by the urban dwellers that have moved westwards. Thus there is a clear need to create a framework of policies, to develop
rural infrastructure and promote local enterprises, and tackle this issue.
Fourth, there also appears to be an increasing consensus on the need to financially support farmers for their rural stewardship of the countryside. Thus the
role of farmers in rural areas is no longer merely seen in terms of agricultural
production, but as one of public service. While this has always been an implicit
motivation behind CAP, future policies are likely to explicitly tackle this issue.
The MacSharry and Fischler reforms have already partly responded to
these concerns. Indeed, the direction of these three waves of reform sketch in
the likely direction of further reform. Thus it is clear that the CAP, particularly
through decoupling and an increased role for national agricultural ministries
in the dispersal of European funds, has been increasingly heading in the direction of market liberalisation. This would then be likely to lead to a two-speed
European countryside. Productive and internationally competitive farms and
agricultural enterprises would stand next to a large number of economically
marginal producers whose major role would be to supply the public with environmental goods through stewardship of rural areas.16 This latter aspect would
clearly require continuing public financing, the only question being whether it is
seen as a European- or domestic-level public service (and thus whether it should
be funded at the European or the national, or even regional, level).

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

148

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 148

DAUNIS AUERS

Liberalisation is strongly favoured by a number of the older Member States
which have smaller, and more efficient, farming sectors that are likely to benefit from a freer market in agricultural goods. In December 2005, the British
government published its own vision of the future development of the CAP.17
The paper proposes a radical reform of the CAP (although it details the desired end-result, rather than the difficult-to-map road to getting there) to create
an, it is argued, internationally competitive European agriculture sector independent of government subsidies. Government aid (whether it be European or
domestic financed) would be re-focused to providing business training, consultancy and best-practice for a new breed of farmers-entrepeneurs. Thus these
reforms envisage farmers and agriculture becoming more mainstream participants of the market economy. It also recognised the environmental publicgood that farmers produce, and the need to recognise, and thus finance, this
good. The British model maintains a central role for the European Union, in
terms of setting, and overseeing compliance with, minimum food safety, quality,
and other regulations needed to create a genuine single market in agricultural
produce.
The recent increased role of national governments in disseminating and
structuring CAP funding, as well as the British proposal for market liberalisation of CAP both hint at a change in ‘ownership’ of agricultural policy. Thus in
2003 an influential Swedish economist argued for the re-nationalisation of the
CAP.18 It was claimed that a transfer of agricultural policy to the domestic
level, by bringing this policy arena closer to the voter, would allow politicians
to reign in the interest and lobby groups that so distort CAP at the European
level (while maintaining the common customs system and single market in
agriculture). This, the author argued, would then lead to a more rational system. However, the nationalisation of the CAP is strongly opposed by the
French government and other major net beneficiaries of agricultural funding,
and they would be likely to veto any radical reforms discussed during the
mid-term review.
In addition to market liberalisation and nationalisation, there is an emerging
consensus, clearly seen in the last decade of CAP reform, on emphasising three
distinct elements of a future CAP that would replace the current two-pillars
structure: an agricultural policy; a rural development policy; and a land management policy.19 Agricultural policy would encompass the regulations, and possibly funding mechanisms, governing agricultural policy. A rural development

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 149

Friend of Foe? Latvia and Europe’s Common Agricultural Policy

149

policy would tackle rural depopulation by investing in rural infrastructure
(roads, schools, housing etc.) in order to maintain or attract enterprises to rural
regions. Finally, land management policy would recompense and frame activities
focused on rural stewardship as a public good. Indeed, this changing nature of
CAP is encapsulated in the emerging new vocabulary being used in the discourse. Thus agriculture is increasingly referred to as land management, farmers
as rural businesses, and the CAP as a rural or land management policy.
This article will now turn to look at Latvia and the CAP. The following
section will sketch out the development of the agricultural sector in Latvia over
the last half-century. It will then discuss four key Latvian values relating to
agriculture, and consider how they shape Latvia’s interests in the emerging
debate on the future of Latvian agriculture.

Latvian agriculture: from Soviet Union to European Union
Latvia has experienced three significant periods of agricultural reform since
the end of the Second World War. The collectivisation of agriculture during the
Soviet occupation. The privatisation of land and the nationalisation of agricultural supports during the 1990s, following the renewal of independence from
the Soviet Union. And, finally, the pre- and post-accession period to the European Union.
The Soviet era saw the rapid expropriation and collectivisation of agricultural land. By 1951, 98.4% of smallholder farms had been incorporated into collective farms.20 There were also substantial changes to the rural-urban population.
While in the inter-war period the overwhelming majority of the population
lived in rural areas (indeed, about two-thirds of the population lived and
worked on farms), this situation was reversed by the 1950s, and by 1980, 70% of
the Latvian population lived in urban areas.21 The collectivised farms were large,
averaging around 3,000–4,000 hectares (compared with the average 16 hectares
of family farms in the inter-war era).22 However, the agricultural sector in
Soviet Latvia was hugely inefficient. The World Bank described it thus:
‘The agriculture sector was characterised by a lack of market orientation or
response to consumer demand, inefficient production, irrational pricing of
inputs and outputs, and a misallocation of resources. These institutional factors,
combined with deteriorating soil fertility, the degradation of other resources

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 150

150

DAUNIS AUERS

on which agriculture depended, and the gradual obsolescence of technology
led to a decline in the area of cultivated land and slow sectoral growth. Agriculture grew by an average of less than one percent a year over 1970–90.’23
In light of this, it is perhaps unsurprising that Latvian agriculture underwent a traumatic period in the 1990s. The implementation of neo-liberal economic reforms meant that there were few subsidies available to the agricultural
sector. Thus agricultural production declined sharply in the 1990s, plunging
the depths in 1996. About one-third of agricultural land was taken out of
production, and output in 1999 was less than half the 1990 level. The biggest
decline was in animal production (66%), while the fall in crop production was
32%.24 At the same time, however, there has been a growing consolidation of
the farming sector. A decline in the total number of farms since 1994 was
accompanied by an increase in the size of the average farm from 7 hectares in
1994 to 14 hectares in 1998.25 Indeed, it has continued to grow to 22.8 in 2003,
and then 29 hectares by 2005.26

The EU’s 2002 country report on Latvia further characterised the situation
prior to accession.28 Latvia had a negative agricultural trade balance, importing
more agricultural products than it exported. This is, perhaps, unsurprising given
that Latvia utilises less land for agriculture than the EU-15 average (with a higher
percentage of its land is given over to forestry). Also, the percentage employed
in agriculture – 13.5% – is three-times the EU-15 average, and almost twice the
EU-27 average, and hints at deep inefficiencies in the agricultural sphere.
Accession to the EU has clearly had a positive impact on the Latvian agricultural sector. The physical appearance of Latvia’s rural regions has changed
beyond all recognition in just a few years. Indeed, Latvian agricultural
producers have experienced an increase in their overall income of +5.7% in

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 151

Friend of Foe? Latvia and Europe’s Common Agricultural Policy

151

2005, following a near doubling of income in 2004, the first year of EU membership.29 However, the impact on consumers is rather more mixed. While there
has certainly been a sharp growth in the quality and complexity of Latvian
agricultural produce in recent years, this has been accompanied by price rises.
For example, Latvia had the sharpest growth in milk prices in the European
Union in 2005 (+27.9%).30 Nevertheless, it is clear that after more than a halfcentury of agricultural inefficiency and decline, accession to the European
Union has reinvigorated the Latvian agricultural sector.

Latvian values and the future of CAP
Values are an intrinsic part of any discussion of European agriculture. A
background paper for a recent meeting of EU Ministers of Agriculture, called
to discuss the future challenges for European agriculture, asked the delegations to ponder: ‘Is the European model of agriculture a relevant and valuable
expression of the common values and principles underlying European agriculture?’31 Thus Latvia is not unique in having agriculture hold a central place
among its core values. But what are these values?
Ivars Ijabs’ broad discussion of Latvian and European values in this volume
reveals four key value dimensions that permeate the Latvian perspective on
agriculture: Nationalism (a political value), welfare, and free time (social values),
and historicism (a cultural value). However, these values need to be balanced
and placed into the context of Latvia’s national interests. In an earlier article,
Toms Rostoks and myself identified Latvia’s core interests, in the context of the
EU, to be health, wealth, and security.32 Health in order to reverse Latvia’s
disastrous population decline, as well as lengthen average life expectancy which
is among the lowest in the EU. Wealth, in order to tackle Latvia’s status as the
poorest Member State in the EU-25. Both these interests affect the quality of
life of Latvia’s inhabitants. In contrast, the third dimension, security, is a key
state interest following the many years of foreign occupation and domination
in the twentieth century and before. These values and interests need to be
considered in more detail.
First, the political value of nationalism, and the associated cultural value of
historicism. Agriculture has long been associated with nationalism. Pride in a
nation’s traditional and historic food and culinary heritage, as well as the

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

152

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 152

DAUNIS AUERS

perceived unique features of a nations rural community, rooted, in Latvia’s
case, in a recent peasant past, are central to a nations self-identity. Thus agricultural production has often been a source of conflict between states. In the
1990s, Latvia had a spate of conflicts over pork and other food products with its
two Baltic neighbours of Estonia and Lithuania. In this respect, it is important
for Latvia’s identity to maintain a functioning rural sector that both produces
agricultural products for domestic consumption and export, as well as a welltended rural infrastructure. Achieving these aims also implies increasing wealth,
and resultingly health, for rural inhabitants.
The two social values of welfare and free time also tie-in closely with agriculture and rural communities. Welfare means a redistribution of the economic benefits of society. Indeed, CAP in a European context is strongly based
on the idea of sharing the economic benefits of the single market gained by
enterprises and businesses with rural communities. It also entails ensuring
that agricultural workers have a decent income and standard of living. Free
time in the Latvian case is frequently associated with rural places, whether it
be enjoying the short summer months in the countryside, or participating in
winter sports in the coldest months of the year, as successive advertisement
films made by the Latvian Institute constantly have emphasised.33
Thus a value-oriented approach to agriculture sketches in some basic Latvian interests in this policy area. First, it is important to maintain a functioning
agricultural production sector to maintain national identity, as well as ensure a
decent standard of living for people living in rural communities. At the same
time, Latvia’s small geographic size and existing close links between urban
dwellers and the countryside means that it is important that rural communities
are maintained as a public good. Moreover, this could also have an economic
rationale, as there are sizeable hopes that the number of tourists coming to
Latvia, and visiting the countryside, will rise rapidly in the coming years.
How does this feed into Latvia’s vision for the future of agriculture? The
debate on the future of CAP in Latvia has been much more muted than in the
older EU Member States. This is for several reasons. First, Latvia has only
recently joined the European Union, and as a recent member with a limited
experience, it has also only recently begun to construct its opinion on CAP.
Moreover, the CAP is such a complicated technocratic policy area, that it is not
really possible to have a detailed, constructive, popular debate on its policies and
programmes. Nevertheless, the Latvian Ministry of Agriculture has developed

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 153

Friend of Foe? Latvia and Europe’s Common Agricultural Policy

153

a Latvian Rural Development National Strategy Plan for the 2007–2013 financial
perspective (second pillar financing).34 As the title suggests, this document
sketches out Latvia’s strategy for spending EU second pillar agricultural
financing. However, there are no further documents sketching out Latvia’s future
vision of a reformed agriculture policy. Of course, the Agricultural Ministry is
hugely overstretched dealing with the strains of administrating the CAP and
other rural policies and initiatives. However, the mid-term review of 2008–
2009 will soon be upon us, and Latvia needs to formulate its position through
a wide-ranging but informed discussion on these very important issues. After
all, membership of the EU was sold to Latvians partly on the basis of a favourable agricultural policy. Thus it is crucial that a Latvian opinion must be discussed, articulated, and ultimately heard at the European level.
Bearing in mind Latvia’s core values as regards agriculture, it is possible to
map out a very clear, but broad, Latvian perspective in the context of the
current reform debate. Essentially, in the short- to medium-term Latvia should
oppose market liberalisation and re-nationalisation of agricultural policy, while
supporting a shift in financing from first to second pillar activities in order to
prepare Latvian farmers for the seemingly inevitable long-term move to liberalise
and nationalise. The re-nationalisation of CAP is not an attractive prospect for
Latvian policy-makers. Despite a sharply growing economy, the neo-liberal
flexible economic model of low taxation and low intervention adopted by
Latvian policy makers means that in the short- and medium-term Latvia on its
own would not have the financial resources to compensate its farmers to the
extent that the older and wealthier European Union Member States could.
Nor is the increased liberaliation of the agricultural sector particularly
favourable for Latvia. Latvia’s educational system lags behind that of the older
Member States in almost every indicator except for participation. The overwhelming majority of Latvian farmers lack the skills needed to turn themselves into farmers-entrepeneurs, and it will take many years for this situation
to be reversed. Moreover, it will call for a significant change in mentality, and
thus quite some time, for farmers to accept the need for them to become more
than just the producers of agricultural products. At least Latvia already has an
existing institution, Latvijas Lauksaimniecîbas universitâte [University of Agriculture], that can take on the task of planning and executing appropriate degree,
diploma, full-time, part-time, correspondence programmes for these future
farmer-entrepeneurs.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 154

154

DAUNIS AUERS

Nevertheless, it does seem inevitable that elements of the market will be
increasingly introduced into the farming sector. As a result, it is clear that
diversification of the economy’s and commodities of the rural communities is
certainly something in Latvia’s interests. Latvia’s long harsh winters, coupled
with the lack of a grand culinary tradition, means that Latvian agriculture is
unlikely to be competitive with, for example, the Latvian or French agricultural
sectors. Thus farmers should not just be seen as the producers of food in Latvia.
They can also produce bio-fuels and other renewable sources of energy that
are being produced in many parts of the world. Moreover, there would be
great synergies through a connection between farmers and Latvian scientists
working on these renewable energy issues. This would further tie farmers into
alternative methods of farming, as well as promote the ‘knowledge society’
among rural workers, not just urban workers.
Finally, it is undoubtedly in Latvia’s interest to construct a CAP policy that
adopts a long-term perspective. The original CAP needed to be reformed after
almost three decades of operation. But only because the CAP had proved to be
so successful that it had created enormous surpluses of agricultural products,
and largely achieving its original aims. However, the 1990s agricultural reforms
in Europe have consisted of a ‘short-termism which sellotapes one remedy
onto another, creating dependency, complexity and uncertainty: young farmers
need a long term perspective’.35 This, of course, is particularly important in
Latvia, where there has been an acceleration in rural depopulation following
accession in May 2004, and where unemployment and poverty are much higher
in rural areas. A long-term, securely, and jointly, financed CAP would serve to
attract people back to farming by giving them a career-long perspective and
vision of their own roles in the rural economy, be it as food producers/traditional farmers, land managers, or working in rurally located enterprises. This
is also true for the farming sectors in the other new post-communist Member
States of the European Union.

Conclusions
To recap, in terms of agricultural policy, it is in Latvia’s short- and mediumterm interests to resist nationalisation of agricultural policy, and resist a radical
move to liberalise agriculture and ‘set it free’ into the marketplace. In the long-term,

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 155

Friend of Foe? Latvia and Europe’s Common Agricultural Policy

155

however, the opposite is true. Participation in the current second pillar activities
of CAP, along with the recent MacSharry and Fischler I and II reforms that
indirectly prepare farmers for market competition, will ultimately prepare
Latvian farmers for a liberalised agricultural market. This may also allow for
the eventual nationalisation of agricultural policy. However, it will take many
years for Latvian and other new Member State farmers to reach this level of
competitiveness.
Another challenge, of course, will be to make the Latvian voice heard in the
debate on agriculture. Latvia is not a major player in European agriculture,
having just 1.3% of EU-27 agricultural land, and 0.8% of the agricultural workforce.36 However, this does not mean that Latvia cannot attempt to punch
above its weight. In a previous article I have noted that Latvian officials have
started increasingly cooperating with their Baltic neighbours, as well as the
Nordic-6 of Denmark, Sweden, and Finland.37 Latvia’s CAP interests are certainly closely associated to those of Estonia and Lithuania, and the other seven
post-communist new Member States. In addition, the Nordic states, particularly Denmark which has an extensive farming sector, and Danish farmers
have increasing business interests in the Baltic states, could also join a bloc to
lobby for favourable reforms. In any case, it is important that the debate in
Latvia begins soon, and that all Latvians dealing with European issues and
agricultural issues participate in forming the Latvian position on agricultural
reform. While agriculture is not central to the Latvian economy, it is central to
social cohesion, leisure activities, and the way in which Latvia as a state, and
Latvians as individuals perceive themselves in both nationalist and historical
terms. As such, it is a policy area in which Latvian officials need to doggedly
defend and promote Latvia’s interests and its core values.

KRISTÎNE KRÛMA, IVARS INDÂNS

Migration Policy and the Future of the European Union
Introduction
Debates about immigration issues and the future of the European Union
have become increasingly important on the political agenda in recent times. In
the early 1970s, an oil supply crisis led several European countries to halt or at
least limit labour migration, and the reunification of families became the chief
reason for subsequent immigration.1 The situation has changed substantially,
however, because Europeans are gradually arriving at the conclusion that they
will have to return to primary labour migration. Rights of family reunification,
management of legal migration and control over illegal migration – all of these
are once again important issues on the European agenda.
Immigration policies in the EU are full of controversies, because they depend
both on factors of the global economy and on socio-political factors which
relate to the national interests of the EU’s Member States. In a broader sense,
the situation is also dictated by internal conflicts in terms of the continent’s
political values. The development of the EU’s immigration policy, therefore,
has involved certain steps forward which, in large part, have been dictated by
the challenges of the EU’s external environment, as well as by the globalised
economic environment of the contemporary world. At the same time, however, there have been significant retreats in the process. Political decisions have
been taken on the basis of national interests and domestic socio-political factors
in the various Member States. Trends in this regard emanate from three approaches to EU migration policy – the national approach, the intergovernmental
approach, and the supranational approach. These approaches overlap in EU
law, and they mark out the ambiguities of the process. EU migration policy
depends on the external global environment, and it has everything to do with
the EU’s foreign policy ambitions in the international arena.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 161

Migration Policy and the Future of the European Union

161

Processes of illegal immigration depend very much on the way in which
legal migration is handled. Illegal migration, indeed, is subordinated to legal
migration, and the process is also influenced by geopolitical considerations. In
the area of illegal migration, technical co-operation among law enforcement
institutions is of primary importance. Legal migration, by contrast, is a political
decision, which depends on the relevant country’s visions and forecasts as to
its future development. For this reason, the main focus in this paper is on legal
migration. Illegal migration is mentioned, however, because there are significant EU initiatives in this area, too.
Latvia, as an EU Member State, has extensive opportunities to become involved in the shaping of EU migration policy. First, however, it is important
for Latvia to define its national interests in the EU, so that its positions on
migration issues are consistent and, therefore, easier to defend and explain.
Latvia’s choices are limited by the fact that there are no plans related to immigration policy at the level of executive institutions and overall government
policy in Latvia. There is a lack of co-operation with professional associations,
the business environment and trade unions. Five government ministries are
involved in immigration policy in one way or another, but only in 2007 will the
first serious study of the qualitative and quantitative aspects of the labour
market and illegal employment be completed. This research is being conducted
in collaboration with the EU Social Fund.
At the same time, Latvia has fundamental advantages in comparison to the
countries of Western Europe which have been forced to deal with the consequences of their previous immigration policies. Latvia is not a target country
for immigrants at this time, so it can plan national policies in a timely way,
thinking about how these policies can be linked to EU trends. In the context of
public integration, too, the nationality policies left behind by the former Soviet
Union have created experience which may well relate to the potential immigrants in the future. Latvia can take an active position at the European level,
because EU regulations in the area of migration are only now beginning to
develop with greater velocity.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

162

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 162

KRISTÎNE KRÛMA, IVARS INDÂNS

1. European values and the challenges of immigration
1.1. Values: when the traditional encounters the new
The challenges of globalisation, the pressures of the external economic
environment and the large communities of migrants – all of these have significantly changed the EU’s role in the context of migration. Relevant issues are
becoming more and more important on the EU agenda. Migration policy at
one time was essentially the bailiwick of individual Member States, but panEuropean policy is increasingly coming to the fore at this time. Despite progress
over the last 10 years, however, there have been substantial limitations on the
effective management of migration issues at the EU level.
Both the stimuli and the limitations of migration policy stem from a conflict
between European values on the one hand and the diverse interests of EU
Member States on the other. Traditional values such as nationalism and the
welfare state often conflict with concepts such as human rights, liberalism, and
the values of individualism when migration is the issue. Progress and limitations are also seen in EU legislation which seeks to determine the Union’s
migration policies. Traditional ideas about the sovereignty of nations and
controls over national boundaries are collapsing under this policy. Changes in
one Member State affect other Member States. Mobility and the freedom of movement within certain parts of the EU create problems in terms of interior affairs
and justice – areas in which comparatively greater power still rests with Member
States, not the EU’s supranational structures. Values such as security come up
against values such as freedom. There are really no modern countries which
are completely monoethnic. Increasing human mobility and development
related to that mobility lead people to stop thinking that migrants are dangerous.
The fundamental EU idea of the free movement of individuals, in fact, contradicts the traditional idea of nation states.
European values are also increasingly being influenced by the development
of the consumer society. New value systems are appearing at the everyday level
of Europeans. The diversity of European identity has become a much-debated
topic. People have various senses of belonging (to the state, to a nationality, a
religious denomination, a profession, a lifestyle, a family, etc.).2
The value of the welfare state is very important in relation to migration
policies in Europe. Europeans have special ideas about the forms, manifestations
and importance of welfare in human lives. Welfare has always been declared

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 163

Migration Policy and the Future of the European Union

163

to be a positive value, one which allows people to live lives of full value. Europe
has historically been dominated by the view that very unevenly distributed
welfare is no proper welfare. This assumption, to be sure, has been far from
reality at times, but ‘ideal Europeans’ will not feel happy if they are privately
rich, but all around them there is nothing but poverty.3 This perception is an
important prerequisite for the culture of human rights which prevails in Europe.
From the perspective of value-perception in Western Europe, a welfare State
helps people. It guarantees social and cultural survival, replacing income with
social aid, unemployment benefits or early pensions. If the market does not
ensure sufficient income from work, the state ensures sufficient income on its
own. Over the course of time, this has created a situation in which social support policies no longer promote economic initiative to undertake risk and to
compete with others.
Welfare in Western Europe has become a problem exacerbated both by the
collapse of the Iron Curtain and by the opening up of China. Fully 28% of
humanity suddenly flooded into the Western market system. Integration of
the ‘Asian tiger economies’ proved to be quite difficult in the 1970s and 1980s.
Integration of these economies provided profits to most countries engaged in
the global trading system, but it also created vast problems for the West because
of intensively diminishing pressure on the salaries of unqualified labour. Financial capital and direct investments have flowed from West to East, Western
economies have been forced to specialise in highly qualified and capital-intensive manufacturing which creates fewer jobs, and unqualified immigrants
have been making their way toward the West.
1.2. The future of the EU in the area of immigration
Scenarios as to the future of the EU in the area of immigration must be
analysed in the context of the diverse approaches which Member States and
EU institutions take toward this issue. There are three major approaches to
immigration policy in the EU – the national, the intergovernmental and the
supranational approach. All three approaches have been put to the test, and
the development of EU policy in this regard depends on the dominant approach. The development of European policy indicates that all three approaches
are in place as migration issues are being handled. Most often, however, the
intergovernmental approach dominates.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

164

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 164

KRISTÎNE KRÛMA, IVARS INDÂNS

The national approach is based on emotional and cultural perceptions and
on sentiments of a national identity. The approach is based on the idea that EU
Member States shape their own national policies, and not all countries are
obliged to allow aliens to cross their borders. EU policy has developed considerably, but this approach has been quite powerful irrespective of the level of EU
integration. The national approach is seen, for instance, in those EU Member
States which have closed their labour markets to people from newly admitted
EU Member States. Others insist that the focus of attention in migration policy
must be exclusively on illegal migration. Supporters of the national approach
feel that investments in the countries of origin of migrants will eventually reduce
the negative socio-political consequences of migration. France, Germany, Austria
and the Netherlands are all supporters of this particular approach. The
national approach is also seen in EU legislation. In the area of immigration,
Member States have extensive rights to define their own criteria, on the basis
of their own national interests.
Many Europeans think that the flow of immigrants must be limited even
though birth rates on the continent are very low, and there are increasing concerns about a lack of workers. ‘Europe cannot accept everyone who believes
this to be El Dorado,’ says President of France and former French Interior
Minister Nicolas Sarkozy.4 The EU commissioner for external affairs, Benita
Ferrero-Waldner, told the Moroccan newspaper Le Matin that the flow of political and economic refugees could be reduced if people’s lives were improved
in Africa. ‘We must not concentrate the discussion only on security issues. We
must not ignore the fundamental causes of the migration movement – poverty
and insecurity,’ she said.5 These statements clearly reflect the national approach
in the shaping of EU policy. Policies that are based on the national approach
emanate from traditional values which are of key importance of domestic
balance in the various Member States.
The intergovernmental approach, meanwhile, is based on compromises
which can be agreed by Member State governments so as to ensure the proper
functioning of EU interests. Pragmatism and functionality are the keywords
here. Immigration policies in the EU, according to this approach, are based
exclusively on policies upon which all parties can agree at a specific moment
in time. The Hague Programme is an example of vast and diverse approach to
its policy directions.6 The intergovernmental approach usually seeks the middle
way among various political offers. Sometimes it can do nothing but seek the

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

09:59

Page 165

Migration Policy and the Future of the European Union

165

lowest common denominator upon which all participants in the specific debate
can agree. The main problem, of course, is that problems often develop far more
swiftly than policies do. These are reactive and cumbersome policies, and their
effectiveness suffers as a result of this.
EU policy since the late 1990s has largely been based on the principles of the
intergovernmental approach. Immigration is an ongoing process of the future
of European society. If the flow of immigrants to the EU is managed appropriately, Member States can gain significant economic, cultural and other benefits from the process. This helps to facilitate various processes in Europe, and
strengthens the European role in the world. Effective migration management
in the European Union, in other words, serves everyone’s interests.7
Key aspects of managing migration include not just the fight against illegal
migration, including the process of human trafficking, but also the successful
integration of legal migrants. In the EU as a whole, it is important to make sure
that Member States preserve and develop societies in which newly arrived persons
feel comfortable, ones in which there is mutual understanding and acceptance.
Segregation and discrimination cause discomfort in one part of society, pushing
policies back toward the national approach. France provides us with an example
of the types of internal conflicts which can emerge as a result of this.
The future of the EU in the field of migration depends on two fundamental
scenarios in which the interests of economic development either dominate
value conflicts or are subordinated to those conflicts. The outer world can
significantly influence the pace at which migration policies are developed. The
development of the EU depends in part on how Member States, in dealing
with economic issues, manage to control legal migration, on the way in which
citizens of third countries are integrated so as to reduce the threat of social
discrimination, on how shifts in demographic situations are managed so that
there can be increased reliance on the local labour force, and on the extent to
which scientific technologies and innovations are brought to bear in pursuit of
economic progress.
The supranational approach is an economically rational (mercantile) reaction
to the demand in Europe for labour force. This approach ignores the domestic
policies and identities of Member States. It is based on the EU’s macroeconomic needs and on international competition. The supranational approach is
the result of how the external environment and the process of globalisation
have affected the EU.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

166

12.02.2008
