INGA ULNICÂNE-OZOLIÒA

The Lisbon Strategy for Promoting Knowledge
and Innovation
Europe needs strong economic foundations if it is to preserve and develop
its values in the 21st century. The Lisbon Strategy launched in 2000 is one of
the main initiatives in promoting economic growth in Europe. The strategy
was developed in response to new challenges which the EU was facing – globalisation, aging societies, and enlargement to the East.
The aim of this article is to analyse the development of the Lisbon Strategy
from its announcement through the end of 2006, looking at the strategy both in
the EU as a whole and in Latvia individually. There will also be a focus on one
of the central issues in the strategy – how to promote knowledge and innovation,
and how to deal with challenges in this area. In conclusion, some pre-conditions for successful implementation of the Lisbon Strategy will be identified.

The Lisbon Strategy: from political declarations
to reform initiatives
During the course of nearly seven years, the Lisbon Strategy has gradually
developed from a broad and generalised declaration of socio-economic goals,
as defined by political leaders, to a set of concrete activities at the national,
regional and local level in Europe.
The strategy dates back to March 2000, when the European Council, meeting in Lisbon, announced a new strategic goal for the European Union over
the next 10 years – to become the most competitive, dynamic and knowledgebased economy in the world so as to ensure sustainable economic growth,
more and better jobs, and greater social cohesion.1 Two major goals were

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 199

The Lisbon Strategy for Promoting Knowledge and Innovation

199

defined – preparing for the establishment of a competitive, dynamic and
knowledge-based economy, and modernising the European social model by
investing in people and by establishing an active welfare state. Emphasis in the
first of these goals was placed on the development of the information society,
the establishment of the European Innovation and Research Area, the creation
of an environment favourable for the launch of new businesses (especially
small and medium enterprises), economic reforms to improve the operations
of the internal market, effective and integrated financial markets, and co-ordination of macroeconomic policy. The focus in modernising the European social
model, in turn, has been on education, active employment policies, modernisation of social protection, and greater social inclusion.
The open method of co-ordination was defined by the European Council as
an important mechanism in pursuing the strategy. The open method of coordination is defined as an alternative to the traditional community method
that is based on approximation of laws. The new method is based on guidelines, good practice, regular monitoring, etc. This is a far more flexible approach,
allowing each Member State to select the most appropriate methods and
instruments for pursuing the goals that have been set by all Member States
working together.3
Despite the political pronouncements made at the highest level of the EU,
there was hardly any active pursuit of the Lisbon Strategy’s goals in the first
few years after the relevant summit meeting. There were too many goals. Implementation of some required painful reforms at the level of Member States,
and political support was often difficult to achieve. There was increasing scepticism among members of the public, experts and the mass media as to the
Lisbon Strategy. Particular mockery was devoted to a strategic goal which was
formulated less than successfully – to catch up with the US economy by 2010.
A renewed strategy for Growth and Jobs
Early in 2005, when the EU conducted a mid-phase evaluation of the strategy,
it was generally agreed that Europe was lagging far behind the goals that had
been set out in the Lisbon Strategy. The implementation of the goals was seen
as the Achilles’ heel of the process.3 Headed by José Manuel Barroso, the European Commission announced a renewal of the strategy, this time emphasising
just two key goals – economic growth and greater employment. Thus the

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

200

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 200

INGA ULNICÂNE-OZOLIÒA

strategy took on a new name – the Growth and Jobs Strategy. This represented
a far more pragmatic approach to the process. The EU no longer maintained
the pompous idea of catching up with the United States. Instead, it decided to
focus on sustainable economic growth and new and better jobs.
The renewal of the strategy also meant a new partnership between the EU
and its Member States in drafting the Lisbon Programme and in preparing
National Reform Programmes in each Member State. The Community Lisbon
Programme is focused on those issues which can be resolved at the supranational level.4 That particularly applies to such areas as the internal market,
improvements in infrastructure, and creation of better conditions for business.
The main reforms that are needed in pursuit of the strategy, however, must
be handled at the level of Member States. In the autumn of 2005, each Member
State drafted its own National Reform Programme for the period between 2005
and 2008. These programmes cover all of the social and economic initiatives to
be implemented at the national level. The programmes were drafted in accordance with the Integrated Guidelines for Growth and Jobs in the areas of macroeconomics, microeconomics and employment.5 Each Member State had to select
the appropriate processes in pursuing the goals. The national programmes are
also an instrument for monitoring. Member States must file annual reports on
what has been achieved.
In 2006, the European Council defined four new priorities for the strategy,
ones to be pursued in 2007.6 These include greater investments in knowledge
and innovation, development of the business potential of small and medium
enterprises in particular, expansion in employment opportunities, as well as
implementation of effective and sustainable energy policies.
The Integrated Guidelines and the steps taken in pursuit of the strategy
show that despite the fact that the announced aim has been to focus on the two
main issues of growth and jobs, the strategy still covers a very wide range of
issues. It covers such areas as the environment, energy supplies, lifelong
learning, care for preschool children, liberalisation of taxes and services, etc.
Some of the goals are mutually incompatible – budget discipline and greater
investments in research and development, for instance. So widespread and
diverse a range of issues in a single strategy makes the strategy complicated
and difficult to survey. At the same time, however, it also ensures a complex
approach to issues of socio-economic development.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 201

The Lisbon Strategy for Promoting Knowledge and Innovation

201

Synergy between the Lisbon Strategy and the Structural Funds
All available resources must be mobilised at the EU and the national level if
the Lisbon Strategy is to be implemented. In less developed Member States,
there must be synergy between the Lisbon goals and the activities which are
financed by the EU’s Structural Funds. During the new Structural Fund planning
period 2007-2013, particular attention is being devoted to the way in which the
relevant funding can help in achieving the goals of the Lisbon Strategy –
ensuring that European regions become more attractive for investments and
employment, as well as promoting the development of knowledge, innovation
and employment.7
The framework for Structural Fund spending between 2007 and 2013 states
that in less developed regions, at least 60% of the money must be spent in
pursuit of the Lisbon goals, while in other regions, at least 75% of funding must
be devoted to that purpose.8 This means that at least EUR 200 billion will be
invested in pursuit of the relevant goals, and that, in turn, indicates that there
must be close co-operation between those officials who are responsible for the
National Reform Programmes and those who are responsible for Structural
Fund financing. Regional and local governments must be actively involved in
the pursuit of the Lisbon goals.
Achievements in seven years
The European Commission released its annual report on the Lisbon Strategy
progress in December 2006, concluding that the reforms were beginning to
bear fruit in terms of growth and employment.9 The EU forecast economic
growth of 2.8% in 2006 – significant progress since 2005, when EU growth was
at a level of just 1.7%. All Member States have defined national goals in terms
of financing research and development. If all of the defined goals are achieved,
financing for R&D in the EU will be at a level of 2.6% of GDP in the year 2010.
In 2002, meeting in Barcelona, the European Council defined a goal of 3% in
this regard,10 but even 2.6% will be much better than the level of 1.9% in 2005.
The EU also expects seven million new jobs to be created between 2006 and
2008, which could boost the percentage of employed people from 64% in 2005
to 66% in 2008.
The report also presents several ‘success stories’ – approval of the services
directive, improvements in financial services, new frameworks for supporting

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

202

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 202

INGA ULNICÂNE-OZOLIÒA

research, development and innovation, guidelines for fiscal incentives in promoting R&D, agreement on the 7th Framework Programme for research and
technological development so as to increase R&D spending by 75% between
2007 and 2013, support for major public-private partnerships, as well as support for small and medium enterprises in the context of the Competitiveness
and Innovation Framework Programme.
At the same time, several other important proposals are still on the agenda –
transfer of pensions to promote labour mobility in the internal market, improvements to the way in which transport and energy markets function, establishment of an effective and affordable system of intellectual property rights, a
framework for audiovisual and fee-based services, and modernisation of
important rules in the area of taxes and customs fees. Important goals in 2007
include further opening of markets so as to promote innovation and to offer
better choices to consumers, further work on opening markets throughout the
world, offering new opportunities to businesses in the EU, striking a better
balance between flexibility and security in the job market, and improving the
quality of EU educational systems.
Economic calculations suggest that if the goals of the Lisbon Strategy are
achieved, that could make a significant investment in improving the prevailing
economic and social situation in the EU. GDP would increase by 12 to 23%,
and employment would rise by 11%.11 It must be said, however, that more
time will be needed to evaluate the results of some of the reforms which are
being pursued. For instance, it usually takes some time before investments in
education and research are transformed into economic benefits.
In evaluating the development of the Lisbon Strategy over nearly seven
years, it has to be said that increasing attention has been devoted to the implementation of the strategy. A framework has been established in pursuit of the
necessary reforms and processes of modernisation. In the spring of each year,
the European Council defines major strategic directions, and the work is then
done via the Community and the National Lisbon programmes. Progress reports are issued in the autumn of each year. These are evaluated by the European Commission, which then makes recommendations as to what should be
done in future. This, of course, creates additional bureaucracy, with many new
programmes, reports, guidelines and the like. On the other hand, the framework also provides more opportunities to evaluate achievements and to decide
on what must be done next in pursuit of the strategy. The results of reform in

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 203

The Lisbon Strategy for Promoting Knowledge and Innovation

203

the various Member States can more easily be compared, and Member States
can adapt best practice from one another.
Since the renewal of the strategy in 2005, political leadership has been important at the level of the EU. The president of the European Commission,
Barroso, has been actively involved in the implementation of the strategy. Of
importance in the future will be a review of the renewed strategy in 2008 and
the decisions which will be taken at that time at the European and the national
level.
Latvia’s priority: a knowledge-based economy
Latvia, like other Member States, prepared its National Reform Programme
for 2005–200812 in the autumn of 2005. The programme states that if Latvia is to
continue to enjoy rapid economic growth, it must move from a labour-intensive
economy to one that is based on knowledge. Manufacturing of products with
low added value is common in Latvian business today, while the proportion of
high-tech industries is negligible. One important problem, according to the
Latvian programme, is that people’s education and skills are not in line with
the demands of the labour market. There are also distinct regional differences
in this regard in Latvia. The programme defines five major areas for economic
policy development in pursuit of the Lisbon Strategy’s goals in Latvia. These
are ensuring macroeconomic stability, stimulating knowledge and innovation,
creating an environment which is favourable and attractive for investments and
labour, promoting higher employment, and improving people’s education
and skills.
Latvia’s national Lisbon programme also defines several indicators which
must be achieved by 2010. These are based on Latvia’s actual situation, and in
some senses they lag behind the indicators that have been defined at the EU
level. The EU, for instance, hopes to achieve a level of employment of 70%
among people aged 15 to 64 by 2010, while Latvia’s goal is to achieve a level of
67%. The EU wants R&D financing to be equal to 3% of GDP, while Latvia
plans spending at a level of just 1.5% of GDP. Latvia is moving toward the
goals, it has to be acknowledged – in 2005, employment in the aforementioned
age group was at a level of 63.3%, while financing for research and development was at a level of 0.57% of GDP.13
Latvia, like other Member States, has appointed a single government official
who is responsible for co-ordinating the national Lisbon programme – Mr Lisbon,

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

204

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 204

INGA ULNICÂNE-OZOLIÒA

as he is known. In Latvia, the function has been entrusted to the economics
minister. A Lisbon Strategy supervisory council has been set up to co-ordinate
and control the process, as has a consulting group for the council. The council
is a co-ordinating institution aimed at ensuring that the goals of the Lisbon
Strategy are achieved in Latvia. It defines those government institutions which
are to be involved in the process, focusing on Parliament, local governments
and various social partners.14 The chairman of the council is the economics
minister, and members include 10 other ministers, three representatives of
Parliament, as well as representatives of labour unions, employers, businesses
and local government structures. The consulting group helps to design and
implement the national Lisbon programme at the interinstitutional level. The
group is chaired by the state secretary of the Ministry of Economics, and members include high-ranking civil servants from those ministries which are involved in the process.15
The European Commission evaluated Latvia’s national Lisbon programme
early in 2006 and declared various strengths in the programme. These included
a concentration on R&D and information and communications technologies,
along with reforms in these areas, steps envisaged improvement of infrastructure, and projects aimed at promoting an inclusive labour market.16 The Commission also pointed to areas in which further work is necessary – ensuring
macroeconomic stability, encouraging greater co-operation among research and
educational institutions on the one hand and businesses on the other, strengthening attempts to adapt education to the needs of the labour market, and
preparing and implementing a unified strategy on lifelong learning.
A fundamental prerequisite for the successful implementation of the Lisbon
Strategy in Latvia is political leadership and administrative capacity. Of importance here is the effective work of the supervisory council, but that has not
always been the case. On February 10, 2006, the economics minister asked colleagues to discuss proposals that had been made by the European Commission, but no other minister from among those who are responsible for the
Lisbon Strategy turned up for the discussion.17 Implementation of the strategy
could be aided by the greater involvement of other institutions and experts
who, in their work, pursue the same goals. These could include representatives of planning regions and universities, for instance. Also important in
pursuing the strategy is the ability of ministries to work together in pursuit of
the common goals.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 205

The Lisbon Strategy for Promoting Knowledge and Innovation

205

Latvia’s progress en route to Lisbon
Latvia prepared its first progress report on implementation of the national
Lisbon programme in October 2006. The authors of the report wrote that
progress in implementing the programme indicated that the defined goals
could be achieved. In 2005, Latvian GDP increased by 10.2%, and in 2006 it
rose by another 12% — more than had been forecast in the programme.
Employment figures were also much better than had been expected – unemployment dropped from 10.4% in 2004 to 8.7% in 2005, and 7.2% in the second
quarter of 2006.
The progress report also demonstrates close co-ordination between the
national Lisbon programme and the activities which are financed in part by
the EU’s Structural Funds. Approximately 67% of this money was spent on
Lisbon priorities during the 2004–2006 planning period. Priorities in the national Lisbon strategy are in line with the priorities for Structural Fund monies
during that period – balanced development, business and innovation, human
resources and employment, as well as development of rural areas and the
fishing industry. Similar synergy between the national Lisbon programme and
the Structural Funds can also be seen in the new 2007–2013 planning period. A
defined strategic goal in the Latvian National Development Plan for that period
is ‘Education and Knowledge for Economic Growth and Technological Excellence.’ The relevant priority speaks of ‘educated and creative people, technological excellence and flexibility among companies, and development of science
and research.’18 This corresponds to the Lisbon goals. Many Structural Fund
activities during the planning period are included in the National Lisbon
Programme as related projects.19 Of importance here is co-operation between
the Ministry of Economics, which is responsible for the Lisbon Strategy, and
the Ministry for Regional Development and Local Government Affairs, which
is responsible for Structural Fund financing.
In its evaluation of Latvia’s progress report, the European Commission spoke
to strengths in implementing the National Lisbon Programme – steps taken to
promote the emergence of new companies and to finance innovative small and
medium enterprises. This includes the establishment of new guarantees and a
risk capital fund, as well as a one-stop agency for those who wish to establish
a new enterprise.20 The Commission also praised progress in improving the
labour market in Latvia. When it comes to weaknesses, the Commission

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

206

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 206

INGA ULNICÂNE-OZOLIÒA

issued three recommendations. First, it asked Latvia to ensure economic and
budget sustainability by limiting fiscal policies so as to avoid an overheating
of the economy and to select spending priorities more carefully. Second, the
Commission stressed the need for faster progress in reforming research and
innovation policies, particularly in terms of promoting co-operation between
the relevant institutions on the one hand and the business world on the other.
Third, the European Commission called for more intensive efforts to increase
the supply and productivity of labour by improving regional mobility, helping
the education system to be more in line with the needs of the labour market,
and introducing system of lifelong learning.

The Lisbon Strategy for promoting knowledge and innovation
The search for knowledge has always been at the heart
of the European adventure.
It has helped to define our identity and our values,
and it is driving force behind our future competitiveness.21

Let us now turn to one of the priorities of the Lisbon Strategy – promoting
knowledge and innovation. Schumpeter argued that the process of innovation
in its broadest sense is a driving force of capitalism, stating that ‘the fundamental impulse that sets and keeps the capitalist engine in motion comes from
the new consumers’ goods, the new methods of production or transportation,
the new markets, the new forms of industrial organisation that capitalist enterprise creates’.22 In contemporary societies, innovation and other economic
functions are best handled by market mechanism and capitalist firms,23 but in
some cases the activities of markets and firms must be complemented or corrected (but not replaced or duplicated) by instruments of public policy. There
are two prerequisites for government intervention in the field of innovation.
First, this is acceptable in those cases when markets and companies cannot
handle the function on their own, and there are problems which are not automatically resolved by market mechanisms. State support, for instance, may be
necessary if the private sector is not investing in innovation because the associated risks are too high, or if there have been problems in launching co-operation between scientists and businesses. A second reason for intervention has

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 207

The Lisbon Strategy for Promoting Knowledge and Innovation

207

to do with the administrative capabilities of government institutions in resolving
problems that have emerged in the market. The government needs information
about the situation in the market, as well as skills in dealing with problems
that occur. Otherwise public resources can be wasted. It is important to make
sure that decisions on whether intervention is necessary are based on analytical
considerations, not ideological judgements.
Innovation as a process is no longer of interest just to researchers, experts
and enthusiasts. Today it is seen as one of the main prerequisites for the successful implementation of the Lisbon Strategy at the EU and the national level.
In the spring of 2006, the European Council named investments in knowledge
and innovations as the key priority for implementing the Lisbon Strategy.24
The emergence of a Europe that is modern and friendly to innovation was also
discussed at an informal meeting of EU leaders in Lahti in October 2006.25 In
December 2006, the European Council confirmed that innovation is of decisive
importance in terms of Europe’s ability to deal effectively with the challenges
and opportunities of globalisation.26 An EU innovation strategy was approved
in September 2006.27 It lists 10 activities which are defined as particular political
priorities in the context of the Lisbon Strategy. These are: improving educational
systems; establishing a European Institute of Technology; establishing an open,
unified and competitive European labour market for researchers; transferring
knowledge between universities and research organisations and industries; supporting innovation in European regions; creating a new framework for government support for research, development and innovation; developing a new
strategy related to patents and intellectual property rights; supporting the emergence of new digital products, services and business models; offering support
to promising sectors; and offering support for innovation in public procurement.
Promoting innovation and knowledge is also a priority at the national
level. Nearly all EU Member States have declared in their National Reform
Programmes that this is the main challenge on their reform agenda.28 Latvia,
too, has declared knowledge and innovation to be a priority if sustainable
economic development is to be ensured.
An innovation gap?
Ever since the 1960s, a leitmotif for European technological efforts has been
concern that Europe lags behind the United States in terms of technological

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 208

208

INGA ULNICÂNE-OZOLIÒA

development. America has made investments in research and development,
European scientists often move to the United States to work there, and US
companies have dominated in recently developed high-tech sectors.29 In the
1990s, there were new concerns about the supposed superiority of Japan. Even
more recently, Europe has worried about the rapid growth of China and India.
These concerns mean that when it comes to the EU’s policies vis-à-vis research,
technological development and innovation, it has traditionally been important
to try to understand Europe’s comparative weaknesses and to deal with these.
One of the most popular formulations of the problem is the concept of the
‘European paradox’, which was defined in the 1990s. The idea is that Europe
has excellent scientific foundations, as seen in academic journals, but it is far
less successful than others in using that knowledge to create new products and
economic value. This particularly applies to new technologies.30 Research that
has been conducted since then, however, even disproves the idea that the EU
has excellent scientific foundations, showing that in terms of academic publications, too, the United States surpass Europe.31
In terms of innovations, the superiority of the USA is manifested largely in
greater investments in research and development, as well as higher education.
There is also greater spending on information and communications technologies,
patents,32 as well as human capital33 (see Table 1). It has to be said, however,
that in Table 1, we also see the superiority of the EU in several areas – the number of graduates in the fields of science and engineering, business-financed
university expenditures on research and development, as well as employment
in medium-high and high-technology sectors.

Copying America?
Concerns among the shapers of EU policy vis-à-vis the knowledge and
innovation gap between the EU and America and Japan are the reason for why
several EU activities in this regard are, to a certain extend, an attempt to copy
the USA and Japan. This is seen in the stated goal of investing 3% of GDP in
research and development, in the idea of establishing the European Institute of
Technology, and in attempts to make the work of European universities more
dynamic.
After much hand-wringing about higher R&D investments in the USA and
Japan, European leaders met in Barcelona in 2002 to set the goal of ensuring
that by 2010, Europe’s investments in this area are at a level of 3% of GDP.
Moreover, two-thirds of those investments, according to the plan, are to come
from the business sector. Then the EU would catch up with the USA and draw

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

210

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 210

INGA ULNICÂNE-OZOLIÒA

closer to Japan. As can be seen in Table 1, the EU lags behind both countries,
particularly in terms of business investments in research and development.
The EU invests less than 2% of GDP in R&D, although the situation is by no
means identical in the various EU Member States.34 In Sweden and Finland,
the goal of 3% has already been achieved, and both countries have set the goal
of boosting the level to 4% of GDP by 2010. In 10 Member States, including
Latvia, the level of spending has not even reached 1% of GDP. In pursuing the
overall goal, it is important to remember that of key importance is not just raising
investments in a quantitative sense, but also to ensure that the investments are
used as effectively as possible.
Establishing the European Institute of Technology (EIT) is one of the latest
ideas of how to reduce the innovation gap between the EU and other parts of
the world. The hope is to establish an institute which would rival the Massachusetts Institute of Technology in the United States. European Commission
President Barroso proposed the EIT in February 2005 as part of the renewed
Lisbon Strategy.35 The aim of the EIT would be to strengthen the EU’s capacity
for innovation by integrating the highest-level processes of innovation, research
and education at an international level. The EIT would be a decentralised and
flexible networked structure, one that would be run by a board of high-level
and experienced representatives of business, research and education. There
would be six Knowledge and Innovation Communities (KICs), all related to
strategically important and interdisciplinary areas of activity. These KICs are
to be established by universities, research organisations, public and private companies, financial institutions, regional and local governments and funds. These
would all merge their human, financial and physical resources in pursuit of
the creation, dissemination and use of new knowledge.
The EIT is to launch operations in 2008, initially with 1,000 employees, 600
master’s degree students and 400 doctoral students. The European Commission has calculated that the EIT will need EUR 2.4 million in funding between
2007 and 2013. The money is to come from Member States, regional and local
governments, the private sector, the operations of the EIT itself, and the EU’s
programmes and funds. One of the main requirements for the success of this
initiative will be true interest on the part of business and research sectors.
Another area related to innovation and knowledge in which the EU is worried
about falling behind America is that of higher education and universities.36 A
larger proportion of Americans have completed their higher education, US

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 211

The Lisbon Strategy for Promoting Knowledge and Innovation

211

universities are better financed and can attract the best students and instructors from the whole world, they have more productive co-operation with the
business sector, there are better intellectual property protection, and greater
numbers of new companies are established in high-tech sectors (Table 1).
Universities are important in shaping the knowledge-based economy in terms
of education, research and innovation, but their potential has by no means been
put to full use in Europe at this time. There are plans for a series of projects
aimed at modernising universities so as to enhance their role in the emergence
of the knowledge-based society and to mobilise their investment in the pursuit
of Lisbon Strategy goals. The aim will be to increase the quality of education
and to make sure that education is more in line with the demands of the labour
market. Co-operation between academia and business will be encouraged,
and ways of ensuring better and more effective university financing will be
sought out.
There are other areas, too, in which there are direct or indirect attempts to
copy the United States. This applies to intellectual property rights and the establishment of accessible Community patent.37 The innovation gap between
the EU and the USA can, to a large extent, be attributed to the fact that the
United States post much better indicators in the area of patents.
How can one evaluate the EU’s effort to catch up with the United States
and to bridge the innovation gap? Certainly, it is a good thing that the EU is
evaluating and analysing American experience and learning from it. At the
same time, however, catching up with the USA is not a goal in and of itself. EU
policy makers should not try to copy the United States blindly. Critical assessment is needed to determine those parts of the American experience that are
appropriate for the EU and those which are not necessary. It is also true that
Asian countries, particularly China and India, are becoming stronger and
stronger, and the United States are concerned about competition from those
countries, too.
Moreover, some EU Member States such as Sweden and Finland are ahead
of the United States in terms of some aspects of innovation.38 This suggests
that it might be of greater use for the EU to take a look at successes in its own
Member States than to try to catch up with the USA at all costs.39

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

212

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 212

INGA ULNICÂNE-OZOLIÒA

Is Europe innovation-friendly?
There have been many different explanations as to why processes of innovation are not as active in Europe as they are elsewhere in the world. One popular
idea is that innovation is more active in the United States because the state’s
role in the national economy is lesser there. This is not a convincing argument,
because even though there are many claims about the supposedly minimal
role of the American government in the country’s economy, the fact is that
government financing for R&D is higher there than in the EU (Table 1). There
are mechanisms via which government support is provided for research and
development, such as the Small Business Innovation Research (SBIR) programme.40
In comparing the situation with innovation in the USA and the EU, therefore, we can say that the EU problem is not an excessive state role in research
and innovation, but the problem is insufficient businesses involvement in this
process. In the United States, in addition to a greater state role, there is also
much more extensive business participation in R&D. One of the main issues
for the EU, therefore, is to achieve greater business participation in this area.
Consensus has been reached on the idea that the priority is to set up a framework for businesses which promotes innovation. Requirements include open
markets, appropriate regimes to protect intellectual property rights and patents,
as well as availability of venture capital.41
At the same time, when we compare the situation with innovation in the
USA and the EU, we must also consider the different economic profiles of the
two regions, as well as their comparative advantages and disadvantages.
Long-term analysis of data related to patents, for instance, shows that German
companies have comparative advantages in such areas as mechanical engineering, transportation, consumer goods and automobiles.42 These are sectors
in which innovation appears incrementally and gradually in terms of improvements to processes and products. The comparative advantages enjoyed by
American companies are found in such sectors as medical engineering, biotechnologies and information and communications technologies. All of these
are areas in which radical innovation is not uncommon. The difference between
the USA and the EU, in other words, is based not only on the possibility that
the United States are superior to the European Union, but also on the fact that
each region has its own comparative advantages and areas of specialisation.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 213

The Lisbon Strategy for Promoting Knowledge and Innovation

213

There is another issue, however, if we think about what hinders the process
of innovation in Europe. Could it be that the values, culture and mentality of
Europeans simply do not promote innovation? Public values and attitudes are
of particular importance, because the process of innovation means questioning
the status quo. Innovation is uncertain, it involves search, risk and experimentation.43 Innovation often is a process of trial and error, and that is why societies
in which innovation occurs must be open to change, they must be able to undertake risk, be tolerant vis-à-vis experiments and failures, etc.
Another commonly heard explanations as to why innovation is at an insufficient level in Europe is that public culture and attitudes do not support change
and risk. Micossi argues that a dynamic economy with innovation requires an
economic environment and institutions, as well as cultural values and attitudes,
which offer powerful stimuli for change and promote openness to new players
and new experiments in business.44 Innovation needs an open and dynamic
economic environment which is full of ambitions, competition, self-help and
initiative. It is important, moreover, for the public, along with financial and
legal institutions, to support the risks that are involved. Innovation often proves
unsuccessful, and it is important to make sure that failures in business do not
create major financial or economic problems which cause society to stigmatise
the whole process of innovation. When there are successes, in turn, society
must be prepared to accept the fact that successful experimenters often earn a
great deal of money.
Micossi argues that corporate institutions which have emerged historically
on the European continent have established an economic environment and a
set of cultural values which do not encourage competition. The governing attitude in Europe, he says, is that an individual’s economic position is a matter of
good fortune, contacts or inheritance, not his or her capacities and initiative.
This is an environment in which individuals do not believe in their own abilities.
There are also suspicious attitudes in many European countries vis-à-vis big
profits, particularly when these are divided up under legal or political protection.
Too often this is the case in Europe. Micossi compares Europe, where individuals
are dependent upon and concerned about their status, to the self-reliant culture
in America, where there are family values and efforts to promote education.
The bottom line is that a serious move toward innovation in Europe is not
going to be possible without long-term changes in economic institutions and
public values alike.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

214

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 214

INGA ULNICÂNE-OZOLIÒA

The openness of societies vis-à-vis innovation and the spirit of innovation in
the business sector – these are the areas in which Europe really can learn from
the USA. EU policy documents now stress the need to understand that innovation is an important public value.45 This is an area, however, in which political
activities will not be enough – societies must be open to change, people must
understand the problems which prevail and be prepared to change.
It cannot be said, however, that attitudes vis-à-vis innovation are identical
in all of Europe. There are some Member States which are more open to it,
while others are more sceptical. The Eurobarometer survey has asked people
about innovation, its importance, and new products and services, dividing
respondents up into four groups – ‘anti-innovation’, ‘reluctant’, ‘attracted’,
and ‘enthusiasts’.46 The survey found that 11% of EU residents, on average, are
enthusiasts, 39% are attracted, 33% are reluctant, and 16% oppose innovation.
The greatest percentage of enthusiasts and attracted respondents were found
in Malta, Slovakia, Italy, France and Slovenia. Other countries in which support
for innovation was higher than the EU average were Luxembourg, the Netherlands, Sweden, the Czech Republic, Ireland, the United Kingdom, Belgium,
Denmark and Hungary. The lowest level of support for innovation was found
in Poland and Latvia, where more than 60% of respondents are either opponents (16–17%) or reluctant about innovation (45–47%). Comparatively higher
percentages of opponents are also found in Germany, Greece, Cyprus, Portugal and Spain (20% or so). Interestingly, Finland is one of the countries which
has done very well in terms of innovation, but even there public attitudes are
cautious – 46% of respondents are reluctant, and 13% oppose innovation.
Data about public attitudes show that despite the fact that the discourse of
European political leaders currently presents innovation as a key priority and
need for long-term socio-economic development, many people in Europe are
not yet convinced that innovation is a good thing. Many people are conservative, they have biases, concerns and a lack of understanding. This means that
there must be a broader discussion about the goals and benefits of innovation,
as well as public needs and expectations vis-à-vis these processes. Otherwise
ideas about how to promote innovation will remain an elite process with
questionable public support.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 215

The Lisbon Strategy for Promoting Knowledge and Innovation

215

Challenges in the development of knowledge
and innovation in Europe and Latvia
Europe has to become a truly knowledge-based and innovation-friendly society where innovation is not feared by
the public but welcomed, is not hindered but encouraged, and where it is part of the core societal values and
understood to work for the benefit of all its citizens.47
Successful promotion of knowledge and innovation must be based on a
system in which policy makers have a good understanding and systematic approach to these issues. In public debate, the concept of innovation is often
simplified or narrowed. People associate it only with high-tech manufacturing,
information and communications technologies, or specific and well known
locations such as Silicon Valley in the United States. Policy must not be based
on common stereotypes and myths about innovation. Instead, there must be a
complex approach to the role of innovation in socio-economic development.
The specific situation and needs of each Member State must also be taken into
account when innovation policies are designed. The level of innovation differs
a great deal from one EU Member State to the next, so the transfer of experience
from one country to another must be a very careful process. Each time there
must be consideration of whether one country’s experience will be in line with
another country’s national situation. Comparative research shows that the new
Member States are quite far behind in the area of innovation.48 Calculations
show that Hungary and Slovenia will need 10 years to catch up with the EU average, Lithuania and the Czech Republic will need more than 20 years, Latvia
will require nearly 40 years, and Poland and Slovakia will need 50 years or more.
These forecasts, of course, must be accepted with a grain of salt, because it is
practically impossible to predict future developments with any precision. Still,
the data do show that there is a significant innovation gap within the EU. This
also indicates that solutions which are successful in the older and far more
technologically advanced EU Member States will not always be appropriate
for the developmental needs of the newer Member States.
Let us now look at three challenges in the area of innovation policy – ones
that are important for the EU as a whole and, particularly, for Latvia. First of
all, it is important to make sure that all groups in society and all sectors of the

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

216

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 216

INGA ULNICÂNE-OZOLIÒA

economy benefit from the development of innovation. Secondly, promoting
innovation is a complex issue, it must not be based on just a few prestigious
projects. Thirdly, there must be an all-encompassing strategy to promote investment, as opposed to mechanical increases in financing alone.
Benefits for all groups in society and all economic sectors
One of the dangerous trends in the development of a knowledge-based
economy and the relevant innovation policies is that countries get too carried
away with high technologies. Policies aimed at high-tech development will
affect only small segments of national economies and societies, and might
promote polarisation in society. High-tech sectors are important, of course, but
policy must promote innovation in low and medium-technology sectors, too.
These represent approximately 90% of economic activity in Europe at this
time.49 Low and medium-tech sectors such as furniture manufacturing, textiles,
food production, etc., are open for innovation, as their major segments of sales
volume are made up of new and technologically improved products. These
sectors also use high-level design skills, engineering skills and scientific services.
It may be that the medium and low-tech sectors will continue to play a great
role in the European economy of the future.
Excessive focus on high technologies is particularly dangerous in the new
EU Member States, because it diverts the attention of policy-makers from other,
more important economic issues such as developing the capacity for innovation
among companies in all sectors of the economy.50 Most of the new EU Member
States have said that their national priorities include biotechnology, material
technologies and information and communications technologies. They have
argued that their priorities are in line with priorities in the EU programmes.51
This approach means that policies are focused on a small group of large
companies, leaving other, mostly small and medium-sized companies on the
back burner.
Researchers are sceptical about the plans of new Member States to base
their economic growth on high-tech sectors. This worked in Eastern Asia,
where there was rapid development thanks to these sectors, but that does not
mean that countries in Central and Eastern Europe will be able to repeat that
strategy today.52 Researchers point to two major reasons to question the importance of high technologies in the development of Central and Eastern

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 217

The Lisbon Strategy for Promoting Knowledge and Innovation

217

European countries. First of all, high-tech sectors play a relatively small economic role in terms of employment and output – even in Western Europe, the
proportion is only around 8% or so. This means that it is far more important to
ensure increased productivity in the entire national economy. Secondly, there
is severe competition in high-technology sectors, and it is very hard for new
entrants into the market to conquer any heights therein. This requires enormous
investments, and profits are not always immediate or substantial. Researchers
recommend that thought instead be given to the use of technologies in all
sectors of the economy.
Also important in modern-day economies is to overcome traditional stereotypes which say that innovation and the use of technologies apply only to the
manufacturing sector. It is no less important to promote innovation in service
sectors, too. This is very important in Latvia, where the service sector plays a
very important role in the national economy. Documents related to innovation,
such as the National Innovation Programme for 2003–2006, have never focused
enough attention on the issue of innovation in the service sector.
It is also important to remember that in addition to technological innovations, there must be non-technological innovations such as changes in business
models, improvements in design, and organisation of processes.53 Technological
and non-technological innovations must support one another in pursuit of
economic development.
A complex approach, not just prestigious projects
Another risk in the development of a knowledge-based economy is that
politicians and society can be concentrated on specific prestigious projects, as
opposed to a more complex approach toward promoting innovation. An example is the idea of promoting Rîga as a potential site for the European Institute of Technology.54 This is an idea which should be treated very cautiously,
because there must be a proper evaluation of the costs and benefits of the plan,
given the existing situation with innovation in Latvia. Among the 25 EU Member States before Romania’s and Bulgaria’s accession, Latvia was in penultimate place (24th) on the European Innovation Scoreboard.55 Only 17.5% of
companies in Latvia are innovative, while on average of 45% of enterprises are
innovative in the EU as a whole.56 In this situation, the priority must be the
establishment of innovative capacities in companies and the removal of any

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

218

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 218

INGA ULNICÂNE-OZOLIÒA

obstacles against innovation in the national economy. There could be very few
benefits from the establishment of the European Institute of Technology in
Rîga, because the abilities of the local business sector to gain benefits from an
institute of such importance could be quite limited at this time. If the institute
were to be sited in Latvia, it might resemble a ‘cathedral in the desert’ – one
which has quite little to do with local socio-economic development.
Although the attention of the mass media and of politicians is often attracted by elite and prestigious knowledge economy projects which make use
of high technologies and modern laboratories, the fact is that promoting innovation is really a prosaic process of everyday work. The innovative capacities of
the business sector must be developed gradually, and obstacles against the
process of innovation – shortages of finances, human resources and information, bothersome business regulations, etc. – must be removed.
An all-encompassing strategy,
as opposed to purely mechanical increases in financing
An important component in the Lisbon Strategy is to increase R&D financing
to 3% of GDP by 2010. This has been very significant in Latvia, because the
goal has focused the attention of politicians on research and development
financing – an issue which had largely been ignored ever since the early 1990s.
Owing to the 3% objective that has been set by the EU (1% must come from the
state budget), Latvia’s Law on Research Activity (2005) states that each year,
financing for research activity must increase by at least 0.15% of GDP until the
level of 1% of GDP is reached. Accordingly, financing for R&D has increased in
recent years, and many important activities have been launched. Institutional
funding for research institutes has been established, funding for research activities at higher education establishments has been increased, etc.
Many researchers, however, have argued that mechanical increases in financing to a set goal will not ensure results in and of themselves.57 Increased
financing is more effective if it is part of a broader strategy, one in which political goals and activities aimed at pursuing those goals are defined systematically. Criteria must be elaborated for the evaluation of achievements and the
return on investments. The Law on Research Activity states that Parliament
must approve a 10-year national strategy for science and technology, but two
years on, that has not yet been done. In June 2006, the Committee of the

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 219

The Lisbon Strategy for Promoting Knowledge and Innovation

219

Cabinet of Ministers reviewed a draft proposal for Guidelines for Development of Science and Technology for 2006–2013, mandating the Ministry of
Education and Science to prepare a relevant strategy.58 Public debates of such
documents would make it possible for the public at large to be involved in the
debate over the goals and expected results of scientific and technological
development. It would also be easier for the government to explain to taxpayers how their taxes are being used to support research activities as part of a
broader strategy for the development of science and technology, to explain
what kind of return can be expected for those investments.
Political leadership is of key importance if an effective strategy is to be
prepared and implemented. The draft Guidelines envisage an institution for
consultation and co-ordination – the Strategic Council on Scientific and Technological Development would be chaired by the prime minister, and it would
be analogous to similar institutions in many other European countries. There
must be co-operation between the Ministry of Economics, which is responsible
for innovation policy, and the Ministry of Education and Science, which is
responsible for research policy. In the context of strengthening administrative
capacity, a positive thing here is the establishment of the Department of
Knowledge and Innovation Systems at the Latvian Investment and Development Agency in 2006. The department administers all government aid in this
area, analyses the effectiveness of innovation system and innovation support
instruments, etc.59

Summary: pre-conditions for successful implementation
of the Lisbon Strategy
The Lisbon Strategy is also important for growth and employment in that it
helps to attract the attention of political leaders to important aspects of socioeconomic development. The issues are given greater priority, and financial and
human resources are mobilised in pursuit of solutions. Planned and launched
reforms can only be successful if they are supported by businesses and the
public. Here are a few important conclusions about the implementation of the
Lisbon Strategy in the final stages of its development so as to promote knowledge and innovation in Europe and Latvia.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

220

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 220

INGA ULNICÂNE-OZOLIÒA

During the nearly seven years which have passed since the implementation
of the strategy, the process has moved from generalised political declarations to many specific reform processes with implementation frameworks
and instruments. Since the renewal of the strategy in 2005, it has become
more pragmatic. During the final years of its implementation, it will be
important to evaluate the renewed strategy in 2008, as well as decisions
that have been taken at the European and the national level. Latvia will
have to develop its own positions vis-à-vis national needs and priorities.
■ Some of the goals of the strategy – promoting knowledge and innovation,
for instance – are long-term in nature. High-level political campaigns can
produce a significant stimulus for such processes, but they will not be
adequate in the long run. Thought must be given to what will happen with
these processes after the Lisbon Strategy is closed out in 2010.
■ Ongoing monitoring of progress is necessary so as to evaluate the reforms and achievements. It must be remembered, however, that some of
the reforms – investments in education and research, for instance – will
only produce economic results after a longer period of time.
■ Effective political leadership and administrative capacity are essential for
successful implementation of reform, and that applies to the European
and the national level. Fortunately, this strategy is being implemented not
just by political leaders and administrative officials, but also by their social
partners and local government representatives. The range of people who
are involved could be expanded even further, bringing in representatives
of regions and of universities. The strategy involves a great many processes, and co-operation among various institutions is most important in
doing the work. Given the close synergy between the Lisbon Strategy and
the EU’s Structural Funds, it is particularly important to ensure co-ordination among the institutions which are responsible for these areas.
■ One of the European and national priorities in pursuit of the Lisbon Strategy is to promote knowledge and innovation, and although this used to be
the provenance of just a few experts and enthusiasts, it is now a priority
that has received the blessing of political leaders. This is no less of a priority
in Latvia if long-term economic growth is to be secured and if the country is
to move from a labour-intensive to a knowledge-intensive economy.
■ The EU has a lot to learn from the United States when it comes to knowledge and innovation, but this must be a cautious process in that not all
■

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 221

The Lisbon Strategy for Promoting Knowledge and Innovation

221

‘success stories’ from the USA will be appropriate for the European situation. Moreover, catching up with the United States must not be the main
goal or purpose. Europe must also monitor development in the rapidly
growing economies of Asia, as well as analyse the experience of its own
most innovative countries – Sweden and Finland. These are two countries
which surpass the USA in terms of many indicators of innovation.
■ Of great importance in the realm of innovation is to ensure that members
of the public are open to and supportive of experiments and risks. Sociological research shows that at a time when innovation is clearly a priority
in the discourse of European political leaders, many people are still quite
hesitant about this. There is a need for a broader discussion of public
opinion, needs and expectations vis-à-vis innovation, so that support for
innovation is not just an elite process with questionable support on the
part of society. This is important in Latvia, where a comparatively large
share of survey respondents are reluctant vis-à-vis innovation.
■ Indicators of innovation show that there are great differences among EU
Member States in this regard, and a long time will be needed before new
Member States reach even the EU average. The great gap between certain
countries and others makes it clear that each country must look for solutions
that are appropriate to its own situation and its own needs.
■ When providing support for innovation, it is risky to concentrate on support only for high technologies, because these represent a small share of
national economies and affect a very small share of society. Concentration
on high technologies alone can foment socio-economic polarisation. It is
important to promote innovation in low and medium-technology sectors
and in the service sector if innovation is to serve the interests of the entire
national economy and society. It is also important to remember that nontechnological innovations in such areas as work organisation, design,
marketing, etc., are equally important.
■ Similarly, the knowledge society cannot emerge if there is a concentration
only on prestigious technological projects. In Latvia, it is far more important to develop the capacity of innovation gradually and in all sectors of
the economy, particularly at small and medium enterprises. There must
also be a systemic approach to eliminating obstacles against innovation –
a lack of finances, specialists and information, hindering business regulations, etc.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 222

222
■

INGA ULNICÂNE-OZOLIÒA

The Lisbon Strategy has led to greater focus on R&D funding which had
been on the back burner for far too long. However, mechanical increases
in financing will not be enough to promote knowledge and innovation.
Increased investments will yield only limited results if they are not a part
of an all-encompassing strategy to develop knowledge and innovation –
a strategy with clearly defined goals, achievable results, a systematic set
of projects to be pursued, and a set of clear criteria on the basis of which
the effectiveness of the investments can be evaluated.
