The European Union’s Common Foreign
and Security Policy in the Crossfire,
though not at the Crossroads, of Values and Interests1
The Common Foreign and Security Policy (CFSP) of the European Union
(henceforth also the EU or the Union) has, in recent years, become a point of
reference when measuring and evaluating the successes and failures of the
integration of Europe. Judging from their statements, the views of EU representatives are characterised by delight, even astonishment, over the accomplishments in the various areas of the CFSP. In contrast, the views of politicians
and experts in EU Member States are tinged with pessimism reflecting their
dissatisfaction with the difficult process of reaching consensus on issues of
crucial importance for Europe.
A casual observer, regarding this EU policy from the sidelines, may become
confused when faced with the plethora of contradictory statements and arguments concerning the CFSP. Such confusion could dissipate when it is recognised
that the current situation is rooted in a long-standing paradox, which will
continue to exist in the future. That paradox stems, on the one hand, from the
unwillingness of Member States to give up important attributes of their sovereignty (such as their own foreign, security and defence policies) and, on the
other hand, from their wish to take full advantage of the benefits offered by
acting in concord with the Union. The interests of Member States are well
served if important policy decisions regarding the external international environment are co-ordinated at the EU level, because when the Union can act as a
united political entity, it has greater impact on global developments than do all
the Member States when they act individually. That is why most of them affirm
their readiness to develop the CFSP further. Nonetheless, as soon as it is necessary to act in this direction, each step is reassessed against the national interests

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 227

The European Union’s Common Foreign and Security Policy in the Crossfire..

227

and then the decision-making becomes complicated. The contradictory aspects
of this policy are also evident in the juxtaposition therein of values and interests.
The explanation for this lies in history, interests of international organisations
and of Member States, and European integration.
That is why this chapter will be devoted to delineating the principal trends
and clarifying the salient issues and their interrelatedness. This, in turn, should
help define Latvia’s position toward the CFSP. First, we shall outline those
historical trends which led to the development of the CFSP and affect the
current situation in the area of foreign and security policy. Then we shall
examine the list of European values, which have been proposed and discussed
by Ivars Ijabs2, in the broader context of the CFSP. In the third section, we shall
consider the attitudes of various EU Member States toward the CFSP and seek
to identify those interests which overlap and those which differ from state to
state. We shall also seek to determine the extent to which each country’s position
includes the component of values. This, in turn, will help us to draw broader
conclusions about the relationship between values and interests. In the fourth
section, we shall offer a case study about values and interests as components
of the EU Neighbourhood Policy. In conclusion, we shall focus on the problematic aspects of the CFSP which are relevant for Latvia.

1. The EU Common Foreign and Security Policy
from a historical perspective
The ongoing integration of Europe started in the wake of the Second World
War. The paradoxical elements of the EU Common Foreign and Security Policy
today stem from the post-World War II historical experience of the EU original
Member States. Moreover, the problems attributed to the diverse and often
ambiguous attitudes of EU Member States today toward the CFSP have
emerged over a longer period of time, not just in the last few years. The fact
that the states are moving cautiously toward such a common policy should
not be attributed solely to the divergent views on the war in Iraq; it should be
regarded as one of many examples of a historically grounded phenomenon
that integration of foreign and defence policy has and will continue to serve as
an instrument for achieving the policies and securing the defence of individual
states. Agreement on consolidated action can be achieved only when it does

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

228

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 228

ÞANETA OZOLIÒA, ATIS LEJIÒÐ, GATIS PELNÇNS, DZINTRA BUNGS

not encroach upon the national interests of the states involved. Thus, the CFSP,
per se, and the values that it embodies are of secondary importance.
This observation is corroborated by several important turning points in the
history of the EU. The Maastricht Treaty of 1993 was the first document to talk
about CFSP and lay the legal foundations for that policy. However, we shall
not consider this treaty as the principal reference point in this endeavour,
because our aim is not to investigate the juridical basis of this policy. Our focus
will be on the relationship of values and interests in the formulation of foreign
and defence policies. What is more, foreign and security policy issues have
been omnipresent throughout course of EU development and the process of
European integration, even when they have not been noted in official treaties.
The main impulse for the integration of European states was the wish to
avoid new wars, avoid violence in dealing with common affairs, and prevent
the rebirth of revanchist sentiments in Europe. After the Second World War,
the European states did not seek to exclude potential aggressors, as had been
the case with Germany at the end of World War I. Instead, they opted for policies
of engagement which encouraged the bringing of former adversaries into the
European processes and co-operation with them.
Security considerations had much to do with the development of post-war
Europe, as did the plans for economic restoration. The Marshall Plan of the
United States laid the foundation for economic co-operation among European
countries. The desire of the USSR to strengthen its presence on the Continent
encouraged the establishment of security organisations. The Treaty of Brussels
of 1948 led to the creation of the first collective defence structure3: the Western
European Union, which was established in 1955. As the Cold War intensified
and the Europeans saw that they could not adequately counter the influence of
the USSR in Europe, the United States of America and the European democracies decided to establish in 1949 the North Atlantic Treaty Organisation (NATO),
a structure endowed with the necessary military capacity and arsenal for collective defence.
In 1950, despite the recent founding of the two aforementioned organisations,
France proposed the establishment of the European Defence Community (EDC),
which would be based on sectoral integration, a fundamental principle of the
European Coal and Steel Community. France’s Prime Minister, René Pleven,
hoped that by bringing the Federal Republic of Germany into the new alliance
the threat posed by the Soviet Union would be reduced; at the same time, this

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 229

The European Union’s Common Foreign and Security Policy in the Crossfire..

229

would ensure control over the FRG in the military realm. Thus, the proverbial
two birds would be killed with one stone. The EDC treaty was signed with much
fanfare in 1952. However, after a change of government in France in 1954, the
country’s Parliament rejected the treaty because the parliamentarians did not
wish supranational structures to decide their country’s security and defence
issues. These events – the creation of the EDC and its subsequent fate – illustrate
how interests can supersede values and how contradictory stances have afflicted the EU in its own development.4
The rejection of the EDC treaty had a serious effect on the course of European integration. Until 1970, the Member States of the European Communities
(EC) focused on achieving a common market and economic integration moved
forward successfully. They avoided both discussions and co-ordination of
foreign and security policy issues and excluded these topics from the agenda.
However, as economic integration progressed, co-operation in the political
realm became an inevitable consequence. To a certain extent, this process was
accelerated with the EC decision to admit new members: Denmark, the UK
and Ireland. In 1970 the EC foreign ministers drafted a report in which they
called for co-operation in those areas where there was the greatest convergence
of opinions. Foreign policy was listed as one such area, and a consultative
mechanism, the European Political Co-operation (EPC), was established for
the exchange and co-ordination of views. While the EPC could be considered
as a stepping stone toward the CFSP, it is only a feeble reflection of the ambitious intentions of the EDC of 1950.
At the same time, it appears that the maturation process, which occurred
between the years 1950 and 1993, was necessary for the European states so as
to progress from consultations and harmonisation of views to the CFSP. The
following figures illustrate the fact that at first Member States were very reserved about discussing and resolving foreign policy issues and that only
gradually did they come around to dealing with these issues together and
supporting a common policy. As has been documented by Michael Smith,
Member States reached agreement on eight common positions and projects
between 1970 and 1974, on seven between 1975 and 1979, on 24 between 1980
and 1984, on 26 between 1985 and 1989, and on 94 between 1990 and 1995.5 If
it took the EU 43 years to reach agreement on common activities, then it should
come as no surprise that among the four instruments of the CFSP, the ones
which are the most highly developed are those which speak to guidelines,

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

230

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 230

ÞANETA OZOLIÒA, ATIS LEJIÒÐ, GATIS PELNÇNS, DZINTRA BUNGS

common strategies and common positions, where the emphasis is on common
values, rather than on common actions which would entail taking into account
the national interests of individual states. A vivid example of this phenomenon
is the unanimity of EU Member States when the Union’s common strategy on
relations with Russia was approved. The implementation of these principles,
however, depends on actions emanating from specific policies based on a country’s
national interests in different areas (energy supply, nuclear safety, environmental
protection, bilateral partnership in a global context) rather than on values (human
rights, rule of law, democracy, market economy, civil society, etc.) which, in this
context, could hinder the achievement of specific goals.6
The developmental process of the CFSP is marked by another tendency
which in the 21st century is becoming more and more evident on account of its
contradictory elements. When the EPC was launched, Member States of the
European Communities agreed on the issues deserving greater attention. These
represented a symbiosis of interests and values. For instance, the assistance
programmes developed as part of the development co-operation with the
countries of Africa; they were influenced by a mixture of sentiments consisting
of a sense of responsibility for post-colonial countries and a values-based
desire to enhance welfare, security and peace in a region with which many
European countries had historical ties. The development of co-operation with
the countries of the Mediterranean Sea region is also very directly linked to the
close interaction between interests and values. The EC strengthened relations
with international institutions such as the United Nations, the Council of Europe
and the CSCE/OSCE and thus proved that Member States were moving away
from the classical paradigm of realism in international relations – a paradigm
based on national interests. In effect, by supporting universal human values at
the global level the EC was moving toward liberalism.
Up to the conclusion of the Maastricht Treaty, the EU Member States managed to preserve a balance between interests and values in foreign and security
policy without having an official common policy in this area since activities
outside the EC occurred in regions and countries which could be seen as part
of the proximate environs. When the EU announced the CFSP, which represented
the Union’s aim of becoming a major player in the international arena and
speaking with one voice, no testing had been done of the Union’s ability to act
in regions and countries with which there had been no previous longer-term ties
or economic contacts. This applies also to foreign policy activities heretofore

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 231

The European Union’s Common Foreign and Security Policy in the Crossfire..

231

atypical for the Union. This basic failing was painfully evident from the EU’s
inability to deal with violence in a neighbouring region when the wars of the
former Yugoslavia broke out.

2. An assessment of values:
which are the values exemplifying the CFSP?
In view of the trends in the historical development of European integration,
it is clear that foreign and security policy concerns of Member States are not to
be isolated from the framework of values which contributed to the development of a common platform for internal policies and a European view of the
international setting. The powerful presence of values in the representation of
the individual interests of Member States in Europe and beyond its borders
has served as a counterweight between ‘the good and the necessary.’7 The
contributing authors of this compilation would like, however, to emphasise
those values, as identified by Ijabs, which, regardless of time, Member States’
interests, or international events, continue to be relevant in the ongoing debate
about the future of the CFSP:
Citizenship. At first glance, citizenship may seem unimportant. Nonetheless, the high level of democracy in the EU which ensures that public opinion
is not ignored when decisions are taken on foreign and security policy issues is
grounded in citizenship. This, in turn, brings us to civil society. Arguably, the
broader notion of citizenship, especially as it relates to a civil society, has made
it more difficult for the EU to ‘invest’ in some of the countries important for
Latvia in terms of foreign policy, such as Georgia and Moldova. The development of civil society will increasingly influence Latvia’s own foreign and security policy; from a future perspective, an important issue when defining foreign
policy priorities is whether there is a balance between the interests and values
of the political elite and those of the society. Development co-operation is of
key importance in EU policies in various parts of the world. This means that
the people of Latvia must be prepared to invest the taxpayer’s money in Africa
and Asia.
The rule of law is one of the fundamental values of the European Union
and as such it fits into the values central to the development of its foreign and
security policy. Member states have been more active in thinking about the

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

232

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 232

ÞANETA OZOLIÒA, ATIS LEJIÒÐ, GATIS PELNÇNS, DZINTRA BUNGS

rule of law when determining common positions vis-à-vis countries, such as
Turkey, Iran, Belarus, whose place and role have been debated actively in Europe
and elsewhere. The rule of law, however, tends to be forgotten in situations where
interests impinge upon values. It would be welcome if the Union applied the
notion of the rule of law in its relations with countries, such as Russia, not only in
crisis situations (such as the interruption in energy deliveries, which stemmed
from a conflict between Russia and Belarus at the beginning of 2007), but more
frequently. Political pluralism and respect for differences are values not just in
the context of the diverse policies of countries which are outside of the EU, but
also, with regard to the specific political interests of EU Member States. This would
seem to suggest that populist calls for a single foreign and security policy will
yield no result, especially since the existence and recognition of pluralism are
absolute cornerstones of the Union itself. The rule of law, however, will continue
to be relevant and important in enhancing the role of international law in the
future. Let us recall that in the last decade the number of conflicts has increased
in the world. Yet, various countries acting together have not been able to agree
on effective means to end these conflicts. This situation has led to a kind of
clustering into two rather fluid groups: in the one there are the countries hoping
for an increased role of international law, while in the other there are the countries
preferring the balanced application of various means at their disposal. Most
EU Member States are in the first group.
Another value which will become more and more important for all Member
States has to do with the harmonisation of that which is national, regional and
global. There are clear divisions among those EU Member States which position
themselves not only against the regional but also global surroundings and
those which view the global context as a warning that calls for the creation of
a protectionist policy. This contradiction in values will affect the CFSP in the
future: the more the Union seeks to act as a global player, the greater will be
the disharmony, deriving from the divergent attitudes toward the effects of
globalisation, among the member states.
Prosperity is another important value. It is also one of the cornerstones of
the European Union. The Member States have achieved the highest level of
prosperity in the world and for several decades they have worked individually
and together to expand the area of welfare and stability. Those regions and
countries which the EU prioritises are the recipients of the ‘fruits’ of EU prosperity. Prosperity will remain a value also in the future, especially since sharing

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 233

The European Union’s Common Foreign and Security Policy in the Crossfire..

233

and exporting prosperity will bring Member States together and help establish
a common platform for foreign and security policy.
Historicism also has an important place in the CFSP. This is a value which
reveals vividly the attitudinal differences of Member States toward the Union’s
international environment. It is a factor now and it will continue to be a factor
in the future. We can delineate several groups of states which have been influenced by their historicism. First, there are those EU Member States (the UK,
France, Belgium, Spain et al.) which used to be colonial powers; this fact tends
to explain why the EU, in its foreign policy, tends to pay more attention to the
former colonies of these members. In general, this interest beyond the Union’s
borders enhances the EU global role. Second, there are the countries (such as
Malta, Cyprus, Ireland and Sweden) which were neutral during the Cold War;
they have tended to maintain a low profile in foreign affairs or operate in regions considered as their customary turf. Then there are the post-Communist
countries which have brought a new component of historicism into the EU:
increased attention toward Russia and the territory of the former USSR. The
diverse historical experiences of EU Member States tend to expand the horizons of the CFSP. In this way historicism can be seen as furthering the EU goal
of becoming a global player. At the same time, historicism has fostered discord
among Member States over the priorities and content of the common policy.
The EU relationship with Russia is a vivid example of this. The post-Communist countries want the relationship to be based on a balanced set of interests
and values, because only a democratic country with a certain level of prosperity
will shape relations with partners on the basis of the rule of law, equality and
mutual respect. The countries in the first group, however, are interested in
maintaining their global role through bilateral relations; they, therefore, emphasise interests over values in their relations with Russia. The diminished
role of values has led to a situation where the heretofore multi-faceted relations
between the Union and Russia are being reduced to a single component: energy
(more specifically, the Union’s ‘dependence’ on its relations with Russia for
the timely delivery of energy resources). The deterioration of relations to such
a primitive level will lead to a decline in the EU international standing and
role in the future.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

234

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 234

ÞANETA OZOLIÒA, ATIS LEJIÒÐ, GATIS PELNÇNS, DZINTRA BUNGS

3. Priorities in the foreign and security policies
of Member States – commonalities and differences
An assessment of the future development of the Union’s CFSP requires not
only a review of the values, but also an evaluation of the foreign and security
policy priorities of EU Member States. This makes it possible to analyse the
commonalities and differences in this regard among the Member States and
determine the proportion of the common features to the differences. The scope
of this paper allows us to focus only on the following major indicators of the
CFSP: approaches of Member States to EU integration (intergovernmental or
supranational), attitudes toward global processes, regional interests, and the
role of values.
The CFSP in the eyes of the Big Three
Germany favours the shaping of a supranational common EU policy and
regards the CFSP in this light. What is more, Germany supports a federal
model for the Union, and this idea of the EU future institutional architecture
stems from the national institutional structure of Germany itself.8 The CFSP is
viewed as one of the main elements leading toward ‘deeper’ EU integration,
which, in turn, would develop Europe into an independent player in the international arena, one that can act as necessary and use the appropriate military
capacities and civilian capabilities. The development of civilian capabilities in
the context of the European Security and Defence Policy9 is seen as a priority,
while military capacities are seen as a last-resort instrument for dealing with
crises.10 Germany argues that greater EU involvement and investments in international peacekeeping processes would ensure a more equitable sharing of
costs and obligations between the United States and the EU; furthermore, by
assuming greater responsibility, the EU would enhance its role in international
policy-making.11 In the White Book on Germany’s defence policy drafted in 2006,
the government defines the country’s national and international interests. It is
important to note that this publication particularly stresses Germany’s role as
an active EU member in the international processes. The White Book also
sends a message to Member States and the international community regarding
the political changes that have occurred in Germany since its new chancellor
assumed office. When it comes to values which need to be transmitted beyond
a country’s borders, the document speaks to human rights and an international

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 235

The European Union’s Common Foreign and Security Policy in the Crossfire..

235

order which is based on international law. It advocates a free and open global
market, one that would help reduce the differences between the world’s poor
and wealthy regions.12
According to the White Book, co-operation in the area of development is
another important factor in the development of Europe’s security policy.
Europe is a leading provider of aid: currently, about 60% of international aid
distributed in the world comes from the Union.13
Germany considers that the EU should focus its attentions on countries in
Eastern Europe, the southern Caucasus, Central Asia and the Mediterranean
region within the context of its Neighbourhood Policy. Of major importance
for Germany is ‘sustainable and flexible co-operation with Russia in the area
of security.’14 Germany addresses globalisation in connections with the strengthening of national and EU links of an economic and political nature with the
Asian-Pacific Ocean region, Australia, Japan, China and India.15 Already in the
1960s Germany expressed its views vis-à-vis global processes. At that time
France and several other countries sought to direct the Union’s development
co-operation toward the former colonies in Africa. Germany and the Netherlands objected to these efforts, emphasising instead that aid should be distributed to the poorest countries first and in different parts of the world.16
The approach of the United Kingdom (UK) to EU integration emphasises
the importance of co-operation among individual countries. This policy stands
in stark contrast to Germany’s supranational views.17 Great Britain’s reluctance
to transfer some of its sovereignty to a supranational structure is best explained through its historical experience, especially as an empire. Concerning
the CFSP, the UK emphasises the primacy of international law over the laws
of the European Communities,18 and the United Nations as the main security
forum in the world.19 At the same time, the UK sees NATO as its main security
guarantor; in this context, historical links with the United States are an important factor in its attitude toward the ESDP. Britain supports development of the
ESDP in the context of its support for NATO and, in this regard, emphasises
the Berlin+ agreement.20 The Union is, therefore, accorded a secondary role in
that it would act in the areas where NATO does not act.21 Overall development
of the ESDP, the UK feels, would ensure stronger Transatlantic links.22 By
emphasising the Berlin+ agreement, Britain stresses the primacy of the EU’s
civilian capabilities; at the same time, it does not reject further development of
the Union’s military capacities.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

236

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 236

ÞANETA OZOLIÒA, ATIS LEJIÒÐ, GATIS PELNÇNS, DZINTRA BUNGS

On the one hand, the UK says that there are no geographic borders for the
CFSP (i.e., it sees this as a global EU policy),23 and it directs its foreign policy
toward Africa, Latin America, the Middle East, the western Balkans, and Southwestern Europe,24 as well as toward the development of strategic relations
with Russia.25 On the other hand, the UK has encouraged increased EU attention
toward the former British colonies in Africa.26 The situation in the western
Balkans is viewed separately in that the UK emphasises humanitarian and
technical assistance, as well as macroeconomic support to promote social security (welfare).27 Britain also tries to direct attention to immigration problems
in the EU and utilise the development co-operation programmes. In 2002, Great
Britain and Spain proposed that EU development assistance be contingent on
the effort of recipient countries to reduce migration to the EU and to repatriate
illegal immigrants.28 Britain’s concern for development co-operation was evident
also in 1990, when its Foreign Secretary, Douglas Hurd, argued that the Union
support those countries which advocate the rule of law, political pluralism
and market principles.29
France can be described as favouring something between the supranational
approach of Germany and the interstate approach of Great Britain. France believes in a strong Europe, but it argues that this goal must not reduce the
power of national governments.30 France’s attempt to straddle two different
approaches should be viewed with reservation, because the call for a more
powerful and unified CFSP is based not on the common interests of the EU,
but rather on France’s status as a regional power. Experts have noticed this
and said that the main goal of French foreign policy after the Second World
War has been to renew and strengthen France’s status as a power, including as
a military power.31 It seems, therefore, that the country’s declared aim in postwar Europe of renewing a stable and democratic Europe in the security and
economic sphere32 has been nothing more than political rhetoric. France’s
main goal in the EU context is to defend its national interests, because France
wants to see a powerful Europe, but it sees it mainly as a resource to develop
its own national ambitions.33 In other words, France considers the goals of the
EU’s CFSP to be closely linked to its own national interests, and the EU is
emphasised as the ‘main’ actor on the international stage – an actor with a
strategic vision, its own diplomacy and its own instruments to manage civilian
or military crises. The words ‘its own’, however, should be replaced in this
case with the word ‘French.’34

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 237

The European Union’s Common Foreign and Security Policy in the Crossfire..

237

France tries to differentiate between ‘European issues’ (the Balkans) and
‘non-European issues’ (for example, the September 11 attacks in the United
States, the Iraq conflict, the spread of nuclear weapons).35 Such differentiation
provides and explanation for France’s various views against involvement in
the war in Iraq. Furthermore, it implies that Europe’s interests are distinctly
regional. This, in turn, suggests that France’s approach toward Europe’s international role may be anti-global.
According to France’s military programme for the period between 2003 and
2008, the principal goal of the its defence and security policy is to protect the
people of France and their interests at home and abroad. The programme calls
for increasing the autonomous decision-making capacity and then, if necessary,
moving forward individually. To a certain extent, this refers to the possibility
of individual activities in relation to France’s defence agreements in Africa and
the Middle East (historicism). The programme emphasises France’s potential
to become ‘lead nation’ in Europe – ‘something which may be necessary in a
coalition situation in terms of relying particularly on the ‘lead country’, which
can bring other participants together on the basis of its own capacities.’36
The UN is considered as important, largely because France is a member of
the Security Council. European – Transatlantic co-operation is emphasised as a
key element of security.37 It is significant that France, unlike the other EU Member
States which also belong to NATO, assigns only a secondary role to the alliance despite being its member.
France’s views on further enlarging the European Union tend to be equivocal.
It does not take a firm stand against enlargement; yet it argues that enlargement cannot be seen as a positive factor in terms of the CFSP and ESDP. The
negative aspects of the process, as far as Paris is concerned, include the fact
that new member states are not always able to adapt their institutional mechanisms to the new situation, nor can they make a substantial contribution to
the CSFP because they are small and less developed economically.38
The Mediterranean countries
Greece has always been a ‘complicated’ partner in the shaping of EU
foreign policy, because it has often distanced itself from the Union’s common
policies. For Greece, history continues to be major factor in the shaping of a
common defence dimension with Cyprus.39 Nationalism, harking back to

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

238

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 238

ÞANETA OZOLIÒA, ATIS LEJIÒÐ, GATIS PELNÇNS, DZINTRA BUNGS

Hellenic culture, has been a powerful argument in the development of Greek
positions on international issues; in historical terms, it has been important in
shaping the policy vis-à-vis the United States and in helping to determine the
EU policy toward Islamic countries. Historically Greece has preserved good
relations with Syria, but its national interests are linked more with Cyprus,
Turkey and the Balkans. Though Greece has traditionally distanced itself from
the processes of the EU common foreign policy, it has insisted that its own
interests be considered at the EU level.40 At the same time the government in
Athens has been passive toward the issues it considers as unrelated to Greece.
This suggests that Greece sees the EU as an instrument for pursuing its own
interests. Greece stresses that ESDP programmes have created conditions for
co-operation in the area of defence, ones that will, in the long term, reduce
defence spending and ensure proper conditions for economic and technological
development.
The Greek position vis-à-vis the CFSP is focused on the notions which speak
to mostly non-military crisis management, the leading role of the UN Security
Council in ensuring global security and peace through international law enforcement institutions, co-operation, economic development, democracy, human
rights (other countries emphasise human rights, too, but Greece does so with
particular vigour), as well as individual rights.
Greece wants the EU to be a strong actor in global policy, one that is able to
mobilise its economic, trade, diplomatic and, if necessary, military capabilities.
Greece supports deeper integration in the context of the ESDP, and members
of the country’s political elite are unified in their vision of Europe as a community (the supranational approach). This, however, is not an unequivocal position
in the context of EU integration, however, because there are certain signs which
suggest that Greece favours both the supranational and the interstate approaches.
While stressing the need to expand qualified majority voting in all areas, Greece
also feels that military issues must be agreed upon on unanimously.41
The foreign policy of Italy underscores two components which are important in the EU’s CFSP. First of all, Italy specifies the geographic area (Africa,
Asia, Antarctica, America, Europe, the Mediterranean and the Middle East) of
its national interests, which indicates a global view of international processes,
even if Italy looks more toward Africa, the Balkans and the Mediterranean
region. Second, Italy’s stated political priorities are: Euro-Atlantic relations,
co-operation in the Mediterranean region, stability in the Balkans and the fight

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 239

The European Union’s Common Foreign and Security Policy in the Crossfire..

239

against terrorism. The government in Rome also devotes much attention to
human rights in the world and to the fight against racism and discrimination.42
The United States are very important in Italian foreign policy, and close cooperation with the USA is often seen as more important than co-operation
with the larger countries of the EU.43 Italy points to the need for ‘fundamental
strategic co-operation’ between the United States and the EU, and it positions
itself more as a country which is focused on NATO.
One of the basic priorities for Italy is the development of co-operation with
countries on the southern shore of the Mediterranean Sea. Italy wants to have
a long-term European and Mediterranean Free Trade Area by 2010. Italy is one
of the countries arguing for balance between Europe’s Eastern and Southern
policy dimensions.44 The Eastern Dimension mostly relates to co-operation
with Russia. In situations where one EU Member State tries to emphasise its
preferred region, such a balanced position from Italy could help to reconcile
divergent views. Italy supports the Union’s closer integration in the realms of
defence and security; this has been manifested in Rome’s efforts to expand the
use of qualified majority voting in the context of the CFSP45. Here again we see
a supranational approach.
Spain seeks closer co-operation with France, Germany and the United States,
and it views the EU as a guarantor of Spain’s increased role in the international arena. Its foreign policy interests lie mainly in Latin America and the
Mediterranean region. Spain is involved in developing relations between the
EU and Cuba (historicism). In the context of the CFSP, Spain has not singled out
any ‘particular’ values, although in general it endorses multilateralism, human
rights and the rule of law.46 Madrid sees the development of EU military and
civilian capabilities as equally important for the purpose of crisis-prevention
and other operations. Regarding the European Neighbourhood Policy, Spain
supports those projects which relate to countries that are in its region.47 Spain
favours closer CFSP integration, but mostly on the basis of an interstate approach.48
Portugal’s priorities lie in the regions of Sub-Saharan Africa, the Mediterranean,49 and Latin America.50 Portugal has tried to direct the CFSP toward its
former colonies in Africa51 and, in particular, on East Timor.52 Unlike Spain,
Portugal wishes to move closer to the United Kingdom as a strategic partner;
that would also foster the development of closer co-operation with the United
States.53 NATO is seen as the ‘structural axis of Portugal’s security and defence
system’ and a fundamental guarantor of military security, while the ESDP is

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

240

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 240

ÞANETA OZOLIÒA, ATIS LEJIÒÐ, GATIS PELNÇNS, DZINTRA BUNGS

viewed as an instrument for crisis management.54 When it comes to the EU’s
CFSP, Portugal supports an interstate approach which allows each Member
State to think about areas of further integration and development.55
The Benelux countries
In the context of the CFSP, Belgium’s focus is on Central Africa (Congo,
Rwanda, Burundi), Russia (mostly strategic co-operation in the area of energy
supplies), the Middle East, the Mediterranean region as a network for advantageous mutual co-operation, and the Balkans as a region where there are
opportunities for development in regional co-operation and economic affairs
so as to ‘stabilise the European continent.’56
Belgium supports the development by the EU of the non-military techniques
and mechanisms at its disposal. The non-military component in particular
makes the EU different from NATO, and that means that the EU is better at
various aspects of crisis management such as observation, stabilisation and
restoration. The Transatlantic dialogue is important to guarantee international
security.57 As instruments in the civilian dimension of the ESDP, Belgium emphasises diplomacy, immigration policy, the fight against organised crime, and
trade and development policies. Belgium has been very actively interested in
the development of civilian resources (training police officials, civil defence
specialists and judges) so that they can be mobilised for crisis management.
Even before the tsunami crisis, Belgium had proposed the establishment of an
EU first aid and support team (EU-FAST). The proposal, however, was rejected;
Since then, Belgium has devoted much attention to strengthening Europe’s
capacity for providing assistance in emergencies and disasters.58
Belgium firmly believes in a supranational Europe. As Prime Minister Guy
Verhofstadt has put it, ‘the foundation for Europe’s future is the establishment
of a federal Europe.’59
When it comes to CFSP and ESDP, the Netherlands prioritises conflict prevention, crisis management and human rights issues.60 Concerning the ESDP,
functions , such as civilian crisis management, are seen as important. The
Hague argues that the Berlin+ agreement, which deals with the Union using
NATO military capacities in the future, could be supplemented with a similar
accord allowing NATO to use the EU’s civilian capabilities.61 Also important for
the Dutch foreign and security policy is co-operation with the United States.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 241

The European Union’s Common Foreign and Security Policy in the Crossfire..

241

In addition to peace and welfare, the Netherlands emphasises such EU
values as freedom, rule of law and human rights, pluralism, tolerance, nondiscrimination and solidarity. These are fundamental to the notion European
citizenship now, and that will remain true in the future.62 Unlike Belgium, the
Netherlands supports an interstate approach to EU integration. It sees the EU
as a union of independent countries which have come together on the basis of
co-operation and legally binding rules.63
For Luxembourg , a small country, participation in international or regional
organisations is particularly important for the articulation of its interests. In
the EU, Luxembourg has always defended ever closer integration among
Member States in all areas, including the CFSP.64 Given the level of economic
development in Luxembourg and the strength of its financial sector, welfare
and competition may well be the country’s highest priorities in the EU and at
the national level.
Luxembourg’s closest partners are its neighbours (France, Germany, Belgium) and together they have assembled a joint battle group under the ESDP.65
Four Member States and neutrality
Austria’s priorities confirm its desire to strengthen the EU’s international
position by deepening co-operation in the context of the CFSP and the ESDP.
Regional priorities include intensification of neighbourly relations with the
western Balkan countries, Russia, as well as the countries of the Mediterranean,
the Near East and the Middle East.
Other priorities include stronger Transatlantic links on the basis of common
values, greater authority for the United Nations, defence of international law,
human rights and minority rights, and strengthening of the principles of multilateralism (the principle of political pluralism).66
In the context of the CFSP, Austria emphasises the need for the EU to operate internationally as a unified entity, particularly when expressing its position
on armed conflicts, human rights, and other issues which relate to the Union’s
basic principles and values.67 At the same time, however, Austria wishes to maintain an individual view on issues such as Turkey’s admission to the Union. In
geographic terms, Austria sees the CFSP as currently concentrating on the
western Balkans, Near East, Iraq, Iran, the southern Caucasus, Central Asia
and Africa.68

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

242

12.02.2008

Page 242

ÞANETA OZOLIÒA, ATIS LEJIÒÐ, GATIS PELNÇNS, DZINTRA BUNGS

‘For Austria, the ESDP is the highest institutional priority in the development of its defence policies,’69 but its neutrality means that it must emphasise
the development of civilian capabilities within the policy. Austria supports a
balanced development of the ESDP’s civilian and military capacities in monitoring missions, civilian administration and support for the rule of law. Austrians participate in the following ESDP operations: EUFOR ALTHEA, EUPM,
EUJUST LEX, EU monitoring in Aceh, the police mission EUPOL COPPS, the
EUBAM border monitoring mission in Rafah, and the EUPAT in Macedonia.
In other words, Austria is contributing actively to global security.70 The security
of the EU is seen as the basic element in Austrian security (regionalism), even
though the Austrian doctrine on security and defence also recognises a total
defence concept in line with Austria’s ‘vital interests.’ Of particular importance to Vienna are Europe’s peripheral regions, ones in which political transformations are taking place. These changes affect stability and welfare in the
relevant regions. Austria is a country which has found that membership in the
EU and the undertaking of global obligations under the auspices of a regional
organisation has compelled some fundamental changes in its traditional foreign
policy, such as departure from the concept of neutrality.
Ireland’s policy of military neutrality also stresses the importance of the
development of civilian capabilities in the context of the EU’s CFSP.
Ireland’s emphasis in international policies is largely based on multilateralism, which affects its positive attitude toward the ongoing enlargement of the
Union and the missions in countries which need help with stabilisation, welfare and the rule of law (Congo, Darfur, Georgia). Ireland sees its EU membership as a way of enhancing its role at the international level. In the context
of the EU’s Neighbourhood Policy, Dublin pays particular attention to Ukraine,
Belarus, Uzbekistan and Moldova.71 In this sense, Ireland is one of the few old
Member States of the European Union which is interested in the same countries
as Latvia.
‘Malta is on the cultural border between Europe and northern Africa,’72 and
that determines its role in the CFSP. Malta’s official position is that the CFSP is
not incompatible with Maltese neutrality.73 The Mediterranean region is most
important for Valetta, with particular attention focused on Libya, Tunisia, Egypt
and the Israeli-Palestinian peace process. Malta supports the EU Neighbourhood Policy, in other words, in shaping external relations, and it supports the
EU strategic partnerships in the Mediterranean region and the Middle East, as

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 243

The European Union’s Common Foreign and Security Policy in the Crossfire..

243

well as relations between the EU and the Arab League.74 Malta is also interested
in the EU relations with Russia, the United States, China and India.75 Owing to
its advocacy of neutrality, Malta is not involved in the military segment of
ESDP operations, but it does take part in the establishment of the EU’s rapid
reaction forces insofar as humanitarian and rescue operations are concerned.76
Cyprus has never formulated clear priorities vis-à-vis the CFSP, though
Cypriot diplomats have expressed constructive support for the policy. Of particular importance is the geo-strategic position of Cyprus in the Mediterranean
Sea.77 Cyprus, like Malta, focuses on the European-Mediterranean dimension
of the EU Neighbourhood Policy,78 and on the crisis in the Middle East.79
Cyprus argues that the EU should be developed on the basis of community
principles, which uphold the equality of Member States and prohibit the emergence of a hierarchical structure which ignores the interests of small countries.
Cyprus is among those Member States which views with reservation the
expanded use of qualified majority voting in the context of the CFSP.80
The most significant problem in the context of EU enlargement is the
complicated historical relationship which Cyprus has had with Turkey. Ankara has never recognised the ‘Greek section’ of Cyprus, nor has it opened its
ports and airports to Cyprus. It is believed that Turkey’s accession to the EU
will be impossible until this matter is resolved.81
Because of its neutrality, Cyprus takes part in the European Police Forces
and other civilian programmes of the ESDP, and it is assisting in the formation
of an EU battle group with Greece, Romania and Bulgaria, but its participation
is limited to civilian personnel.82
The Nordic countries
Sweden also has a tradition of neutrality in its foreign relations. When it
comes to the CFSP, Sweden insists on an intergovernmental model of co-operation, which actually harks back to this tradition. Concerning universal values,
Sweden stresses human rights, arguing that these must be ‘the cornerstone for
Swedish foreign policy.’83 Sweden also emphasises welfare; for instance, the
Mediterranean partnership must be aimed at reducing the gap in welfare
among the countries of the region.84
An indication of Sweden’s global approach to international security, is its
insistence that humanitarian and peacekeeping missions should ‘make the

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

244

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 244

ÞANETA OZOLIÒA, ATIS LEJIÒÐ, GATIS PELNÇNS, DZINTRA BUNGS

world a safer place.’85 Sweden’s foreign policy is based on the position of
globalism, so its interests involve both Africa (particularly Darfur) and Latin
America.86 Within the EU, Stockholm shows particular interest in relations
with Russia, Ukraine’s wish for close relations with the Union, democratisation in Belarus, and the further development of the Northern Dimension.87
The Swedish foreign policy is rather remarkable: Sweden, while emphasising
its historical neutrality and the fact that it has not joined in alliances, will become involved internationally and participate in different missions if such involvement strengthens its position in the world. For instance, Sweden is the
leading investor in the countries around the Baltic Sea. When the EU Northern
Dimension was first instituted in 2000, Sweden had little interest in it, but as
the influence of the policy grew, Stockholm became more and more involved.
Sweden is also becoming more active in the EU Neighbourhood Policy. Thus,
Latvia, which is also active in the ENP, may encounter greater competition in
this area.
Neutrality and policies of non-alignment have influenced Sweden’s position
on European defence policies, but full participation in the CFSP and ESDP
keep it from advocating genuine neutrality. Sweden’s national defence is still
based on the principle of ‘total defence.’88 The basic influence of neutrality has
to do with an emphasis on the peacekeeping and humanitarian aspects of the
ESDP.89 Accordingly, Sweden’s key priority in the ESDP is the development of
crisis management capabilities.90
Finland, like Sweden, is a ‘neutral’ country which participates in the CFSP and
ESDP, and which emphasises crisis management as its priority in EU foreign
policy.91 Finland argues that among the most important issues are: greater Transatlantic ties, EU-Russia relations, EU Neighbourhood Policy and, especially,
the further development of the Northern Dimension.92 Finland’s geopolitical
interests also include Africa93 and the Balkans. In terms of values, Helsinki
wants Europe to enhance its competitiveness in global markets and to include
human rights policies in EU policy. These issues were cited as priorities for the
Finnish presidency of the EU.94
Finland has supported qualified majority voting with regard to the CFSP,
but it also argues that defence issues in the EU must be resolved on the basis of
unanimity.95 Member States, according to Helsinki, must always have a choice
as to whether they wish to take part in the EU common defence policy.96 Finland actively participates in the development of Northern Dimension policies,

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 245

The European Union’s Common Foreign and Security Policy in the Crossfire..

245

and during its two periods of EU presidency, it has presented itself as an expert
on Russia. Moscow accepts this role, too. Other EU Member States also find it
more acceptable to have Finland as an expert, as opposed to any attempt by the
Baltic States to present themselves in that role. The fear is that Baltic positions
would be dominated by historical issues, not pragmatically based arguments.
Denmark does not participate in the military part of the CFSP, but in the
ESDP it is involved in issues related to the first EU pillar, such as the fight
against terrorism.97 Copenhagen favours a positive model of intergovernmental
co-operation. The EU is important for Denmark in terms of enhancing security
through political and economic instruments, but the EU is relatively unimportant when it comes to defence.98 Denmark recognises that the development of
Europe’s military capacities is a natural manifestation of the EU’s more active
role in the world.99 NATO is seen as the cornerstone for Danish defence policy,
and observers have noted that the more ‘Atlantic’ is the EU foreign policy, the
more attractive it seems to Copenhagen.100
Denmark’s foreign policy, when addressing security, stresses the need to
ensure both the country’s security and welfare. They go back to the fundamental values upon which Denmark’s open society is based. The values include
individual and community freedom, democracy and security. European and
Transatlantic components are seen as prerequisites for the global dimension of
Danish foreign policy.101 Copenhagen is also interested in Africa and the Middle
East;102 these elements indicate the global aspects of the policy. Bilateral cooperation is focused mostly on the countries around the Baltic Sea region and
the western Balkans; in such cooperation, Denmark emphasises security and
stability, democracy and human rights, legislative reforms, the development of
the civil society, the environment and energy, as well as the development of
business the economies in the Baltic Sea region.103 It is significant that unlike
Sweden, Denmark has never promoted itself as a regional leader; moreover, it
has continued to focus on the Baltic Sea region also since the enlargement of
the EU and NATO. This could be advantageous for Latvia as it implements its
foreign and security policies.
Central European countries
For the Czech Republic, foreign policy priorities include the EU, NATO,
good relations with its neighbours, fight of terrorism, and the development of

12.02.2008

human rights at the international level. Prague particularly stresses co-operation
with Austria and Germany (regionalism). Common battle groups are to be
established with those countries; the Czech Republic is also working with Austria
in the ALTHEA operation in the Balkans. Historicism is palpable owing to the
end of the Cold War and the changes it wrought leading to the ‘era of global
security and stability.’ Human rights in the Czech Republic is linked specifically to the abuses thereof during the Soviet era, and that is why human rights
remain one of Prague’s key priorities.104
The Security Strategy of the Czech Republic cites risks related to threats
against the population, the country’s independence, the territorial integrity of
the state, the democratic system, the rule of law, internal order and property
principles.105
When it comes to strategic interests, Prague focuses on economic security
via global economic stability, strategic raw materials, products and services,
diversification of capital and protection of the strategic infrastructure; greater
competitiveness for the domestic defence industry; and the spread of the principles of freedom, democracy and the rule of law.106
As is the case for other ‘new’ Member States, the main pillar for the Czech
collective defence is NATO.107 The EU’s CFSP, in turn, must work closely together
with the North Atlantic alliance.108 In the context of the CFSP, Prague supports
the developing the EU role as a global actor, but it also argues that when it
comes to security issues, the EU must concentrate primarily on Europe and its
neighbouring territories. Accordingly, the Czech Republic supports the development of the EU’s crisis prevention and post-conflict stabilisation capacities
as being of fundamental importance.109
The Czech Republic has identified three regions as its priorities in the EU’s
CFSP, and this is the result of the country’s traditional links and existing interests. The regions include Russia and other countries of the former USSR (Belarus,
Ukraine, Moldova), the Western Balkans and the Middle East. According to
Prague, Russia’s size, resources and powerful ambitions have led the EU to
consider it both a partner and ‘riddle’ which the Czech Republic can help to
solve. In the Balkans, the Czechs have historical ties from the times of the AustroHungarian monarchy and even earlier. The Czech Republic is actively involved
in restoring order and the rule of law in this region.
The Czech political elite have not agreed on a unified approach toward EU
integration. An intergovernmental approach is usually preferred, but Prague

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

247

also thinks that supranational instruments are necessary to ensure more effective decisions in the CFSP.110
The National Security Strategy of Poland makes reference to human rights,
democracy and the rule of law as cornerstones of international security; it also
addresses economic growth and involvement in the global dialogue of culture
under the auspices of the United Nations (tolerance).111
Poland positions itself as a country which is distinctly oriented toward
NATO and does not support the establishment of an autonomous EU military
command structure that is separate from the alliance.112 Before accession to the
EU, other new Member States also viewed the Common European Security
Policy with some caution, emphasising in most cases that the establishment of
a European security space would mean an overlap with NATO. If the Czech
Republic is typified by the attitude that the Berlin+ agreement is enough and
that no competing alliance is needed, then Poland is a special case in this regard. Poland insists on strong transatlantic links, but it also argues that Europe
needs its own security capabilities.113 Poland’s proposed solution is the establishment of an EU army which is directly linked to NATO.114
Poland places great importance on the defence of its national interests
under the auspices of the CFSP,115 and on establishing its own image as a
responsible country at the international level.116 Foreign policy interests relate
mostly to Russia (energy supplies), Ukraine (the free market economy), and
Belarus (minority rights and the Polish diaspora in Belarus). Warsaw works
with Georgia, Moldova and Armenia, stressing historicism in terms of their
common experience with the USSR.117 Another indicator of historicism is that
Poland has been involved in peacekeeping operations since 1953. Poland was
the only member state of the Warsaw Pact which could send its forces on international missions.118 This experience is relevant to the country’s desire in
current times to be involved in international processes. Poland is actively involved in the Middle East peace processes, and that is facilitated by good relations with Israel. Another important foreign policy priority in the context of
the CFSP is its relations with the Mediterranean countries.119 Among the new
EU Member States, Poland stands out on account of its strong interest in the
global processes. This allows Poland to play a role among the larger countries
of the EU.
For Slovakia, NATO is the centrepiece of security, so the country sees the
ESDP as part of the NATO collective defence system.120 Before joining the

Union , Slovakia considered the ESDP as an ‘unnecessary part of EU integration,’121 and it had some reservations about the EU defence policy in general.
At the same time, during the Slovak negotiations on EU membership, one of the
easiest discussions was about the ESDP, and that section of the negotiations was
completed rather quickly.122 Slovakia supports an intergovernmental approach
to the CFSP, but concerning the ESDP, Slovakia focuses more on co-operation
with NATO.123
Despite its reserved attitude, Slovakia participates in the EU external security
processes. Since 2003, it has taken part in the EU operation in Bosnia and Herzegovina and a monitoring mission in Macedonia.124 Currently, it is working on a
joint EU battle group together with Poland, Germany, Latvia and Lithuania.
Slovakia has also demonstrated an increasing interest in the development
of democratisation and a market economy in Ukraine.125 Its geographic location
would suggest also a natural interest in the Balkans.126
The National Security Strategy of Hungary emphasises the importance of EuroAtlantic integration processes,127 along with universal values such as democracy,
the rule of law, human rights and fundamental freedoms in international politics.
Among the national security interests, the document lists support for EU
integration at the vertical and horizontal level, equality and solidarity among
EU Member States, and improvement of the organisation’s methods.128 Like
Slovakia, Hungary emphasises the need to ensure that the ESDP is compatible
with NATO.129 When it comes to taking decisions about the CFSP, Hungary
supports both the need for unanimous decisions and for qualified majority
voting.130
Hungary pays particular attention to relations with Russia, Ukraine, the
Caucasus and the Middle East. Budapest thinks that the CFSP offers greater
opportunities for Hungary to defend its interests in the EU, while membership
in the EU helps it to have greater influence in global processes.131
For Slovenia, the basic security guarantor is NATO. Regarding the ESDP,
Slovenia emphasises crisis management.132 This position reflects the country’s
thinking that conflicts should be resolved peacefully, without the use of force.133
The basic values in its foreign policy are the security and welfare of the country’s
residents. This is to be achieved by ‘strengthening Slovenia’s national identity
and being open to the world.’
When it comes to the CFSP, Ljubljana recalls that it co-operated with the
authors of that policy even before it signed an accession treaty in 2003, doing

so through the Accession Countries Network.134 It also argues on behalf of development co-operation via the EU Neighbourhood Policy, focusing in particular
on Russia and the countries of the Mediterranean region (the EuropeanMediterranean Partnership).135 The Balkans are Slovenia’s most important
foreign policy priority, and it takes part in EU operations in Bosnia and Herzegovina, as well as Macedonia.
Slovenia supports the expansion of qualified majority voting in the CFSP. It
says that the exclusion of a national veto is based on the need to find more
effective solutions to refugees and asylum seekers from beyond the EU’s borders (supranationalism).136
The Baltic States
The focus of Estonia’s security policy is on ensuring the country’s sovereignty, territorial integrity, constitutional order and public safety. Among the
principles of the Security policy, the document includes participation in crisis
management and peacekeeping operations run by various organisations (NATO,
the UN, the OSCE, the EU). NATO is seen as the basic security guarantor. The
development of the ESDP is secondary for Estonia.137 Estonia has been quite
passive in terms of CFSP activities, although Tallinn says that it does see a
chance to make use of the policy in the future.138 Estonia’s priorities in the
ESDP include a greater role for the EU in the world, security and stability in
neighbouring countries, support for economic growth and democracy on the
basis of market economies, and strengthening of transatlantic links. Estonia
monitors the development of the EU Neighbourhood Policy, focusing in particular on Ukraine (where Estonia takes part in an EU border assistance mission),
Moldova and Georgia, as well as Azerbaijan and Armenia, where Estonia participates in development co-operation and technical assistance projects. Recently
Estonia began work on a development assistance project in Egypt.139 Estonia
has said that the CFSP does not need to be focused on the EU itself, because
internal relations are resolved through the foreign policies of its member states.140
Lithuania considers the EU’s CFSP a part of its security policy, but NATO
remains the cornerstone of its security. Lithuania is, nonetheless, an active
participant in the CFSP. It was an initiator of the EU civilian mission on the
rule of law in Georgia; there the aim was to lay a solid foundation for the rule
of law by reforming the country’s system of criminal law. Co-operation with

Georgia recalls historical links which are the result of common experience
under the Soviet regime. In the context of the European security strategy,
Lithuania calls for a multilateral approach and open consultations with the
transatlantic partners before important decisions are taken.141
According to Lithuania’s National Security Strategy, the country’s most
important interests are sovereignty, territorial integrity, a democratic constitutional order, a civil society, protection of human rights and freedoms, as well
as welfare. In the context of the EU, the strategy notes equality, co-operation,
solidarity and the rule of law. Among the priorities in international co-operation
and good-neighbour policies, there is co-operation within the Baltic Sea region,
a strategic partnership with the United States and Poland, relations with Russia
(particularly with regard to the Kaliningrad enclave), relations with Belarus,
and interregional co-operation (the Balkans, the southern Caucasus, Central
Asia). Poland is Lithuania’s most important partner in the area of security, but
foreign policy interests also cover democratisation in Belarus and the establishment of strong ties with the USA.142
Latvia’s attitude vis-à-vis the EU’s CFSP is based on a broader international
context. Latvia has formulated the desire to defend its national interests in the
future, and that represents a fairly clear intergovernmental position. At the
same time, Latvia also seeks closer co-operation within the Union: ‘Latvia’s
strategic goal within the EU is to continue strengthening EU unity in internal
processes and in foreign policy, to promote the EU’s global competitiveness,
and to increase the level of economic development and public welfare in Latvia
as quickly as possible.’143 Although this is a clumsy statement, it basically does
not differ from those presented by most other Member States which choose to
balance out the intergovernmental and the supranational approach to further
European integration. Both the aforementioned Latvian foreign policy document
and Latvia’s national security concept accent the global processes and multilateralism, as shown by Latvia’s assertion that it respects the principles of the
UN and the OSCE.144 Regarding the CFSP and ESDP, Latvia prefers close cooperation with the EU and NATO and advocates a balanced and well justified
use of both military and civilian resources: ‘Latvia considers co-operation and
consultations between the EU and NATO to be fundamentally important. These
should continue to develop on the basis of common interests related to issues
which have to do with security, defence and crisis management, creating, with
the help of military and civilian methods, effective solutions to crises.’145

When it comes to the regional components of the CFSP, absolute priorities

countries and proximate regions, long-term stability emerges, democracy is
strengthened, there is further economic and social development, and the policies
of the said countries are oriented toward co-operation with European institutions. Latvia shall devote particular attention to Eastern European countries,
with which it will continue to engage in intensive political dialogue and cooperation so as to transfer to them the reform experience which has been accumulated over the last several years.’146
The new Member States
Bulgaria and Romania are busy with fully establishing themselves as members of the Union, and they have had little to say about priorities in the further
development of the CFSP. It can be expected that both countries will join
Hungary and Slovenia to encourage greater focus on the western Balkans.147
Bulgaria has not stated specific priorities in the context of the CFSP, except for
an increased interest in the western Balkans and the area of the Black Sea. Of
high priority in Bulgarian foreign policy is the development of good relations
with its neighbours, particularly with Greece and Turkey. Also important is a
Balkan initiative on regional security and co-operation.148 Bulgaria is talking
about participation in a battle group project that is being led by Greece.149 Sofia
considers the best model of EU integration to be the interstate approach.150
Romania stresses the principles of freedom, security and justice,151 as well
as the need to strike a balance between the United States and Russia in global
policy.152 Moldova is of particular interest to Bucharest.153

of each EU Member State, and the basic values upon which these policies have
been based, a correlation of the findings (see Table 1) will allow us to see the
results in a broader perspective, draw some general conclusions and delineate
several options for the future.

after we correlate the priorities and approaches toward foreign and security

From the information in Table 1 we see that all EU Member States have
similar scales of values which are applied in the formulation and establishment
of the CFSP. The spectrum is a fairly wide and ranges from assessing the global
processes, facilitating of democracy and welfare, political pluralism, to the
presence of historicism in issues of foreign and security policy. Analysts may be
sceptical about the importance of values in the development of the CFSP, but
the fact is that values serve as the foundation for a common platform. All
Member States have agreed to co-operate within the CFSP, and that means that
no individual country will refuse to take part in international processes.
The greatest differences are seen in the area of regional interests. This does
not, however, suggest that the EU is about to split up. If the involvement of EU
Member States in the nearby and more distant regions becomes a ‘division of
labour’ and a process where the best practices and expert information are
transferred via co-ordinated activities, then there will be no unnecessary competition over resources and influence. There are also differences in the approaches upon which the EU Member States base themselves. At the level of
values, Member States position themselves as supporters of the supranational
approach; however, with just a few exceptions (Germany, Greece, Italy, Belgium,
Luxembourg), they also seek an interstate approach to the CFSP. This is true
even though these positions can differ in relation to other policies. Still, it is
important to note the trend of attaching greater weight to the elements of a
supranational approach, and this is something which is the result of external
pressures (Russia, Iran).
