12.02.2008

10:00

Page 254

ÞANETA OZOLIÒA, ATIS LEJIÒÐ, GATIS PELNÇNS, DZINTRA BUNGS

4. The relationship between values and interests
in the EU Neighbourhood Policy
In discussions about the European Union and the European Neighbourhood Policy (ENP), invariably there are references to Europe’s common values.
Moreover, the ENP documents emphasise the importance of these values. This,
in turn, raises a number of questions, including the following: has the Union
not been too idealistically naive in conceiving a Neighbourhood Policy so as to
foster an acceptance of its values in the regions surrounding it? Are these
values only asserted ‘on paper’ – in other words, do they provide a convenient
smokescreen for the Union’s ambitions of enhancing its political and economic
influence in the world? A closer look at the ENP shows that these very different
impressions are not unfounded, though, at the same time, they are also misleading. We also see that the ENP is still developing, and all relevant concepts
have not yet been harmonised. This makes it difficult to answer two fundamental questions: how and to what extent do the EU common values comprise
the ENP, and how effectively does the ENP introduce and implement its policies
in the neighbouring countries? Since all these questions are valid now and will
remain relevant in the coming years, they deserve to be addressed in detail. Let
us, therefore, begin by taking a brief look at how the ENP developed. Then let us
compare the European values which are mentioned in the latest ENP documents
and the values which have been analysed by Ivars Ijabs in his study. To conclude this segment of the compilation, let us evaluate the Union’s success in
imparting its values among the neighbouring countries.
A brief look at the ENP and its development
The European Neighbourhood Policy came into being on May 12, 2004, when
the European Commission approved its ‘European Neighbourhood Policy
Strategy Paper.’154 The EU commissioner for external relations is responsible
for the policy, and it is financed from several sources. As of 2007, money for
ENP-related projects and activities comes from the European Neighbourhood
and Partnership Instrument (ENPI). Countries which work with the EU in the
context of the ENP can receive funds and loans from the ENPI.155
The EU tends to consider the notion of neighbours broadly and inclusively.
The Internet homepage of the ENP lists 16 countries as partners. These are

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 255

The European Union’s Common Foreign and Security Policy in the Crossfire..

255

countries in Europe, the Middle East, the southern Caucasus, and northern
Africa. Levels of co-operation with the Union are as diverse as are these countries
and their geographic location.156 Two to three documents define the nature and
scope of co-operation with each country, except for Belarus, Syria, Algeria and
Libya, which have no such documents.
Without focusing on the history of the EU in depth, let us note that since the fall
of the Iron Curtain in Europe in the late 20th century, the EU’s interest in the
surrounding area has expanded enormously and has manifested itself in two ways:
■ the European Union tries to integrate its neighbouring countries into the
EU processes and, eventually, into the EU itself;
■ if it turns out that this manner of integration is not effective or not appropriate, the EU aims to ensure stable relations with the neighbouring
countries in the region and stability at the Union’s borders.
The European Neighbourhood Policy is a creation of the 21st century.157 It
evolved as a policy and continues to develop to this day. The emergence of the
ENP was facilitated and influenced by the grand enlargement of the EU on
May 1, 2004, when 10 new Member States joined the community. As a result,
the external borders of the EU changed radically. The Union grew not only in
terms of territory but also in terms of self-confidence. This led to an increased
desire to take part in the shaping and determination of global processes. The
new situation also called for a new policy, one that would be both friendly and
differentiating toward its new surroundings. The EU wished to have at its
borders a ring of friendly states. At the same time, the EU wanted to distinguish
between the Union and the rest of the continent without creating new borders
or divisions in Europe.
Broadly speaking, the goals of the ENP are to promote security, stability
and prosperity in the entire region which surrounds the enlarged EU. Early
drafts of the policy stressed in particular the idea that this is an unselfish policy,
one that is benevolent and inclusive, one that brings everyone together under
the aegis of shared European values. Over time, the initial exuberance has subsided, even if it has not disappeared altogether.158 This exuberance eclipsed
somewhat key aspects of the ENP that were always there:
■ the policy is based on common interests and benefits;
■ the aim of the policy is to promote security in the Union and in its environs
(the ENP, let us remember, was harmonised with the European Security
Strategy of 2003);

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

256

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 256

ÞANETA OZOLIÒA, ATIS LEJIÒÐ, GATIS PELNÇNS, DZINTRA BUNGS

countries affiliated with the ENP have the opportunity to become more
closely integrated into the European Union. The end result of such integration, however, remains unclear. On the one hand, the ENP is not envisioned as a policy leading toward EU membership of the participating
countries. On the other hand, the ENP does not suggest or specify any
other final result.
The goals of the European Neighbourhood Policy are to promote the development of a stable democracy, the rule of law and prosperity in the entire
region surrounding the enlarged EU. The strategy for the ENP states that the
relationship must be based on ‘mutual commitment to common values,’ both
in domestic and foreign policy.159 Specific manifestations of this commitment
would be adherence to the rule of law, good governance, respect toward individuals and minorities, good neighbourly relations, a market economy, sustainable development, fight against terrorism, non-proliferation of weapons of
mass destruction, abidance by international law, and peaceful resolution of
conflicts.
In pursuit of all of this, the EU and each neighbouring country formulate an
agreement of co-operation. Together they define the priorities which they can
implement by co-operating. The aim of the Neighbourhood Policy has never
been to force the partner countries to do anything. Instead, the neighbouring
countries are encouraged to move closer to the EU in a voluntary and gradual
way, even though the results of this rapprochement are not really clear. The
priorities are used to draft a plan of action with several sections (political dialogue and reforms; economic and social co-operation and development; trade
issues; judicial affairs and interior affairs; sectoral issues such as energy, transport, environmental protection; and the human dimension, covering things
such as education, health and cross-border co-operation). The action plans are
based on the assumption that the relevant neighbouring country or partner
has already ratified the principal international agreements on fundamental
human rights and freedoms and abides by these agreements. In December
2006, action plans had been implemented in 10 partner countries, and specific
goals and activities had been defined for the subsequent three to five years.
The action plans stipulate regular monitoring and progress reports. The
very first report on the ENP offered a brief and very general look at the vast
scope of ENP activities. It was published on November 22, 2005.160 The second
report was essentially made up of nine separate reports, and it was issued on
■

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 257

The European Union’s Common Foreign and Security Policy in the Crossfire..

257

December 4, 2006.161 The set of reports is both general and detailed, and we
will discuss it in greater detail a bit later. We shall try to find an answer to the
question: how successful has the ENP been in implementing the EU common
values? We shall also review the initiatives announced by the European
Commission on December 4, 2006, regarding ways in which the EU can
strengthen relations with its neighbours.
Values in the European Neighbourhood Policy
The ENP documents always present the common, shared values in an attractive way; often, however, the formulations are also vague and inconsistent.
Very seldom are there precise statements as to what those common values are
and how they are manifested. Moreover, the precise statements are not always
identical. A serious shortcoming of the ENP texts is that the concept of values
is never really defined. Definitions are also lacking for other concepts which
could be described as manifestations of those values.
The most concise statement about values and their role in the EU and the
ENP can be found in the ENP Strategy Document of May 2004. We read: ‘The
Union is founded on the values of respect for human dignity, liberty, democracy, equality, the rule of law and respect for human rights. These values are
common to the Member States in a society of pluralism, tolerance, justice, solidarity and non-discrimination. The Union’s aim is to promote peace, its values
and the well-being of its peoples. In its relations with the wider world, it aims
at upholding and promoting these values.’162
The document also says that with the ENP, the Union encourages the partners
to make a commitment to the shared values. The further development of relations
within the context of the ENP will depend on the extent to which the common
values of the EU are accepted and implemented.163 That is why action plans include priorities which are aimed at strengthening the commitment to values.164
Even though all of the action plans affirm the importance values in the
neighbourhood policy, there are only three in which there is a precise definition
of this principle. The action plans for Tunisia and Morocco, for instance,
contain an extensive list of values – democracy, the rule of law, good governance,
respect for human rights, a market economy, free trade, sustainable development, reduction of poverty, and strengthening of political, economic, social
and institutional reforms.165 The Israeli action plan, by contrast, lists just

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

258

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 258

ÞANETA OZOLIÒA, ATIS LEJIÒÐ, GATIS PELNÇNS, DZINTRA BUNGS

democracy, respect for human rights, the rule of law and fundamental freedoms. That is because the Union believes that the relationship between Israel
and the EU is already based on common values.166
In response to the question about the shared values on which the ENP is
based, the homepage of the European Neighbourhood Policy states: ‘The ENP
is based on the concept of shared values and common interests. The shared values
are those which ensure our prosperity, stability and security i.e. democratic
reforms (fundamental rights, rule of law), market economy and sustainable
development (including reforms in sectors such as trade, competition, energy
and transport, environment, people-to-people contacts etc). These reforms will
enable us to develop joint responses to the common challenges we face in the
twenty-first century e.g. prosperity gaps, migration, crime, environmental
issues, public health, extremism and terrorism.’167
If we compare these lists, we see that there is no unanimity on the values
formulated, shaped and pursued by the ENP. We also see that the values
which are mentioned most often include liberty, basic freedoms, democracy
and democratic reforms, the rule of law, market economy and sustainable
development. This offers us only an approximation of what the EU considers
to be its common values and how these values are manifested in the context of
the ENP. Thus we are led to conclude that the activities of the ENP which are
pursued in the name of values are based on the assumption that there exists an
undefined but widely accepted understanding of what Europe’s common
values are and are not.
ENP values and European values
Table 2 offers a comparison of the description of values in the aforementioned
ENP documents and in the chapter of this book contributed by Ivars Ijabs. It
should be noted that although values in ENP documents are not categorised,
for the sake of clarity and comparability, in the table below we shall use the
categories proposed by Ivars Ijabs.
This table reveals some serious differences. Many values are listed in just
one column, not both. We also see that sometimes there is talk of comparable
concepts, ones that have been formulated or assigned differently. Why these
differences? How important are they? The scope of this paper does not allow
us to analyse each element of discordance individually, but we can say that the

main reason for the differences is the way in which values are classified and
assigned. Another important factor is the significance of individual values in
this context. Thus, for instance, the ENP documents seldom mention culture as
a value, but that should not suggest that the authors of the policy think that
culture and its related values are unimportant. It is more likely that they

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

260

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 260

ÞANETA OZOLIÒA, ATIS LEJIÒÐ, GATIS PELNÇNS, DZINTRA BUNGS

believe that in order to achieve the goals of the policy, they must first focus the
ENP on political, social and economic values.
If we look at the list of ENP values, we must remember that only rarely is
there unanimity about the shared values in policy documents. What is more,
there are many inconsistencies in the classification of values in basic documents
of the ENP. Sometimes concepts are described as values, while elsewhere they
are described as manifestations of values. The ENP action plans for Morocco
and Tunisia, for instance, consider the reduction of poverty to be a value, while
the ENP Strategy Document lists it separately from values.168
A lack of overall unanimity is also admitted by Ivars Ijabs, who points out
in his study that the separation of European values into the political, social
and cultural category is conditional and that the values which are reviewed
here do not form a single, integrated system. That partly explains why the list
of European values presented by Ijabs does not include democracy or a section
on economic values. That is not the only explanation, however, and that is why
Ijabs presents others. Choosing to approach democracy from a broader perspective and not analysing it from the perspective of the ENP, he writes:
‘Values which are important today, such as democracy, individual rights and
constitutionalism, hark back to the ancient ideal of citizenship in Europe –
democracy, individual rights and constitutionalism. True modern democracy,
of course, first developed in the United States, so it cannot really be termed a
European value, but it is European in that modern democracy is based on the
idea of the individual’s political status, namely citizenship.’
Ivars Ijabs also reviews the market economy and other aforementioned economic values indirectly, this time through the prism of competition: ‘Among
other cultures, Europe is distinguished by the belief that competition among
individuals can serve the public interest if it is honest and sensibly regulated.
[..] The market, of course, is the most important area for such productive
competition. [..] One of the greatest achievements of post-war Western Europe
is based specifically in this understanding: a flexible and free market economy
which is, nevertheless, socially responsible.’
The ENP and the introduction of values
Although fundamental questions about the interpretation of values in the
European Neighbourhood Policy still remain, this has not hindered the

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 261

The European Union’s Common Foreign and Security Policy in the Crossfire..

261

policy’s further development. The practical implementation of the ENP began
two years ago, and development is continuing. At a press conference on
December 4, 2006, the European Commission announced its evaluation of the
work that had been done under the auspices of the ENP and the progress that
had been made in the relevant countries. The Commission also disclosed its
initiatives on the further development of the ENP. The Commission published
nine reports (two general ones, and progress reports about the seven partner
countries). These documents offer us a look at the way in which the ENP has
been implemented and allow us to formulate various general observations
and conclusions about the role of values in the ENP, as well as the development of the ENP in the future. The documents do not, however, answer the
question of what constitutes the EU shared or common values.
A more systematic review of the documents allows one to present several
general observations. Central to the ENP is the desire to promote friendly relations with neighbouring countries on the basis of common values; hence, the
ENP action plans and progress reports speak first and foremost to a closer relationship between the Union and the participating countries. It is through this
prism that the achievement of specific EU-recognised values is understood.
The action plans and progress reports focus on specific activities which promote the development of such links. These activities – possibly larger or smaller
reforms – might mirror only indirectly the specific values. The set of progress
reports, therefore, cannot be seen as a systematic report on or an official assessment of the introduction of EU values. Instead, they are a reflection of the relationship between the EU and the partner country, of the work that has begun.
This is a process in which the achievement of goals is based on the recognition
of common values. As noted above, even if the framework for the action plans
is identical in all cases, the content of the reports is individualised and in line
with each country’s interests, desires and capacities. This applies to mutually
supported priorities, missions and specific activities. All of the work is based
on a mutual investment and on the principle of voluntarism. This also
means that if an action listed in the plan is not pursued, this does not lead to
sanctions. Progress, in other words, cannot be achieved with force or harsh
criticism.
Directly or indirectly, all of these circumstances make it difficult to evaluate
achievements in any objective way. The summary of the progress reports itself
notes that the progress reports do no lend themselves to a cross-country analysis

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

262

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 262

ÞANETA OZOLIÒA, ATIS LEJIÒÐ, GATIS PELNÇNS, DZINTRA BUNGS

on the implementation of the ENP, although some trends can be identified and
compared.169 Keeping in mind these caveatas, let us review Table 3, which
shows the values that are mentioned directly or indirectly in the various ENP
progress reports. We can look at what is happening in the introduction of each
relevant value. The table offers a summary of what is found in the progress
reports and what each country has achieved. The purpose is not to measure or
to compare the overall introduction of EU values in the relevant countries. The
authors have chosen indicators which are fairly conditional – development
(D), activities (A) and stagnation (S). (D) speaks to achievements, (A) – to whether
or not specific things are being done to introduce values, and (S) – to a situation
in which nothing is happening or in which the situation is deteriorating.
An asterisk (*) means that the ENP progress reports make reference to the
relevant values but that implementation thereof has largely or completely
stopped. For instance, the exacerbated conflict between Palestine and Israel in
2006 brought to a standstill the peace process which had begun. The report on
the Palestinian government particularly emphasises the implementation of
positive values until the beginning of 2006, when lawful elections to the Palestinian Legislative Council led to the election of a greater number of Hamas
representatives than of the moderates halted the reform course of the government. In the progress report on Israel, the authors refer to the ‘special status’ of
Israel’s relations with the EU, referring to the EU-Israeli Association Agreement, which has been in force since 1994, as well as to the overall high level of
development in the country. There is also reference to the ENP action plan
which was approved on April 11, 2005, and which is the next step in the development of the bilateral relations. The authors also affirm that since the action
plan took effect, the EU relations with Israel have developed further than ever
before in many areas, including politics, trade and investments, the rule of law,
security, science and technology, as well as education. The progress report,
however, does not overlook the problem areas and deals, in particular, with
Israeli-Palestinian relations; the wording, however, is diplomatic and the fact
that Israel has occupied Palestinian territory for many years is not mentioned
directly: ‘During the initial period of the Action Plan, the EU has been more
closely involved with Israel in the peace process, in particular through a
number of specific actions designed to assist certain aspects of Israeli-Palestinian relations.’ The fact that the column on Israel has the mark ‘–’ alongside
three values means that the values are not mentioned in the report. In the case

The ENP: an effective policy for spreading common values?
In conclusion, let us return to the two questions which we posed at the
beginning of this section of the compilation:
■ Do the EU common values appear in the European Neighbourhood
Policy, and if so, to what extent?

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

264

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 264

ÞANETA OZOLIÒA, ATIS LEJIÒÐ, GATIS PELNÇNS, DZINTRA BUNGS

How good is the ENP at introducing and implementing these values in
the EU neighbouring countries?
Only general answers can be provided. With respect to the first, we can say
that the ENP really does try to achieve friendly relations with neighbouring
countries, and it does so on the basis of shared, common values. Progress reports about the ENP partner countries show that the process of rapprochement
is based on specific activities intended to help partner countries assimilate the
values in a direct or an indirect way. Some values are consistently emphasised –
democracy, human rights and sustainable development are examples of this.
Other values surface indirectly when discussing the one or the other area. In
addition, there are cases, where the values are not specifically mentioned. The
progress report on Moldova, for instance, contains just one single mention of
market economy, and that is the context of developing strategies for strategic
rather than economic development. Despite all this, one does get the sense in
reading all of the action plans and progress reports that they have been written
on the basis of all of the aforementioned values.
The second question can be answered very directly. The results of ENP
activities are commensurate with the efforts that the two sides have expended.
The authors of the ENP understood this when they said that the ENP is ‘joint
property.’ Full implementation of action plan requirements is still something
for the future, but the most recent reports on the ENP do show the ‘weak points’
in the process, as well as the achievements that have been made in partner
countries and in the Neighbourhood Policy as such. From the perspective of
the ENP, achievements include accomplishments in introducing values, while
failures relate to those areas in which there is stagnation or even decline.
Morocco, Jordan and Ukraine have done well. They are three very different
countries, but they all have stated a clear intention of drawing closer to the
European Union.
Considering the achievements, we must also remember that:
■ positive movement toward the specific goals, which both sides have agreed
upon, is just the start of the road toward developing their relationship in
line with the values which both of them recognise and implement;
■ for partner countries, this process requires many deep reforms which, even
with the best will, cannot be introduced fully in just a few years’ time.
That this could be a long and difficult process is implied by the fact that
only 10 of the 16 countries which are involved in the ENP have their own
■

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 265

The European Union’s Common Foreign and Security Policy in the Crossfire..

265

action plan. Table 3 covers only seven countries, and that is because action plans
with Georgia, Armenia and Azerbaijan took effect only in November 2006. ENP
progress reports, therefore, have not yet been prepared about these countries.
The European Commission is well aware of all of this. The Commission has
reviewed its own work, along with the offer which the EU makes to partner
countries. It has also evaluated the effectiveness of the ENP so far. The conclusion
is that the ENP is necessary and that it has proven its value171 and that is why
the Union must strengthen its links to the ENP. At a press conference on
December 4, 2006, where the progress reports were released, the Commission
announced its conclusions, as well as new initiatives in improving the ENP
and its results. It was declared that the ENP was generally successful, but that
there were also very obvious problem zones. The Commissioner for External
Affairs, Benita Ferrero-Waldner, said that even though economic and political
progress had been made in the neighbouring countries since 2004, poverty,
corruption, unemployment, unbalanced economic development and weak governance continue to be serious problems, as do long-standing conflicts in the
Middle East and the southern Caucasus. These are and will remain challenges
for the EU, said Ferrero-Waldner, because they facilitate illegal immigration,
insecure energy deliveries, environmental damage and terrorism.172 All this
prompted the Commission to draft a new ENP strategic document, which declares that:
■ the ENP budget between 2007 and 2013 will be increased by approximately 32% to a level of 12 billion euros;
■ a Neighbourhood Investment Fund will be set up to encourage developmental banks to offer larger loans to ENP countries in pursuit of greater
development;
■
300 million will be added to the action plan funding so as to strengthen
good governance in the participating countries;
■ there will be a clearer formulation of the ENP vision as to future relations
with the neighbouring countries, offering even closer economic and trade
integration with the EU than could be possible under the auspices of free
trade proposals which have been made before (the ENP, however, still
does not offer neighbouring countries a chance to begin EU membership
negotiations anytime in the future);
■ procedures related to the issuing of visas to known groups of guests will
be improved significantly;

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

266

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 266

ÞANETA OZOLIÒA, ATIS LEJIÒÐ, GATIS PELNÇNS, DZINTRA BUNGS

partner countries will be given a chance to engage in ongoing or ad hoc
meetings with ministers and experts from EU Member States to discuss
subjects such as energy, transport, the environment and health care from
a broader perspective than is possible in bilateral relations;
■ political co-operation and systematic association with the neighbouring
countries about the Common Foreign and Security Policy will be boosted
with regard to the Common Foreign and Security Policy to reach agreement on positions as to international organisations, and to ensure the
participation of neighbouring countries in the agencies of the EU (foreign
policy declarations, positions in international forums as well as participation
in key programmes and EU agencies);
■ the role of the EU in conflict resolution in the region will be made more
effective;
■ there will be a stronger regional approach to the East on the basis of existing co-operation in the Black Sea region.173
All of this shows that the EU is determined to develop the Neighbourhood
Policy and to make it more appealing to and important for the neighbouring
countries. The Union wants the fruits of the policy to be visible and real in the
nearest future. Hence, the development of the ENP is continuing.
In what direction is the ENP moving? Can we conclude that the course
remains unchanged, or are there changes which can be identified? The documents which were published on December 4, 2006, suggest that the course has
not changed, but there may be shifts in emphasis in the future. The new strategic document particularly emphasises economic development and co-operation.
In the introduction we read: ‘The premise of the European Neighbourhood
Policy is that the EU has a vital interest in seeing greater economic development
and stability and better governance in its neighbourhood.’174 Interestingly, the
document has nothing to say about concepts such as values and democracy.
Values have not been completely forgotten, however, because human rights
are mentioned briefly. These are nuances which do not allow us to draw any
far-reaching conclusions, but we should continue to watch the process so as to
see whether the European Commission, in placing an emphasis on economic
development, regional development and good governance, does not ignore
democratic reforms and fundamental freedoms to an excessive degree. If that
happens, that will represent a fundamental shift in the Neighbourhood Policy,
whose defining elements so far have been both interests and values.
■

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

10:00

Page 267

The European Union’s Common Foreign and Security Policy in the Crossfire..

267

Conclusions
The current and future development of the European Union’s Common
Foreign and Security Policy does not give reason for particular optimism. The
authors of this policy will have to continue to manoeuvre between pronouncements that are made by government officials as to the need for a tighter and
more effective CFSP, with politicians promising that it will be fully in place at
least ‘tomorrow’, and national interests which could be called egotistical, when
it comes to the EU’s joint operations outside of the Union. This situation has
existed for decades, and there is no reason to expect a fundamental shift. This
means that it would be short-sighted for Latvia’s government to emphasise
only joint elements of foreign policy in its own foreign and security policies.
The way in which the CFSP has developed so far clearly confirms several facts
of life which Latvia must keep in mind. First of all, it is comparatively easy for
the EU to reach agreement on foreign policy activities which apply to regions
of the world which have traditionally been the Union’s concern, – northern
Africa is an example. Just because the EU is successful with the CFSP in one
region, however, does not mean that the CFSP will serve as a successful model
for activities elsewhere. Secondly, there is still a vast diversity in views among
EU Member States about the countries and regions which are of particular
interest to Latvia.
It will be important to follow closely the attitudinal developments toward
the changes in the CFSP in different EU Member States. History tells us that in
certain cases even countries which take a supranational approach toward the
future of the EU as a whole choose to use an intergovernmental approach in
specific policy areas. An example of this is France and its relationship to the
European Security Community, which it proposed in 1951 and then killed in
1954 by refusing to ratify it. Relying exclusively on the pronouncements of
politicians – pronouncements which are characterised by the ongoing political
discourse tinted by values and which do not take the long-term interests of the
state into account – can create a false impression of the country’s policies, its
potential allies, and its positions in the future.
It would be risky to set the CFSP apart from the overall course of development of the EU. Since the European Constitutional Treaty was rejected by
voters in France and the Netherlands, countries have become more cautious
about strengthening the joint aspects of EU policy. The Union’s largest and

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

268

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 268

ÞANETA OZOLIÒA, ATIS LEJIÒÐ, GATIS PELNÇNS, DZINTRA BUNGS

most influential countries have shifted their attitudes in this regard. Germany
has become more active as an EU leader, and that is the result of the personality
of Chancellor Angela Merkel, as well as of the fact that her country has taken
over the rotating presidency of the EU. France, meanwhile, has become more
cautious. As Kramer puts it, it has increasingly served its own interests and
withdrawn into a defensive pose.175 France claims to support increased supranational elements in the work of the EU, but this has not been based on any
desire to facilitate the emergence of a Europe which is unified on the basis of
common values. Instead, France’s thinking is based on national interests, including the way in which France can increase its influence in international
politics as a regional and, later, a global power. France has fallen victim to its
own policies, because its people do not support the EU which has been shaped
by the political elite. In international terms, France is not even a regional superpower. Steven Kramer suggests that the activities of French politicians during
the last several years have replaced the dream of a unified Europe which was
proposed by Robert Schuman and Jean Monnet with a union of countries
which ‘is hard to love, of which it is easy to be afraid, and which can be denied
without the slightest regret when voting.’176
Another important issue is how various external factors will affect the
CFSP. Socio-psychological theory, when applied to international relations, tells
us that external pressure leads countries to move toward closer consolidation
and co-operation,177 but the fact is that this principle is not valid in all cases in
the EU (we can point to the war in Iraq, as well as to the EU’s relationship with
Russia). This means that when we evaluate external factors, we must identify
those which will lead to closer integration among EU Member States and those
which may hinder that process. From the perspective of foreign and security
policy, it is evident that the EU will have to review its complicated relationship
with the United States. There are very different relationships between the EU
various Member States on the one hand and the superpower on the other side
of the Atlantic Ocean on the other, but the fact is that all 27 EU Member States
are dominated by strategic relationships of partnership. Washington has said on
more than one occasion that it wishes to see a more consolidated EU, one that
is stronger in the global arena. The EU does not have sufficient economic or
military potential to undertake full responsibility for global processes, and it will
always need strong allies. At the global level, that really can only mean the USA.
Balance between the two sides is the best guarantee of international security.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 269

The European Union’s Common Foreign and Security Policy in the Crossfire..

269

Another important factor is NATO. The EU must overcome its inferiority
complex and the resulting biases against the alliance. The sooner the EU is
prepared to develop the structures of co-operation which are already in place,
the sooner it will be able to present itself in the security arena as an important
force in dealing with conflicts in the world. A Litmus test for the EU will be the
way in which relations with Russia are developed and managed. As has been
argued several times in this paper, the EU must decide whether Russia will be
just a supplier of energy, in which case relations will be maintained through a
trade dialogue, or whether Russia will be a country with which the EU must
establish a far broader range of relations. If the latter option is chosen, the EU
will need to review its strategies and tactics, and that will represent a challenge
to the balance of values and interests in the foreign and security policies of the
Union’s Member States.
An analysis of the history and the existing status of the CFSP, as well as of
the position of EU Member States toward the CFSP, allows us to conclude that
a truly common foreign and security policy in the EU will be possible only if
the federalists’ dream of an EU becoming a true state comes true.178 Member
States are not prepared to take this step, however, and that means that at least
for the next decade, the CFSP and its level of consolidation or fragmentation
will depend both on internal factors in the various Member States and on the
regional and international environment which prevails.
Latvia’s position vis-à-vis the CFSP must be based on its own views of the
future model for Europe. In the medium term, Latvia must be prepared to deal
with a ‘cocktail’ of malleable positions and national policies. This means that
we must be ready for several sets of options concerning the foreign and security policy including and to act in line with the desirable version of the future,
the priorities that form the nucleus for Latvia’s position, and the possible areas
of activity where Latvia can participate on the basis of policies that are implemented by other Member States. Latvia’s situation is made more complicated
by the fact that the countries with which the EU has reached or may reach
agreement related to enhancing the integration of the CFSP are not of priority
interest to Latvia – here we refer to the Mediterranean region, Africa and Latin
America. Those issues (energy policy) and countries (Russia, Georgia, Moldova,
Ukraine) which are more central to Latvian foreign policy and which are important for domestic stability and security are also the source of the greatest
contention among the EU Member States, and indeed, these issues are the ones

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

270

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 270

ÞANETA OZOLIÒA, ATIS LEJIÒÐ, GATIS PELNÇNS, DZINTRA BUNGS

which hamper the Union from further developing the CFSP. This ambiguous
situation means that Latvia will have to manoeuvre among the positions of
various Member States within the EU, as well as remain aware of global policy
changes and react appropriately.
