GATIS PURIÒÐ

Thoughts about the Future of the EU:
from Market to State
Back in the early 1970s, when French President Georges
Pompidou first took to speaking airily of a ‘European Union’,
Foreign Minister Michel Jobert once asked his colleague
Eduard Balladur (the future French Premier) what exactly it
meant: ‘Nothing,’ replied Balladur. ‘But then that is the
beauty of it.’1

What exactly is the European Union (EU)? It is likely that no one can find a
very precise answer to this question. At best you will be told that it is a new
supranational entity in the history of the world, one that is still undergoing
development. No one will tell you what might be the possible or desirable
results of this development.
When the ‘fathers of Europe’ started to bring the continent together, they
always produced breathtaking visions, but the fact is that each major step toward integration has been the result of unwilling, difficult and usually long-term
bargaining. Results have always been based on pragmatism and considerations
which are of importance at the given moment.
This is where the ‘impossible’ contradictions of the EU appear. The visionary
nature of the ‘fathers’ who founded the EU has turned into a set of values
which seem not to coincide with the lowlier interests of politicians today. This
problem proves impossible to solve, however, only if we forget that the values
which were formulated back then represented a rationalisation of the interests
which prevailed at that time.
The end of the Cold War caught politicians throughout the world unaware,
and that applied to the European political elite just as much as to anyone else.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 279

Thoughts about the Future of the EU: from Market to State

279

This led to a seemingly qualitative leap toward the further development of a
unified Europe, apparently removing all contradictions between values and
interests. Germany’s reunification led to the emergence of the euro zone, and
the collapse of the Soviet empire forced Europe to seek out instruments for the
redistribution of spheres of influence. An unprecedented wave of EU integration and enlargement began, but it, too, developed without any defined goal.2
Declarations of values were offered instead.
Signatories to the Treaty of Rome derived proposed values from the interests
of their own countries. By comparison, declarations made by leaders today
tend to be abstract, it seems that they have little to do with political reality.
Instead, they serve as a smokescreen for market deals.3
It was only once the so-called EU Constitutional Treaty was rejected by
voters in France and the Netherlands that the European Union really took a
serious look at the question Quo vadis? The failure of the most serious attempt
by the EU to define itself was frightening in terms of what the future would
bring, but that has not disturbed politicians or the EU citizens living their lives
as if nothing had happened. People hope that the ‘perpetual peace’4 which has
existed for the last 60 years will last for at least one more ‘perpetuity’, as will
the nice welfare state in which they live. Tony Judt, a student of the post-war
history of Europe, argues, however, that the EU will not be able to survive
forever as an undefined phenomenon.5 The EU has always tended to flow
along with the stream of events, but this threatens its existence in the future. If
Europe does not define itself on the basis of its ‘national interests’, then it will
be defined by events caused by other rational actors of the international
system. This is even more important for Latvia than to the big powers of
Europe if it is not to fall victim to history once again.

1. The EU between market and state
60 years ago, on September 19, 1946, Winston Churchill delivered the historical address in which he called on the European nations to form the United
States of Europe.6 The project to create a unified Europe began four years later,
in 1950, and with greater or lesser effort, Member States have always been able
to reach agreement on issues of an economic nature. When talk has turned to
foreign policy and defence, however, Member States have almost always

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 280

280

GATIS PURIÒÐ

chosen to preserve the status quo, because these are issues which, by definition,
are the most important elements of an independent state. In June 2000, French
President Jacques Chirac appeared before the German Parliament and once
again confirmed the chilly attitude of national leaders toward Churchill’s
vision. Chirac said that what is needed is ‘a United Europe of States rather
than a ‘United States of Europe.’ He went on to say that ‘neither you [the
Germans] nor we [the French] envision the creation of a European super-state,
which would supplant our nation states and mark the end of their existence as
players in international life. In the future, too, our states will be the main point
of reference for our peoples.’7
The sociology of history tells us that economic integration is usually followed by political integration.8 In the case of Europe, however, there have been
material prerequisites for the next logical step, but the further development of
the Union has largely been hindered by various idealistic constructs. It is often
claimed that the cornerstone for a successful political community must be a
unified identity, with authors arguing that Europeans do not have any sense of
such a community. Identity, however, is largely an imagined concept or social
construct.9 It emerges from the need of individuals and groups to ensure cooperation in pursuit of interests, competing with other groups of individuals
in the battle over limited resources. This means that before we can understand
whether further EU integration is needed and, if so, how far that integration
should go, we must evaluate the likely interests of the EU as a unified and
rational actor on the international stage. Only then can considered policies be
prepared with the aim of creating a common European identity. One must
logically ask, in that case, whose interests the EU might satisfy. Theoretically,
there are at least four categories of interests – the interests of humanity, the
interests of the community of European citizens, the interests of EU Member
States and their citizens, and the interests of the EU as a structure, which exist
in parallel to those of its Member States.
1.1. A global (absolute) EU
The assumption that an organisation which represents one-fifteenth of the
world’s population might have the goal of representing the interests of
humanity seems a priori absurd, but the fact is that such ambitions are, at least,
partially derived from the idea of the EU as a normative power.10 The German

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 281

Thoughts about the Future of the EU: from Market to State

281

sociologist Ulrich Beck, for instance, has proposed the idea of a ‘cosmopolitan
state’ which differs from a traditional nation state in that it emphasises solidarity with foreigners both domestically and abroad.11 This is a utopian idea,
however, and it immediately poses two problems. First of all, why should the
EU citizens of EU Member States be concerned about the entire world?
Second, why should the world accept a situation in which the EU represents
its interests?
There are many psychological reasons why the idea that EU interests overlap with those of humanity may appear in European public discourse. First of
all, individuals and groups of individuals tend to generalise their interests and
apply them to other individuals or groups. In other words, people tend to
universalise their interests, declaring that things which serve the interests of an
individual or a group correspond to things which serve the interests of others –
in this case, those of humanity. Second, many Europeans seek to define an
identity which is quite separate from that of the United States, which dominates in the world. As part of this, they present themselves as defenders of the
Third World. Third, the US nuclear umbrella and the economic boom which was
the result of the US Marshall Plan created greenhouse conditions in Europe
which allowed Europeans to become accustomed to a lifestyle in which existential national interests disappeared and were replaced by so-called post-modern or
post-material values.12
If such psychologically based ideas are to be sustainable, and if they are to be
accepted in the rest of the world at least in part, two necessary but insufficient
conditions must be in place: (1) those who wish to represent global interests
must be part of a globally dominant power; (2) there must be sustainable
opportunities for general growth.13
On the one hand, the EU seems to have the greatest potential power if its
overall GDP is compared to that of other major powers. On the other hand, its
actual military power is fairly insignificant at this time, and it is continuing to
decline in relative terms, at least right now. Other major powers are enhancing
their military power and also experiencing a rapid increase in their latent
power. The EU, meanwhile, is experiencing economic stagnation.
The relative power of the EU is also reduced by the fact that despite its high
level of economic integration, it still cannot be seen as a unified structure in
political terms. The nation states which are its members are still the primary
actors in internal EU relations and in relations with other countries. At least in

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

282

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 282

GATIS PURIÒÐ

certain cases, the relationship among Member States does not add up positively, instead creating a zero sum game. The true power of the EU does not
represent the collective power of all of the Member States, which would be
shown in the following equation: N=n1+n2+… n27, where n refers to the power
of each individual Member State, while N refers to the total power of the EU.
Instead the equation is this: N=(n1+n2+…+n27)-[(n1-n’2)+(n1-n’3)+…(n1-n’25)+
(n2-n’1)+(n2-n’3)+…(n27-n’26)], where n’ represents the lost level of power
in contacts among the relevant Member States. This represents a zero sum
game.
Robert Kagan argues that this is specifically why ‘Europe is turning away
from power, or to put it a little differently, it is moving beyond power into a
self-contained world of laws and rules and transnational negotiations and cooperation. It is entering a post-historical paradise of peace and relative prosperity, the realisation of Kant’s ‘Perpetual Peace’.’14 At the same time, however,
the USA, according to Kagan, is still stuck in history and is continuing to focus
on traditional realpolitik because it has sufficient power to implement such
policies. At the regional level, other major powers are doing the same.15
The Iraq crisis, proved that the United States not only wanted to, but was
also able to pursue its own political course while ignoring the various rules of
international organisations. The EU, upon encountering this bit of reality, did
not present itself as a rational and unified actor. Instead, each Member State tried
to act in accordance with its particular ideas as to its own national interests.16
Eastern European countries, anxious of their own security, ‘amorally’ joined
the ‘aggressive’ invading force led by Washington. Paris, for its part, defended
the interests of French oil companies in Iraq, and it ‘morally’ refused to join in
‘aggression’ against a sovereign country. Because of these internal contradictions
and sense of insecurity, the EU was even unable to deal with the crisis in the
Balkans without US involvement – a territory which is in the EU sphere of
influence, both from the perspective of traditional realism and of the formal
distribution of power on the European continent today.
On the other hand, the rest of the world is not prepared to accept the EU as
a representative of its interests, even if the power deficit of the Union is not
taken into account. As Carr has pointed out, those who do not experience a
similar improvement in welfare think that the concept of harmonised interests
is hypocritical, and they revolt against it. When hegemonic domination of the
market is replaced by a system in which everyone competes against everyone

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 283

Thoughts about the Future of the EU: from Market to State

283

else, the idea of harmonised interests loses importance.17 Empirical data show
that most people in the world have no reason to feel satisfied with life.
At the turn of the 20th century, the inhabitants of the world’s richest countries
represented approximately 20% of the global population but consumed more
than 80% of global GDP. The inhabitants of the bottom 20% of the world’s poorest
countries, in turn, had to make do with just 1% of global GDP. It has been said
that the free movement of goods, finances and individuals covers the whole
world, that this economic activity should promote an overall expansion in
wealth. The reality is that the gap between the rich and the poor is not shrinking,
but it is expanding. In 1960, the top 20% of the wealthiest inhabitants of the world
had 30 times more resources than the bottom 20% of the poorest inhabitants,
but by the end of the 20th century the proportion was 78:1.18
There is also no reason to hope that the standard of living in the Third World
might rise to the current standards in the West, because the resources available
to humanity are limited. The global ecological system simply could not withstand such a burden.
The paucity of resources cannot be prevented unless it is possible to intensify
the use of existing ones or to find new resources which have not been available
until now. In either case, rich countries cannot reduce the consumption of
resources so as to redistribute them to the poor countries, because achievement
of one or other of the stated goals will require maximal use of existing resources in rich countries. This means that redistribution of the world’s material
resources is possible only on the basis of ethical or altruistic motivations or,
perhaps, through violence. A flourishing economy and its benefits are very
important resources for power, however, and it is quite questionable whether
rich countries will, for reasons of ethics, be prepared to waive their influence
in the international system voluntarily so as to help those who are impoverished
in other parts of the world.19
We must conclude, therefore, that even if the EU really is a new kind of
international actor, one which subordinates the interests of the group which it
represents to the interests of all of humanity as a species,20 the fact is that it
does not have the resources needed for such idealistic plans, and humanity is
not prepared to accept such ‘altruism’.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 284

284

GATIS PURIÒÐ

1.2. The EU as a community of citizens
The assumption that the EU is a rational and unitary player in the international system, one which represents the interests of its community of citizens,
seems to be far more logical than the aforementioned and purely theoretical
idea that it represents the interests of humanity. This, too, is a questionable
idea, however. Before the EU can really represent the interests of its citizens, it
must ensure two aspects. First of all, the community of EU citizens must exist
as a group of people who are really unified by their interests – people who can
understand unity and wish to maintain it on a sustainable basis. Second, there
must be a mechanism which makes it possible to formulate the group’s
common interests and project them in contacts with the external world or with
others (strangers) in the international system.
There is reason to doubt whether these two prerequisites could ever be
ensured. According to Judd, ‘because it [the EU] is not a state – because its citizens’ primary loyalties remain to the country in which they find themselves,
whose laws they obey, whose language they speak and whose taxes they pay –
the EU has no mechanism for determining or enforcing its own security interests. [..] The European Community and its successor the EU have for many
decades been extremely effective in advancing and defending their interests in
international forums and against foreign competitors. But those interests have
from the outset been defined in overwhelmingly economic – or more precisely,
protectionist – terms.’21
No matter how people try to explain the ‘no’ vote which was cast by the
citizens of France and the Netherlands in May and June 2005 when they were
asked to vote on the EU Constitutional Treaty,22 the fact is that voters in both
countries were also denying the existence of a community of European citizens. The vote reflected contradictions between the so-called ‘old’ EU Member
States and the Eastern European countries which had just joined the community of Europeans, as well as between the so-called Anglo-Saxon and the European social model. The director of the Centre for European Reform, Charles
Grant, has argued that voters did not have a clear sense of the goals of the EU’s
future existence, nor the real borders of that existence.23 Accordingly, there
was also no definition of who should be included in the community of ‘Europeans’. There was no sense of the characteristics of individuals who shape the
community – characteristics which would allow members to differentiate

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 285

Thoughts about the Future of the EU: from Market to State

285

between one’s own people and strangers. The ethnic researcher Jack Snyder,
writing about the theory of collective action, argues that ‘successful groups [..]
producing collective goods, such as the infrastructure of defence and economies, need boundaries to exclude non-contributors and to identify outsiders
who might act opportunistically in transactions with group members. They
also need boundaries to prevent crowding, which would diminish the value of
membership in the club.’24
On the one hand, the previously described inequality at the global level
continues to govern the internal affairs of the EU, albeit to a lesser extent. This
causes a diversification in the interests of Member States, and that makes it
more difficult to establish a common identity for EU citizens. On the other
hand, the situation is made all the more complex by the EU’s ‘endless’ process
of enlargement. Citizens in the ‘old’ EU have not yet been able to form a
common identity, and they have, with great difficulty, reconciled with the ‘noncontributors’ who have recently been admitted to the club (Polish plumbers,
for instance). Now they have to deal with new and ever new ‘non-payers’ who
are coming through the EU’s door. Bulgaria is an example of this. What is more,
each new round of enlargement means that the community of EU citizens will
be expanded with poorer people who bring with them even greater cultural
diversity.
What is more, consolidation of the European community of citizens is by
no means promoted by the discourse of political correctness which dominates
at this time, because the multicultural and cosmopolitan approach which is
proposed therein is in direct contradiction to the known psychological mechanisms for consolidation of large groups. This does not help in the social construction of European identity. This, in turn, allows political parties which are
populist and Euro-sceptical to enhance their popularity.
In addition to the fact that there is a shortage of psychological mechanisms
to encourage the emergence of a European community of citizens, there are
also very weak mechanisms for political consolidation. The only representative institution which all EU Member States have in common is the European
Parliament. This could be an analogue to national legislative institutions, but
in fact it is, at best, a consultative institution. The existence of the European
Parliament at such a politically unimportant level does not promote the emergence of a community of EU citizens. On the contrary, it has a negative effect
on any such process.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

286

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 286

GATIS PURIÒÐ

The EU is not a federal structure, and this means that the European Parliament, even though it is a directly elected unicameral body of representatives,
cannot depict the proportions of political forces in the EU’s Member States. If
the European Parliament, for instance, is dominated by right wing parties, that
by no means suggests that the same is true in all of the parliaments of Member
States. Political power truly rests in the hands of national parliaments, and
nothing has changed in this regard. This means that EU Member States may be
pursuing rightist and leftist policies all at the same time, but if there is not a
federal mechanism to reduce the political contradictions and to reach a level of
harmonisation, the relationship among Member States becomes contradictious,
and this problem can even be exacerbated artificially.
The absence of a federal structure also means that there are no mechanisms
which, in a democracy, ensure that the minority yields before the majority.
Suppose that the European Parliament, while dominated by politicians from a
certain wing of the political spectrum, approves a declaration which is not
legally binding but does foment political discourse. Suppose further that the
declaration is in contradiction to the positions taken by a Member State which
is ruled by politicians from the opposite political wing. In that case, the majority
of citizens in that Member State will consider the EU’s institutions to be alien
and hostile, they will feel that those institutions should be attacked. This may
encourage people to have a hostile perception of other Member States and their
citizens, too, thus deepening the existing objective contradictions. A democratic
nation state with more or less stable political processes ensures the so-called
political pendulum – power is taken by right wing and left wing politicians in
turn. At the level of the EU, by contrast, the political pendulum is by no means
synchronised.
It must be understood here that the unsynchronised nature of the political
pendulum in the EU is enhanced by the unsynchronised nature of economic
cycles. Different Member States are at different phases of the economic cycle.
When there are politically determined contradictions in the economic courses
that are pursued by the various countries – ones which are enhanced by the
unsynchronised nature of economic cycles – protectionism becomes the simplest
and most predictable response. The rejection of the European Constitutional
Treaty, particularly by France in May 2005, largely represented the protectionist
reaction of ‘old’ Europe against the lower labour costs, relatively younger
populations and lower tax rates of the new Member States of the European

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 287

Thoughts about the Future of the EU: from Market to State

287

Union. The fact is that these advantages have ensured that the newcomers are
serious competitors in the battle over attracting investments.25
EU citizenship as a political instrument is also nothing more than a derivation of citizenship in the Member States. This encourages citizens to link their
primary identity to the Member State in which they live, although on the other
hand, as the different Member States have diverse rules as to naturalisation
and immigration, it is difficult for the indigenous population of a single Member State to identify with the newly created citizens of another Member State.
For instance, if Germany were to naturalise the Turks who live in that country,
at least some ethnic Germans would perceive them as ‘political Germans’, but
it is likely that no Latvians would perceive them as Germans or Europeans,
and vice versa.26
All of this allows us to conclude that none of the initially mentioned prerequisites exists in reality, or perhaps these prerequisites (a group of people
unified by interests who understand and wish to maintain this unity, as well as
a mechanism which makes it possible to formulate the group’s common interests and to project these in contacts with others, with aliens) are still in their
infancy. Neither are there concepts as to the establishment or construction of
these prerequisites – concepts which are defined and more or less politically
and publicly accepted. The assumption that the EU represents the interests of
its citizens’ community in the international system, in other words, is false.
1.3. The supranational EU (a Europe of Nations)
If, as is suggested above, the EU cannot be seen as a representative of humanity or even of its citizens’ community in the international system, then it is
only logical to assume that the EU represents its Member States and, via them,
the interests of the citizens of those Member States. This suggests that when
the EU presents itself as a participant in the international system, it is more of
a traditional international organisation, more of a ‘regional UN’. The UN,
however, is an organisation in which only 15 of the 191 member states make
up the Security Council, which is the only part of the organisation which has
the right to take binding decisions on security issues. Only five countries,
furthermore, are permanent members of the Security Council, and only they
have the right of veto. By comparison, all 27 of the EU’s Member States have
veto rights.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

288

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 288

GATIS PURIÒÐ

As has been noted before, the interests of EU Member States are by no means
homogeneous. Each Member State has veto rights on foreign policy issues,
and that means that in the international system, the EU can, at best, represent
those interests which are the lowest common denominator among the Member
States. The veto rights could be eliminated through the process of so-called
qualified majority voting. In that case, there could be no more talk about the
idea that the EU represents the interests of all of its Member States, because the
interests of some Member States would be excluded in certain situations.
Ignoring the national interests of an actor in international politics if those
interests concern national security and survival is an example of casus belli.
Another problem is differentiating between the interests of Member States
and those of their citizens. EU Member States are democratic by definition, so
their interests should reflect those of the majority of citizens, not those of a
narrowly drawn political elite. If the interests of the majority of citizens are
excluded, then there is reason to question the idea of a common EU society.
There are people who face discrimination because of their nationality, for instance.
This is yet another obstacle against the emergence of a unified community of
EU citizens.
The EU could also serve as a forum for the national interests of its Member
States, which is what the UN does. In that case, the vector for the EU as an
organisation and, even more, as a rational and unitary participant in the international system becomes unclear. If interests of Member States coincide, further
integration is possible, but on the other hand, if interests are contradictory, then
the Union must continue to exist in latent form or even collapse altogether. We
all know that power in the international system is dynamic, it tends to increase
or decrease both in absolute and in relative terms. This applies to individual
countries, including EU Member States, and it applies to relations with third
party countries. This means that the projection of national interests can be
short-lasting. Accordingly, the existence of the EU as a forum of interests is in
contradiction with the ultima ratio of its establishment – limitation of nationalism
as a result of the fact that if individual countries have national interests, the
moment inevitably arrives at which contradictions become insuperable and the
continent must return to the situation which governed before both world wars,
i.e., it must deal with circumstances of historical anarchy.
In a situation of anarchy, an alliance is the typical result of the common
national interests of various countries. There are both formal and factual

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 289

Thoughts about the Future of the EU: from Market to State

289

reasons to doubt whether the EU might exist as this kind of expression of
common interests. The 1992 Maastricht Treaty speaks to the Common Foreign
and Security Policy (CFSP), and the 2001 Nice Treaty clearly defines the need
to establish a common EU defence, but the fact is that the EU and its Member
States do not offer any security guarantees to each other.
From the perspective of security policy, in other words, the EU is more reminiscent of a collective security system. Henry Kissinger has pointed out that
unlike alliances which ‘are created when a group of countries decides to defend
a territory or fundamental principles’ or when ‘a line is drawn and it is declared
that crossing it is a casus belli [..], a collective security system does not define
the territory to be protected or the resources to be used for that purpose; it is
first and foremost a purely legal concept. [..] When all participants agree, the
need for collective security is minimal; when their views differ, it is impossible
to implement it.’27
It is understandable, therefore, that once the Cold War came to an end, the
existing and potential EU Member States which felt a deficit in security
decided to join the Transatlantic security community instead of creating a new
alliance within Europe.
One can claim, accordingly, that the EU presents itself as a representative of
its Member States only in part when it comes to the international system. It
does so only in those cases when the interests of all Member States coincide.
1.4. The bureaucratic EU
If the EU does not pursue the interests of humanity or its community of citizens in international contacts, or if it pursues the interests of Member States
only to a limited extent, then one can assume that the process is based on the
interests of an entity or actor that is diverse from aforementioned ones. In
other words, we could assume that the EU is an entity which stands apart from
its Member States and citizens. Usually this separation is perceived through
the concept of ‘the bureaucracy of Brussels.’
Any organisation, irrespective of its initial goals, expresses its specific interests
over the course of time. These interests are based on the organisation’s need
for self-reproduction and expansion. Brian C. Smith has written about major
administrative organisations as political systems which stand apart from various
interest groups. Smith argues that various interest groups form coalitions with

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

290

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 290

GATIS PURIÒÐ

certain parts of an administration so as to influence the political agenda and
the decisions that are taken. Bureaucratic agencies, for their part, pursue their
specific interests through the support of their clients. This means that bureaucracy can mobilise and regulate pressure groups so as to influence the development of public policy. A modern country demands a larger bureaucratic
apparatus, and this expands the capacity of ‘expertise’ of this apparatus in
comparison to political leaders. Indeed, the structures often have a monopoly
on ‘expertise,’ because laws that are approved by politicians cannot deal with
every possible contingency, the values of individual bureaucrats inevitably
have an effect on the way in which policies are implemented. Each agency
tries to maximise its power and the resources which are given to it. Smith also
emphasises, however, that bureaucrats are paid by the state, and their jobs
tend to be strictly and, usually, narrowly defined.28
Bureaucracy, thus, can perhaps be seen as another actor in the process, one
with its separate and specific interests. On the other hand, dependency upon
the state is a prerequisite for the existence of bureaucracy. This, in turn, means
that the interests of bureaucracies cannot be in contradiction to the interests of
the states which finance them.
In the EU, there is no political superstructure of this kind, and this can create
the impression that bureaucracy in the Union is autonomous. It is premature,
however, to declare bureaucratic ‘sovereignty’ in the EU, because the fact is
that EU bureaucrats operate exclusively on the basis of authorisation from the
political elites of the various Member States, and representatives of those elites,
in turn, are dependent upon their voters. Any authorisation, after all, can be
withdrawn. If the EU were to cause serious losses to a significant share of citizens
in a Member State, the threat of withdrawing authorisation would become
very real, indeed. If the losses occurred in a large and wealthy Member State,
then financing could be withdrawn, too. Consider the United Nations. Its leading
source of financing has always been the United States, but the USA has been
using UN budget subsidies for some time now to influence the organisation’s
policies. In 2005, other wealthy western countries began to do the same, demanding bureaucratic reforms at the UN. Voters are likely to support their governments in such cases, because governments provide them with existentially
important public products – security first and foremost. According to Michael
Reisman, ‘international bureaucracy lacks the resources and the incentives
necessary to fulfil the essential value demands that individuals make on their

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 291

Thoughts about the Future of the EU: from Market to State

291

political communities: security, productive economic arrangements, health
services and the various ‘safety nets’ in the event of recession or calamity.’29
This means that EU Member States and their citizens may well review their
relationship with the EU if it turns into a parallel bureaucratic apparatus
which pursues interests that are regularly in conflict with those of the Member
States and citizens. Authorisation can be limited, financing can be halted, or
Member States may even decide to withdraw from the EU or to talk to other
Member States about shutting the Union down entirely. The EU bureaucracy
can pursue its particular interests only insofar as these are not in serious
contraction to the majority of citizens in the majority of EU Member States.
1.5. The EU as a sui generis structure30
As noted, there are certain difficulties in determining those interests which
the EU might represent as an actor in the international system. Students of the
EU are seldom bemused by this fact, because the EU is often described as a
fundamentally new (sui generis) actor on the international stage. Specialists try
to differentiate between the EU as an institution on the one hand and its interests on the other. Sometimes they even seek to substitute values for interests.
This represents an effort to construct a political entity which simultaneously
serves as a model for an ideal world where values prevail over interests. This
ideal world would be governed by a harmony of interests, and things would
be done because they are good, not because they are necessary. The problem is
that the concept of needs would have to be eliminated before this goal could
be achieved – needs which have always been the decisive factor in the international system.
One of the prophets of such idealistic constructions is Ian Manners, who has
proposed that the EU be perceived as a normative power that can be compared to previous leading actors in the international system – those which used
to be known as superpowers and which derived their influence specifically
from military power. The main component for the normative power of Europe,
argues Manners, is the fact that the EU differs from previous political entities
and that this encourages it to act in a normative way. Manners believes that
there are three reasons why this is true. First there is the historical context –
after the Second World War, the EU was established through a process in which
Member States ceded sovereignty so as to rid themselves of nationalism.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

292

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 292

GATIS PURIÒÐ

Second, the EU is a hybrid entity – it uses both supranational and national
forms of governance. Finally, the constitutional norms of the EU include the
principles of democracy, the rule of law, human rights and social justice, and
this shapes the Union’s international identity.31
Robert Cooper, a former foreign policy advisor to British Prime Minister
Tony Blair, argues that the EU Member States have become post-modern states.
These, he writes, are typified by the following things:
1) no differences between domestic and foreign policy;
2) mutual intervention by countries in areas which have traditionally been
seen as internal affairs, along with mutual oversight;
3) rejection of the use of force in dealing with mutual conflicts, instead
making consistent use of a codified system of self-control in observing
the rules of international behaviour;
4) less meaning for borders, both because of shifting roles for states and of
the development of modern technologies;
5) security is based on transparency, mutual openness and dependency, as
well as mutual vulnerability.32
These and similar ideas could be considered seriously, perhaps, if they
were applied only to politics within the EU. Once they are applied to the global
international system, however, it is easy to see that other actors do not perform
on the basis of rules which are proposed by the idealists. This means that implementation of those rules would require overwhelming power. The EU
could then be compared to empires of the past in establishing Pax Europeana,
just as the Roman Empire established Pax Romana, and the British Empire
formed Pax Britannica. That, of course, would by no means correspond to the
ideas which supporters of normative Europe hold when it comes to that which
is good and that which is evil.33
When the literature presents the EU as a normative power, the dominant
view is that the EU prefers civilian to military instruments. Back in 1972,
Francois Duchêne proposed the idea that Europe could expand its model of
stability and security through economic and political, not military resources.
This, according to Duchêne, created the power of the European Community as
a new international actor.34 As has been pointed out, however, Kantian Europe
is perhaps Kantian specifically because of its weakness.
Descriptions of the EU as a normative power are often based on comparisons to the United States, which openly uses military force to disseminate its

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 293

Thoughts about the Future of the EU: from Market to State

293

value system globally and to create Pax Americana. At the same time, there is a
long-lasting tradition of describing US policy as idealistic.35 I do not see any
reason to think that the EU is fundamentally different from the USA. Both
were established on the basis of ideals and common values which represented
a rationalisation of the important interests of the specific period in time.
In drawing parallels between the EU and the USA, moreover, it must also
be remembered that the history of the latter is four times longer than the
history of the former. This suggests that in its future development, the EU may
pursue a similar course of action to that which was pursued by the USA. At
the beginning, the USA was not a state, it was a sui generis entity which, to a very
large extent, was similar to the transitory EU of the present day. The situation in
the USA differed at that time, however, in that it was geographically isolated
from the international system.36 As Kissinger has noted, that was a time when
American leaders ‘imagined a world in which countries behaved as partners
in co-operation, not as untrustworthy competitors. American leaders rejected
the European idea that the morals of states are based on criteria other than
those of the morals of the individual.’37 Thomas Paine observed that war is
nothing more than the result of a poor system of governance and that war can
be prevented if governments are based on ‘the right principles.’38 When modern
European integration began, the geographic isolation of the USA was replaced
or compensated by the Iron Curtain and the American nuclear umbrella. The
welfare which this created for Europeans allowed them to develop the illusion
of transition toward a post-modern world.
Under these isolated circumstances of post-modernity, relations among
European countries were largely depoliticised, and this created the illusion
that the political regularities described in international relations by realists
were no longer valid. According to Carr, the term ‘political’ is not to be applied
to all of the activities of a state, but only to those which relate to the conflict of
power. Once a conflict is resolved, the issue is no longer ‘political’, it is instead
one of a routine administrative nature. When countries work with one another
to provide postal or transport services, to prevent the spread of disease or
drugs trafficking, these are ‘non-political’ or technical activities. As soon as such
an issue involves the power of one country in relation to the power of another,
however, the issue once again becomes political.39 Politics returned to international relations when the Cold War ended and the discipline of alliances created
under the aspects of a bipolar confrontation deteriorated.

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

294

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 294

GATIS PURIÒÐ

The distribution of power in the world today probably does not satisfy
anyone, because:
1) the ‘West’ has no absolute and global monopoly on power, which means
that the ‘West’ is always threatened by those countries which are dissatisfied with its dominance in the international system or with the
distribution of resources in the world. In line with the theory of a balance
of power, it would be logical if the ‘West’:
a) tried to enhance its global positions on the basis of its current
dominance,
b) tried to prevent the emergence of a unified ‘anti-Western’ alliance,
instead seeking to make use of regional contradictions and attracting
individual non-Western countries as allies or compradors;
2) countries which are unhappy with the dominance of the ‘West’ have
limited resources of power, and so they are trying to:
a) avoid any open conflict with the ‘West’ while simultaneously trying
to achieve the status of hegemony in their own region so as to
increase their power in the international system,
b) make use of the internal contradictions of the ‘West’ so as to prohibit any consolidation of the ‘West’ in a way which would ensure
its irreversible hegemony in the international system.
That is why ‘Western’ countries are countries of the status quo in that they
maintain or enhance their dominant positions in the international system which
prevails today. Other, ‘non-Western’ countries, thus, can be seen as revisionist
countries. It is likely that in the future, ‘the main problem will be the disappointment of countries which are catching up with the West. Eventually they
will doubt the sensibility of their hurriedly altered socio-political and economic
foundations. There will also be increasing offence among those elements
which have been rejected by society. They will respond to forced attempts at
recultursation with flashes of violence. The time will come for a ‘conflict among
civilisations’, for a rebellion of the poor against the rich, for a violent redistribution of the shrinking resources of the world.’40 Under such circumstances
any desire to preserve the EU as a sui generis entity would be suicidal.
‘Unified Europe’ emerged as a direct response to the Cold War, and its form
was in line with the ‘quasi-war’ circumstances which prevailed at that time.41
When the Cold War ended, this was no longer possible, and the EU had to
adapt to circumstances of ‘quasi-peace’. Forms appropriate for an anarchic

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 295

Thoughts about the Future of the EU: from Market to State

295

environment had to be defined – a conglomerate of unevenly integrated countries
had to be transformed into a federation of countries, into the ‘United States of
Europe.’ The EU can exist as a sui generis entity only in transitory terms. Sooner
or later it must return to normalcy, to the status of a state. The only question is
whether the EU will become a super-state or whether it will once again be
replaced by the major powers of the European continent competing amongst
themselves.

2. The new European project: from market to state
Everyone seems to understand that ‘the influence of [European countries]
in the future will depend on how they manage to strengthen their unification
in the European Union. A unified Europe will be a major power once again. If
it breaks up into nation states, it will have only secondary status,’ as Henry
Kissinger has put it.42 In the official discourse, however, the establishment of a
federative Europe is being replaced by something that is called ‘deeper integration.’ Preservation of the sovereignty of EU Member States is also being
emphasised, as noted before. Since political power and the resources of that
power are still organised on the basis of nation states, power is still concentrated
in London, Paris and Berlin, not in Brussels, if we look at the EU from the
perspective of international relations.
I believe that there are three fundamentally important circumstances which
hinder any clear formulation of the further developmental goals of the EU:
1) there are still contradictions among Member States, there is a divergence
in their understanding of the nature of threats in the international environment;
2) leaders in Member States are dependent on national electorates which
do not wish to yield their political control by delegating the political
power of their national leaders to the impersonal and alien bureaucracy
of the EU;
3) the political leaders of Member States, too, do not wish to transfer power
to the EU level without closely monitoring it, because at the Union level,
no clear mechanisms for delegating power exist, and neither are there
any clear promises as to the political careers of national leaders.
The opportunities which were created by the establishment of the European Convention, alas, were wasted. The very first mistake was that the

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

296

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 296

GATIS PURIÒÐ

‘fathers’ of the Convention immediately turned to the preparation of a ‘European Constitution’ instead of using their far-reaching mandate to launch a
pan-European discussion about the goals and policies of the EU in the 21st
century. The Convention failed in creating a document which could be seen as
a true Constitution from the legal and the political perspective.43 This mistake
was based on the desire to discuss the EU at the theoretical level through institutional and policy analysis, concentrating on abstract administrative schemes
and ignoring existentially important issues. This was true even though, as the
founding fathers of the United States put it, ‘the issue concerns nothing more
or less than the survival of the Union, of its security, of the welfare of its components, and of the destiny of what, in many ways, is the most interesting
empire in the world.’44
The idea of ‘Europe’s core’ can be seen as a certain attempt to overcome these
obstacles. On May 31, 2003, two of the giants of European intellectual discourse,
Jürgen Habermas and Jacques Derrida, proposed their ideas on the future of a
unified Europe and its foreign policy: ‘During the Brussels Convention, the opposition has become plain to see among those who seek a genuine strengthening
of the EU and those who have an understandable interest in freezing the existing mode of intergovernmental administration as it currently exists, or at best
in only making cosmetic changes to it. Now the existence of this animosity can
no longer be hidden. [..] To avoid the disintegration of Europe, these countries
[‘of core’] must immediately enact the mechanism of ‘enforced co-operation’
established at Nice in order to form not only a common foreign policy but also
a common security and defence policy. [..] This will create a benchmark which
other members will not long be able to ignore.’45
Six months later the leaders of France and Germany were calling for an
enhanced process of EU integration through the creation of the core of Europe.
This would mean closer relations among these countries in institutional terms
(the possibility of a common military and foreign service, a seat at the UN
Security Council) and at the political level (common positions and actions).
Understandably, this bit of news upset some of the EU existing and future
Member States, because such integration would mean the emergence of a powerful political and economic centre within the EU. The initiative has all but collapsed at this time, even though it is still frequently discussed in the public
discourse. The idea of a European core was intended to promote integration,
but in reality it simply expanded the gap between the old and the new

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 297

Thoughts about the Future of the EU: from Market to State

297

Member States, increasingly pushing Eastern Europeans toward the United
States in terms of security concerns.
These results are only logical, because the first of the aforementioned obstacles
against the further unification of Europe cannot be removed unless the two
other problems are resolved together – i.e., if there is no debate about the transformation of the structure of EU political power, introducing clear principles
of federalism instead.
First of all, the EU must get rid of its limited perception of security policy.
Right now that policy is focused mostly on peacekeeping operations in Third
World countries which are of little significance to the citizens of Europe – the
Democratic Republic of Congo, for instance. From the perspective of international
politics, this demands the establishment of unimportant joint police forces.
Expansion of the perception of security must be based on a return to the
traditional theory of international relations. Threats must be seen where they
are – in large countries which exist outside the post-modern world. These are
seeking to consume a significant share of limited resources, and as they become
stronger in economic terms, they are also accumulating military power which,
in the future, could be turned against Europe’s national interests. As the discourse on security is expanded, Europe must simultaneously constitute itself
as a unified and rational actor on the international policy stage. I believe that
there is only one way to overcome the existing alienation between the EU and
its citizens, as well as among the EU Member States — the creation of a political
structure which allows the citizens of the EU Member States to formulate and
agree on interests which they have in common while also regulating contradictions which exist. Such a structure must include a bicameral parliament,
one which involves representation of political forces, as well as equal representation for Member States. There must also be an executive structure or
‘cabinet’ that is responsible before this parliament. Furthermore, the competences of the federal institutions and federal countries (into which existing EU
Member States would turn) must be strictly defined and separated.46
A federal EU would seemingly diminish the influence of individual Member
States, but the importance of the citizens of all Member States would increase,
and inevitably there would be a confluence of currently diverse national interests. The eastern border of Latvia would be perceived by EU citizens as the
eastern border of their own country, not as that of Latvia, which is found
somewhere in Eastern Europe. It is also very unlikely that a European super-state

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

298

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 298

GATIS PURIÒÐ

would allow any third party to interfere in the internal affairs of the union or
its federal lands. This would eliminate the situation which Latvia faces now –
ongoing complaints about the supposed violation of international human
rights which are inspired by third countries.
If, in this context, I ask myself what I want and do not want for the EU, then
as an individual, I can certainly say that as a consumer, I want security and a
full stomach. As a shareholder in the company which is called ‘Latvia’, I do not
want anonymous bureaucrats in Brussels to impinge upon my national identity by interfering in the way in which my country grows strawberries or
dictating the size of the berries.
I believe that the interests of Latvia, as a small country, would be well served
if ‘high politics’ were transferred from London, Paris and Berlin to Strasbourg
and Brussels. Then we might finally be able to participate in the development
of those policies. When Brussels starts to deal exclusively with ‘high politics’,
it will probably leave the size of strawberries up to the federal states, which
will be in line with the principle of subsidiarity. Who will care about strawberry plants if the debate is about bombing Teheran? Politicians from the larger
countries will find that such a federal Europe opens up tempting career opportunities. If someone were to become prime minister of the European superstate, Europeans would at long last be able to feel that they are equals when
that prime minister speaks to the man or woman in the White House.
Paradoxically, but it is specifically the small countries of Europe which are
most interested in the further development of federalism in the EU. The process which we know as globalisation does not reduce the strength of the state
as an institution. On the contrary, it demands greater strength. Big European
countries cannot hope to balance out the hegemony of the USA in the foreseeable future, but they do find their place in the international system. Small
countries, by comparison, have limited resources of military, economic and
political power, and these are insufficient to allow any small country to be
truly independent in dealing with the challenges of the modern world. As Carr
has pointed out, utopians sometimes denounce the ‘alleged “dictatorship of
the Great Powers”’ as an immoral policy which is occasionally pursued consciously by certain countries, but the fact is that the process instead creates
something similar to the ‘law of nature’ in international politics.47 This is
manifested through a situation in which smaller and weaker countries gather
around larger and stronger ones. If Latvia wishes to survive in the anarchic

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 299

Thoughts about the Future of the EU: from Market to State

299

environment of the present day, it will have to count on this law as it pursues
its foreign policies. Given that the interests of states can shift, it is in the interest
of Latvia to find itself in a more stable entity than a group of friends.
I see little point to the European effort to sniff at the prospects that are
proposed by US history, choosing instead to present Europe as something unique
in the history of the world. As Jonathan Clark wrote in The Times during the
European Convention, ‘the true discussion occurred and was concluded long
ago. The true discussion of the advantages of federalism occurred in 1787 and
1788, when the United States of America debated ratification of their Constitution.’48 While the Americans agreed to establish a unified country, the Europeans remained apart, and over the last 100 years, they have carefully forged
US hegemony.49 This creates such great concern among European intellectuals
who, at the same time, are snooty in rejecting any use of American resources in
the forging of European power.
Were Europe to transform itself into a state, that would resolve the last confusing issue about Europe’s borders and community of its citizens. If Europe
reconstituted itself into a state, further enlargement would be limited both
internally and externally. Admission to the club, which currently are determined
by a narrow political elite, would be controlled through the mechanisms of
democracy by the people of Europe. On the other hand, other countries would
no longer see EU enlargement as a situation in which new Member States join
an international organisation or, at worst, as a situation in which the sphere of
influence of the EU’s major powers expands. Instead, this would be seen as
annexation, and military confrontation could be the only possible response to
it – provided that other concerned states had sufficient power and resources.50
If ‘us’ are kept apart from strangers (‘them’), that creates good conditions
for solidarity among club members, as opposed to competition against the
outer world. The raison d’être for the EU’s ongoing existence must be sought
out in statehood, which would give Europe sufficient power resources in the
battle over its national interests in the international system. The continent will
be protected against the possible return of anarchy, and this political entity
will be given purpose and value(s) in the eyes of its citizens.
In conclusion, I should like to refer to words that were spoken by one of the
fathers of the United States, Alexander Hamilton, in 1787: ‘Is it not time to awake
from the deceitful dream of a golden age and to adopt as a practical maxim for
the direction of our political conduct that we, as well as the other inhabitants

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 300

300

GATIS PURIÒÐ

of the globe, are yet remote from the happy empire of perfect wisdom and
perfect virtue?’51 When Europeans try to determine the goals of further EU
existence, they should understand that they are in the same phase of history
which their American brethren faced more than 200 years ago. The future of
Europe depends on the courage of the Europeans, on their ability to overcome
their narrow national interests in pursuit of common interests. As the founder
of unified Europe, Robert Schuman, put it when discussing the situation in his
own country at the end of the Second World War, if France did not join in some
broader alliance, ‘Malthusianism will wipe it from the face of the Earth.’52

The authors

Daunis Auers is a lecturer at the Department of Political Science , University
of Latvia. He earned his Ph.D. from the School of Slavonic and East European
Studies at University College London, and his Master’s degree at the London
School of Economics. He is the vice-president for academic development of
the Association for the Advancement of Baltic Studies. He was a Fulbright
scholar at the University of California, Berkeley, in the 2005–2006 academic
year. His major research interests focus on political parties, party systems and
the democratisation of post-Communist countries, as well as higher education
policy, and European Union enlargement.
Dzintra Bungs is the author of many studies about the Baltic States and the
USSR which have been published by Radio Liberty and Radio Free Europe.
After the restoration of Latvia’s independence, she focused mostly on issues
concerning the Baltic States, the European Union and NATO. Dr Bungs is the
author of the book ‘The Baltic States: Problems and Prospects of EU Membership’,
which was published in 1998 by the Foundation for Knowledge and Policy in
Germany. She is a Senior Research Fellow for the Latvian Institute of International Affairs and a holder of the Latvian Order of Three Stars.
Ivars Ijabs is a doctoral student and lector at the Department of Political
Science of the University of Latvia. He has studied philosophy and political
science in Latvia, Germany and Iceland and is the author of many scholarly
and popular articles, as well as translations of academic literature. Ivars Ijabs
has received scholarships from the Eurofaculty Rîga, the Konrad Adenauer
Fund and the University of Latvia (the Morbergs scholarship). His research

Latvijas_skat_uz_ES_anglu.qxd

The authors

12.02.2008

10:00

Page 307

307

interests focus on the discourse of the civil society in modern political theory,
the history of political ideas in Latvia, and analysis of political discourse.
Ivars Indâns received a Master’s degree in political science at the University of
Latvia and is a researcher for the Latvian Institute of International Affairs. His
research interests focus on the development of public processes in the context
of international relations. He has written about immigration and free movement of labour, as well as public integration processes in the context of
European Union policy. Ivars Indâns has served as an expert in evaluating EU
Structural Fund projects in the area of employment – a process which is organised by the Latvian Ministry of Welfare.
Andrejs Jakobsons received his Master’s degree in economics at the Indiana
University in the United States and currently is a researcher for the Baltic
International Centre for Economic Policy Studies (BICEPS). His research focuses
on macroeconomic analysis, the international economy and the labour market.
Andrejs Jakobsons is presently participating in a Welfare Ministry study, ‘An
Optimal System of Taxes and Allowances to Promote Employment.’
Kristîne Krûma is a lecturer at the Riga School of Law and a doctoral student
at Lund University in Sweden. She has been a guest researcher at the Centre
for Migration Rights at Radboud University (the Netherlands) and the Max
Planck Institute for Comparative Public Law and International Law in Heidelberg (Germany). She has also been trainee in the Legal Service of the European
Commission. Kristîne Krûma is a member of the Odysseus Network, which is
an academic network for legal studies on immigration and asylum in Europe.
She also is on the Editorial Board for the Baltic Yearbook of International Law
and a member of the young scholars forum of the Strategic Analysis Commission. In January 2007, she was nominated by the Cabinet of Ministers to be
Latvian arbitrator of the OSCE Court of Reconciliation and Arbitration.
Atis Lejiòð is the founder and director of the Latvian Institute of International
Affairs, specialising in Transatlantic relations, the EU Common Foreign and
Security Policy, the EU Neighbourhood Policy, and energy security. He has
taken part in international working groups on reforms of Georgia’s security
sector and the elaboration of a security strategy for Armenia. Atis Lejiòð is a

The authors

member of the Bertelsmann Foundation’s ‘Strategy Group on the Future of

and CFSP.
Professor Þaneta Ozoliòa is head of the Department of Political Science of the
University of Latvia and chairwoman of the Strategic Analysis Commission of
the President of Latvia. Her research interests focus on European integration,
EU and NATO enlargement, international and regional security, as well as
security in the Baltic Sea region. Dr Ozoliòa is the author of more than 70 scholarly articles and editor of several publications. She serves on the Foreign
Affairs Council of the Latvian Foreign Ministry and the Consulting Council of
the Latvian Institute. She has served as an expert in the drafting of Latvia’s
National Development Plan and Latvia’s growth model. She regularly delivers
lectures at foreign universities.
Gatis Pelnçns is a Master’s degree student in political science at the University
of Latvia. He earned his bachelor’s degree in political science in 2006. He is coauthor of a book on the Baltic Sea region after the enlargement of the European
Union. In November 2006 he took second place in a competition of scholarly
articles for students, ‘Security Challenges and NATO in the 21st Century’, with
his paper ‘NATO in the War on Terrorism’. Since August 2006, Gatis Pelnçns is
working as a senior desk officer at the Planning Control and Analysis division
of the Latvian Defence Ministry’s Policy and Planning Department. His research
focuses on security, international politics, terrorism, environmental policy and
the Baltic Sea region.
Gatis Puriòð received his Master’s degree in political science at the University
of Latvia in 2006 after defending the thesis ‘The European Union as an Actor in
International Relations: A View from the Perspective of Realism in International Relations Theory.’ He works as an analyst for the Latvijas Fakti social
research company and is working toward his doctor’s degree.
Iveta Reinholde earned her doctorate in political science at the University of
Latvia and is an assistant professor at the Department of Political Science of
the same university. Her research interests focus on issues of public governance and administration in Latvia and the European Union. She has studied

the pace and effectiveness of governance reform in Latvia, and she has reviewed

Toms Rostoks earned his Bachelor’s and Master’s degrees in political science
at the University of Latvia and is now a lector at the university’s Department
of Political Science. He has studied at the Northern European Institute of
Humboldt University in Berlin and, as a Fulbright scholar, at Rutgers University in the United States. Toms Rostoks has authored several scholarly
publications on regional co-operation in the Baltic Sea region, Kaliningrad,
relations between Latvia and Russia, Latvia’s interests in the European Union,
and Latvia’s developmental co-operation policy. His research interests concern
regional co-operation in the Baltic Sea region, analysis of Latvia’s foreign
policy, and the external relations of the EU.
Inga Ulnicâne-Ozoliòa is a researcher for the Advanced Social and Political
Research Institute of the University of Latvia and the Centre for Science and
Technology Studies of the Latvian Academy of Sciences. She has participated
in international and European research projects on innovation and science
policies and evaluations, the development of universities, and the field of new
technologies. She teaches international political economy and public administration at the University of Latvia and is pursuing her doctorate at the Central
European University in Budapest. Her Bachelor’s and Master’s degrees are in
political science. Inga Ulnicâne-Ozoliòa has also studied at universities and
research institutes in Oslo, Umeå, Prague, Madrid, Hamburg and Brussels. She
is active in the European Political Science Network epsNet.
